# Commentary: *Erste Philosophie I* (1923/24): *Kritische Ideengeschichte*

This commentary works paragraph by paragraph through the main text of the first volume of Husserl's *Erste Philosophie* lecture course (Husserliana VII), covering the First Part, *Kritische Ideengeschichte* (Critical History of Ideas), from the opening lecture (First Lecture) through the transition from Descartes to Locke (Eleventh Lecture). This corresponds to Husserliana VII, pages 3–77. Citations follow the Husserliana VII pagination printed in the margins of the critical edition; page numbers are given at the head of each lecture and at major transitions. The unit of commentary is the typographically distinct paragraph, the native unit of Husserl's continuous lecture prose. Headings are the editor's reconstructed Parts, Sections, Chapters, and lecture-titles; only Husserl's own divisions are reproduced, translated into English.

A cluster of characteristic traps besets this text, and the commentary guards against them throughout. First, Husserl's "history" is not historiography but *teleological* reconstruction: he reads the past as the gradual self-clarification of a single idea — philosophy as rigorous, self-justifying universal science — so his Plato, Aristotle, and Descartes are figures interpreted "from the present" toward phenomenology, not portraits answerable to philological scholarship. Second, *Erste Philosophie* must not be collapsed into Aristotelian *prōtē philosophia* or into "metaphysics"; Husserl revives the term precisely to drain it of those sediments and refill it as a discipline of *beginnings*, a self-grounding universal methodology. Third, the contrast of "first" and "second" philosophy is not a ranking by dignity but a relation of *grounding*: first philosophy supplies the apriori principles on which the factual sciences rest. Fourth, *Evidenz*, *Einsicht*, *Apodiktizität*, and *Selbstverantwortung* are technical: evidence is the self-givenness of a matter, not a felt certainty, and self-responsibility is the demand that every step be answerable from insight. Fifth, the "transcendental" turn the historical narrative is built to motivate is *not* Berkeleyan or psychological idealism; when Husserl praises Descartes's recourse to "pure self-knowledge" he tracks the discovery of the field of pure consciousness as the ground of philosophy, not a metaphysical thesis that only minds exist. Sixth, the recurring distinctions readers tend to collapse — *Konsequenzlogik* versus a *Logik der Wahrheit*, formal apophantics versus formal ontology, the *ontic* versus the *epistemological*, psychology of cognition versus a transcendental theory of cognition — are load-bearing and held apart consistently here. Seventh, "constitution" never means causation or fabrication: the constituted object is the identical pole of a manifold of subjective modes of givenness, an accomplishment of identifying synthesis, not a mental image and not a thing manufactured by the mind. Finally, the early lectures fold in material from Husserl's essay *Die Idee einer philosophischen Kultur*; that philosophy-of-culture register should be read as such where it surfaces.

# First Part: Critical History of Ideas

## **First Section: From Plato's Idea of Philosophy to the Beginnings of Its Modern Realization in Descartes**

## **First Chapter: The Idea of Philosophy and Its Historical Provenance**

### **First Lecture: On the historical task of giving phenomenology the developmental shape of a first philosophy**

Husserl opens with the name itself. "First philosophy" was coined by Aristotle as the title of a philosophical discipline but, in the post-Aristotelian period, was displaced by the word "metaphysics," which slipped into use only by accident. Husserl deliberately takes the older, disused term back up, and he is explicit about the advantage he draws from its very unfamiliarity: it awakens in us only its literal sense and not the manifold sediments of historical transmission that the vague concept "metaphysics" drags along — the jumbled memories of the metaphysical systems of the past. The move is methodologically characteristic. The term is chosen as a clean vessel, prized for its emptiness, into which a sense derived not from Aristotle but from the idea of a self-grounding science can be poured.

This literal sense once served, as is natural at the origin of a piece of terminology, as a *formale Vorzeichnung* (formal foreshadowing, a preliminary sketch) of the theoretical intention that the new discipline was meant to realize — its determinate problem-content still to be fixed more precisely later. However far the science to which these lectures are devoted may diverge in its problem-content from Aristotle's first philosophy, that formal foreshadowing can still serve us excellently. So Husserl takes over the word and attaches his first reflections to it. He marks the distance from Aristotle openly: continuity of name is not continuity of doctrine. The editor's note points the reader to Beilage I, where Husserl works out the relation to the Aristotelian conception at greater length.

What, then, must be read out of the literal sense? Evidently a philosophy that, among philosophies generally — which in their totality and wholeness make up the one philosophy — is precisely the first. Since sciences do not take on order in free combinatorics and at will, but carry order, hence principles of order, within themselves, "first philosophy" will naturally name the one that is first "in itself," that is, first from inner essential grounds. Husserl thus immediately refuses any external or conventional ranking: the order at stake is one the sciences possess intrinsically, in virtue of what they are.

He flags two possible senses of this priority. It could mean first in *value and dignity*: as it were the holy of holies of philosophy, with the remaining "second" philosophies serving merely as its necessary preliminary stages, the antechambers of that sanctuary. But the meaning can also be another, and on essential grounds an even more obvious one; and this, Husserl says, is the sense to be preferred here. The reason follows immediately. Sciences are *Werkgebilde*, works produced out of purposive labor; and the unity of a purpose creates, in the rational sequence of the purposive activities belonging to it, a unity of order. The decisive shift is from "dignity" to "ordering by grounding": first philosophy is not the noblest province but the *founding* one.

Husserl develops this through the inner structure of a science. Each science for itself offers an endless manifold of spiritual formations, which we call truths. But the truths of a science are not an incoherent heap, just as, correlatively, the scientist's activity is not an isolated and planless searching and producing of truths. All individual productions stand under higher guiding purpose-ideas, and ultimately under the supreme purpose-idea of the science itself. As the rule for the shaping work is thereby prescribed, so too all individual truths take on a systematic, that is, a teleological shape stamped upon them. In fixed orders, individual truths enter into truth-complexes of lower and higher purpose-form — they combine, for example, into inferences, proofs, theories — and at the very top there belongs to the whole science an ideal all-unity of theory, a universal theory that in the endlessly onward-striving science endlessly expands and shapes itself ever higher. The vocabulary of *Zweckidee* and teleology does real work: a science is held together not by logical entailment alone but by an animating purpose that governs which truths are sought and how they are ordered. The editorial note directs the reader to Beilage II for the fuller working-out of this conception of theory.

This must hold of philosophy too, insofar as we think it as a science. Philosophy must then have a theoretical *beginning* for all its truth-productions and produced truths. The name "first philosophy" would accordingly point to a scientific discipline *of the beginning*; it would lead us to expect that the supreme purpose-idea of philosophy demands, for the beginning or for a closed domain of beginnings, its own self-contained discipline, with its own problematic of beginnings — a problematic requiring intellectual preparation, exact formulation, and then scientific solution. From an inner, inseparable necessity this discipline would precede all other philosophical disciplines and would have to found them methodically and theoretically. The entrance-gate, the beginning of first philosophy itself, would accordingly be the beginning of all philosophy whatever. This is the decisive reconception toward which the whole opening was building: first philosophy is the *discipline of beginnings*, and its problem is the problem of how philosophy can rightfully begin at all.

Husserl draws the consequence for the philosophizing subject. In the true sense, a *beginner* of philosophy is the one who actually shapes first philosophy from its beginning onward — in absolutely abiding truth, that is, in most perfect insight. So long as this has not succeeded in original research, there is in this sense no beginner of philosophy yet, just as there is no first philosophy itself in actual realization. But once it has succeeded, there can also be beginners of philosophy in the ordinary, other sense of the word: namely apprentices who reproduce, in their own insightful thinking, the truths thought out in advance by others, and thereby re-shape within themselves a beginner of first philosophy. The doubled sense of "beginner" — the originating researcher versus the learner who re-enacts the originating insight — already prefigures Husserl's pedagogical aim: the lectures mean to make the listener a co-originator, not a recipient of results.

With these reflections, oriented to the literal sense of "first philosophy," the first formal foreshadowing of the intention of the lectures is at the same time given. It is to be a serious attempt to satisfy the idea of a first philosophy, and, in the didactic presentation, at the same time an attempt to lead the self-actively co-thinking listener along paths of necessity on which he can become, in the true sense, a *co-beginner* of first philosophy itself, and thereby a beginning philosopher überhaupt. Husserl then states a polemical premise bluntly: the desideratum of a first philosophy is by no means already fulfilled, long since, in any one of the historically transmitted philosophical systems — fulfilled, that is, in the form of a genuine science of compelling rationality. So the task is not merely to bring old historical inheritance back to life and, in this respect, only to ease for the student the labor of intellectual appropriation.

This implies, Husserl continues, that he is unable to recognize any of the historical philosophies whatever as a philosophy of definitive form — that is, of the form of strictest science unconditionally demanded for a philosophy. Without a strictly scientific beginning there is no strictly scientific advance. Only with a strict first philosophy can a strict philosophy überhaupt, a *philosophia perennis*, come into appearance — as ever-becoming, to be sure, since infinitude belongs to the essence of all science, yet nonetheless in the essential form of definitiveness. The phrase *philosophia perennis* is reclaimed from the scholastic tradition and redefined: not a fixed body of perennial doctrine but an endlessly self-correcting science whose every step is, in its place, definitively justified.

On the other side, Husserl declares his conviction that in the breakthrough of the new transcendental phenomenology a first breakthrough of a true and genuine first philosophy has already been accomplished — but, so to speak, only in a first, still imperfect *approximation*. In several Freiburg lecture courses he has tried, in various forms, to raise this approximation to as high a level as possible: to bring the guiding ideas, the methods, the basic concepts to the most perfect clarity, and at the same time to give phenomenology the developmental shape demanded by the idea of a first philosophy — namely the shape of a philosophy of beginnings that shapes itself, in the most radical philosophical self-consciousness, in absolute methodical necessity. In the introductory course of the previous winter (*Einleitung in die Philosophie*, 1922/23) he believed he had in the main reached this goal; in the present course he hopes to carry through still further simplifications and improvements. The claim is carefully modest: phenomenology is not first philosophy already achieved but its first *approximation*, and these lectures are a renewed attempt to perfect it.

Husserl previews the widening scope. He hopes, at any rate, to show anew that the idea of first philosophy expands step by step; that it realizes the necessary and genuine idea of a universal *Wissenschaftslehre* (theory of science); that it thereby encompasses the entire theory of a life of reason — a universal theory of cognizing, valuing, and practical reason; and again, that it is called to reform our whole scientific enterprise and to redeem us from all scientific specialism. The reach from a theory of science to a theory of valuing and practical reason signals that first philosophy is not narrowly epistemological: it is the grounding science of *all* rational life, and so carries an ethical and cultural mission against the fragmentation of the specialized sciences.

Husserl announces the plan of the course. He sends an introduction on ahead, meant to procure for us the indispensable inner presuppositions for our undertaking. We do not yet even know which of the many, and unfortunately very unclear, concepts of philosophy we should choose as our guide. Whichever we chose would at first confront us only as an empty, abstract, formal verbal thought; so it would not have the power to set our souls in motion, our volitional energies in movement. For — as has been said — what is at stake is nothing less than a reform of the whole of philosophy and, contained therein, a universal reform of all the sciences überhaupt. Wherever, in whatever cultural realm, a radical and universal reform is at stake, the driving force is a deeply moving spiritual *Not* (distress, need); the general spiritual situation fills the soul with so deep a dissatisfaction that to go on living in its present forms and norms is no longer possible. The appeal to *Not* is not rhetorical filler: Husserl names the motivational condition under which a radical beginning becomes existentially possible, anticipating the crisis-diagnosis of his late work. The editorial note here points to Beilage III.

If, then, the possibilities of changing this situation — of forming satisfying goals and methods for the spiritual life of the sphere in question — are to be weighed, then evidently there is need of penetrating *Besinnungen* (reflective self-examinations) on the inner sources of motivation of such a situation, and on the whole spiritual structure of a humanity laboring restlessly within a hardened typology of spiritual activity. Such reflections, however, gain full light only from history, which, interpreted from the present, in turn illuminates the present understandingly. So Husserl proposes to go back, from the confusing manifolds that present-day science and philosophy offer us, to times of primitive beginnings. A historical retrospect is thus to serve first as *spiritual preparation*; it is to awaken primally powerful motivations that can set our interest and our will in motion. This states the rationale of the entire critical history of ideas: history here is propaedeutic and motivational, read backward from the present task, not pursued for its own sake. The mutual illumination of present and past — each interpreting the other — is precisely the hermeneutic circle that the late *Krisis* will make programmatic.

Husserl names the figures who, looking back over the whole history of European philosophy, shine out before all others — two, or rather three: the names of the greatest beginners, the way-openers of philosophy. In first place he names Plato, or rather the incomparable twin-constellation Socrates-Plato. The creation of the idea of true and genuine science — or, what for a long time meant exactly the same, the idea of philosophy — together with the discovery of the *problem of method*, leads back to these thinkers, and as completed creation back to Plato. The coupling of the idea of science with the discovery of method sets the agenda of the whole Greek part: Plato matters here because he first poses science and method as *problems*, not because of any particular doctrine.

In second place Husserl names Descartes. His *Meditationes de prima philosophia* mark an entirely new beginning in the history of philosophy in that, with a radicalism unheard-of until then, they attempt to discover the absolutely necessary beginning of philosophy and to draw this beginning out of absolute and wholly pure *Selbsterkenntnis* (self-knowledge). From these memorable "meditations on first philosophy" stems the tendency, running through the whole modern age, toward the reshaping of all philosophy into a transcendental philosophy. And with that there is designated not only a basic character of the philosophy of the modern age but — as can no longer be doubted — that of all scientific philosophy whatever, and for all future time. The guard against the Berkeleyan misreading applies here directly: "pure self-knowledge" names the discovery of the field of pure subjectivity as the ground of philosophy, the seed of the transcendental, not a doctrine that only minds exist.

Husserl turns first to the older, Socratic-Platonic beginning of a genuine and radical philosophy, prefacing a few preliminary remarks. From here on, the editor notes, Husserl's text down to a marked point is taken over, with slight alterations, from his essay *Die Idee einer philosophischen Kultur*; the philosophy-of-culture register that now enters belongs to that source. The first, naïvely outward-world-directed philosophy of the Greeks underwent in its development a break through the *sophistic skepsis*. The ideas of reason, in all their basic shapes, appeared devalued by the sophistic argumentations. What is in itself true in every sense — what is in itself existing, beautiful, good — these argumentations had set forth as deceptive illusion, ostensibly proven so through impressive reasonings. With that, philosophy lost its goal-sense. The skeptical assault is presented not as one doctrine among others but as the loss of the very idea of an in-itself, hence of the very possibility of science.

For a being, a beautiful, a good that is in principle only subjectively-relative, there could be no in-themselves true propositions and theories, hence no science — or, what at that time meant the same, no philosophy. But not only philosophy was affected. The entire acting life was robbed of its firm normative goals; the idea of a practical life of reason lost its validity. Socrates was the first to recognize the problems lightly dismissed in the sophistic paradoxes as the fateful problems of humanity on its way to genuine *Humanität*. He reacted against skepsis, as is well known, only as a practical reformer. Husserl thus sets the stage: the sophistic crisis is a crisis of reason as such, theoretical and practical at once, and Socrates' answer is at first ethical rather than scientific.

Plato then shifted the center of gravity of this reaction into science, and so becomes the scientific-theoretical reformer. At the same time, not letting go of the Socratic impulses, he leads the way of autonomous human development — in the sense of its development toward a humanity of reason — first by way of science, by way of science reformed in the new spirit out of radical insight into method. Here the two impulses are bound together: the ethical demand for a life of self-responsibility and the scientific demand for a method of genuine cognition are, for Husserl, the same demand pursued on two fronts. The whole later identification of phenomenology with an ethics of radical self-responsibility is already in view.

Husserl proposes to explain, in order and in their decisive principal doctrines, the sense of the Socratic and then of the Platonic life-work; with respect to the former, following the rich foreshadowing that Plato has handed down to us. Socrates' ethical life-reform is characterized by the fact that he interprets the truly satisfying life as a life out of pure reason. This means: a life in which the human being, in untiring self-reflection and radical rendering of account, exercises criticism — ultimately evaluating criticism — on his life-goals, and then of course, mediated through these, on his life-ways, on his respective means. Such rendering of account and criticism is carried out as a cognitive process, and indeed, according to Socrates, as a methodical return to the original source of all right and of its cognition: in our language, by a return to perfect clarity, "insight," "evidence." Husserl translates Socratic self-examination directly into his own terms — *Einsicht*, *Evidenz* — making the Socratic *elenchus* an early form of the demand that every conviction be answerable to the self-giving of the matter itself.

All waking human life, Husserl continues, is carried out as outer and inner striving and acting. But all acting is moved by opinions, convictions: being-opinions, related to environing real actualities, but also value-opinions, opinions about beautiful and ugly, about good and bad, useful or useless, and so on. For the most part these opinions are wholly vague, far from any original clarity. The Socratic method of cognition is a method of perfect *clarification*. In it, the beautiful and good itself, which has come forth in the completed clarification, is set normatively over against what is merely opined to be beautiful and good, and thereby a true knowing of it is won. The pattern is exactly that of Husserl's own method: the merely meant is measured against the matter brought to genuine self-givenness, and only this measuring yields knowledge in the strict sense.

This genuine knowing, which originally produces itself through perfect evidence, is alone, Socrates now teaches, what makes the human being truly virtuous — or, what is equivalent, what alone is able to procure for him true blessedness, the greatest possible pure satisfaction. Genuine knowing is the necessary (and, according to Socrates, also the sufficient) condition of a rational or ethical life. Unreason, the blind living-along in unclarity, the inert passivity that omits to strive in clarifying fashion for genuine knowing of the beautiful and good itself — that is what makes the human being unblessed, what lets him chase after foolish goals. In the reflective making-evident of what one is really aiming at, and of all that one has therein unclearly presupposed in the way of supposed beauties and ugliness, usefulness and harmfulness, the true and false, the genuine and ungenuine separate themselves. They separate because precisely in the completed clarity the essential content of the matters themselves comes to intuitive realization, and with it, in one stroke, value-being and disvalue-being themselves. Husserl reads the Socratic identification of virtue and knowledge as the thesis that evident insight into the matter discloses its value-character along with its being-character — a unity of theoretical and axiological evidence.

Every such clarification at once gains exemplary significance. What, in the individual single case of life, of history, of myth, comes to intuition as the true or genuine itself and as the measure of the unclear mere opinion, offers itself without further ado as an *example* for something universal. In the purely essential intuition that naturally sets in — in which everything empirically contingent takes on the character of the inessential and freely variable — it is intuited as the essentially genuine *überhaupt*. In this pure (or apriori) universality it functions as a valid norm for all conceivable single cases of such an essence whatever. Here Husserl reads back into Socrates the procedure of eidetic variation: the single case becomes an example, the contingent is varied away, and what remains is grasped as the essence that norms all cases of its kind. This is anachronistic by design — Socratic induction is interpreted as proto-*Wesensschau* — and it is the hinge on which Husserl will hang the claim that Plato discovered the apriori.

Thinking, then, more concretely — in place of the example from daily life, from myth or history — of "any human being whatever," as valuing and striving in such-and-such situations überhaupt, turned toward goals of such-and-such a kind, acting on ways of such-and-such a kind überhaupt: it then becomes generally evident that goals and ways so constituted are genuine überhaupt, or, in the opposite case, that they are generally ungenuine, unreasonable — the latter, of course, when the beautiful and good itself, which appears in the clarification, evidently conflicts with what was opined in advance, and thereby annuls the opinion as without right. The lecture closes the Socratic exposition on the note of *Rechtgebung* and *Aufhebung*: clarification either confirms the opinion's claim of right or strikes it down. The pun on *aufheben* — to annul a groundless claim — points forward to the recurrent Husserlian theme that evidence is a tribunal before which every meaning must justify its claim to validity.

### **Second Lecture: Plato's dialectic and the idea of philosophical science**

Husserl summarizes the Socratic result. Socrates, the ethical practitioner, in reaction against the sophistry that contested every reasonable sense of life, placed the basic opposition of all waking personal life — that between unclear opinion and evidence — at the focal point of the (ethical-practical) interest. He was the first to recognize the necessity of a universal method of reason, and recognized the basic sense of this method, expressed in modern terms, as an intuitive and apriori critique of reason. Or, more exactly: he recognized its basic sense as a method of clarifying self-reflections, completing itself in apodictic evidence as the original source of all definitiveness. The retrospective is also a translation: the *elenchus* is recast as a "critique of reason" — the Kantian phrase is invoked precisely to be claimed for a Socratic-Platonic and ultimately phenomenological pedigree, and *apodiktische Evidenz* is named as the terminus of the method, the source of finality.

Socrates was the first, Husserl continues, to intuit the in-itself-subsistence of pure and general essences as absolute self-givennesses of a pure essential intuition. With reference to this discovery, the radical rendering of account that Socrates demanded generally for ethical life acquires *eo ipso* the significant shape of a principled norming and justification of the active life according to the general ideas of reason to be brought out through pure essential intuition. Husserl here makes explicit the move only implied at the close of the first lecture: Socratic clarification is read as *Wesensintuition*, and the essences it grasps are credited with *Ansichbestehen* — an in-itself subsistence that is given, not posited. The whole later Platonism of the *Logical Investigations* is being projected back onto Socrates.

This may all, Husserl concedes, given Socrates' well-known lack of theoretical-scientific intentions, lack a properly scientific formulation and a systematic carrying-out as a scientific theory of the method of genuine life-praxis. Yet it may be held as certain that with Socrates there in fact lie the germ-forms of the basic thoughts of a critique of reason, whose theoretical and technological shaping and most fruitful further development are the imperishable glory of Plato. The concession protects the teleological reading: Socrates supplies the *Keimformen*, the seeds; the systematic science is Plato's. To Plato, Husserl now turns.

Plato transferred the Socratic principle of radical rendering of account onto science. Theoretical cognizing, researching, and grounding is, after all, at first only a particular kind of striving and acting life. So here too there is need of radical reflection on the principles of its genuineness. The editorial note again flags that this stretch of text down to a later marked point is taken over from *Die Idee einer philosophischen Kultur*. The decisive step is the *transfer*: the demand for self-responsibility, first ethical, is now applied to cognition itself, which is treated as a species of action and so subject to the same demand for justification of its goals and means.

Socrates' life-reform had been directed against the sophists insofar as, through their subjectivism, they confused and corrupted the general ethical convictions; Plato now directs himself against them as corrupters of science (of "philosophy"). In both respects the sophists found so little resistance and exercised such harmful effects because, just as a genuine life of reason überhaupt was still lacking, so too was a genuine scientific life of cognition. Here too all rationality was a merely naïve pretension, in itself unclear about the ultimate possibility and rightfulness of its final goals and ways. The diagnosis is uniform across the ethical and the scientific: the sophists could prevail because reason had not yet become reflective about its own grounds — naïveté is the vulnerability the skeptic exploits.

A genuine life of reason, Husserl states, in particular a genuinely scientific researching and achieving, must wholly and entirely surmount the stage of naïveté through radically clarifying reflection; it must — ideally speaking — have ready, for all its steps, the fully sufficient justification, and at the highest level the justification out of insightfully drawn principles. This is the regulative ideal that governs the whole work: a science answerable at every step, grounded ultimately in principles seen with insight. The "ideally speaking" marks it as an idea in the Kantian sense, an infinite task.

Through the high seriousness with which Plato seeks to overcome the science-hostile skepsis in this Socratic spirit, he becomes the father of all genuine science. He becomes so by subjecting the sophistic argumentations against the possibility of an in-itself-valid cognition and a science binding for every reasonable being — rather than taking them lightly — to a deeply penetrating principled critique; by undertaking, in one with this, the positive grounding of the possibility of such cognition and science, and that (guided by the deepest understanding of Socratic maieutics) in the spirit of an intuitive essence-clarification and the evident bringing-out of its general essential norms; and finally by striving with all his powers to set genuine science itself on its course on the ground of such principled insights. Three moments define Plato's founding act: critique of skepticism, positive grounding of the possibility of knowledge through *Wesensklärung*, and the actual launching of sciences. The middle moment is the phenomenological one — possibility grounded by intuition of essences and their norms.

One can say, Husserl continues, that only with Plato did the pure ideas — genuine cognition, genuine theory and science, and, encompassing them all, genuine philosophy — enter into the consciousness of humanity; as he is also the first to have recognized and treated them as the philosophically most important, because most principled, research-themes. Plato is also the creator of the philosophical problem and of the science of method: namely the method of systematically realizing the supreme purpose-idea of "philosophy" laid out in the essence of cognition itself. Genuine cognizing, genuine truth (in-itself-valid, definitively determining), and in the true and genuine sense existent being (as the identical substrate of definitively determining truths) become for him essential correlates. Here the *Korrelation* that organizes all of Husserl's thought is read into Plato: truth and being are correlates, and the totality of in-itself-valid truths corresponds to the totality of what truly is. The editorial note points to Beilage VI.

The total inbegriff of all in-itself-valid truths to be attained in possible genuine cognizing necessarily forms a theoretically connected and methodically to-be-realized unity, the unity of *one* universal science. That, in Plato's sense, is philosophy. Its correlate is thus the totality of all that truly is. The unity of philosophy is grounded not in subject-matter but in the systematic interconnection of truth itself; and to that unity on the side of knowing corresponds, on the side of being, the one all-encompassing realm of the existent. This is the idea of philosophy as *one* universal science with the totality of being as its correlate — the idea Husserl will claim phenomenology finally makes good.

A new idea of philosophy thereby steps onto the scene, determining all further developments. It is now to be not merely science überhaupt, the naïve formation of an interest purely directed at cognition; nor merely, as already before, universal science; but at the same time absolutely justified science. It is to be a science that in every step and in every respect strives for definitiveness, and that on the ground of actually exercised justifications, which by the cognizer (and by every co-cognizer) are at any time to be answered for, in completed insight, as absolute. The accent falls on *Endgültigkeit* and *Verantwortung*: the new idea is not just universality but universal answerability — every step defensible as definitive before anyone who would re-think it.

Already with Platonic dialectic, this beginning of a new epoch, it is intimated that a philosophy of this higher and genuine sense is possible only on the ground of principled preliminary investigations of the conditions of the possibility of a philosophy. Therein lies enclosed, as in a living germ, the idea — significant for the future — of a necessary grounding and articulation of philosophy into two levels, a so-called "first" and a "second" philosophy. As first philosophy there goes in advance an absolutely self-justifying universal methodology; or, theoretically grasped: a science of the totality of the pure (apriori) principles of all possible cognitions and of the totality of the apriori truths systematically enclosed in these principles. Here the architectonic of the whole course is announced and attributed, as germ, to Plato: first philosophy is the apriori, self-justifying universal methodology — a science of principles — and "first" means grounding, not nobler. The guard against the dignity-reading is built into the very introduction of the term.

As is to be seen, Husserl adds, the unity of all the apriori sciences ever to be realized — inseparably linked through the essential interconnection of all principled fundamental truths — thereby delimits itself. On the second level there results the totality of the "genuine," that is, the "explanatory" factual sciences proceeding in rational method. In all their justifying groundings referred back to first philosophy, to the apriori system of possible rational method überhaupt, they draw from its constant application a thoroughgoing rationality — precisely that of the specific "explanation" that is able to exhibit every methodical step as definitively justified out of apriori principles (hence at any time in the insight of apodictic necessity). The "explanatory" factual sciences are rational not in themselves but by derivation: their rationality is borrowed, through application, from the apriori principles supplied by first philosophy. This is the grounding-relation that the term "first" was meant to name.

At the same time these sciences gain — always ideally speaking — from the recognized systematic unity of the supreme apriori principles themselves the unity of a rational system; they are disciplines of the one "second philosophy," whose correlate and domain is the unity of factual actuality. The factual sciences thus form a single system, "second philosophy," correlated to the one factual world, just as first philosophy forms a single system of the apriori. The mapping is complete: two levels of science, two correlates (the apriori and the factual), one grounding the other.

Returning to Plato himself, Husserl now stresses that Plato by no means wanted to be merely a reformer of science. In his ultimate aim he remained, even in his scientific-theoretical efforts, at all times a Socratic, hence in the most universal sense an ethical practitioner. So his theoretical research had still a deeper significance. The point guards against reading Plato (and Husserl's Plato) as a pure epistemologist: the theory of science is in service of an ethical-practical end, the reform of life.

Briefly put, Husserl says, it is a matter of the following basic conviction, whose full sense and whose whole and rightful range have by no means yet been measured out: definitive grounding, securing, justification of any human activity of reason whatever is carried out in the forms and through the medium of the predicatively judging theoretical reason — and is carried out ultimately by means of philosophy. The elevation of humanity to the height of true and genuine humanness presupposes the development of genuine science in its principally rooted and connected totality. Science is the cognitive site of all rationality; from it even the called leaders of humanity — the *Archonten* — draw the insights according to which they rationally order communal life. The thesis is strong and characteristically Husserlian: all justification of reason, theoretical or practical, passes through theoretical-predicative reason, and so through philosophy as its highest form. Practical reason is, in the end, answerable at the bar of theoretical insight.

Through such views, Husserl continues, the idea of a new kind of culture is foreshadowed: a culture in which not only does science, among other cultural formations, grow up and strive ever more consciously toward its *telos* of "genuine" science, but in which science is called, and ever more consciously strives, to take over the function of the *hēgemonikon* of all communal life and thereby of all culture überhaupt — similarly to how in the individual soul the *nous* stands over against the other parts of the soul. The Stoic and Platonic terms — the *hēgemonikon*, the ruling part, and *nous* — are pressed into service for a thesis about culture: science is to govern culture as reason governs the soul. The development of humanity as a process of cultivation is carried out not only as development in the individual human being, but as development in the cultivation of the "human being writ large."

The supreme condition of the possibility of humanity's cultivation into a true and "genuine" culture is the creation of genuine science. It is the necessary means for the elevation and best-possible attainment of all other genuine culture, and at the same time itself a shape of such culture. Everything true and genuine must be able to exhibit itself as such, and is itself possible only as a free product springing from the evidence of goal-genuineness. Ultimate exhibiting, ultimate cognition of everything genuine takes on the shape of judging cognition and stands as such under scientific norms. It has its highest rational shape through principled justification, hence as philosophy. The argument closes a circle: since anything genuine must justify itself, and justification culminates in predicative-judging cognition under scientific norms, philosophy is the form in which culture's claim to genuineness is finally redeemed.

Such thoughts too — here of course further developed — Plato has, in essential features, prefigured, prepared, and even grounded in their primitive forms. And certainly, the tendency above all characteristic of European culture — toward universal rationalization through a science that first shapes itself rationally — first awoke in the genius of Plato. And only as a consequence of his after-effect does it take on the ever more vigorously developing form of a norm recognized in the general cultural consciousness itself, and finally (in the epoch of the Enlightenment) the form of a purpose-idea consciously guiding cultural development. Husserl sketches a genealogy: the Platonic impulse becomes first an implicit cultural norm and then, in the Enlightenment, an explicit guiding telos — the rationalization of culture as Europe's defining vocation. The *Krisis*'s narrative of Europe as the form of life founded on the idea of infinite reason is here in nuce.

Breaking new ground in these connections was in particular the recognition that the individual human being and his life must necessarily be regarded as a functioning member in the unity of the community and its communal life, and that thus the idea of reason too is not a merely individual but a communal idea — under which therefore the socially bound humanity and the historically become forms of social life-shapes are also to be normatively judged. Husserl credits Plato with the discovery that reason is constitutively communal, so that the norms of reason apply to communities and their histories, not only to individuals. This grounds a *social* ethics and a critique of historical forms of life from the idea of reason.

Plato, as is well known, calls the commonwealth — with a view to its normal developmental shape, the state — the "human being writ large." Evidently he is guided by the naturally grown apperception, generally and unavoidably determining the thinking and acting of practical-political life, which regards communities, cities, states analogously to individual human beings as thinking, feeling, practically deciding, acting — as something like personalities. And in fact, like all original apperceptions, this one too has an original right in itself. Husserl treats the personification of the community not as mere metaphor but as an "original apperception" with its own legitimacy — an anticipation of his later analyses of communal or higher-order personalities (*Personalitäten höherer Ordnung*). So Plato becomes the founder of the doctrine of social reason, of a truly rational human community überhaupt, or of a genuine social life überhaupt — in short, the founder of social ethics as the full and true ethics.

This social ethics had for Plato, entirely in the sense of the above, a special stamp through his principled idea of philosophy. Namely: had Socrates grounded the rational life on insightfully self-justifying knowledge, then now with Plato philosophy — the absolutely justified science — steps in for this knowledge; further, for the rational individual life there steps in the communal life, for the individual human being the human being writ large. So philosophy becomes the rational foundation, the principled condition of the possibility of a genuine, truly rational community and its truly rational life. Husserl maps the Socrates-to-Plato advance onto two substitutions at once: knowledge becomes science (philosophy), and the individual becomes the community. The result is that philosophy founds not only individual but communal rational life.

If this is in Plato still restricted to the idea of the state-community and thought through in a temporally conditioned way, the universal extension of his basic thoughts to an arbitrarily widely conceived communalized humanity is easy to carry out. The way is thereby broken for the idea of a new humanity and human culture — namely a humanity and culture out of philosophical reason. Husserl explicitly de-restricts the Platonic *polis* to the idea of an unbounded rational humanity: the *Republic*'s city becomes the regulative idea of a rational human community as such, founded on philosophy.

How this idea would be further shaped out in pure rationality, how far its practical possibility reaches, in what respect it is to be recognized as the highest practical norm and brought to efficacy — these are here open questions. In any case, however, the Platonic basic thoughts of a strict philosophy as the function of a communal life to be reformed through it exercise *de facto* an unceasing and increasing effect. Husserl brackets the normative-political questions as open, but insists on the historical efficacy: whatever their ultimate validity, the Platonic ideas have in fact shaped European culture.

Consciously or unconsciously, they determine the essential character and the fate of European cultural development. Science spreads over all domains of life and everywhere claims, so far as it has matured or believes itself to have matured, the significance of an ultimately norming authority. The lecture closes the philosophy-of-culture excursus on this double-edged note: the spread of science as the "ultimately norming authority" is both the fulfillment of the Platonic telos and — the late Husserl will say — the source of a crisis when science forgets its own grounding in the life of reason. The editorial note marks the end of the borrowing from *Die Idee einer philosophischen Kultur*. With this the first chapter, on the idea of philosophy and its historical provenance, closes; the second chapter turns from the idea of philosophy to the actual development of logic and its limits.

## **Second Chapter: The Grounding of Logic and the Limits of Formal-Apophantic Analytics**

### **Third Lecture: The Aristotelian-Stoic-traditional logic as a logic of consequence or consistency**

Husserl recalls the result of the previous lecture: we have come to know the Platonic idea of philosophy. What now interests us above all is the development of European science — how and to what extent the Platonic impulses play themselves out. The turn is from the *idea* of philosophy to its *history of realization*. The narrative will now follow the actual sciences as imperfect realizations of the Platonic demand, and ask where the demand for full rationality was, and was not, made good.

The new philosophy issuing from Plato's dialectic — logic, general metaphysics (Aristotle's first philosophy), mathematics, the natural and human sciences in their various disciplines (physics, biology, psychology, ethics, politics) — were only imperfect realizations of the Platonic idea of philosophy as absolutely self-justifying science. One can say, Husserl notes, that the radicalism of the Platonic intention toward full and ultimate rationality of all scientific cognition weakened itself precisely by the fact that lower levels of rationality were climbed — both in the systematic shaping of a logic functioning as a professional general methodology that lights the way ahead for concrete scientific work, and in the carrying-out of individual scientific disciplines themselves. This is the diagnosis that organizes the whole chapter: the very success of the actual sciences and of logic *weakened* the radical Platonic demand, because a workable lower-level rationality could be achieved without the ultimate, principled grounding Plato envisaged. The sciences advanced by lowering the bar.

These disciplines, Husserl continues, now really grew up, under constant critical fore- and after-consideration of their methods. In this respect — especially in the mathematical cognitive spheres, preferred from the start — they soon gained a rationality that went far beyond what the appointed leader, logic, could justify from scientifically fixed normative laws. The mathematical sciences ran ahead of the logic that was supposed to ground them: their *de facto* rationality outstripped any rational *theory* of that rationality. This gap is the theme. Logic, the appointed *Führerin*, lagged behind the very sciences it claimed to lead.

Understandably, Husserl adds, the development of logic and the development of the sciences went hand in hand from the start. In the orientation toward critical justification, and therein toward the principled, hence toward pure generalities, a firm framework of ideal forms and form-laws had to force itself upon attention already in the primitive theoretical achievements of the oldest mathematics, in its inferences and proofs. It had to strike one that the elementary and complex judgment-formations growing up in judging activities are, in evident necessity, bound to fixed forms if they are to be true at all, are to be insightful as adequate to their states of affairs. The genesis of formal logic is located in reflection on mathematical proof: the recurrence of fixed forms in valid inference forces the recognition that truth is bound to form.

In genuinely Platonic spirit, Husserl says, the pure judgment-forms came — if not completely — to ideal-conceptual grasp, and the purely rational laws grounded in them were discovered, in which formal conditions of the possibility of judgment-truth (and likewise of judgment-falsity) express themselves. So there grew up the basic pieces of a pure, namely formal logic, or, as we may also say, the basic pieces of a purely rational theory of science, whose norms, precisely by virtue of their formal generality, had to be of simply universal validity. The "Platonic spirit" is the eidetic one: the judgment-forms are grasped as ideal, and the laws grounded in them are apriori. Their universality follows from their formality — being indifferent to all matter, they bind every possible science.

Science überhaupt, every conceivable science, wants to attain truths; in its asserting activity it wants to produce assertion-contents that are not merely judged by the asserting subjects but evidently confirmed by them and at any time evidently re-confirmable judgments. Thus it is clear that the formal-logical laws, precisely as the laws forming the pure forms of possible true judgments, must have normative significance for all conceivable sciences and simply necessary validity for them all. The argument for logic's universal normativity: every science aims at re-confirmable truths; the formal-logical laws govern the forms of possible truth; therefore they norm every science.

Husserl now credits the Stoics. The Stoic logic, developing further the great stroke of the Aristotelian analytic, has the great merit of having first worked out, in some purity, the necessary idea of a really strict formal logic. It laid the ground for this through its significant — though set aside, indeed wholly forgotten — doctrine of the *lekton*. In it the idea of the proposition, as the judged of judging (judgment in the noematic sense), is first precisely grasped, and the syllogistic lawfulnesses are referred to its pure forms. The historical recovery is also a conceptual claim: the Stoic *lekton* is read as the proposition in the *noematic* sense — the judged-content as opposed to the act of judging — and so as the proper subject-matter of a formal logic. Husserl thereby projects his own *Investigations*\-era distinction (act / content / object) back onto the Stoics and makes the *lekton* the ancestor of his "proposition in itself."

Essentially, however, Husserl states, this logic, and the whole logic of the tradition, was not a proper *logic of truth* but a mere logic of non-contradiction, of consistency, of consequence. More exactly: it was the rational theories propagating themselves through the millennia that made up the core stock of the otherwise variously modified logic — restricted to the formal conditions of the possibility of holding fast, consistently, to judgments once made, according to their merely analytic sense, prior to all questions about their material truth or possibility. This is the central thesis of the chapter and one of the load-bearing distinctions of the whole work: traditional logic is a *Konsequenzlogik*, a logic of consistency, not a *Wahrheitslogik*, a logic of truth. It governs the holding-together of judgments by their bare analytic sense, leaving aside whether they are materially true. Since the matter is highly significant, and since Kant's doctrine of analytic thinking aimed at it but neither Kant nor his successors brought it to the needed scientific clarity, Husserl inserts a systematic excursus to make the point fully transparent.

He begins the excursus with a model. Imagine someone judging one after another and stringing judgment to judgment, in such a way that the judgments already made go on holding inwardly for him; then there arise not merely sequences of judgments überhaupt, but sequences that continually remain meant in the unity of a co-validity, a total judgment: a judgment-unity runs through all the individual judgments. The decisive point is that these are *not* judgments that merely appear in succession in a stream of consciousness. Rather, after their actual judging production they remain continuously in the mental grip, and so, gripped together in succession, in *one* grip. The contrast is between mere temporal succession of mental events and a *synthetic* unity of sense in which earlier judgments keep holding while later ones are made — the precondition of any reasoning at all.

They have, Husserl continues, a unity linking judgment-sense with judgment-sense, building itself up meaningfully in the progress of judging — the unity of a composite, overarching judgment founded in the individual judgments, which lends to them all the unity of an inwardly cohering validity. In this way the manifold assertions of a treatise, and in its way every theory and every whole science, have an all-overarching judgment-unity. The thesis is that an entire theory or science is itself, formally regarded, *one* judgment — a single overarching unity of sense founded in its constituent judgments. This makes the whole of a science a fit object for a formal logic of consequence.

Within every such encompassing judgment-unity, judgments can stand insightfully in special relations to judgments, or subsequently enter into such. They can form judgment-unities of a special kind, namely the unities of consequence and inconsequence. So every inference is a judgment-unity of consequence. In inferring, the so-called inferred judgment does not merely appear *after* the premise-judgments; it is not merely judged in succession, but *out of* the premise-judgments the conclusion-judgment is judged forth. What is already enclosed in them — and indeed judgment-wise — is "inferred"; what is already "prejudged" through them is now actually and explicitly judged. Husserl distinguishes mere succession (one judgment after another) from genuine consequence (one judgment drawn *out of* others as already contained in them). The conclusion is "prejudged" in the premises and made explicit in the inference — consequence is the unfolding of what the premises' bare sense already contains.

For example, Husserl illustrates, we judge, and indeed in one: every A is B, and every B is C; then it may be that we judge "thereupon," and as evidently co-enclosed therein: every A is C. So the conclusion is not a judgment-product for itself but a judgment produced out of the premises. So long as we remain with these premises as our opinions, so long as we hold fast to them in their validity for us, we can not only go on judging "every A is C" but see that this judgment is at any time producible out of those premises, hence in a certain way "lies in" them, as "prejudged." The example fixes the sense of "lying in": the conclusion is producible at will from the premises so long as the premises are held, and this producibility is *seen* — consequence is an evident relation, not a mere habit of inference.

Sometimes, Husserl notes, we go on judging from some premises to a new judgment in the opinion that it lies therein. But if we look exactly at the premise-judgments we had judged before, and at this new judgment itself, if we make our judgment-opinions distinct, we sometimes see that the conclusion is not really enclosed in them. In other cases, however — as in every insightfully proceeding inference — we can see that the conclusion is a real conclusion of these premises, really determined as to-be-co-posited through their judging positing. The fallible cases matter: the mere opinion that something follows can be corrected by making the senses distinct and *seeing* whether the alleged consequence really holds. This is the self-correction internal to consequence-logic.

We thus recognize, Husserl continues, that being-enclosed is a *relative* property that really belongs to the conclusion-judgment, as identical assertion-proposition, in relation to the premise-judgments as such; just as, conversely, these have the corresponding property — belonging to them in themselves as identical judgments of their sense — of carrying this conclusion sheltered within them; that they are starting-judgments for an at-any-time-possible evident transition, to be carried out in actual judging, in which the conclusion steps forth evidently in its character of consequence. The relation of consequence is grounded in the *identical sense* (the proposition) of the judgments, not in the passing acts; it is a property the propositions have in themselves, exhibitable in an evident transition. This is what makes consequence a topic for a *formal* discipline: it attaches to ideal senses, not to subjective acts.

The counter-character of inferential consequence, as it belongs purely to judgments as judgments, is inconsequence or contradiction. If, for example, we had judged "every A is B," it may come about, while we still have this conviction, that we judge — say because a particular experience teaches it — "this A here is not B." But as soon as the glance goes back to the earlier judgment and it becomes distinct as to its sense, we recognize that the new judgment contradicts the earlier, as conversely the earlier the later. If we must, say on the ground of experience, hold fast to the new judgment, then at once, in view of this situation, there follows a surrender of the earlier judgment and a transformation of it into the negative: not every A is B. Contradiction is presented as the counterpart of consequence: a relation among identical senses, exhibitable evidently, that forces the surrender or negation of one of the conflicting judgments. The "must" here is the experiential motive; the *logical* point is only the incompatibility of the senses.

Finally, Husserl names a third relation that results alongside the two of being-enclosed and being-excluded (inclusion and exclusion): propositions, say A and B, can stand to one another so that they are in neither an inclusion- nor an exclusion-relation — for example the propositions "U is X" and "Y is Z"; they then have the compatibility that is called *consistency* (non-contradiction). Three pure relations among propositions are thus distinguished: inclusion (consequence), exclusion (contradiction), and mere compatibility. These exhaust the formal relations of judgments as judgments.

We recognize at once, Husserl insists, that these are not contingent empirical occurrences in our judging life, but that the issue here is *essential laws*, generally insightful and purely ideal universal validities — pure laws referring to consequence, inconsequence, and consistency — and that for these laws exclusively the pure judgment-*forms* are determinative. So, for instance, in what was just said concerning inconsequence we recognize at once the law: if B contradicts A, is "excluded" by A, and A is posited, then the positing of B is annulled. The laws are eidetic and purely formal: they depend only on the *forms* of the judgments, not on their matter. The sample law is the principle of non-contradiction in its consequence-logical form.

Following such laws, Husserl continues, we recognize that judgment-consequence and contradiction, judgment-wise being-included, being-excluded, and compatibility are judgment-relations that stand in connection with one another through overarching ideal laws. There also separate themselves, on closer view, mediate and immediate consequences and contradictions; and, taking all this into account, systematically following the various judgment-forms and forms of possible premise-combinations, we arrive at a manifold lawfulness that closes together into the unity of a complete systematic theory. The result is that consequence-logic is a closed, systematic, purely formal theory — the syllogistic and its extensions — built on the forms of judgments and their combinations.

It is now of importance, Husserl stresses, to note the following. Pure judgment-consequence and contradiction as inconsequence, as well as compatibility, concern the judgments purely as judgments, and without any question whether they are even possibly true or false. Here two things must be sharply distinguished. First: the making-insightful of judgments in the sense of *confirmation* — by convincing oneself, through return to the "things themselves," whether they are true or not — and likewise the insightful clarifying of judgments in the manner of bringing out their possibility, their possible truth or falsity, possibly their apriori possibility or apriori impossibility (countersensicality). This first kind of insight reaches through to the matters and decides truth, possibility, impossibility — it is the business of a *logic of truth*.

Second, Husserl continues — something wholly different — is to make judgments merely "analytically distinct," by attending to what is co-judged in them, purely as propositions, in consequence, or what is excluded by them as contradiction. He speaks of the *analytic judgment-sense* (the mere meaning-unity) of the assertion-proposition; he understands by it the judgment-opinion to be grasped out of every judging or asserting, and to be evidently identified again and again in repetition — whose evident grasping is wholly insensitive to whether one recurs to the judged subject-matter through clarifying and confirming intuition or not. The *analytischer Urteilssinn* is the bare propositional meaning, graspable and re-identifiable without any recourse to the things — and it is precisely this that consequence-logic operates upon. The contrast with the first kind of insight is total: confirmation reaches to the matters; analytic distinctness stays within the sense.

We thereby separate, as we may also say, the "mere judgment" (the mere meaning-unity) from the material possibility or even truth corresponding to it, which the other concepts of the equivocal expression "sense" designate. Husserl flags the equivocity of *Sinn*: it can mean the bare propositional meaning (the "mere judgment") or the material possibility/truth answering to it. Consequence-logic deals only with the former. This terminological care is essential to keeping the two logics apart.

The whole traditional syllogistic, hence almost the whole traditional formal logic according to its apriori core-content, really expresses only laws about the conditions of preserving non-contradiction, or laws of the bringing-out and correct-keeping of consequences, of the elimination of inconsequences. Accordingly, the concept of truth, and the concepts of possibility, impossibility, necessity, do not properly belong within the formal discipline to be purely delimited here — the discipline of the essential conditions of thoroughgoing non-contradiction and of thinking in pure consequence. The rational lawfulness of consequence is seen by looking only at the judgments as the pure assertion-meanings überhaupt and bringing their pure forms to full distinctness. How judgments can come to material adequacy, how one can decide about truth and falsity, material possibility and impossibility — that remains here out of consideration. Husserl draws the boundary firmly: truth and the modalities (in their material sense) fall *outside* consequence-logic, which concerns only consistency and the preservation of consequence among bare senses.

To be sure, Husserl concedes, truth and truth-modes on the one side, and mere judgment-inclusion, \-exclusion, and \-coexistence on the other, are not without close connection. The connection establishes itself in that, for example, no judgment — and no synthetically unitary judgment-system, which after all itself represents *one* judgment, hence no theory — can be true if a contradiction is demonstrable in it. The link between the two logics is asymmetric and one-directional at this point: consistency is a *necessary* condition of truth (a self-contradictory theory cannot be true), though not a sufficient one. This is why consequence-logic is a genuine "lower level" of the logic of truth, not a separate game.

Every contradiction is false, Husserl states — understanding by a contradiction simply a judgment composed of judgments, among whose judgment-members some one at least excludes another, contradicts it. But we can also formulate the law: if B contradicts A, and A is true, then B is false; and if B is true, then A is false. Corresponding laws hold if, instead of truth, we take possibility and necessity, or their opposites. We further have similar laws for the relation of consequence, of pure judgment-inclusion. Above all the fundamental law: if the enclosing proposition is true (possible), then the enclosed proposition is true (possible); and if an enclosed proposition is false (impossible), then the enclosing proposition, then its entire premises, are false. These are the "bridge-laws" connecting consequence to truth: truth is transmitted downward from premises to conclusion, falsity upward from conclusion to premises. They are the points at which the logic of consequence touches the logic of truth.

All such connecting-laws must be carefully set up as proper principles, separated from the pure consequence-propositions. In pure concept-formation one must then also separate the concepts of validity belonging to different spheres. In the logic of consequence the law "if the conclusion does not hold, the premises do not hold either" means only this much: the surrender of the inferred judgment conditions the surrender of the enclosing one. This hangs together with the other law, that every inference-relation is reversible: the negation of the conclusion has as consequence the negation of the premises. Husserl insists on keeping even the *concepts of validity* distinct across the two spheres: in consequence-logic "validity" means only the holding-as-judged of a judgment, and the contraposition-law concerns only the surrender of judgments, not their truth-values.

In the logic of truth, however, Husserl continues, there is no talk of that holding and not-holding which makes a possible judgment into a judgment or denies the judgment-positing to it as already judged, but of validity as *truth* and as that of its derivatives. The same contraposition-form, read in the logic of truth, concerns truth and falsity, not the mere positing or surrender of judgments. The two readings of one inferential form must not be confused — this is exactly the conflation that the chapter exists to prevent.

With reference now to such groups of formally general connecting-laws, a formal logic of mere consequence and non-contradiction proves to be a valuable *lower level* of the logic of truth — but only a lower level. The proper cognitive interest, however, goes toward the enabling of true judgments and of what is thoroughly true, and at the highest level toward the enabling of a universal cognition, the production of a system of universal and absolutely justified truth, a philosophy in the Platonic sense. Husserl returns the discussion to the Platonic demand: consequence-logic, however rational, is only a stepping-stone; the real interest is in *truth*, and ultimately in the system of absolutely justified truth that is philosophy. The "lower level" verdict re-situates the whole traditional logic within the unfinished Platonic project.

Accordingly, Husserl concludes, beyond the consequence-logic — which, to be sure, is highly rational and moves in pure essential laws — there was need of a purely rational methodology of the *attainment of truth*. In this respect, however, one did not get far, not even with respect to the most general, and in fact difficult enough to access, problems of the enabling of a truth überhaupt — leaving aside at first the much further-reaching problems of the enabling of genuine science and even of a philosophy. The chapter closes by naming the deficit: a rational *Wahrheitslehre*, a methodology of truth-attainment, was never developed — the tradition stalled at consequence-logic. This deficit is the gap phenomenology will be called to fill, and the next lecture's excursus pursues the structure of the missing logic of truth.

### **Fourth Lecture: Excursus on the universal logic of consequence as analytic mathematics, the correlative treatment of formal ontology, and the problem of a logic of truth**

Husserl recalls the prior characterization. In the last lecture we characterized the rational theories of formal logic, which — conceived by Aristotle under the title Analytic, and in the sequel supplemented and purified — made up, so to speak, the iron stock of traditional logic. According to its main core this logic was a rational systematics of the essential lawfulnesses governing consequence, inconsequence, non-contradiction. He tried to make clear (what the tradition itself had not seen) that a proper, self-contained *discipline* thereby delimited itself — one which, if one grasps its sense purely, does not yet at all include in its own theoretical stock the concept of truth and its various derivates and modalities. Derivates of truth are concepts like possibility (as possible truth), necessity, probability, and so on, with their negations. The recapitulation fixes the gain of Lecture 3: consequence-logic is a closed discipline, indifferent to truth and its modalities.

Our separating-off of a logic of consequence, Husserl continues, was grounded — to come back to it once more — in the fact that judgments as mere *judgment-senses* (propositions) — or, as we may also say in the sphere of asserting judgment, identical *meanings* of assertion-propositions — are to be evidently grasped through "mere making-distinct." This evidence lies, we showed, prior to all question about possible or actual truth; or, what is equivalent, it is independent of whether the judgment is, in relation to its state of affairs, an intuiting one, and whether then its opinion is more or less saturated with the fullness of intuition or not. The ground is restated: the evidence of *Verdeutlichung* (making-distinct) reaches only the identical sense and is wholly independent of whether the matter is intuited and the meaning "filled." The vocabulary of *Fülle*, fullness, signals the *Investigations*' doctrine of signitive versus intuitive intending standing behind the distinction.

It makes up the essence of this *evidence of mere making-distinct*, Husserl stresses, that it in no way depends on testing the respective assertion-meanings for their truth, or even merely for their possible truth — that is, on passing over to the clarifying or confirming intuitive presentation of this assertion-meaning (hence of *what* one there judgingly means). Thereby a making-evident of a wholly different kind and direction would rather be accomplished. The two directions of evidence are opposed sharply: making-distinct stays in the sense; clarification/confirmation passes to the intuited matter. They are not degrees of one thing but different *acts* with different objects.

Terminologically distinguishing, Husserl continues, we can set over against each other *analytic making-distinct*, which brings out the identical "analytic" sense of the assertion — for example in the assertion "2 is smaller than 3" — and the *material clarification or confirmation* and the possibility or truth coming forth therein. Here a wholly different concept of sense designates itself; in particular, in negative speech it is then said, for example, "2 is greater than 3" "has no sense" — that is, it of course has an *analytic sense*, it is a proposition that is, according to what would be meant in the judging assertion, wholly distinct; but the *material sense*, possibility and truth, is here to be missed, as becomes evident in the clarification, in the return to the material intuition of the "2" and "3" and "greater." The example "2 \> 3" pins the distinction down: it has a perfectly clear analytic sense (it is a well-formed, distinct proposition) yet lacks material possibility (it is materially false, indeed a priori so). To say it "has no sense" is loose; precisely, it has analytic sense but no material truth. Husserl uses the example to show that analytic distinctness and material possibility come apart.

For the evidence directed at the analytic sense, Husserl notes — that of analytic making-distinct — there suffices, we could also say, a *merely symbolic, merely verbal judging*, which for its part yields nothing at all for possibility and truth, just as nothing for necessity, probability of validity, and for their opposite. The point sharpens the contrast: consequence-logic can be carried out in merely symbolic-verbal thinking, the kind that operates on signs without intuiting the matters. This is why it is so powerful and so portable — and also why, by itself, it decides nothing about truth.

And now, Husserl says, with reference to this distinction it was said: the whole syllogistic, purely grasped, is — if we want to use the Aristotelian word — "Analytic"; it concerns the mere identical ideal assertion-meanings or judgments as stocks of analytic making-distinct: precisely because relations like consequence and inconsequence, being-included and being-excluded, likewise being-compatible in the manner of non-contradiction, concern exclusively these judgments as pure judgment-opinions, judgment-senses. The Aristotelian title "Analytic" is thus, on Husserl's reading, exactly apt: syllogistic is analytic because it concerns only the analytic sense of judgments.

But traditional logic did not want to be merely a logic of analytic consequence and non-contradiction. It spoke constantly of *truth* and its derivates — and that not merely incidentally, in connections with consequence, but it wanted to be the *method of truth*. And of course it could not even want the former properly, because it had not theoretically appropriated the double evidence belonging to judging, of which we spoke before, and therewith not the various belonging concepts of judgment-sense. Accordingly it did not give, in a necessary methodological separation of consequence — what belongs to consequence — and then, in separation of truth and the truth-modalities, what specifically belongs to these, hence what can be asserted, on the ground of the evidence of material adequation, for judgments in the shape of apriori laws of formal universal validity. Husserl indicts the tradition for a confusion: it wanted to be the method of truth while possessing only the resources of consequence-logic, because it never made the distinction between the two evidences and the two senses. So it could give neither a clean consequence-logic nor a logic of truth.

A great imperfection of methodical procedure thus clings to historical logic — to it, which, as the universal and principled methodology of all cognition, should in its own procedure satisfy the highest methodical requirements. If it remained, with respect to itself, stuck in unclarities and half-measures, then its methodical norms for all cognizing überhaupt had themselves to remain insufficient, unclear, piecemeal. The reflexive point bites: a logic that fails the standard of method in its own self-construction cannot serve as the norm of method for everything else. The methodology must be exemplary in its own method.

In fact, Husserl continues, logic did not get very far, apart from what has been carried out. It remained inadequate even in the, as we shall see, principally one-sided dimension in which alone it theoretically unfolded. A very significant defect of impermissible restriction is to be established here. Traditional logic showed itself unable to do theoretical justice to the *correlation* between predicatively determining judgment and judgment-substrate, and accordingly also not to the correlation between predicative truth and truly existent objectivity. The sense of every predicative assertion refers (in itself) to some objects, about which it asserts, which it judgment-wise names and about which it determines this and that. The new charge is *one-sidedness*: traditional logic attended only to judgments (the predicative side) and neglected the correlative side of *objects* — the substrates the judgments are about. This neglected correlation is the seed of formal ontology.

Formal theories that concern consequence and truth of predicative judgments überhaupt, Husserl argues, demand correlatively also formal theories for nominal objectivities as such — as objects of possible judgments, as thinkable (i.e. judgment-wise positable) in pure consequence or non-contradiction; and then likewise theories for objects überhaupt that are not only consistently thinkable but existent in possible truth. To every formal theory of judgments there corresponds a formal theory of objects: one for objects merely consistently thinkable (the analytic-formal-ontological side answering to consequence-logic), and one for objects existent in possible truth (answering to the logic of truth). The parallelism of the two logics is reproduced on the side of objects.

Explaining somewhat more closely: it can be asked what holds a priori and in formal generality for objects überhaupt — in formal generality, that is, for all conceivable objects überhaupt, and purely as conceivable; but that means nothing other than: for object-senses, as they may occur in possible judgment-senses (in propositions in the logical sense) as substrates of the properties, relative determinations, and so on, ascribed to them (simply or hypothetically or conditionally, in certainty, surmise, probability, etc.). Every judgment is a judgment about this and that, and the relevant substrates belong, as sense-moments, as object-senses, themselves into the connection of the unity of sense that is called the judgment. The "objects" of formal ontology are *object-senses* — the substrate-poles within judgment-senses — not real things. Formal ontology, like formal apophantics, operates in the sphere of *Sinn*, not of material reality. This is the crucial qualification that keeps formal ontology "formal."

Nothing other than such object-senses, Husserl continues, are what analytic mathematics (in set theory, arithmetic, theory of manifolds) designates as thought-objects. More precisely, the question here is not only about the possible synthetic linkings of possible judgments connected through identical substrates (meant as identical according to sense), but about such synthetic connections in which the judgments are *consistently* connected — hence, correlatively, the identical objects are thought as determined through *non-contradictory* determinations.
