Analytic mathematics — set theory, arithmetic, manifold-theory — is thus interpreted as the theory of object-senses and their consistent determinability. Its objects are "thought-objects," substrate-senses; its question is which determinations can be consistently ascribed to them.

If one thinks object-senses in formal generality as substrates of judgment-senses of arbitrary sense-shapes überhaupt, or of certain shapes selected from the a priori possible and conceptually constructible shapes, then the question is about the apriori shape-systems in which the same substrates are positable in consistency, and the consistent determination-forms they take on in these. Every form of consistent determination is at the same time a law for objects überhaupt — namely as non-contradictorily determinable in such a form. Husserl describes the project of a general theory of consistently determinable objects: which systems of determination can be consistently built on the same substrates, and the forms such determinations take. Each such form is at once a law for objects in general.

The systematic setting-up of the immediately evidently consistent systems of determination-ways of possible objects überhaupt, and the constructive analytic deduction of all the determination-shapes enclosed in them in consequence, is the task of the *theory of manifolds* (*Mannigfaltigkeitslehre*). The doctrine of the something or the somethings überhaupt — that is, of objects überhaupt as substrates of possible predicative senses that are to be consistently judgeable in ongoing predication — is *formal ontology*. It is only a correlative way of regarding the doctrine of consistent judgments überhaupt and the forms in which they close together into consistently coherent judgment-systems. A *fully* conceived apophantic logic is of itself a formal ontology, and conversely a *fully* carried-out formal ontology is of itself a formal apophantic. The central thesis of the excursus: formal apophantics (the doctrine of judgments) and formal ontology (the doctrine of objects-überhaupt) are two correlative aspects of *one* discipline; each fully developed is the other. The *Mannigfaltigkeitslehre* is the highest reach of this discipline. This is the conception Husserl had worked out in *Formale und transzendentale Logik*; here it is presented in compressed form.

The categorial concepts, Husserl continues — that is, the a priori possible determination-forms through which thought-objects determine themselves in possible consistently-to-be-judged judgments — differ from the concepts through which the judgments themselves determine themselves; and so ontological categories and apophantic categories stand over against each other. On the other side, however, "proposition" or "judgment" — we can also say "thought-state-of-affairs" or "thought" state-of-affairs as such — is itself an ontological category, insofar as each makes possible judgment-formations in which it functions as determination-substrate. Two sets of categories are distinguished — ontological (forms of objects) and apophantic (forms of judgments) — yet the proposition itself is also an ontological category, since one can judge *about* propositions. The two correlative aspects interpenetrate: apophantic objects can become ontological substrates.

To investigate all possible shapes of products through which thought-objects grow out of thought-objects, and the determinations resulting for them, is of course itself part of the task of a formal ontology. It comprises all possible judgment-shapes, in which, on the other side, all possible determinations of thought-objects must occur. The reflexive completeness is noted: formal ontology must include even the formations whereby objects are generated from objects, so it comprises all judgment-shapes as well.

But enough, Husserl says: one sees that here inseparable correlations bind object and judgment (or "objects" and "states of affairs" — both, in the present attitude, as mere positing-senses, mere "thought-nesses"), and that it is a *single* apriori science which, referred back upon itself, treats objects and states of affairs — now directing itself specially at the state-of-affairs- or judgment-shapes and the consequence-laws belonging to them, now at object-substrates and their consistent determination. The unity of the discipline is reaffirmed: one apriori science, viewable now apophantically, now ontologically, treating senses (positing-senses, "thought-nesses") on both sides.

All concepts occurring here, Husserl notes — the analytic-logical categories — are concepts drawn purely from the "senses." As for the propositions there is talk only of consistency, and not of truth, so for the objects only of their non-contradictory thinkability, and not of their material possibility or actuality. So the entire formal ontology *or* formal apophantic, each taken really fully comprehensively, is *Analytic*. The verdict generalizes the "Analytic" label to formal ontology too: it deals only with consistency / non-contradictory thinkability, never with material truth or actuality. Both wings of the *mathesis* are analytic in the strict sense.

How imperfectly traditional logic went methodically to work, Husserl continues — how distant from it remained this idea, only imperfectly winning through first in the *Leibnizian* spirit under the title *mathesis universalis*, of a universal formal logic and of a formal ontology enclosed in it — emerges from the fact that among the special-scientific disciplines set over against logic there also appeared individual, namely *mathematical* disciplines which, like *arithmetic*, fall entirely under the idea of *formal ontology*, as important but small branches of it. Leibniz is credited as the one who glimpsed (imperfectly) the *mathesis universalis* — the unification of formal logic and formal ontology. The proof that the tradition missed this is that arithmetic, a branch of formal ontology, was classed as a separate special science alongside logic rather than recognized as belonging with it.

So what lay apart in the historical consciousness of scientific humanity under the titles logic and arithmetic — and lay as far apart as logic and physics, or logic and politics — really belonged quite closely together; arithmetic and apophantic logic (e.g. syllogistic) both order themselves, as branch-disciplines, under the complete idea of one, and indeed already of a purely analytically-to-be-grasped, logic. On the other side, what in historical consciousness was intimately one, like arithmetic and geometry, had to be separated. The historical classifications are overturned: arithmetic belongs with logic (both formal-analytic), while geometry must be split off from arithmetic, despite their traditional pairing as "mathematics."

Geometry, Husserl explains, needs spatial intuition; its concepts must go back to a material sphere, that of spatiality. In arithmetic, by contrast, the concepts express modalities of the something überhaupt, like set and number, and in principle the evidence required here is of the same kind as that which lets the logical-apophantic concepts of judgment-consequence be won. Exactly seen, the whole of arithmetic, and so the whole of analytic mathematics, is in fact only a *differently directed Analytic*, a differently directed logic of consequence; namely, instead of being related to predicative positings, to judgments, it is related rather to the positing of "thought-objects." The criterion of the division is the kind of evidence: geometry rests on material (spatial) intuition and so is *not* analytic; arithmetic rests on the same purely formal evidence as apophantic logic and so *is* analytic — it is consequence-logic turned toward thought-objects rather than judgments. Husserl breaks off, saying he may not go further and must content himself with these mere indications.

The indicated defects of traditional logic, Husserl now says, hang closely together with certain quite radical methodological defects from which the treatment of the *idea of truth* and of *true being*, as well as of the other ideas essentially connected with these — the modal modifications — had to suffer. If logic in fact, and in the working-out of the great intentions of Platonic dialectic, wanted to be a *universal and radical methodology* for the attainment of truth, then research had to be thematically directed not merely at the correlation-plane just designated, truth and true being, but yet another correlative pair — itself standing in correlation with the previous one — had to become theme. Husserl now opens the *third* dimension, the one that the whole work is built to reach: beyond (1) judgments/propositions and (2) objects/true being, there is (3) the *subjective* life of judging and cognizing in which both are given. The logic of truth, fully developed, must thematize this third correlate.

He states the thesis in emphatic form. *Judgment is judged in judging activity, and this is a subjective life.* To judge originally truly is to confirm oneself in insight, and truly existent objectivity is objectivity giving itself to the experiencing subject in experiencing or otherwise self-beholding and grasping lived experience, and determining itself in insightful judging. Objectively true judging is a judging that necessarily confirms itself, or can confirm itself, insightfully for everyone, and so on. Here the correlation is pushed to its root: truth and true being are not free-floating but are constituted in the *subjective* accomplishments of insightful judging and self-giving experience. Objective truth is referred to the possibility of insightful confirmation by anyone — objectivity grounded in intersubjective re-confirmability.

There is need, Husserl continues, of a judgment- and truth-research, an object- and actuality-research, not only with respect to judgments as identical assertion-senses and with respect to objects as identical substrate-senses, but also with respect to *the subjective of judging*, of insight, of intersubjective and definitive self-confirmation, of object-positing and object-experiencing — and therein especially with respect to the subjective *modes* in which, in cognizing lived experience, in *consciousness*, all such things — meant and true object itself, judgment as proposition, and truth — give themselves. The desideratum is named: a research into the *subjective modes of givenness* of objects, judgments, and truths in consciousness. This is the theory of cognition / transcendental phenomenology toward which the historical narrative drives. The "modes of givenness" — the *Wie* of the *Gegebenheit* — are its proper theme.

Since the path-breaking and most admirable investigations of Aristotle in the *Organon*, Husserl observes, logical research went on, in the main, in the dimension that the concepts proposition, true proposition, object, actually existent object indicate. And in fact that was a quite natural going-on, after a piece of subjective-reflective reflection had done its service. The history is explained: logic naturally settled into the "objective" dimension (propositions and objects) once an initial bit of subjective reflection had launched it. The subjective dimension, having served as the springboard, was left behind.

Whoever as a scientist has to defend himself against a universal skepsis, Husserl continues — and the warding-off of the sophistic skepsis was indeed the historical motive that forced upon Greek thinking the development of a principled methodology — whoever, then, begins to reflect radically on how far in cognizing activity truth and true being are attainable, will at first look at the pretended contents of scientific achievement, at the *propositions and theories*, but will necessarily then be drawn into subjectively directed reflections that have the cognition-side as their goal. There he makes clear to himself the differences of evidence and blind opining, of consistent and contradictory judging, and the like; and from this there grows a first way of justifying cognition, and that breaks the path for a first grounding of science. The lecture closes by tracing the dialectic of the whole history in miniature: skepticism forces reflection on cognition; this yields a first sense of *Rechtfertigung* (justification) in the differences between evidence and blind opinion; and this is the germ of a grounding of science. The motive that drove logic into the objective dimension was, at root, a subjective-reflective one — and it is that suppressed subjective dimension that the next chapter, on the first reflections on cognizing subjectivity occasioned by the sophistic skepsis, will recover. With this the second chapter closes.

## **Third Chapter: The First Reflections on Cognizing Subjectivity Occasioned by the Sophistic Skepsis**

### **Fifth Lecture: The discovery of the cognition of ideas and the Greek beginnings of philosophical, rational sciences**

Husserl recalls where the last lecture closed. In the closing words of the last lecture he began to speak of how the researches of Platonic dialectic — those radical methodological reflections — soon worked themselves out in a logic, a scientific methodology, but how this logic, on account of its one-sidedness, in no way realized the intended idea of a fully sufficient methodology and of a philosophy to be effected through it, a philosophy of Platonic sense. He characterized as one-sidedness that this logic never reached a scientific theoretization of the thematic plane designated by the correlation-pair truth and true being, and, still more generally, judgment (proposition-meaning) and judgment-object. The recapitulation re-fixes the diagnosis and prepares its remedy: the missing dimension is the subjective one.

He pointed, however, at the same time, Husserl continues, to a second correlation, which sets these ideal unities in relation to cognizing subjectivity — to the fact that the identical that we call assertion-proposition, and truth in manifold judgment-modes, is given in subjective ways of the *how* of subjective lived experience; likewise the judgment-object in different ways, as it is clear or unclear, experienced or otherwise conscious. The *Wie der Gegebenheit*, the how of givenness, is named as the proper theme of the missing inquiry. The ideal unities (proposition, truth, object) are always given in subjective modes, and it is these modes that a theory of cognition must thematize.

Let us transpose ourselves, Husserl proposes, into the motivation of the beginning — here into the historical motivation that determined the Socratic-Platonic reaction and thereby brought to development the idea of a philosophy of new sense and of a methodology serving it. Whoever as a scientist stands before the fact of skepsis, with its contesting of the possibility of any objective cognition whatever under the title "science" or philosophy, will at first direct his glance at the contents of contemporary or transmitted philosophy, hence at its doctrines and theories. But necessarily he will soon be drawn into subjective reflections that concern the *cognition-side* of these theorems, the *how* of their subjective springing-forth. Husserl re-enacts the originating reflection: confronted with skepticism, the thinker is forced from the contents of doctrine back to the subjective genesis of those contents — to the *how* by which they arise in cognition.

At first he will make this clear to himself: that judging überhaupt, putting up judging propositions — even in the most lively conviction — is not yet reasonable judging, not yet cognition in the genuine sense. He will contrast insightful judging — judging that beholds the things and states of affairs themselves and determines in beholding — with vague, thing-remote "opining." He will say to himself: such mere opining must first exhibit its truth-value, and can do so only through adequation to corresponding intuition that places the things themselves before one's eyes — and not through an arbitrary intuition but a particular one, in short through making-evident, and so on. The first result of the reflection is the contrast of *Evidenz* and blind *Meinen*: a mere opinion, however convinced, must justify itself by adequation to the self-giving of the matter. This is the germ of the whole phenomenological concept of evidence as fulfillment.

Likewise, and with the same aim, Husserl continues, he will reflect on the value of the thing-giving intuition, or possibly its mere pretension — for example in the case of outer experience. He will perhaps make clear to himself that outer experience indeed subjectively gives itself as a beholding and grasping of the experience-object itself, but that the experiencer thereby always gets into his hand only a flowing, and never a definitive being itself; that what he there has in hand at any time is always still burdened with mere opinion, which never comes to actual fullness of being itself, not even in the most eager going-on to supplementing experiences; and that thus outer experience is never a consciousness that is able to satisfy its pretension of having-itself, self-grasping of the object itself. Here Husserl reads into the originating reflection the structure of outer perception he had analyzed in *Ding und Raum* and *Ideen I*: external experience presents its object "itself" yet only ever inadequately, in flowing perspectival givenness, never fully self-given. The object of outer perception is given in *Abschattungen* (adumbrations), so its givenness is always presumptive, never closed. This inadequacy is the very thing that motivates the search for a domain of *adequate* self-givenness.

But science, Husserl stresses, does not aim at truth überhaupt in the usual lax sense, but at *objective* truth. What belongs to this attainment of an objectivity? The question sharpens the demand: not just any truth but objective truth — valid for everyone, definitively confirmable — is what science seeks, and the reflection must ask what its attainment requires. This is the question that will drive the whole subjective turn.

To such reflections, then, Husserl says, the sophistry compelled — as a universal skepsis that negated the possibility of the cognition of objective truth überhaupt and of any true being überhaupt. The purpose of this reflection was *justification*, or a general reflective-critical reflection on what lies, lived-experience-wise, in cognition itself, in the very different ways of representing and judging, of the intuitive and the non-intuitive; and on what therein gave ground to speak of differently perfect or genuine and imperfect cognition — at the highest, however, of scientifically objective cognizing; and what finally had to give possible sense to all normative concepts. The purpose is named: the originating reflection aims at *Rechtfertigung*, and it must clarify the lived-experiential differences (intuitive/non-intuitive, perfect/imperfect cognition) that alone give sense to the normative concepts (true, evident, objective). Without this subjective clarification the norms float free.

If in this way cognition-reflections, with the direction of glance upon the subjective modes of givenness of what is meant in experience and judgment, stood at the head of the development, Husserl continues, it is not thereby said that it very soon came to a comprehensive successful *theoretical working-out* of this sphere of subjective cognition-modes — hence of cognizing subjectivity überhaupt and as such — that was here opened up. Indeed, millennia passed before one could develop the method for the researches lying in this subjective direction, necessary for purposes of a critical self-justification of cognition, and thereby penetrate to the development of a radical and genuine methodology of cognition. The historical claim is stark: the subjective dimension was opened at the Greek beginning but its *theoretical* working-out had to wait millennia — for phenomenology. The opening was a beginning, not an achievement.

Not, Husserl qualifies, as if the first epistemo-critical reflections, as if already the untiring and profound preliminary investigations of Plato and the never-again-abandoned cognition-reflections of his great successors, had remained without scientific fruit: quite the contrary. Only this is to be said: that the necessary working-out in the form of a truly rational essence-doctrine of cognition in subjective respect was lacking, and that instead it came relatively quickly to the development of *special sciences*, whose relatively satisfying perfection in no way contributed to lessening that deficit. How much that signifies we shall soon learn to understand. The qualification protects Plato's stature while sharpening the point: the Greeks bore enormous fruit in the *special sciences*, but precisely this success masked the missing rational doctrine of cognition. The flourishing of the positive sciences is, paradoxically, what let the foundational deficit go unnoticed — a thesis the *Krisis* will make central.

Now, Husserl says, some closer working-out. The first deeper reflections on the *subjective kind of genuine cognition* brought with them, as greatest and earliest success, the discovery of the *cognition of ideas* as a cognition of apodictic truth. There is an originally insightful production — and also a perfect one — of pure essence-concepts, and in them are grounded essence-laws, laws of insightful apodictic universality and necessity. This discovery worked itself out at once in the purification and principled completion of the already existing mathematics, in its recreation into a *pure* mathematics, as pure science of ideas. The great early fruit of the subjective reflection is the discovery of *Ideenerkenntnis* — eidetic cognition — yielding apodictic essence-laws. Its first effect was the transformation of mathematics into a pure eidetic science. Here Husserl folds his own doctrine of *Wesensschau* into the Platonic discovery of the ideas.

Let it be noted here, Husserl interjects, that one indeed, with good ground, traces the history of the strict and especially of the in the narrowest sense exact sciences far back behind the Platonic epoch, but that to their pre-Platonic formations only the character of scientific *pre-forms* is to be granted. So mathematics above all wins its specific scientific stamp only thanks to the subjective-methodological preliminary work accomplished in Platonic dialectic. Only thereby does it become a *pure* geometry and arithmetic, which has to do with *ideally possible* space- and number-formations, thought in normative relation to intuitively to-be-beheld limit-ideas, against which all such possibilities approximate. The historiographical guard: pre-Platonic mathematics is only a "pre-form"; genuine pure mathematics dates from the methodological reflection. Pure geometry deals with ideal limit-shapes (the exact straight line, the exact circle) toward which empirical figures only approximate — the doctrine of *Limesideen* that the *Krisis* will use to explain the idealization underlying Galilean science. The editorial note flags that Beilage VII gives a variant of the following text.

And upon these pure approximation-ideals ("pure" units, "pure" straight lines, etc.), Husserl continues, immediate essence-concepts and essence-laws are now referred, which for their part, as "axioms," carry the whole edifice of pure deduction. The first classical systematizer of pure mathematics, Euclid, was, as is well known, a Platonist. Supported by great predecessors like Eudoxus, he gives, in the *Elements*, the first carried-out sketch of a purely rational science according to the ideal of the Platonic school; though we should say more exactly: geometry was the first science designed and succeeding *outside* the general methodology according to the ideal of rationality grounded by it. The axioms are immediate essence-laws about the pure ideals, carrying the deductive edifice. Euclid is the Platonist who first realized the ideal of rational science — but, Husserl notes carefully, geometry was realized *outside* the methodology, as an application of its ideal, not as a part of logic.

It was the *first science*, Husserl emphasizes, that created its basic concepts in pure idea-beholding and shaped idea-laws, essence-laws — laws that are evident in apodictic evidence, hence as unconditionally valid necessities. It is the first science that systematically ordered lays immediate essence-laws at the ground and, building up systematically in the forms of pure consequence, discloses all the essence-laws mediately enclosed therein; that accordingly rationally explains all particulars and facticities to be presented in its application out of these stocks of purely rational lawfulness, makes them insightful as apriori necessities. Geometry is the model of a rational science: immediate essence-laws (axioms) at the base, pure-consequential deduction building upward, and rational explanation of particulars as apriori necessities. This is the "explanatory" rationality of the apriori sciences anticipated in Lecture 2\.

On the other side, however, Husserl underscores, the ideal of rationality sprung from the epistemo-critical preliminary investigations procures for itself a systematic working-out *within the methodology itself*, and that simultaneously with that transformation of mathematics into a purely rational mathematics. He means here, of course, the *Analytic* already grounded by Aristotle, the personal pupil of Plato — which, for all the imperfection of its further development as formal logic of propositions, of truths, of true being, nonetheless from the start developed basic pieces of a discipline rational in the same sense: a systematic and deductively progressing bringing-out of the essence-laws of consequence and truth, methodologically destined to norm rationally the particular and factual judging according to its meant truths and possibilities, its meant consequences and inconsequences, and so on. The parallel is drawn: as pure mathematics realized the rational ideal *outside* the methodology, Aristotle's Analytic realized it *within* the methodology — formal logic as a rational, deductive, norming discipline. The reference to "propositions, truths, true being" recalls the one-sided dimension of Lectures 3–4.

So the *general methodology of cognition*, Husserl summarizes, began as a preliminary investigation thinking through the contested possibility of genuine cognition, meditating on it in general reflections; from this it won a *first ideal of rationality*. And, now realizing this in its own methodological circle in a certain direction, it began to shape itself in this direction — namely in the dimension designated by the ideas judgment, judged object, truth, true being — into a *rational* methodology. Out of its immanent, in it self-actively produced motivation, a development was thereby inaugurated in which it began to shape itself into a purely *rational* scientific discipline. The methodology is reflexive: it generates from itself a rational ideal and then applies that ideal to its own self-construction, becoming a rational discipline in the one (objective) dimension it developed.

A purely rational one, Husserl adds, according to the idea it had itself previously sketched — exactly as, according to the same idea, *outside* the methodology, arithmetic and geometry were designed as rational and genuine sciences, and likewise, in further sequence, other sciences. Here is to be named the rationally explaining natural science, pressing toward development already in antiquity according to its first and admittedly most primitive beginnings — the beginnings of physics and astronomy. The same rational ideal generates, outside the methodology, not only the pure mathematical sciences but also the beginnings of a rational natural science. The pattern of "design according to a previously sketched idea" governs them all.

To be sure, Husserl qualifies, this natural science could not itself become a purely rational science, but it nonetheless had (long enough misunderstood) the *new shape of rational explanation of facts*, insofar as it, through the employment of pure mathematics as instrument of method, procured for empirical cognition a share in principled necessity. Natural science is not *purely* rational (its objects are factual, given in experience), but it achieves a *rational explanation of facts* by using pure mathematics as its instrument — thereby lending empirical knowledge a share in apriori necessity. This is exactly the mathematization of nature whose origin and hidden sense the *Krisis* will interrogate; here it is presented affirmatively as the "new shape" of factual explanation.

The rational sciences that in this way came to creation both within and outside the frame of the methodology, Husserl continues, were sciences of a historically wholly new type. They embody a prefigured methodological ideal (admittedly first more fully determining it in their embodiment) that for the whole further future, and so even today, makes up the concept of genuine science. The new type of science embodies the rational ideal and, in embodying it, first gives it fuller determination — a circle of ideal and instance that still defines what "genuine science" means.

But however great the things they accomplished, Husserl now turns, and however much, above all others, pure mathematics represented the idea of genuine science for the general consciousness, so to speak in *archetypal* fashion, and functioned for millennia as the most admired model for sciences to be newly grounded — : they and all the succeeding sciences were merely "*special sciences*," or, as we better say, were only *dogmatic sciences*, which we must with good ground set over against the philosophical sciences. The decisive verdict drops: for all their grandeur, the rational sciences — mathematics included, the very archetype — are merely *dogmatic* sciences, to be contrasted with *philosophical* sciences. The term "dogmatic" is technical and not pejorative-as-irrational; it marks a science that proceeds without having grounded its own cognition critically.

What is this opposition of *dogmatic* and *philosophical* sciences to mean? Husserl asks. Our previous course gives us the guidance in advance to understand, at least in foreboding, a still unfulfilled but necessary desideratum of all dogmatic rationality. *Philosophical sciences* — that can for us, so long as we hold fast to the Platonic idea of philosophy as the supreme purpose-idea of cognition, mean only: sciences *out of absolute justification*, hence sciences that can vindicate their cognition in every respect. The contrast is defined: a dogmatic science is rational but not self-grounded; a philosophical science is one of *absolute justification*, answerable in every respect. The Platonic idea supplies the standard.

Or, otherwise said, Husserl continues, sciences in which the scientist is able to justify fully every cognition-formation in every conceivable respect, so that no question of right to be posed here remains unanswered, no peculiarity of cognition in any way relevant for such questions remains unconsidered — whether it concern the analytic sense of the assertions, or the corresponding intuitive material contents, or the various subjective modes in which the cognizing plays itself out and in which alone what is asserted and cognized can appear. The full definition of philosophical science gathers up all three dimensions developed across the chapter: the analytic sense (consequence-logic), the intuitive material content (truth-logic / formal ontology), *and* the subjective modes of cognizing (the theory of cognition). A philosophical science leaves no *Rechtsfrage*, no question of legitimacy, unanswered across all three. The third dimension — the subjective modes — is the one the dogmatic sciences, however rational, entirely omit.

How it now stood with this in-every-respect ultimately-justifying rationality in the case of the newly appearing sciences, Husserl closes, will be our next question. The lecture ends by posing the question that the Sixth Lecture takes up: do the rational sciences, mathematics foremost, possess the ultimate, all-sided justification that defines philosophical science? The answer — that they do not, because they leave the subjective cognition-side unthematized — is already foreshadowed.

### **Sixth Lecture: The demand for a theory of cognition implied in the Platonic idea of dialectic**

Husserl recalls the closing question. We closed the last lecture with the question of how it stands with the rationality of the sciences of the new type — those sciences that liked to call themselves "rational." Did they really correspond, did even Euclidean geometry, this true world-wonder of rationality, correspond to the Platonic idea of a philosophical discipline as one that creates true and genuine knowing and thereby tells us, in every true proposition, *ultimately* what the existent is in truth? Ultimately — that is, in such a way that all reasonable questioning thereby comes to its end. The test is posed at its sharpest: does even Euclid's geometry, the marvel of rationality, satisfy the Platonic demand for an *ultimate* knowing that ends all reasonable questioning? The qualifier *letztlich* is the crux; the dogmatic sciences are rational but not *ultimately* so.

Let us consider, Husserl says. In the original grounding and the taking-over re-production of the scientific theories that had grown up under the titles formal or pure logic, pure arithmetic, geometry, explanatory natural science, the propositions were not put up at random or taken over in blind opining. There it was not merely judged überhaupt, but *insightfully* judged — whether in immediate insight or in the insight of mediate consequence, hence in the consciousness of the necessity of the consequence. Insightful thereby were the respective judgment-thoughts, the meaning-contents of the respective assertions, adapted to the objectivities themselves, the states of affairs themselves of the respective domains, in visibly perfect adequation. Husserl grants the dogmatic sciences everything: they judge insightfully, with adequate adaptation of meaning to matter. The question cannot be whether they have evidence; they do. So what could they lack?

What was thus won, Husserl continues, was won in the consciousness of a *succeeding achievement*, and that it was a succeeding one, of this the researcher and grounder convinced himself in accompanying reflective re-examination. What more could be demanded here? Yet — should not here in fact a *more* be thinkable, a higher achievement over against those testing reflections that the scientist constantly carries out during his thought-work? The scientist already checks his work reflectively; the question is whether a *higher* achievement than this occasional self-checking is possible. Husserl is setting up the distinction between the scientist's piecemeal, case-bound self-justification and a thematic, general theory of cognition.

Such reflections, Husserl explains, consist in a mere looking-at the course and outcome of the thought-action, at the meaning-contents producing themselves, at the experiences or other clarifying and confirming intuitions brought about and self-actively carried out; and in particular at whether the meaning-contents thereby saturate themselves, as it were, with corresponding intuitive contents, and whether thus the meant purely as such — what we called the mere analytic sense — adapts itself exactly, in the fullness of sense, to the intuitively present, or whether at the end it here and there does not fit and the opinion must be given up or changed. The scientist's reflection is described in Husserl's own terms of fulfillment: it watches whether the meaning "saturates" with intuition, whether the analytic sense fits the intuited matter — and, if not, revises the opinion. This is evidence as the coincidence (or failure) of meaning and intuition.

Always thereby, Husserl notes, the scientist is directed at the *one* object that he has set himself the goal of theoretically determining; but in the course of his procedure he can ask himself whether, for example, he has already seen it from near enough, whether he must not consider it also from the other side, and so on; and if, in consequence of such new considerations, changes in the determination of the object turn out to be necessary, he justifies these before himself by saying, for example: "the object is in reality not as I first opined; a new aspect that I have gained of it has taught me otherwise," and so forth. The scientist's self-correction stays bound to the object and proceeds through changing aspects: he revises his determination as new sides of the object show themselves. The example with "aspects" and "sides" again invokes the adumbrational structure of givenness.

From such considerations, Husserl draws out, it becomes clear that the scientist, in the reflective turns of glance occasionally undertaken for the purpose of justifying his activity, makes clear to himself that in his determination of the object — which, as the one and same, he always has in view — its manifold *subjective modes of appearance*, in which it presents itself to him, are nonetheless decisive. The hidden premise of all the scientist's self-justification surfaces: the identical object is determined *through* its subjective modes of appearance, which are therefore "decisive" even when unthematized. The scientist relies on these modes without making them his theme.

This he may do, according to need, more or less carefully and deeply penetrating, Husserl continues; in every case it is a mere looking-at, and a practical doing — of acknowledging and impressing-on-oneself retaining, or of rejecting and reconsidering — that holds itself within the frame of this subjectively directed seeing. Continually such seeing and doing clings to the *individual case*, as it is itself a component piece of the singular scientific acting. The limit of the scientist's reflection is named: it is bound to the singular case, an ingredient of his particular research; it never rises to generality. This case-boundedness is exactly what a theory of cognition would overcome.

But should not more be demanded here? Husserl presses. Could and must one not pose *general* questions? Is it not here a matter of generally-to-be-circumscribed occurrences of cognizing life in possible cognition-subjects überhaupt — occurrences most worthy of a proper theoretical interest? For in the justifying considerations occasionally undertaken by the scientist, only *side-lights* (*Streiflichter*) fall on the processes in cognizing subjectivity. What he there occasionally gets into view, in aspects of the object, are only a few among the countless *modes* in which the object is always given to him, so long as he has it in view as the one and same — as the one and same that he sees now from the front, now from behind, has now in perception, the other time in memory, at which, sunk in his research, he looks exclusively, which then again, at a distraction, steps into the background of consciousness, which stands now clearly and distinctly, now blurredly before his eyes. The image of *Streiflichter* — glancing side-lights — captures the deficit: the scientist catches only a few of the countless modes of givenness (perception/memory, front/back, focal/marginal, clear/blurred) in which his object is constantly given. The vast subjective field of which these are mere glimpses is precisely what a theory of cognition would make its theme.

Must there not, then, Husserl asks, be a theoretical investigation of *all* this — a research that makes *cognizing activity überhaupt*, according to all its modes, the *theoretical theme*, and then further cognizing activity of the general kind called scientific — must such a research not deliver general insights that would also be of great use to the respectively working scientist of the various sciences, indeed perhaps enable him a *justification of higher style*, a principled norming of his singular activity? He himself, then, the scientist of every science, is here very much interested. For it is a matter of the theoretical investigation of the great manifolds of the *living life* (*lebendiges Leben*) running off in the cognizing scientist during his thought-work, in which — while it remains hidden from him — his cognizing achievement itself consists, or in which the *inner* of the shaping consists of that which lies, as cognition-formation, cognition-goal, and cognition-path, continually in his view. The project is named: a theory of cognition that thematizes cognizing life as such, offering the scientist a "justification of higher style," a principled norm above his case-by-case self-checking. The phrase *lebendiges Leben* names the constituting accomplishment that runs off anonymously beneath the scientist's gaze — the operative life in which his achievement consists but which itself stays hidden.

Thinking theoretically and carrying out theoretical achievements, Husserl continues, he *lives* in these processes, which he himself thereby does *not see*. What he has in view are the results and result-paths shaping themselves in them: the experienced giving itself, in changing experiencing, in changing subjective views and perspectives, as one and the same thing; or the judgment giving itself, in changing asserting-judging activity, as an identical — as one and the same proposition, say "2 × 2 \= 4," to which one can always return; then, in the exhibiting cognition, the propositions in their adaptation to objectively beheld matter, the character of correctness identically exhibiting itself in every exhibiting. The fundamental contrast: in the natural cognizing attitude one *lives in* the constituting processes and sees only their *results* — the identical thing, the identical proposition, the identical correctness. The processes themselves remain unseen. This living-in-and-not-seeing is the structure the reduction is designed to reverse.

Only when the scientist passes from this naïvely-carrying-out thinking into the new *reflective attitude* — which he needs also for the purpose of subjective justification of his activity — does something step out of the previously hidden subjective life: these or those moments of the subjective mode of givenness of his experience-objectivities, of the judgments, of the correctness of these judgments, step into the grasping gaze, but, as we said before, merely occasionally, in concrete singularity, and nothing less than as theoretical theme. The reflective turn brings the constituting processes into view — but ordinarily only piecemeal, in the singular case, never as a theme for theory. The difference between this natural reflective glance and the *thematic*, eidetic reflection of phenomenology is exactly the difference between the scientist's self-checking and the theory of cognition.

It is clear, Husserl says — and made palpable as a great desideratum through the more exact realization of what lies in cognizing as scientific achieving — that the theoretical and then all-sided investigation of this cognizing life, of these exceedingly many-shaped cognition-activities of representing, judging, grounding, testing-justifying, and however else the vague general titles of language may here run, is most necessary. They are, after all, life-activities in which, for every cognizer, in various and ever-newly-to-be-set-in-play acts, the identical cognition-unities, the identical experience- and thought-objects, the identical assertion-propositions, and finally also the identical truths and falsities subjectively, consciously shape themselves out. What he has, he has only as the *had* of his having, the *experienced* of his experiencing, the thought-wise *built-together* of his thinking — as something that somehow "makes itself" in his subjective life. Husserl states the constitutive thesis directly: every identity the cognizer "has" — object, proposition, truth — is a *had* of a having, an *experienced* of an experiencing, something that *makes itself* in subjective life. This is the correlation of constituted unity and constituting life, the core of transcendental phenomenology, here derived by reflection on scientific work. The guard against the fabrication-reading: "makes itself" does not mean the mind invents the object, but that the object is constituted as the identical pole of a having.

And if it is thereby called "one" and "same" — this one and same perception-object to which new perceptions and memories can reach back, this one and same judgment, this same truth won in repeated insight — it is nevertheless called "same" only thanks to the subjective *identifying*, in which various subjective acts, life-moments, come to *synthesis*; hence thanks to a *unity-consciousness* in which this "identical" subjectively somehow shapes itself. For the cognizer, something can only be, can only be called "one" and "identical," because it springs precisely in such subjective lived experience, called identifying. Identity itself is referred back to a *synthesis of identification*: the "same" object or truth is constituted as identical through a unity-consciousness binding many acts. This is one of Husserl's deepest theses — that objective identity is an accomplishment of identifying synthesis — and the guard is that this is not psychologism: the object is not *made up* but *constituted* as the identical pole of a manifold of givenness.

The same cognition-unities, Husserl continues, and again the same kinds, genera of cognition-unities (things überhaupt, objects überhaupt, or experience-propositions überhaupt, more generally still: propositions überhaupt) point us, from the outset, to the fact that the manifold subjective modes in which they can consciously shape themselves in cognizing life run off in a *fixed and corresponding kind and generic typology*. It is to be expected in advance that to the *universality of the cognition-unities* there will correspond a *universality of regulated typology of the subjective cognition-modes*, in which alone such unities can subjectively give themselves. The correlational law that grounds the whole project is announced: to each type of objective unity there corresponds a *regulated typology* of subjective modes of givenness — the apriori correlation of constituted and constituting that phenomenology will investigate eidetically. The modes are not chaotic but type-bound; this is what makes an eidetic science of them possible.

He gives the everyday evidences that point to this correlation. We find it self-evident, Husserl says, that every object we represent, that we think, is representable, thinkable *for everyone*; likewise that every judgment-thought, every arbitrary assertion-meaning, is *re-understandable* by everyone and ever again. Therein lies that in everyone equivalent subjective lived experiences of the representing, understanding, sense-constituting consciousness are possible, in which the same sense would form itself out. We find it self-evident that a truth we have insight into, everyone could have insight into. The *universal validity* of truth is the universal and at-any-time *re-producibility* of the corresponding subjective lived experiences of insight; and so for everything objective and logical. Husserl reads objectivity and universal validity themselves as *correlates of re-producible subjective accomplishments*: a truth is universally valid because the insight that gives it is re-producible by anyone — objectivity grounded in intersubjective re-producibility of constituting acts. The "for everyone" is built into the very sense of objectivity and refers to possible equivalent acts in possible subjects.

Therein lies, in advance, Husserl continues, the indication that the as-a-rule hidden play of subjective life — in which meant objects, meant judgment-contents, cognized truths, inferred consequences, and so on, become conscious — runs off in determinate *typical shapes*, and, so running off, achieves again and again the same; and that thus in fact a *regulated correlation* obtains between typology of cognizing and unity-shape of the cognized. Particular characters of the "actually being," of the "true," appear at the ideal-unitary consciousness-senses, at the identically meant, in so-called insight; here cognizing life will have to have, under the title "insight," "evidence," a particular shape — that of *rationality* (*Vernünftigkeit*), the kind of right-creating life, of cognizing in the pregnant sense. Which its essential shapes are, and how they must be theoretically grasped, will be especially important questions. Husserl locates *evidence* and *rationality* as particular shapes of cognizing life correlated with the characters "true" and "actually being" — and flags the eidetic investigation of these shapes as a central task. Evidence is thus a *structural shape* of constituting consciousness, the "right-creating" life, not a feeling — the guard against the evidence-as-feeling misreading is built into the description.

Husserl now poses the institutional question. *Which* is the science, where is it, that has its thematic "domain" in this direction? Naturally logic, will say the one accustomed to grasp logic as the universal methodology of cognition — hence to understand under it the full science laid out in the Platonic dialectic. The candidate is raised in the form a reader would expect: logic in the broad Platonic sense. It will be refused.

Yet the formal logic issuing from the Aristotelian analytic, Husserl insists, is in any case *not* this science — at least not if we give it the thoroughly necessary clean delimitation we discussed earlier. It is then a firmly self-contained rational science that has as its domain, its thematic plane, the correlation of object überhaupt and judgment überhaupt — possibly being object überhaupt, true judgment überhaupt, with all belonging formal modifications. But to set up apriori laws for thought-objects and possible objects überhaupt does *not* mean to set up laws for the subjective modes in which objects become conscious, in which modes they give themselves in subjective cognition. And likewise, to set up apriori laws for judgments überhaupt, judgment-relations of consequence überhaupt, of truth of judgments überhaupt, does *not* mean to have as theme the subjective modes in which judgments occur in the carrying-out of judging activities, or the evidence-modes in which they subjectively characterize themselves as truths or probabilities, and to set up apriori laws about these. Husserl rules out formal logic as the wanted science: its domain is the *objective* correlation (object/judgment, true being/true judgment), not the *subjective* modes of givenness. Laws of objects-as-such are not laws of consciousness-of-objects; the two are sharply distinct.

"Judgment" in formal logic means, Husserl reminds, the identical assertion-meaning exhibiting itself in manifold subjective acts of asserting and at any time to be cognized out — the identical proposition, e.g. "2 × 2 \= 4." Propositions überhaupt in apriori universality, as they are the theme for formal logic, form a peculiar sphere of *ideal objectivities*, just as in arithmetic the numbers. Similarly to a proposition, a number is an identical-ideal, here an identical in subjectively very various modes of counting and number-thinking. So, just as in arithmetic purely the numbers and not the subjective activity in counting and other arithmetical consciousness form the thematic sphere, so in formal apophantics the propositions. Husserl assimilates the proposition to the number as an *ideal objectivity*: formal logic, like arithmetic, thematizes the ideal identical (proposition, number), not the subjective acts in which it is given. This confirms that formal logic belongs wholly on the *objective* side of the correlation.

In general we see, Husserl concludes, that *pure formal logic* as a rational discipline stands in this respect on a par with all other sciences of the new rational sense: like them all, it is *ontic, not epistemological* — not directed at cognizing subjectivity and subjective modes. This holds not only for those rational disciplines which, by our indications, belong thematically together from the outset with the first-formed syllogistic, or better the apophantic logic — hence not only for arithmetic and all other disciplines of formal analytic mathematics. The decisive classification: formal logic, despite its special role, is *ontic* like every other rational science — it is about objects/propositions, not about the cognizing of them. The term "epistemological" is reserved for the still-missing science of subjectivity. This *ontic/epistemological* distinction is one of the load-bearing oppositions of the whole work.

If the more narrowly or widely grasped formal logic has a distinguished position over against all other sciences, Husserl continues — if it belongs in the frame of a universal methodology for all sciences überhaupt, if it pronounces ideal laws of which all sciences can possibly make use and to which all know themselves bound — that lies in the fact that logic and the *mathesis universalis* embracing it speak precisely of objects überhaupt and judgments or truths überhaupt, and of all modes in which objects are thinkable, and all forms of possible judgments, concerning whichever objects. Naturally, however, in all sciences theories, hence judgment-formations, build themselves up; in all, objects are judged; so a formal logic, and all logical-mathematical disciplines, must hold for all sciences, for all conceivable scientific domains, and for all conceivable scientific propositions and theories — or, as we can also say, formal-logical laws, once found, must have the vocation to norm all sciences with respect to their theoretical contents and so to function for them as principles of justification. Husserl grants formal logic its genuine universality and norming function — but precisely as an *ontic* universality: it governs the objective contents of any science, which is why it cannot be the science of cognizing subjectivity. Its very breadth is on the object-side.

On the other side, however, as said, Husserl closes, formal logic, mathematical analysis included, stands on a par with all other sciences in that it, as little as they all, has its research-domain in cognition-subjectivity. Yet the *postulate* of a science related to the *subjective* of cognition has become palpable to us through this consideration — a science that systematically investigates the subjective of cognition überhaupt and of the cognition of all object- and science-domains. It steps out of the row of all other sciences through the unique peculiarity that it is, in wholly equal manner, related to all conceivable sciences and has, in relation to them all, the same task: to investigate their *subjective cognition-side*. The lecture ends by naming the wanted science by its task — the systematic, eidetic investigation of the subjective side of cognition for *every* domain — and marking its uniqueness: unlike every "ontic" science, which has its own object-region, this science is related equally to *all* sciences, since it studies cognizing as such. This is the place that transcendental phenomenology, as theory of cognition and first philosophy, is to occupy; the Seventh Lecture will sketch its full idea.

### **Seventh Lecture: Systematic sketch of the full idea of logic — a logic of truth — as a science of the subjectivity that cognizes and accomplishes überhaupt**

Husserl opens by sharpening the parallel and the disanalogy between the wanted science of the subjective and formal logic. The postulated *Wissenschaft vom Erkenntnis-Subjektiven* (science of the cognition-subjective) stands in a certain parallel to formal logic, yet the manner in which it relates to all sciences and embraces them all is, he insists, *totally other*. All sciences relate cognizingly, and in the content of their theories, to objects; in all of them objects are objects of actual and possible judgments, substrates of actual and possible truths. But these theoretical contents, precisely as *cognition-unities* (*Erkenntniseinheiten*), have an original and abiding relation back to actual and possible cognizing subjects — subjects who shape the identical objects, the same judgments and truths, within themselves consciousness-wise in manifold subjective modes of cognizing, and who can at any time so shape them. The point reorients the whole parallel: where logic embraces, in its concepts and laws, everything objective of all sciences, the wanted universal science embraces, thematically, all possible *subjective* of the cognizing of all sciences.

This yields a formula Husserl now states with care: a logic as rational science of objectivity überhaupt — however far its idea is to be widened, perhaps even beyond a *mathesis universalis* — would have as its necessary counterpart a *Logik des Erkennens* (logic of cognizing), a science, and perhaps too a rational science, of cognition-subjectivity überhaupt; and both sciences would stand in the relation of *necessary correlation*. The retained word here is *Logik* itself: Husserl justifies extending it to the subjective side by recalling that *logos* indicates not only, on the objective side, the cognized, the assertional meaning, the true concept, but also *Vernunft* (reason) — and thus the subjective, cognition-related side. The reader should not let the familiar word "logic" collapse the new science back into apophantics; Husserl is exploiting the breadth of *logos* precisely to name a correlate that traditional logic never thematized.

He then notes a structural peculiarity, again by way of the parallel. If in this logic of cognizing the cognition-subjective itself becomes theme, it becomes so, naturally, once more *in a cognizing*; it is then object for new assertions and truths, which for their part again shape themselves in a cognizing of the scientist in changing subjective modes. Hence the postulated universal science of the cognition-subjective has the remarkable property that it relates *to itself* — to its own cognition-subjective. Husserl shows this is not a defect by exhibiting the exact analogue in objective logic, which as objective universal science also relates to itself, though only insofar as it itself, in its concepts and propositions, brings objectivities into relief. Every law, every logical law, is a proposition; the principle of contradiction, asserting a truth for all propositions überhaupt, refers back to itself insofar as it is itself a proposition and falls under the universally valid truth it pronounces. So objective logic as a whole is thematically self-referred — and a similar, only correlative self-reference must hold for the logic of cognizing subjectivity: under the general lawfulness of subjective cognition-activities that it establishes must fall also all those cognition-activities through which these very laws become knowable. The self-application is structural, not paradoxical; Husserl flags it here to forestall the objection that a science of consciousness saws off the branch it sits on.

A further remark concerns the *scope* of the demanded science, and Husserl uses it to widen the field decisively. If we grasp it as a logic directed at the subjective cognition-life, we think at first of general insights serving as principles of justification on the subjective side, and we think at first of *scientific* researching and thinking aimed at true theory. But, Husserl objects, genuine cognizing cannot be normed and researched for norming-purposes without thorough study of *ungenuine* (*unecht*) cognizing — which by most general generic characters still deserves the name "cognizing"; and what we call theoretical or scientific cognizing is only a *distinguished high-formation* (*ausgezeichnete Höhengestaltung*) that refers back to lower cognition-stages: to the many-shaped sensuous intuiting and sensuous phantasying, with their attendant sensuously intuitive modes of judging, which not only precede the scientific historically as typical forms of the cognition-life of prescientific humanity (and are even found already in animals), but which also play their role *for scientific thinking itself* as ever and necessarily co-functioning substrates and ingredients. The full shape of a science of cognizing subjectivity must therefore reach as far as the material connections of its field reach; and the field must be taken as widely as generic-material community can reach — just as no one would establish a science of triangles and beside it a separate science of circles, so here one will demand not merely a science of cognizing *scientific* reason but a fully embracing science of *cognizing überhaupt in the widest sense*, in which the totality of even the most primitive formations of perception, memory, and playing phantasy is theoretically in question as much as any shape of a priori and empirical scientific theorizing.

But Husserl is driven still further, and here the lecture makes its widest move. Who could want to separate the cognizing subjectivity from the feeling, striving, desiring, willing, acting subjectivity — from the subjectivity that values in every lower and higher sense and accomplishes purposively? Theoretical reason is set in parallel to valuing, e.g. aesthetically valuing, reason, and again to practical reason, where one thinks especially of the right manner of ethical life-shaping. Yet the subjectivity does not fall apart accordingly into separated pieces lying externally side by side; elements of feeling and striving, of goal-conscious willing, lie *in* cognition, and elements of cognition in all other act- and reason-kinds. Everywhere, intimately interwoven, parallel problems arise, problems of the same *type* we have come to know for cognition. The correlation between subjective cognition-life and the cognition-unities becoming conscious in it has its evident parallel in the correlation between feeling-valuing and work-creating life and the value-unities and purpose-unities becoming conscious in it.

Husserl makes this concrete with the example of the artwork, and the reader should weigh how exactly it transposes the analysis of perception into the aesthetic register. Just as in the cognition-sphere we distinguished the many-shaped subjective experiencing from the consciousness-wise one and same experienced object — which, while standing visibly there as this one and same, has an endlessly changing subjective look and can be conscious for us only by somehow *looking* for us — so we must analogously distinguish, in the case of an artwork, a symphony, a piece of sculpture. The beautiful formation is there for us *as beautiful* only insofar as our feeling speaks, and in certain subjective manners; and that in turn presupposes that the tones of the symphony are conscious in certain subjective modes of appearance, in certain subjectively felt intensities, in a certain subjective tempo — or, for sculpture, that the marble image is seen subjectively from certain sides, in certain perspectives, in certain subjectively effective lightings. Only then does feeling speak, and speak precisely in the form of aesthetically feeling consciousness. In aesthetic enjoyment a certain rhythm of presentational modes and feeling-modes founded through them runs off, a determinately ordered subjective experiencing. But the beautiful itself, which is therein conscious, is not this many-shaped life, this consciousness in which it is conscious; what the beholder consciously has before himself and aesthetically enjoys is this one beautiful formation and its aesthetic value-properties, while the subjective and many-shaped cognition- and feeling-life in which the aesthetic having-conscious of the formation consists is naturally hidden from him. The hiddenness of the constituting life that Lecture 6 located in cognition is here shown to be perfectly general: it recurs wherever an objectivity stands over against a subjective life that brings it to consciousness.

From this Husserl draws the parallel result for the aesthetic: we strike here too upon problems of an aesthetic *unity* and aesthetic *subjectivity*, and indeed problems of an aesthetic *reason* related to the *truth or genuineness of the beautiful* — and so evidently *everywhere where in any sense reason is spoken of*. All these problems are interwoven in their solution; the cognizing, the aesthetic, the ethical subjectivity does not carry out, under the titles cognizing, feeling, valuing, acting, separated acts foreign to one another in content, but inwardly interweaving, constantly mutually founded acts, with unity-accomplishments that themselves exhibit corresponding foundings. Therefore — and Husserl marks this as something we see *in advance* — there will be only *one* complete science of subjectivity: of the subjectivity which, and insofar as it, shapes within itself all possible unities of consciousness as unities of meaning and possibly of rational confirmation. To speak of consciousness as *having-conscious* of something — of a thing, a number, a proposition, a beautiful and good, a purpose-formation, a work-active action — is not an everywhere-equal, in-itself undifferentiated having of such unities, but, according to these unities and already with one and the same, an exceedingly many-shaped subjective life, as the most fleeting reflection shows. It is a life that, in the way it runs off in the subject, brings about the unity as the respectively meant and, for it, possibly in the manner of truth and genuineness intuited; *having-conscious is only as consciousness-accomplishment* (*Bewußtseinsleistung*). This last formula consolidates the constitution-thesis of the prior lecture into a single principle: to be conscious of anything at all is already an accomplishment, never a mere static containing.

One further point Husserl insists on clarifying, and it controls how the whole science is to be conducted. The science now under consideration is to be the universal science of the subjective überhaupt, as that in which everything objective comes and can ever come to consciousness; its theme is to research everything related to consciousness-subjects and to consciousness itself as consciousness *of* something. It would have to take into consideration every conceivable way in which subjects can show themselves as consciousness-active, and how they thereby determine themselves — as rationally or irrationally cognizing, valuing, willing — and would have to research determiningly all distinguishable genera and species of consciousness, *and that under constant regard to the consciousness-objects*, to the unities meant in consciousness itself. Husserl drives the distinction home: the same unities are possibly themes of other, objective sciences, or themes of practical life; but it is one thing to be objective theme, theoretical or practical, and another to be, *as objective of manifoldly related consciousness*, the (subjective) theme of the science of consciousness-subjectivity — and in particular to be considered in it under the viewpoint of *how* the manifold subjective modes of appearance, apperceptive formations, subjective characters look and determine themselves, in which one and the same objective of this or that kind gives itself in consciousness.

The decisive contrast follows. We have sciences we call objective, and all objects order themselves into objective sciences; and yet all objects belong at the same time into our science of consciousness-subjectivity. As objects of objective sciences they separate into scientifically divided fields — each such science has its field, every other another; but at the same time *all* objects of *all* sciences belong, *in one*, into that universal science of cognition- and consciousness-subjectivity überhaupt. Objective sciences want to determine the objects of their field, on the ground of concordant experience, in theoretical truth — natural science those of nature, linguistics those of language, and so on. If the consciousness-science researches the same objects, and therein all kinds of objects in one, this has another sense and means a researching of wholly other kind. Not *what* the objects, grasped in concordant experience according to their actual being, are singly and in relation to one another in theoretical truth, is here the question, but *how the cognizing looks* and determines itself theoretically, and how every possible consciousness besides determines itself, in which such objects and objects überhaupt can become conscious as unities, as identical objects. That says, e.g., how the experiencing looks and how experiential concordance looks, in which the experienced becomes conscious as actuality and continuingly subsisting actuality — but also how an experiential course looks in which the experienced is afterward discredited as illusion; which modes of appearance of spatial things, which subjective differences of the here and there, the right and left, which subjective differences of shape- and color-perspective could come into account as subjective manners in which the objective offers, and must offer, itself to the experiencer and then further to the judgingly thinking one.

Husserl thus states the topic of the new science in its full generality: it treats *every kind of objective* as *objective of consciousness* and as giving itself in subjective modes; consciousness-subject and consciousness itself are not considered separated from the conscious objective, but on the contrary, consciousness bears the conscious itself within itself, and as it bears this within itself, so it is research-theme. And this holds not only for cognition-objects in some restricted consciousness-sense, but for the valuing and practical consciousness-life of every kind and particularity. He adds the observation that all kinds of consciousness-unities lie ready at any time for a possible cognition and can thus become theoretical objects, so that sciences can relate, and indeed already do relate, to them all — sciences of aesthetic objects such as art-science, of economic goods, and so on; whence a complete science of cognizing subjectivity must, on this ground too and *eo ipso*, extend over all manners of the consciousness-life that shapes unities of whatever kind.

Having reached this point, Husserl turns the systematic result back into the historical inquiry, and the turn defines the agenda of the chapter to come. It is time to ask — and herewith we flow again into the historical consideration — must not already Greek antiquity have felt the need for such a science of subjectivity, of the subjectivity accomplishing, under the title consciousness, consciousness-unities? For Greek philosophy went forward, in its universal cognition-striving, with the grounding of ever-new sciences in all directions. Could it escape it that the interest exclusively given over, in the natural naive course of life, to the cognition-unities and the value- and purpose-unities can also undergo a *turning-back*, in which the consciousness previously hidden from itself in naive consciousness-accomplishment becomes visible and researchable to the I? Could it escape it that hereby questions are posed to all kinds of objects which none of the rational objective sciences of these objects answers, and that an otherwise ever-so-rational science, which leaves a whole dimension of questions unconsidered for its objects, could impossibly satisfy fully the idea of a philosophical science? These rhetorical questions, posed as the threshold of the historical chapter, fix the criterion by which Husserl will now read the ancient achievements: not whether they grounded objective sciences, but whether they opened the *third dimension* — the turning-back upon constituting subjectivity — without which no science, however rational, attains the full Platonic idea. The Eighth Lecture takes up the first historical attempt at such a science of subjectivity, in Aristotle's grounding of psychology.

## **Fourth Chapter: The Historical Beginnings of the Science of Subjectivity**

### **Eighth Lecture: Aristotle's grounding of psychology and the basic problem of a psychology überhaupt**

Husserl answers the questions with which the prior lecture closed by tracing the two motivating routes by which antiquity *was* in fact led to turn a theoretical interest upon subjectivity. From *logic*, which was in the making as a methodology of genuine cognition and genuine science, and in parallel from *ethics*, which had likewise begun to develop as a methodology of practically rational, "ethical" acting, one was determined from the outset to turn a theoretical interest to the cognizing and acting subjectivity in its rational and irrational doing. The manner of the *sophistic attacks* against the possibility of cognition had, precisely in this respect, motivating force. And the path to be taken was prefigured by the natural naive consideration of the world: reason and unreason of every kind are names for human *soul-faculties* (*Seelenvermögen*), faculties for certain spiritual accomplishments realizing themselves in science, in practical wisdom and virtue, in politics, in constitutions. This leads, then, to *the human being* and his soul-life as *scientific theme*, and from there, with respect to the lower stages of such life, also to the animals and animal soul-life; the *psychological* theorizing accomplished itself here in the *connection of the logical and ethical problematic*.

But soon, Husserl continues, one was led to the *desideratum of a psychology* apart from the reason-theoretical needs of these methodologies. After the general idea of a rational science had been projected and had pressed through, through Plato and in fruitful continuation through Aristotle, minds stood under the spell of the task determining the whole further development: to realize this idea in ever-new rational sciences, whether through logical reshaping of the old philosophies or sciences into rational ones, or through grounding wholly new ones in all reachable domains. Naturally, then, as for physical nature so for living nature, for animals and humans, then for social life, new sciences had to be created; foremost in interest went the science of the human being, *anthropology*, the psychical naturally interwoven with the physical, since in the natural objective consideration soul-being and bodily being are really interwoven in the animal unity. So there grew already in antiquity, in the powerful spirit of an *Aristotle*, a *first project of a universal science of subjectivity*, namely as a psychology — and it had to treat, like all soul-functions, so the functions of human reason; one of the objective sciences in the row of the experiential sciences treating the universe, one beside others, steps thereby into a distinguished relation to logic and ethics, and through them to all other sciences and their fields.

Yet — and here Husserl introduces the verdict that organizes the entire chapter — the way psychology steps onto the scene, it is properly a *standing cross* (*beständiges Kreuz*) of the philosophical minds. From the beginning it was unable to master precisely the problematic that has accrued to us in the last lectures in our starting-out from cognition and the cognition-unities and in connection with the methodological disciplines of logic and ethics. However much one spoke of the faculties of cognizing and practical reason, there was lacking the *method* to bring the *act-spheres* to which these faculties refer — and thereby consciousness as consciousness *of* something — into a properly systematic-descriptive relief and into a theoretical grip. That, however, was a radical lack, one that had to make impossible the development of psychology into the firm shape of a genuine science progressing in rational descriptions and explanations. For in every life-pulse human and animal soul-life is consciousness *of* this and that; as a whole it is to be characterized as a continuously unitary stream of ever-newly shaping consciousness — presenting, judging, feeling, striving, acting consciousness — a consciousness of exceedingly manifold forms, in which, constantly changing according to objects and subjective modes of appearance, on the one hand subjective lived-experiences themselves become conscious (sensation-data, feelings, volitions), but on the other hand also, and in one with this, things in space, plants and animals, mythical powers, gods or demons, manifold culture-formations, socialities, values, goods, purposes. How could a psychology come onto the right track *without penetrating to a systematic elementary analysis of consciousness as consciousness of something* — the ABC, as it were, of the soul-life\! The reader should mark that Husserl's complaint against ancient psychology is not that it lacked data but that it lacked the one method — the analysis of intentionality, of *Bewußtsein von etwas* — without which the data remain mute.

Husserl now specifies *why* this lack interests him, and the specification keeps the historical narrative tied to the systematic stakes. The defect in consciousness-research interests him here by no means as a mere defect adhering to psychology in and for itself, as one objective science among others — a defect of method hindering it from climbing to the stage of a genuine rationally explaining science and so becoming the worthy companion of mathematical natural science. Rather, consciousness-research comes into question *for logic and ethics*, and there it is psychology's *claim* in this respect — to be the foundation for these principial methodologies, the original power-source for all principial normings in science and life-praxis, and thereby to tower above all the other objective sciences otherwise coordinate with it — that is at issue. It might at first appear quite self-evident that psychology is that science of subjectivity from which a methodology of cognizing and acting is theoretically to be drawn; but that was really self-evident only if logic and ethics wanted to be, and could be, nothing more than empirical-technical rule-systems for human procedures in scientific and ethical doing.

The decisive question is whether logic was in fact meant only as an empirical technology of cognizing — an empirical art-doctrine after the manner of architecture. By its origin, Husserl answers, certainly not. Logic offered from the beginning a priori laws for objects überhaupt, propositions and truths überhaupt, and aimed accordingly, in subjective respect too and quite evidently, at a priori norms for cognizing, for judging and insight überhaupt. And here Husserl deploys the classical anti-psychologistic argument — the same he had forged in the Prolegomena — now in the service of the historical diagnosis. The question lies near: could such a priori laws, i.e. propositions to whose purely ideal sense unconditioned universality and necessity of validity belong, be dependent on the contingent facticity of the human being, this factual animal species *homo* within the fact of this universe?
