Must not such a dependence mean that all logical laws have merely the validity of *zoological* laws — that a change of the human species, a fitting change of the factual rule-courses of human cognizing doing, could and would carry with it a change of the logical laws? But if we surrender the absolute validity of these laws, we fall into grave embarrassments.

Husserl presses the embarrassment into an explicit circle, and the reader should follow its two turns, for the circularity is the precise lever by which psychologism is overturned. How does it stand, if logical laws really have a merely empirical-anthropological validity, with the *fact* of the human species itself and its biological, including psychological, peculiarities, which are here presupposed? And how with the fact of the whole world, no less presupposed? Knowledge of these is given by science, specifically for the human being by physical and psychical anthropology. Only if this science really has validity can we in truth say the human being is and stands under such and such psychological laws. But if that which gives this, as all science überhaupt, its principial right from beginning to end — namely logic through its logical principles — should itself depend on the fact of the human being, then it would depend on what could be valid as legitimately existing *only through it*. An evident circle. And the circle steps forward already if we adduce even the very first logical principle: had the principle of contradiction a merely empirical-relative validity dependent on the fact of the human species, then it would lie therein that a change of this species is thinkable such that it no longer holds — but then it would have to be said even of this otherwise-constituted human being that he both is and is not, is so and not so, is an otherwise-constituted human and not otherwise-constituted, and so on. The supposition refutes itself the moment it is stated.

From this Husserl draws the general lesson that frames the limitation of all ancient science. The self-evidence with which any science whatever is straightaway related to the world — whereby this world is presupposed as the unquestioned fact of experience — carries difficulties with it; so here above all the self-evidence in which logic counts as related to the fact of this world and specifically the fact of cognition-capable humans. In its original lay-out and determination as Platonic dialectic, logic was to be the radical science of the *possibility of cognition überhaupt*; it wanted to treat quite principially of the possibility of attaining truth-accomplishments in cognizing doing, since it turned against the sophistry that flatly and quite generally denied this possibility. Hence, were it really radically projected, it had from the beginning and quite principially to put in question the possibility of every cognition and truth — and therein lies that it might not even *use* the existence of the human being and the presumed self-evident existence of a world as a settled fact of experience. For this too is fact only out of cognition, and as a cognition-fact must be put in question as to its possibility. This is the methodological seed of the transcendental reduction, planted here as a demand the ancients could not meet.

For however much *Plato* strove to ground a logic in this radical spirit, he did not press through to the necessary beginnings and methods; and already *Aristotle* fell back into the very natural self-evidence of a pre-given world, thereby surrendering every radical grounding of cognition. So it came about that ancient science, for all its pretension to be philosophy — a really self-ultimately-justifying and fully satisfying science — for all its admirable accomplishments brought about only what we call *dogmatic science*, which we let count only as a preliminary stage of genuine philosophical science instead of itself. So long as the cognizing subjectivity — which must be thought in as essential correlate to all actual and possible cognitions and sciences — is not researched, so long as a general and pure science of all possible cognizing consciousness, in which all true being exhibits itself as subjective accomplishing, is not grounded, so long is no otherwise-ever-so-rational science rational in the full and every sense. Over against all sciences stands, as discussed, a science of cognition-subjectivity; and this, most widely understood, is a science treating of consciousness-subject, consciousness, and consciousness-wise meant objectivity überhaupt. It stands over against all others as correlate in such a way that it makes everything they accomplish consciousness-wise, in every step and already after the lowest experiences, principially understandable according to this subjective accomplishing, and thereby first *ultimately rational*.

The warning Husserl attaches is the hinge of the chapter and must not be missed: *as soon as one identifies this science with psychology* — as soon as one mistakes its principial proper standing, misses the radical method that opens its field — all science and all cognition-objectivity, hence the universe itself, is afflicted with darknesses, riddles, contradictions that bar from us the pure and genuine sense of the world and of all being. Science can be science in the ultimate sense, philosophy, only if it determines the world and all cognition-objectivities theoretically in such a way that every true assertion coming to cognition-accomplishment is free of all conceivable darknesses and contradictions that confuse the cognition-object in any direction. The science of subjectivity Husserl wants is therefore *not* psychology, even though psychology was its first historical mask; the difference is one of method and of principial standing, and to blur it is to forfeit ultimate rationality. This is the distinction the later phenomenology will mark as that between psychology and transcendental phenomenology, and the lecture lays its ground polemically, in the gap between Aristotle's psychology and the radical science still unborn.

But at first, Husserl closes, the necessity and proper character of such a science remains invisible to the whole of antiquity, while nonetheless its lack makes itself somehow continually felt — the insufficiency of the hitherto science makes itself felt. The *historical index* of this state of affairs is, so to speak, the *immortality of skepticism* (*Unsterblichkeit des Skeptizismus*). As an unconquerable spirit of negation it accompanies the blossoming development of the ancient sciences, untiring therein to set against every new shape of philosophy a new one of anti-philosophy. Generally speaking, it persists in demonstrating, with finest-spun arguments, the impossibility of every philosophy — i.e. the impossibility of a self-ultimately-justifying science — and that despite all the refutations by which one supposed one could overcome it in the philosophical schools. The skeptical *Hydra* grows ever-new heads, and even the struck-off ones soon grow again. This luxuriant survival of skepsis, which in its argumentations spares none of the particular sciences, not even the most exact mathematics, is a testimony that the post-Platonic science did not in truth accomplish what, by its pretension as philosophy, it ought to have accomplished: cognition out of absolute justification. Otherwise it would have had to make a skeptical enterprise impossible, to resolve its paradoxes cleanly, doing justice — by regress to the ultimate sources of their seductive and subjectively convincing force — to what in them was true force, in the positivity of its own principial justifications. However many and valuable insights philosophy owed to this constant struggle against skepticism (Husserl's note here refers to Beilage VIII, Hua VII pp. 328 f.), it could not strike it to the heart so long as skepticism drew its force secretly out of that very dimension for which philosophy had not yet become seeing — namely the dimension *of pure consciousness*. With this diagnosis — skepticism as the symptom of an unthematized dimension of pure consciousness — Husserl sets up the Ninth Lecture, which will read the history of skepticism as the negative pressure that finally drives philosophy, in Descartes, to discover that very dimension.

### **Ninth Lecture: Skepticism — the fundamental significance of its "immortality" in the history of philosophy. The decisive step of Descartes**

Husserl begins by recovering the *truth-content* that lay even behind the oldest skeptical argumentations, those of the old Sophistic, which philosophy could never master. Already in these oldest sophisms, he says, highly significant philosophical motives knocked at the gate, and it was not opened to them; in the moment in which this happened, a new realm of cognition opened — and indeed that realm from out of which all cognition must ultimately show its dignity. It is therefore indispensable to make our own the deepest truth-sense of the sophistic argumentations. The methodological point the reader must hold onto is that Husserl reads skepticism not as an error to be refuted but as the bearer of a suppressed insight; the whole modern turn will consist in *making good*, at a higher level, what the skeptic glimpsed.

The essence of all skepticism, Husserl states, is *subjectivism*; it is originally represented by the two great Sophists *Protagoras* and *Gorgias*, and he reconstructs the principial thought each first brought to bear. The first thesis: everything objective is originally present for the cognizer only through his experiencing it; but to experience it means that it appears to him subjectively somehow, in these or those modes of appearance. Once the thing looks so, another time otherwise, and everyone sees it as it looks for him in the respective experiencing of the moment. What each can indubitably assert is the respectively actually given, the so-looking *as* so-looking. The being in itself (or a being itself), detached from every look, being in itself, absolutely identical with itself, is not experienced and not experienceable. Here, Husserl notes, the thought could turn in two directions: either a being in itself is principially unexperienceable — which is the same as, is unthinkable, so that a truly existing thing to which the subjective appearances refer as their objective is a nonsense; or it might be meant that there may well be such a thing, but no subject, dependent on experiences and so on changingly appearing, could ever know anything of it. Protagoras' insight — that everything thing-like, everything objective überhaupt, is experienceable for a cognition-subject only in changing subjective modes of appearance — is in itself significant, and the reader should see in it the kernel of Husserl's own doctrine of *Abschattung*, here voiced by a skeptic to skeptical ends.

*Gorgias*, the more radical and therefore philosophically the eminently interesting one, represents the first, more extreme thesis, but — in the sense of his transmitted main argument (the second of the triad bound to his name) — without supporting himself on Protagoras' insight. Gorgias' thought was simply this: self-evidently everything I cognize as existing is my cognition, presentation (in the sense of the presented) of my presenting, thought of my thinking. But if a presenting makes an "external," a transcendent-to-presenting, present, then it is precisely the presenting in itself that presents this "outside"-being; and in this respect it is indifferent whether the presented is valued as experienced or as fictitious, say as a chariot-battle on the sea. Husserl follows this not-quite-clearly-transmitted argumentation to its last consequence, and casts it deliberately in the *I*\-speech, because that grammatical form is where its force and its trap both lie: if I measure "confirming" experience against experience, if I distinguish and prefer an insight resulting in rational thinking precisely as "insight," as "evidence," as *epistēmē*, over against a blind opinion, a mere *doxa*, I yet remain necessarily within the frame of my subjectivity. And nothing changes in this whatever characterizations I posit for it — feelings of thought-necessity, consciousness of unconditioned universal validity, and the like. Within my presenting, my subjective consciousness, occur all differences, all preference-characters that I am ever to be able to ascertain. But if that is so — if everything to be characterized as "true," as "necessary," as "law," as "fact," is so characterized only in my "presenting," and if thus only presented of my presenting is positable at all, while anything else is not even thinkable — then it has no sense to assume a being in itself, something that allegedly is, whether it be presented or not.

Husserl now states the world-historical significance he assigns to these paradoxes, and the reader should weigh how strong the claim is. In such ingenious paradoxes, in skeptical argumentations of which one does not rightly know how far they are really meant in earnest, there enters — still in primitive and vague form — a *wholly new motive of most universal significance* into the philosophical consciousness of humanity. For the *first time* the naive pre-givenness of the world becomes problematic, and from there the world itself as to the principial possibility of its cognition and the principial sense of its being-in-itself. Otherwise expressed: for the first time the real universe, and in later sequence the totality of possible objectivity überhaupt, is considered *"transcendentally"* — as object of possible cognition, of possible consciousness überhaupt; considered in relation to the subjectivity for which it is to be able to be there consciousness-wise, and purely in this relation. That is, the subjectivity too is considered purely as such, exercising transcendental functions, and its consciousness, the transcendental function itself, as that in or through which all conceivable objects as such receive, for a consciousness-subject, every content and sense they are to be able to have for this subject. Here Husserl is locating the very birth of the transcendental attitude in a skeptical provocation — and defining *transcendental* with precision: not a region of special objects, but the regard of all objectivity back upon the consciousness that lets it be there with sense.

In antiquity, Husserl emphasizes, precisely this transcendental impulse of the Sophistic and of the skepsis proceeding from it did *not* come to effect. The blossoming philosophy in its specialscientifically successful dogmatic objectivism, but also the skeptical new philosophy, did not raise themselves to an understanding of the material seriousness of the problematic that here comes to light and needs radical working-through. So it remains essentially until the modern age. Whatever the historians dispute about how far the old-beloved division of European history into antiquity, Middle Ages, and modernity has its inner grounds, as regards philosophy and the history of scientific culture there can be no dispute: here it is indubitable that the philosophy of the modern age designates a developmental series new in fundamental character over against the philosophy since Plato, and that *Descartes* with his *Meditationes de prima philosophia* grounds the new epoch, giving the stream of philosophical-historical becoming its wholly new turn. The reader should note Husserl's care: the periodization is not arbitrary literary convention but is fixed by a single methodological event, Descartes' turn to the *prima philosophia* of the ego.

What is new in Cartesian and thereby in the whole modern philosophy (Husserl's note refers here to Beilage IX, Hua VII pp. 329 f.) consists in this: that it takes up the struggle against skepticism — the still-unconquered skepticism of the general developmental situation — anew and in a wholly new spirit, that it really seeks to grasp it radically at its last principial roots and from there definitively to overcome it. Inwardly leading therein is the conviction that such an overcoming has nothing less than the function of merely clearing burdensome negations out of the world, about which a successfully creating objective science need not trouble itself; rather, that in the skeptical argumentations lie motives of fateful significance for an objective science and a universal philosophy — more closely, the conviction that in these argumentations radical unclarities, methodical imperfections of the objective sciences make themselves felt, and that the purification and theoretical unfolding of the valuable core of these argumentations had to lead to securing the hitherto science and at once filling it with a new spirit, bringing it in a new way to clarity and self-justification. And all this finally issues in the conviction that on this path alone we would be enabled to lend the original and thoroughly necessary idea of a universal philosophy a progressing realization. Skepticism, in other words, is not a nuisance to be swept aside but the index of a methodological debt that, once paid, transforms science itself.

From the completed development, Husserl says, we can also state the deepest sense of modern philosophy: that there has inwardly accrued to it the task whose driving-force, however unclarified, sets it continually in motion — namely, *to make good the radical subjectivism of the skeptical tradition in a higher sense*. Its development goes toward overcoming the paradoxical, playful, frivolous subjectivism that denies the possibility of objective cognition and science, through a new kind of *serious* subjectivism, a subjectivism to be absolutely justified in most radical theoretical conscientiousness — in short, through *transcendental subjectivism* (Husserl's note here refers to Beilage X, Hua VII pp. 330 f.). The retained phrase *transcendental subjectivism* must not be heard as a synonym for the skeptic's subjectivism it overcomes; it is its redemption, the same insight carried through with the rigor the skeptic lacked. This is the formula toward which the whole *Erste Philosophie* tends: phenomenology as the serious, self-justifying fulfillment of what skepticism could only insinuate.

With *Descartes* the modern age begins, Husserl explains, because he first sought to do theoretical justice to the indubitably true that underlies the skeptical argumentations; he first made theoretically his own the most general being-ground that even the most extreme skeptical negations presuppose and to which they argumentatively refer back — namely the *cognizing subjectivity certain of itself*. In a certain way Augustine had indeed already appropriated it; already he had pointed to the indubitability of the *ego cogito*. But the new turn springs up in Descartes through this: that he makes, *out of an anti-skeptical point of a mere counter-argumentation, a theoretical establishment*. Insofar as he considers this transcendental subjectivity under the aspect of the question, awakened by the skepsis, after the possibility of a philosophy, it necessarily becomes for him a theoretical fundamental-theme. The distinction from Augustine is the hinge: the same indubitable *cogito* becomes, in Descartes, not a debating-stroke but the ground-stone of a science.

Husserl then guards against an over-reading of the *cogito*'s force, and the qualification is exact and important. The *cogito* in its indubitability does indeed straightaway refute the playful extreme of an absolute negativism that denies any truth überhaupt — hence not only objective truth but also any subjective truth of the title *ego cogito* itself. But not yet thereby refuted is that skepsis which traditionally turns against the possibility of a *philosophy*, and properly wanted to turn only against it: against the possibility of a cognition of "truths in themselves" concerning "in-themselves" existing objects. That would concern above all an "objective," in-itself existing world, and then, closely connected, also in-themselves existing Platonic "Ideas," in-themselves valid logical and mathematical principles, in-themselves valid sciences of every kind — objective sciences, as we also say. This skepsis, and only it, had the great historical mission of forcing philosophy into the path of a *transcendental philosophy*. The reader should mark the precision: the *cogito* alone disposes of total negativism, but the serious skepsis about objective truth still stands, and it is precisely that standing skepsis which compels the transcendental turn rather than being merely silenced by it.

In Descartes' sense, but not in Augustine's, the "I think" is the *"Archimedean point"* on which, supported, a systematic and absolutely secured ascent of true philosophy itself is to follow. On the absolute ground of *pure self-knowledge* (*reine Selbsterkenntnis*), and by virtue of a thought-process carried out within the frame of this self-knowledge in absolute self-justification, genuine philosophy is to grow as an *immanent product* — to grow precisely as a doing justifying itself absolutely from the absolute beginning and in every step. So the *ego cogito* is to be the first and only foundation for a philosophy building itself purely thereon, for a *sapientia universalis*. The retained term *reine Selbsterkenntnis* should be read in the technical sense already fixed in the misreading-guards: not introspective self-acquaintance but the discovery and methodical exploitation of the field of pure consciousness as the ground of all justification.

Finally Husserl highlights the status of the *Meditationes* themselves, and this characterization governs the next two lectures. The Cartesian *meditationes* do not want to be accidental subjective reflections of Descartes, or even a literary art-form for transmitting the author's thoughts. Rather, they evidently give themselves as the *necessary* reflections, necessary in the manner and order of their motivations, that the radically philosophizing subject as such must necessarily pass through; it must make them as a subject that has chosen the idea of philosophy as the guiding *purpose-idea* (*Zweckidee*) of its life, and that, precisely in realizing it self-actively in its cognition-life, is to become a genuine philosopher. Therein lies the *eternity-significance* of the Cartesian *meditationes*: they draw, or try to draw, the necessary *style* of philosophical beginning. Only meditating can the philosopher begin — but the course, the method of this meditation, has a necessary shape. And correlatively, in objectively theoretical respect, the beginning of philosophy itself must therein grow — the beginning theory, the method, and the guidelines of its problematic; both in one must mark themselves out in the becoming, and both in their kind scientifically. With this double demand — a necessary subjective style of beginning and, correlatively, the objective birth of method and problematic — Husserl sets the task of the Tenth Lecture, which enters the Cartesian Meditations themselves to test how far Descartes realized, and where he fell short of, this idea of an absolute beginning.

### **Tenth Lecture: The Cartesian Meditations**

Husserl opens by registering the historical magnitude of what Descartes set in motion. A glance into history shows that a powerful impulse was hereby given, working itself out at once in a great becoming and the complete reshaping of development. Since the *Meditationes*, philosophy exhausts itself in the unremitting endeavor to bring the at-first unclearly struggling-upward new kind of problems to that stage of principial clarity and purity which first makes a really fruit-bearing working-through of them possible. Yet it does not succeed in this, despite ever-new beginnings and powerful exertions of force, in a fully satisfying way. Already the *starting-point* of the whole development is afflicted with fateful unclarities. The reader should hear in this the governing thesis of the lecture: Descartes makes the founding discovery yet immediately misconstrues it, and modern philosophy inherits both the discovery and the misconstrual together.

In the two first and most important of the six Meditations, Husserl grants, lies a *great discovery* — and precisely the one that had first to be made in order that a transcendental philosophy could begin: namely the discovery of the *transcendentally pure, in itself absolutely self-enclosed subjectivity*, which can become aware of itself at any time, in absolute indubitability. But of the proper sense of this discovery Descartes himself could not take possession; behind the apparent triviality of its all-known pronouncement *ego cogito, ego sum* there open in fact all-too-great and dark depths. Husserl's image is the famous one and worth preserving for its exactness: it went with Descartes as with Columbus, who discovered the new continent but knew nothing of it and supposed merely to have discovered a new sea-route to the old India. This lay, in Descartes' case, in the fact that he did not grasp the deepest sense of the problem of a philosophy to be newly and radically grounded — or, what is essentially one with it, the genuine sense of a transcendental grounding of cognition and science rooted in the *ego cogito*. And this in turn has its ground in that he had not gone to school with the skepsis in the right way. The reader should note the diagnosis: Descartes' failure is not a failure of discovery but of *apprenticeship to skepticism* — he reached the ground the skeptic presupposed without absorbing the lesson that drove the skeptic there.

To explicate this, Husserl recapitulates in rough strokes the Cartesian course of the *Meditationes* — a course that, he notes, will occupy him more thoroughly in the staging of his own strict manner of grounding genuine philosophy (the editorial note refers here to *Erste Philosophie II*, Hua VIII). All hitherto science, Descartes says, is still no truly strict, absolutely grounded science. To attain such a one, to win in an absolutely reliable and systematic construction a universal science, a philosophy, we must make a clean table, must put all hitherto cognitions überhaupt in question. Our principle is to let nothing count that does not stand so firm that it absolutely withstands every even-conceivable doubt. But then there vanishes at once, from our circle of validity to be acknowledged, the entire universe in the ordinary sense, this entire world given through our sensibility. For sensibility can — we all concede this — deceive; at any time the possibility is open that we, following it, err. But if I can now doubt all world, and perhaps even do doubt, one thing is indubitable: precisely *that* I doubt, and further, that this world appears to me sensibly, that I now have these and those perceptions, judge so and so about them, value feelingly, desire, will. I am; *sum cogitans*, I am subject of this streaming-away consciousness-life with these perceptions, memories, judgments, feelings — and am, in the streaming, absolutely sure of it, in absolute indubitability. I am, even if the universe, my body included, were not; I am, whether this doubt-possible world be or not. So there results my absolute being and being-for-myself with my absolute life as an in-itself absolutely self-enclosed being — and precisely this is what we for our part earlier designated as the transcendental subjectivity. Husserl thus aligns Descartes' *ego* with the transcendental subjectivity of the prior lectures, while reserving the verdict that Descartes misapprehended its sense.

Husserl now presses the analysis past where Descartes stopped, drawing out the question Descartes ought to have asked. This I, evidently, is nothing other than the purely grasped concrete I as I, the purely spiritual subject, from which all co-positing of such as it is not itself and in itself is held off. But if now this pure I sensibly experiences an objective world in its consciousness and builds sciences in its cognition-acts, in how far is this not a mere inner having of subjective appearances and subjectively produced judgments in subjective evidences? If it is the evidence, the insight of reason, that gives scientific judgments the preference over the vague and blind ones of everyday, then it is itself a subjective consciousness-occurrence. What justifies giving this subjective character the worth of a criterion for an *in-itself valid truth* — one that may claim, beyond the subjective experiencing, a validity? And, all the more, where the cognizing goes upon an allegedly extra-subjective world: what can justify me — since I am immediately and indubitably certain only of myself and my subjective lived-experiences — in giving the belief that this world is, and that this objective science really holds, an extra-subjective worth that it demands? The reader should mark that Husserl is not raising a skeptical doubt of his own but exhibiting the genuine transcendental problem that the *cogito*, once reached, makes unavoidable: the problem of how subjective evidence can carry trans-subjective validity.

Descartes, Husserl observes, loses himself here — in the attempt to prove the right of evidence and its trans-subjective reach — in early-seen and much-lamented *circles*. He infers, no matter how, from the finite peculiarity of the human pure *ego* the necessary existence of God — that God cannot deceive us with the evidence-criterion; now the use of this criterion is permitted, and, guided by it, the objective validity of mathematics and mathematical natural science is inferred, thereby the true-being of nature as this science cognizes it. Then the two-substance doctrine is grounded, according to which the true objective world consists, in ultimate philosophical truth, of material bodies and the spiritual beings causally bound with them, each in itself and for itself absolutely existing, after the manner of my *ego* (the editorial note refers here to Beilage XI, Hua VII pp. 335 ff.). Husserl does not pause to re-litigate the Cartesian circle in detail; what matters for him is that the circle is the symptom of the deeper failure — Descartes treats the *ego* as a little tag-end of the world, a *mens* causally coupled to bodies, and so loses the transcendental sense of the very subjectivity he discovered.

So, Husserl says, looks the train of thought that determines the new development. Its first summit, the *ego cogito*, was certainly a discovery all-understandable up to a certain degree — so new and so incomparably significant an insight that it could not fail of its powerful and lasting effect. For the first time, and firmly framed, there was brought into relief the subjectivity immediately conscious of itself in its in-itself- and for-itself-being, absolutely indubitably experienceable for itself, in its pure for-itself-being, in its consciousness-stream that makes up its pure life; and it was made visible that whatever is there for an I and in any way positable, thinkable, is only as appearing in its consciousness-life, as somehow subjectively conscious to it. Thereby precisely the region of that "merely subjective," to which the skeptical relativism reduced — only just *skeptically* — all knowable being, was scientifically brought into relief: in the thought that if everything thinkable, knowable is appearing, then only subjective data, called appearances, are knowable, and there is no cognition of an in-itself-existing, of a true. Husserl's point is that Descartes scientifically secured the very field the skeptic had pointed to — but, missing its sense, left the skeptic's reduction looking unanswered.

For, as Husserl already said, there was lacking in Descartes a deepening into the proper sense of the task that this relativism poses to philosophy, and that is quite unrejectable: the task of a philosophy, a science überhaupt, that might now no longer work on in naive self-trust of reason, in trust in the evidence of its methodical procedure, in sheer naivety. What had been put in question by the skepsis? The general possibility of objective cognition — the possibility of winning cognitions reaching beyond the momentary consciousness and the momentarily indwelling opinions and appearances, as such as pretended to cognize in-themselves-existing objects, in-themselves-subsisting truths. As soon as, through the skepsis, the transition was carried out from the naive cognition-devotion to the offering objects into the *reflective attitude*, in which the cognizing consciousness steps into view and the cognized had to be considered as unity of manifold cognizing and in relation to it, the possibility and sense of an in-itself-being and \-validity had to become at once enigmatic. On the one side one stood before the fact that all objects mean, for the cognizer, what they mean, are valid what they are valid, are what they are, *through his cognizing*, through the sense-giving and judgment-accomplishment carried out consciousness-wise in manifold shapes in himself. On the other side, the world demanded as self-evident fact its right, and one saw oneself pressed to the question: how does it then stand with sense and right of "outer" real being, and no less with the in-itself-being of ideal objectivities? What can a purely inner cognition-accomplishment mean for an extra-psychical existence, for something that is in and for itself somewhere "outside," and for other in-and-for-itself-existing of whatever other sense?

Here, Husserl insists, one ought finally once to have come to the *Besinnung* — the reflective taking-stock — that even this talk of outer and in-itself-being draws its sense exclusively from cognition, and that every assertion, grounding, cognition of an outer being is a judgment- and cognition-accomplishment carrying itself out *within cognition itself*. At least this steps forth quite evidently in the moment where, through Descartes, the pure subjectivity, the in-itself-self-enclosed *ego cogito*, was brought into relief. But then must one not have said that all the unclarities and embarrassments into which one fell here — through attending to the cognizing consciousness and through the necessary relating-back of all objectivities and truths to possible cognition — that all the unintelligibilities and riddles into which one entangled oneself ever deeper, stemmed from this: that one had precisely *not yet studied consciousness as accomplishing consciousness*? This is the lecture's central diagnosis: the riddles of in-itself-being are insoluble not because being-in-itself is impossible but because the *leistendes Bewußtsein*, the accomplishing consciousness in which alone any sense of "in-itself" can arise, had never been made theme.

All scientific studies had hitherto been objectively directed, Husserl continues, had everywhere had, presupposed, objectivity in advance in naive experiencing and cognizing. But never had it been made principially theme, and pure theme, *how* the cognizing subjectivity in its pure consciousness-life brings about this sense-accomplishment, judgment- and insight-accomplishment "objectivity" — not how it determines, theoretically progressing, an objectivity it has in advance in experience and experiential belief, but how it comes already in itself to this *having*. For it has only what it accomplishes in itself; already the simplest having-one-thing-over-against-oneself of perceiving is consciousness and carries out, in over-rich structures, sense-giving and actuality-positing — only that reflection and reflective study belong to knowing something of this, and indeed something scientifically usable. The reader should weigh the strength of this: even the barest perceptual standing-over-against of a single thing is already a constitutive accomplishment of overflowing structure, hidden from the natural gaze and disclosable only in reflection.

First the Cartesian bringing-into-relief of the pure subjectivity, and thereby of the purely in-itself, in its immanent self-enclosedness to be considered connection of consciousness, made it possible to keep the sense of *this* task unconfused over against the task of all objective research. Where objective research wants to determine theoretically objects pre-given to the cognizer, the transcendental research now become necessary wants something so very other that it may principially *not let count* a being-pre-given, a sheer-being-there of objects. Its task consists in researching, generally and in every kind and stage, *how in cognition objectivity as such, and objectivity of every category, constitutes itself as such subjectively, for the cognizer and in his cognizing "having"* — how, then, cognition already as simplest perception accomplishes pre-givenness of this and that object, and how it thereupon carries out higher cognition-accomplishments. Here the technical term *konstituiert sich* must be heard in its established sense: not that consciousness fabricates objects, but that the object stands forth as the identical pole of a manifold of subjective givenness, the accomplishment of an identifying synthesis. To "not let pre-givenness count" is the methodological refusal — the seed of the *epoché* — to take the being-there of objects as a premise rather than as itself a problem of constitution.

So transcendental science has in fact a totally other theme than all objective sciences, separated from them all, and yet as correlate related to them all. Everything, Husserl says we see in advance, depends for this new kind of science on its being able to keep its task pure, and thus its research free of all relapses into the naive-objective research-attitude. But this is first made effectively possible precisely by the Cartesian discovery and its method — which, however, as we shall yet hear, is essentially still to be *purified*. The reader should hold onto this double valuation, which is the lecture's signature: the Cartesian *via* is indispensable as the opening of the field, yet defective in execution, and the task ahead is its purification, not its abandonment.

Husserl then carries the reflection a stretch further, in the style proper to these lectures — bringing the transcendental motivation hidden in the skepsis to completed clarity — and in the conviction of thereby producing only cognitions that already lay in Descartes' horizon, once he had in the first two Meditations brought the transcendentally pure subjectivity into relief, so that he would have needed, as it were, only to grasp. If the insights just won are correct, soon further consequences result. It becomes now also fully clear *that* and *why* objective science, be it ever so exact, is still no philosophy in the sense of the Platonic idea — no science able to give us ultimate answers and to justify itself absolutely. Objective, and even purely rational science after the manner of mathematics, cannot do this, and in not a single one of its ever-so-evident propositions. Only when the rationality of the straight research-direction is — not contested, but — put in question as to its principial sense, as to the essence (capacity) of its accomplishment, and now that rationality is won which springs from the study of the transcendental cognition-accomplishment; only when all confusions and misinterpretations that grow from the un-understanding of the essence-relations between objective being, objective truth, and cognizing-accomplishing consciousness are removed through the positive clarifications of transcendental science — only then can a philosophy grow. The reader should note the precision of the qualification "not contested": Husserl does not impugn the truth of objective science, only its claim to be ultimate; what is in question is the *sense and reach* of its rationality, not its results.

It is here not a matter of trifling clarifications, Husserl stresses, that could be appended to the objective sciences so incidentally and that at bottom did not much concern them. So long as the sense of in-itself-existing objectivity is unclear and enigmatic — as one that yet can stem only from cognizing consciousness — so long is also the sense of the universe pre-given in naive self-evidence, and so finally the sense of all the actualities and truths cognized in the objective sciences, unclear. And where unclarity rules, there too the *countersense* (*Widersinn*) is not far. In fact the rationality even of the most perfect objective sciences, mathematics included, did not hinder a fullness of countersensical theories that attached themselves, changing in the course of the times, to their results, and that have their source throughout in transcendental misunderstandings. Already the skeptical negations enclosed as correlate a countersensical position concerning the totality of realities to be cognized — namely that of *solipsism*: the universe reduces to myself, I am alone, all else is in me subjective fiction; at least, I can have knowledge only of myself. But also those who acknowledge and highly esteem objective sciences fall into ever-new countersensical theories, be they called materialisms, manifold idealisms, psychomonisms, platonizing realisms. The reader should observe that for Husserl these *isms* are not rival metaphysics to be adjudicated but symptoms of one and the same untreated transcendental confusion; even solipsism appears here as the shadow-correlate of an unclarified subjectivism.

That beyond a physics one needs and seeks a *metaphysics*, and similarly for every other science, has for a main part its source, Husserl says, in this: that with the objective theories and sciences otherwise pursuing their own methodical course there attach transcendental interpretations and misinterpretations, which finally often enough confuse their own methodology itself. But should it be that in legitimate scientific cognition there is a necessary gradation, according to which above a lower stage of sciences a higher would build itself under the title "metaphysics," to treat certain highest and last questions of whatever kind, then it is in any case certain to us in advance that such a metaphysics — however it be understood — if it is really to be science of the last and really absolutely grounded science, *needs* the science of transcendental subjectivity, and that it cannot, say, ground that science or contribute any presuppositions to it. That holds for it as for every science. With this Husserl fixes the architectonic place of the wanted science once and for all: transcendental phenomenology is not one metaphysical discipline among others, not even the highest, but the science any metaphysics whatever must presuppose and can never found. The Eleventh Lecture will open the first real prospect upon this transcendental science and mark the transition from the Cartesian Meditations to Locke.

### **Eleventh Lecture: First real prospect of transcendental science. Transition from the Cartesian Meditations to Locke**

Husserl opens by stating, as a principle, what a genuine science may not tolerate: a science may have no unsolved or even unasked questions on whose answering the sense and cognition-worth of all its positings — from the first and most primitive on — depend, and thereby the sense of the entire being it claims to cognize. But such questions are precisely the *transcendental* questions; and they are, already on this ground, of so distinguished a kind that they must precede all objective, all non-transcendental questions, and thus the science of transcendental subjectivity must precede all the others, the objective sciences. Husserl is careful to fix the sense of "precede": not in the sense of historical genesis, but in that which the idea of philosophy — the necessary idea of a most genuine and strictest science — prescribes. For what such a science wants is nothing more and nothing less than really to fulfill its sense as science; and that means it does not want to let itself count as science so long as it does not understand itself, its method and results — so long as it is still in the condition of constantly speaking about matters and setting up theories for them whose principial sense remains unintelligible to it. The reader should mark this as the criterion of the whole work: the priority of transcendental science is not temporal or genetic but a priority of grounding, demanded by the very idea of a science that would understand its own accomplishment.

The opinion here, Husserl insists, is by no means merely that it is the function of transcendental science to hold off from all sciences (and, in reflective relating-back, from itself) certain unpleasant misinterpretations that might attach to its method or to the sense of the objective being cognized in it. That would be almost as if a well-fixed *blind* (*Blende*) against everything transcendental, or a carefully clever fixing of the gaze upon the material connections resulting in straight gaze-direction and evidence — under strict avoidance of all concepts and thoughts stemming from regard to the cognizing, constituting consciousness — could already create strictest, fully sufficient science. But the *absence of misinterpretations* does not yet mean a *winning of correct interpretations*; and unasked questions, perhaps the most urgent, are also unanswered questions. The reader should weigh this carefully against a possible misreading of Husserl's own program: transcendental phenomenology is not a hygiene that merely keeps objective science free of metaphysical contamination; it is the positive science of constitution, and to treat it as mere prophylaxis is to miss it entirely.

Husserl grants a limited legitimacy to the objective blindness even as he condemns its hardening. Perhaps the putting-on of the *transcendental blinkers* (*Scheuklappen*) is at times useful, even a necessary aid, in order to bring about, in objective gaze-direction, the great product of scientific theory, the objectively scientific. But if this spiritual blinker grows fast, as it were, if the ignoring of the transcendental becomes habitual *blindness*, then the advantage of lacking misinterpretations is very dearly bought; for now there falls away every interpretation, including the one that must be carried out so that we know what, properly and ultimately, we are about with the world, and what kind of practical-ethical position-takings it ultimately demands of us. Here Husserl quietly reconnects the transcendental task to the ethical-cultural mission set out in the early lectures: the point of clarifying the world's sense is finally to know how to stand toward it and to act, so that transcendental science is not a withdrawal from life but the condition of a responsible relation to it.

That the relating-back of the world — the world simply existing for us in naive experiential positing — to the cognizing subjectivity (especially when this is Cartesianly intuited as the pure one) has *nothing to say* for the true being itself and its absolute sense, is by no means the case, Husserl argues, and he illustrates with Leibniz. *Leibniz* meant, in his ingenious *aperçu* of a monad-doctrine, that according to its last true being everything existing reduces to monads — and that means nothing other than to Cartesian *egos*. It could in the end be that a transcendental-philosophically grounded world-consideration demanded precisely such or a similar interpretation as a plain necessity. And so it perhaps justifies itself, after all, to bring transcendental consideration of cognition and metaphysics into nearest relation — as then ultimate, absolutely grounded, hence transcendental science must *eo ipso* accomplish this: to lead us, with its sense-clarification of being, to ultimate information about this being. The reader should note the measured modality ("it could be," "perhaps"): Husserl is not endorsing a monadological metaphysics here but indicating that transcendental clarification of sense is not metaphysically idle — that the reduction to constituting subjectivity may carry, as its own consequence, positive results about what beings ultimately are.

Having traversed the horizon of philosophical problematic that opened from the Cartesian *ego cogito* and that at the same time lets the motivation of the skepsis come to full effect, Husserl turns back again to Descartes himself, this great beginner of the modern age — and now the verdict falls in full. We must, he says, unfortunately establish that Descartes knows *nothing* of this great problematic, which yet lay graspably in his horizon. Of the necessity and idea of a transcendental science of consciousness, a *transcendental egology* (*transzendentale Egologie*), as we could also say, he has no inkling — he who yet has the immortal fame of having, through the discovery of this transcendental *ego*, laid free the theme and, in the manifolds of the pure consciousness-life, the working-field for such a science. The retained term *transzendentale Egologie* names, proleptically, the very discipline the *Cartesian Meditations* of 1929 will carry out; here it is introduced as the science Descartes made possible and missed.

The consciousness driving him — of the insufficiency of all hitherto sciences and the necessity of an absolute science secured against every possible skepsis — works itself out, Husserl concedes, in a grand and most impressive train of thought and system; but from the outset *not* in the Meditations that were here the demanded ones, and not in a system that could at least, according to its *style*, anticipate the future philosophy. He did not offer Meditations that would have laid clear the innermost sense of the problematic awakened through the skepsis (as a transcendental one), and the innermost sense of the dogmatic naivety of objective science, and finally the innermost sense of fully sufficient science, and that would have projected the necessary way to it, to it as transcendental. Descartes is the genuine beginner of philosophy — of philosophy itself, the true — but only in the *beginning of the beginning*. For only the beginning of his Meditations, which culminates in the *ego cogito*, prefigures already, despite its still naive and crude conduct of thought, the necessary style that is classical for *meditationes de prima philosophia*. Husserl said "despite a still naive and crude conduct of thought" because in it there rules, instead of the ultimately clear insight into what is here at work, the mere *instinct of the great genius*. The reader should register the exact location of Descartes' greatness and his failure on Husserl's map: the *opening* of the Meditations, the path to the *cogito*, is classical and permanent; everything Descartes builds upon it is naive, instinctual, and finally misdirected.

Descartes, Husserl says in the image that gathers the indictment, remained standing *before* the gate of transcendental philosophy — the alone truly radical philosophy — that he himself opened; the passage into the never-trodden but very well to-be-trodden *"realm of the Mothers"* (*Reich der Mütter*) he did not enter. His philosophical radicalism failed. His conviction that one must go back to the primal-grounds of all cognition in the transcendental subjectivity brought, for him and the following time, not the right fruit, because he was precisely unable to do justice to the deeper sense of such a radicalism. He misunderstands his own good beginning, because he does not drive the clarifying *Besinnung* through to the fulfilling end; therefore he succumbs at once to problems that would otherwise have been recognizable to him as countersensical. With just this hangs together all the great calamity that Descartes brought in one with the new and blessing-rich impulses of the more recent philosophy. His unclarities, his pseudo-problems, his perverse two-substance doctrine — on the underground of a no-less perverse grounding of the mathematical sciences — determine and lead astray the future. So little does Descartes become the founder of a philosophy built on the transcendental ground, the *ego cogito*, and then really transcendental, that he remains thoroughly captive in the *objectivistic prejudice*. The Borrowed Goethean image of the *Reich der Mütter* should be read as Husserl's name for the dimension of constituting origins — the field of pure consciousness as the maternal ground from which all objective sense is born — which Descartes glimpsed at its threshold and declined to enter.

His whole apparatus of philosophically meditating method serves finally, Husserl charges, to *save* the objective world, the substrate of the objective sciences, and these themselves, against the attacks of the skepsis; in particular his aim goes toward according to mathematics and mathematical natural science — in the form and method in which they were in the new becoming — the right of absolute validity and the role of prototype for all genuine sciences. The pure *ego* discovered by him is to him nothing other than the *pure soul*, as the little piece of the objective world given to every cognizer in absolute indubitability and *uniquely* given in immediacy, from which it is a matter of assuring oneself, by inference, of the rest of the world. Precisely because the proper transcendental problematic, upon which he yet strikes with the evidence-problem, did not come to understanding for him, he does not see the *countersense* of this whole conception and of the evidence-theory grounding it. He does not see the countersense of conceiving evidence as a *criterion*, as a mere *index* of truth, and the countersense of every proof that is in turn to secure the legitimacy of this indexing; and he does not see the countersense of all the supposed inferences that are to lead over from the transcendental sphere, out of the pure *ego*, into the objective sphere. The reader should mark the precise philosophical error Husserl pins on Descartes: to treat evidence as an external sign pointing to a truth lying beyond it — and then to need a proof (the veracious God) to license the sign — is to miss that evidence *is* the self-givenness of the matter itself, not a mark of an inaccessible in-itself; and to "infer" the world from the *ego* is countersense because the world's whole sense is constituted *within* the very subjectivity from which one pretends to reason outward.

As an evil inheritance this countersense passes through the modern age, in the form of all theories of a transcendental "realism," and the other countersense-motives enter the future. The *objectivistic basic posture* of the Cartesian philosophy and the whole style of its science-grounding gave the new exact sciences, and all further positive sciences striving after their model, a *seeming right* to hold themselves for absolute sciences and finally to set themselves over against philosophy as originally self-standing sciences. This objectivistic trait demanded the formation of *psychologistic and naturalistic theories of reason*, with whose hidden countersense the centuries now had to toil. Here Husserl draws the great historical parallel that closes the chapter's argument: the situation had some similarity with the post-Platonic one in antiquity, in which we can quite well already speak of psychologism and naturalism. Here as there these words designate fundamentally perverse theories of reason, springing from *minglings* of transcendental question-settings with psychological or biological-natural-scientific ones. In antiquity *Plato* is the beginner who, looking upon the skepsis, puts the possibility of cognition radically in question and meditates upon its positive solution under the title "dialectic," attempting first projects; but already since *Aristotle* the impetus of this radicalism weakens — as Husserl showed — under the impression of just-then-succeeding objective sciences, too impressive for one to have had much inclination still to ponder, so seriously, the depths of the skeptical problematic. And accordingly one feels little the psychologism stuck in the ancient logic and ethics. The reader should hold this analogy in view: the modern repetition of the ancient lapse — radical beginning, then objectivistic relapse, then unnoticed psychologism — is for Husserl no accident but the recurring shape of philosophy's failure to enter the transcendental dimension.

In the modern age, Husserl says, it goes somewhat similarly. The *Cartesian* radicalism, not penetrating deep enough, finds no serious succession, since the sciences shape themselves so splendidly and self-standingly in their own method. Yet — again similarly as in antiquity — the cognition-problem has been set so weightily into play that it could not, and could and must again and again draw the interest to itself over against the positive sciences. Again its theoretical treatments work themselves out in psychologistic and naturalistic shapes. And here Husserl makes the transition that gives the lecture, and the whole main text in this range, its forward edge: of particular significance for the fate of the further development becomes *Locke's* essay on the human understanding — the ground-work of modern sensualistic psychology on the basis of inner experience, and in one with it the ground-work of epistemological psychologism.

A remarkable and instructive *displacement* (*Verschiebung*) accomplishes itself here, Husserl observes, and the reader should follow it closely, for it defines the line that the subsequent (post-p.77) history of the work will pursue through Berkeley and Hume. Of a radical philosophy, of a systematic grounding of an in-advance radically put-in-question science on absolutely secure ground, there is seriously no more talk. The world stands firm, and the possibility of objective science at bottom too. But it is a matter of studying its *tool*, the human understanding, in the right way, in order to be able to further it. What thereby must be the self-evident and concretely taken theme is, according to Locke, nothing other than the *Cartesian ego* — only, indeed, grasped in a naturally objective way as the human soul, purely in itself, our human spirit as it finds itself in evident inner experience. Had Descartes omitted — what, in the connection of his radical grounding of cognition, was fateful enough — to make the *ego cogito* the theme of a proper science, it is the new thing of Locke that he *does* this, but, quite naturalistically attuned, takes the *ego* as soul in the pre-given world. Locke thus advances by one step (he thematizes the *ego*) and relapses by a deeper one (he naturalizes it back into the world), and this double movement is the "displacement" that delivers the transcendental problem over to psychology.

What Locke strives for, Husserl specifies, is not a psychology in the full sense; he expressly excludes all psychophysical, or as he expresses it, "physical" consideration of the spirit — but indeed a discipline that orders itself to a whole psychology as a self-enclosed connection. For the soul belongs to the world as soul of its body, stands thus in the interweaving of psychophysical causality; and to research it both according to its inwardness and according to the causal outer interweavings that stand under the all-binding causal lawfulness is the task of the complete psychology. Locke, however, wants to give a mere *history of the soul*; he wants to study the soul in its own inner being and purely on the ground of inner experience in a merely "historical" way. This comparison with the historical points, Husserl explains, to the fact that it is a matter of a *descriptive* consideration of the purely psychical inwardness, and therein of a systematic description of the psychical development, beginning from the first awakening of the soul-life. And yet that does not yet designate what he is properly driving at; for on the other side the title *Essay concerning Human Understanding* points to the fact that it is the *development of the understanding*, of the cognition-faculty, upon which Locke directs his proper aim.

Precisely on this way, Husserl states — and that is the proper theme of the work — Locke wants to make intelligible the essence, possibility, reach, extent, and limits of the validity of cognition and of all descriptively distinguishable kinds of cognition, and in further sequence to subject to a clarification the essence, ground-kinds, right-spheres of possible and legitimate sciences and of the methods constitutive for them, and by means of the same to win norms that have to guide the human being, as understanding being, in scientific doing. Something similar he then also has in view for human ethical doing and its norming. The reader should recognize in this program the legitimate descendant of the demand for a science of subjectivity grounding logic and ethics — and precisely therefore the fateful character of Locke's naturalization, for he takes up the right task on the wrong ground.

Locke does not see, Husserl concludes — and these are the closing diagnostic strokes of the main text in this range — that the cognition-problems of possible validity, grasped in their purity and principially, are *incompatible* with the objectivism of his method; that they *eo ipso* demand putting the universe of objectivity radically in question — as Descartes had already done — and holding oneself quite exclusively on the ground of pure consciousness. And all the more does he not see what had already escaped Descartes, and what had let him miss a true transcendental science: that the proper task here is to research systematically *consciousness as consciousness of something*, and in particular in regard to the distinguished consciousness-connections in which, for the cognizer, under the titles evidence and insightful grounding, consciousness originally, in its own connection, produces the *self-having and self-confirming* of an objective. He does not see that true objectivity is something that can experience sense and original realizing confirmation *only in consciousness*; or that true being indicates a teleology immanent to the subject, which lets itself be intuitively understood according to essence-peculiarities and laws — and that it is precisely this that must here be accomplished. With these two final formulas — true objectivity as having sense and confirmation only within consciousness, and true being as the index of an immanent teleology of the subject, intuitively understandable in its essential laws — Husserl states, at the threshold of Locke, the whole positive task that the *Kritische Ideengeschichte* has been clearing the ground for: the constitutive, eidetic science of transcendental subjectivity, in which the self-confirmation of objectivity in evidence becomes the proper theme. The main text of the critical history of ideas breaks off here, at Hua p. 77, with Locke's failure named precisely as the measure of what genuine transcendental phenomenology must do; the lectures that follow in the volume (on Berkeley, Hume, and the systematic ascent) carry the history forward from exactly this diagnosed displacement.

## **Second Section: The Beginning-Grounds of the Attempt at an Egology in Locke and Its Abiding Problematic**

## **First Chapter: The Fundamental Limitation of Locke's Horizon and Its Grounds**

### **Twelfth Lecture: The naive dogmatism of objectivism**

Husserl opens the Second Section by restating, in a sharper form, the verdict on Locke with which the First Section closed. What was said at the end of the last lecture can also be expressed thus: *Locke* did not see the radical cognition-problem that the ancient skepticism had posed, and so it is naturally not the theme of his *Essay*. And yet the *Essay* wants to be a theory of the understanding, an *Erkenntnistheorie* (theory of cognition) — and indeed one that would put an end to the endless disputes of metaphysics and procure for all sciences the clarification, necessary to their perfection, about the true sense of their accomplishments, about the ultimate source of their basic concepts and methods. Its aim goes upon the principial: both upon what is common to all sciences überhaupt and as such, and upon what determines the differences of essentially separating science-types, such as the difference of the empirical and purely rational sciences. The reader should note the structural irony Husserl is setting up: Locke wants exactly what a transcendental theory of cognition would want — the principial clarification of all science — but pursues it on a ground that makes the wanted clarification impossible.

If Descartes, in striving after a true and genuine philosophy as the system of absolutely grounded, absolutely self-justifying sciences, had struck upon the cognition-problems and had himself already demanded at least a theory of the understanding that should precede all genuine sciences, then Locke wants really to *carry out* such a theory, and indeed for just such purposes. And yet Locke is not the legitimate heir of the Cartesian spirit, and the most valuable impulse that lay in the *Meditations* he did not take up. Descartes himself, Husserl recalls, we had to reproach likewise — that he struck upon the transcendental cognition-problem but did not properly see it, misunderstood it, and that he therefore had to fail with his project of a radically grounded universal science. Instead of a transcendental egology and the genuine transcendental theory of cognition enclosed in it, Descartes falls onto the wrong path of a *theological* theory of cognition and a dogmatic metaphysics. But Locke surrenders also the *great and significant thing of the Cartesian beginning* — and thereby precisely the source-site of the immediate motivation, guided by which one could at any time have passed over to a better ascent, to a philosophy, and first of all to a transcendental theory of cognition (the editorial note refers here to Beilage XII, Hua VII pp. 343 ff.). Husserl's measured comparative judgment must be held exactly: Descartes misused a genuine beginning; Locke abandoned the beginning itself, sinking back below even Descartes' level of radicality.

Instead of beginning, as Descartes did, by putting all sciences and the experiential world itself in question, Locke quite naively presupposes the validity of the new objective sciences, and all the more does the existence of the experienced world count for him as self-evident. He does not see the *countersense and circle* enclosed in his and every similar doctrine of cognition; and it is of decisive importance, Husserl insists, to assure oneself of this fully. What is theme in the epistemological writings since Locke can be designated in advance with an indeterminately general expression as "clarification of objective cognition überhaupt." *Cognition* (*Erkenntnis*) is therein at first a most general title for all the manifold ways of subjective having-conscious of objective, in which the I in question experiences just that which it can most generally express with the words: I am conscious of an objective — and which it designates, as the case may be, with the particular expressions: I perceive things, humans; I remember them or expect them; I present them unclearly; I do all such in certainty or in uncertainty; they are conscious to me as mere possibilities; I surmise that they are here or there; I was certain and now become doubtful, or end in the conviction of non-being. Here belong naturally also all the predicative (conceiving) and especially the theoretical-scientific judgment-acts. The reader should register that Husserl is here laying out the full breadth of *Bewußtsein von etwas* — the whole field of intentional consciousness with its modalities of certainty — as the field that an *Erkenntnistheorie* must take as its theme.

Over against all such having-conscious — which in the widest sense is called a "meaning" (*Vermeinen*) of such and such a sense and of these or those certainty-modes — stands the mere phantasying or fictionalizing, which is no actual meaning but precisely a phantasying-oneself-into a meaning. This too the title cognition draws into its circle, for easily evident reasons. To carry out cognition of this circle — to be conscious of any objective in this or that special mode of consciousness — does *not* mean to have this having-conscious itself in the actual cognition-domain; it means to be thematically occupied with the object in question, but not to have the having-conscious of the object as theme. The task of the clarification of cognition — more distinctly, of the cognizing *experiencing* (*Erleben*) — has therefore its source and sense in this: to become aware *of that*. The distinction is the methodological pivot of the lecture: in straightforward cognition the object is thematic and may become clear and evident, but the cognizing itself, the subjective experiencing in its changing modes through which the object becomes object for us, remains unknown and unclear — and it is precisely this that the theory of cognition must bring to thematic relief.

But the particular aim of the theory of cognition goes, on the underground of the general study of cognition-formations in this widest sense, upon explaining that particular cognition-activity present in the cognition-accomplishments in the *pregnant* sense. Meaning überhaupt, consciousness überhaupt of every kind, stands under a possible *teleological* appraisal; it has, either from the outset or as it can take up into itself, a directing of the I upon a *telos*, upon the object itself in its true being and being-thus. There separate *meant cognition* (as meaning überhaupt) and *cognition in the pregnant sense*, as that cognition — that distinguished meaning — in which the cognizer has the consciousness of being at the goal itself. In connection herewith there belong, to the mere meanings (the shape of merely aiming, merely intending consciousness), transition-shapes of consciousness: the *confirmations* (*Bewährungen*) and their negative counterpart, the *refutations*; a unitary consciousness, a cognition-path, leads the mere aiming over into the fulfilling aiming or, in the opposite case, into another end-consciousness, in which a self-grasped, an attained goal steps forth, to which the previously aiming meaning conflicts and on which it "annuls itself" (*hebt sich auf*). The reader should mark how Husserl folds the whole doctrine of intention and fulfillment — meaning, confirmation, the synthesis of identification — into the very definition of the epistemological task, so that "cognition in the pregnant sense" is from the start understood as the teleological accomplishment of self-givenness.

Even such distinguished processes of cognizing in the pregnant sense — the teleological processes of *reason-accomplishing* (*Vernunftleisten*) — are, in natural enactment, hidden, unknown, and not themselves again cognized. They need the clarifying bringing-into-relief, the reflection thematically directed upon them that brings them to clear self-grasping, and they need a systematically cognizing research directed upon them, so that it be understood what the cognizing accomplishing of objective properly is and how it manages to bring an objective in attainingly — or how objective is to be understood, once as merely meant and the other time as truly existing, as attained (and then at any time attainable) goal. Husserl gives the standing example: it is unintelligible *how* in our outer experiencing — a stream of subjective experiencing that makes up a constant stratum in our waking consciousness-life — precisely this accomplishment comes about that makes possible the expression "I constantly experience a spatio-temporal nature," "I experience these and those things." In the respective experiencing itself lies as meaning: there is a thing, an objective, so and so constituted, so and so changing, acting upon that other thing there; and there lies in the experiencing that in all its subjective change this thing is one and the same objective, that it, now entering into experience, has not first become now, that it is and continues to be in and for itself even when I "look away."

If one asks how what is in and for itself can be given to me in my subjective experiencing itself, self-grasped, be precisely experiencing my own, the question shows, Husserl says, that here it is unclear and unintelligible *what experiencing is in itself* and how it has objective in itself, makes an in-itself-existing conscious and exhibits it consciousness-wise. That says: in experiencing the experienced becomes known, but not the experiencing, not the essence and sense of experiencing accomplishing. And this is quite natural, since the subjective life that there is called experiencing has, in its own essence, remained hidden and never been studied. The same holds of the manifold subjective lived-experiences in which theoretical thinking carries itself out. Forming concepts on the ground of experiences, judging predicatively, we shape propositions as theoretical insights and join them to ever-higher formations; what we so win we call truths about the truly existing objects, and are convinced that they — formed in our subjective doing — have validity "in itself," similarly as experienced objects are meant by us, given thoroughgoing concordance of experiential confirmation, as in-themselves-existing. Again it needs clarifying investigation, predicatively directed upon the cognizing life and accomplishing, to make intelligible to us what in the immanence of this cognizing life properly comes to accomplishment as meant and attained theoretical truth — or as objective being, as substrate of theoretically true determination.

Having made clear what all epistemological question-settings aim at — what kind of unintelligibilities they are that adhere, in principial generality, to objective cognition überhaupt, and that to transform, in just this generality, into theoretical intelligibilities and clarities is the epistemological task — Husserl draws the methodological consequence that governs the whole critique of Locke to follow: it must now be fully clear and certain to us that in a theory of cognition *every* drawing-upon of the pre-theoretical givennesses of objective experience and *every* premise out of objective sciences is *inadmissible*. Such a drawing-upon would evidently mean a countersensical *metabasis* (μετάβασις εἰς ἄλλο γένος, a crossing-over into another genus). The universal theme of the theory of cognition, the theory of cognizing "reason," comprises all objectivity as cognized of possible cognizing, and yet no objectivity *simply*. To furnish objectivity out of experience or already out of theoretical thinking, and by means of the already acquired to acquire ever-new cognition-possession — that is, in naive-natural manner, to progress from takings-cognizance, from conceiving judgments and insights, to new ones and finally to theories, sciences. But just this is, and in every step, a *riddle*: the done, the deed is in every such step "there," it alone stands in view, is alone "theme," while the consciousness-wise life and accomplishing in which the doing itself consists is precisely lived but not thematic. The reader should hold onto this as the precise statement of the *metabasis* fallacy: a theory of cognition that helps itself to objective premises has already presupposed the very accomplishment it was supposed to clarify, and so has crossed illegitimately from the transcendental genus into the objective one.

To bring this ungseen and therefore unintelligible life — experiencingly and theorizing — into view and into work, to bring the in actual life unseen and therefore unintelligible life to understanding, to theoretical pronouncement, is the new problematic over against any and every problem of the natural attitude upon objectivity. It is therefore clear, Husserl concludes, that it is something thoroughly and radically other to pursue objective, "positive" science and — within the frame of pure subjectivity — to make intelligible all consciousness-wise having, taking-cognizance, acquiring of objective, but also all consciousness-wise meaning and attaining of predicative truths and theories, *as purely subjective accomplishing*. This radical and quite unbridgeable separation would evidently subsist even if never, out of the first confused becoming-aware and not-yet-understanding of the subjective in which objective shapes itself to the cognizer, skeptical motives had grown — inclinations to deny a transcendent world or at least to hold it for unknowable and so to doubt the possibility of a science of the objectively existing. If this happens, it is all the more clear that, where the world is put in question as to being or knowability while the consciousness of it remains unaffected, no objective being and no premise of objective science can be presupposed — namely in investigations that want to meet skepticism through clarification of the sense that objectivity and its cognition have purely out of consciousness.

Husserl then states what Locke would have had to do, and the conditional sharpens the failure into a precise list. Did Locke want — what he and all coming theorists of cognition undoubtedly wanted — to lay clear the general essence of the cognition-accomplishment; did he want that, in order to win principial norms for the cognizing doing, the imperfect be perfected and so first of all genuine science, science out of principial self-responsibility (*Selbstverantwortung*), be made possible: then he had to bring the sense of his undertaking to unconfusable clarity and hold it therein. He had to see that the proper accomplishment of cognition — of genuine, so-called rational cognition — is nothing less than, for the cognizer, to *constitute* objectivity of every kind and shape, truly existing, true assertion, true theory and science, attainingly. It had to be seen and held fast in radical grasp that objectivity, as something *attainable in subjectivity*, can have its site nowhere else than precisely in its consciousness-domain itself (that of actual and possible consciousness), and that it can have no sense to set it outside all possible consciousness, as something by which consciousness could direct itself, say through imaging or indicating — as if that by which a consciousness directs itself, even an imaging and indicating one, could exhibit itself elsewhere than in consciousness, and as if the self-directing itself actualized itself otherwise than as in *syntheses of fulfilling identification*. It must therein above all also be seen that everything simply and self-evidently pre-given as existing is that only as experienced of experiencing acts, and draws sense and validity only from them. Here the established sense of *konstituieren* must again be heard: not fabrication but the attaining bringing-to-self-givenness of the identical objective as the accomplishment of identifying synthesis — and Husserl's point is that objectivity *as attainable* can have no sense except within the field of actual and possible consciousness.

Was the Cartesian *cogito*, with its significant underground of a generally questioned world and science, already discovered, then this, one should think, could with some deepening easily be seen. But too strong is the tendency of the relapse into the natural-naive thought-attitude; and if a Cartesian radicalism did not here suffice, how much less Locke, to whom anything like radicalism was from the outset foreign.
