By letting the Cartesian *beginning* — this real beginning of an overcoming of naive dogmatism — fall uncomprehendingly, sinking wholly into naivety, he made it quite extraordinarily difficult for the future to work its way through to that basic insight on which every beginning of a countersense-free theory of cognition, and thereby also philosophy, depends: namely the insight into the *pure sense of the question-setting itself*. The reader should weigh how much Husserl loads onto this single point: the whole subsequent difficulty of modern philosophy is traced to Locke's loss of the *sense of the question* — the failure to see that the epistemological question, rightly understood, forbids all objective premises.

Locke, firmly persisting in his naively dogmatic attitude, yet wants to solve the principial problems of the understanding, of reason; they then become for him quite self-evidently *psychological problems*. The grounding of genuine objective science and philosophy carries itself out on the ground of an itself objective science: psychology; self-evidently it offers itself, for the naturally attuned, as the science within which the essence and the norms of genuine cognition and scientific method are to be researched. Thus fundamentally different problems mingle confusedly throughout the whole Locke-led modern age, mirroring themselves in the double sense of the names "theory of cognition," "theory of reason" — of principial difference, and yet inwardly essentially connected; two tasks whose separation and, on the other hand, understanding mutual relating was from now on the task of the further philosophical development, if in the confusion of philosophies the one true and genuine philosophy was ever to spring up. Husserl spells out the double sense exactly, and the reader should keep the two meanings apart, for the whole following critique turns on their confusion. "Theory of cognition," "theory of the understanding, of reason," can mean, and legitimately mean: a *psychology of human cognizing* — of human reason as a human soul-faculty making up a component in the whole connection of human soul-life, as the psychology of cognition makes up a component of the full psychology. On the other hand, the same words can also mean: a *transcendental* theory of cognition and reason — for which every psychology is *not* home-site, not a site of usable premises, but, like every objective science and every being-domain assigned to it, *problematic*, namely belongs itself to the problem.

However fateful this *mingling* (*Vermengung*) was for the following time, and however long it barred the way into a genuine theory of reason, it did not, Husserl grants, wholly deflect the developmental direction of the more recent philosophy. For beside the separation of the two-sided problematic he touched already on their inner connection. A mingling running through millennia, in which the problems are always only in treatment as two-sidedly shimmering or squinting, must naturally have its grounds in inner relations, in essence-connections; and these had to prove effective too, once the epistemological interest had come to such liveliness — they had to keep open possibilities for transitions from psychology into the transcendental problematic. In the naivety of the objective psychological method there could lie, for all reinterpretation and ambiguity, valuable moments stemming from those essence-relations, and thereby valuable driving-forces into the future. The reader should note that Husserl does not treat psychology as simply an error to be discarded: the very confusion of psychology with transcendental philosophy rests on a genuine essence-relation (the same lived-experiences are described in both), which is why psychology can keep open a path toward the transcendental even while obstructing it.

And so, Husserl says, we have every ground to speak further of Locke, in depicting the motivation that moves the future development and drives it toward the grounding of a transcendental theory of the understanding and philosophy. In a certain way this psychologism was after all a *progress* — namely as reaction against the *Cartesian and Cambridge Platonism* of the doctrine of the *ideae innatae* (innate ideas). We can quite well characterize this doctrine too as psychologism, and more determinately as *theological* psychologism. What in it is epistemologically in question is the quite distinguished position of certain concepts as basic concepts, and of propositions belonging to them as axiomatic basic principles for all sciences, as the appointed principial norms to which all scientific theory — all theorizing doing — is quite principially bound. It concerns naturally all logical, but also the formal mathematical, and again the ethical basic concepts; for evidently the basic concepts and basic principles of ethics have an analogous position to the whole of life-praxis as the logical concepts to the accomplishment of science: they give themselves as absolutely valid principial norms to which all rational praxis is bound, and accordingly parallel question-settings for a theory of practical reason attach to them. Everyone appropriates to himself all these principial stocks subjectively in his own thinking and grasps in apodictic evidence their absolute right. But the ultimate source of the absolute, trans-subjective right expressing itself in apodictic evidences is, according to the theological psychologism, *God*, who has originally implanted them in every soul — hence a theological-psychological explanation of the trans-subjective validity of the principial elements of all theories, of all rational praxis.

Against this doctrine, with which the already mentioned theological evidence-doctrine in Descartes hangs together, Locke reacts in his famous and, in his time, so effective first book of the *Essay*. What he now himself sets over against this theological psychologism is the new, the *naturalistic* psychologism. His psychology and psychological grounding of the theory of cognition excludes all theological premises; like the new natural science it is science purely out of experience or, more precisely, purely inductive *fact-science*. But, as we already know, it is a psychology of particular limitation, which was to serve exclusively the solution of the problems of cognizing and practical reason and for whose sake it renounced all psychophysical question-settings — hence a psychology purely on the ground of *inner experience*. The reader should mark that Husserl credits Locke with a real, if half-blind, insight here: by confining himself to inner experience Locke gropes toward the right method even as he misframes it.

Therein lay a significant motive, Husserl explains, insofar as it was evidently felt by Locke (and through his concrete presentations made felt all the more to his readers) that only a *descriptive* method would come into question for the cognition-problems. It had become felt that a solution of cognition-problems, and ultimately of problems of rational validity, could, according to its sense, be won only on the ground of directly intuitive consideration of the cognition-phenomena themselves — that it must therefore move in the circle of the Cartesian *ego cogito*, on the indubitable ground of the self-givenness of the cognition-experiences for the cognizer. In fact, where the actual and possible validity of any kind of objective cognition is put in question and to be subjected to a critique, the cognizing life itself is, according to its actual and possible being, the *unquestionable fact* — as that presupposed in every critical question, in this sense indubitable, at any time immediately accessible to reflective consideration. However much, on Locke's side, the *ego cogito* was objectivistically — namely anthropologically-psychologically — misinterpreted, it was yet a great progress that now the formation of a *pure egology*, omitted by Descartes, was at least attempted in psychological turning and misinterpretation — thus as a psychological egology, as a kind of *history of the human inwardness*. With this carefully balanced verdict the lecture closes: Locke's naturalistic, inner-experiential psychology is at once a regression beneath Cartesian radicalism and a progress beyond Descartes' omission, because it at least attempts the descriptive science of the *ego* that Descartes never undertook. The Thirteenth Lecture takes up the *empiricist prejudices* that vitiate this attempt and the psychologism in the theory of cognition.

### **Thirteenth Lecture: The empiricist prejudices — the psychologism in the theory of cognition**

Husserl begins by granting, counterfactually, what Locke's descriptions could have achieved, in order to measure precisely what they lacked. Had it really come, in Locke, to the methodically demanded descriptions of the immanently intuitive consciousness-stocks of an *ego* describing itself reflectively and purely inwardly — to a genuine elementary analysis of the consciousness-life and a genuine exhibition of its construction out of the elementary — then this accomplishment would have been of definitive significance not only for a genuine psychology but also for a *transcendental egology*. The essential content of the descriptions would, after clarification of the misinterpretations and under corresponding further working, have come to the benefit of transcendental science. The reader should keep this conditional in view: Husserl's quarrel is not with description of inner experience as such — that is precisely what transcendental phenomenology will perfect — but with the empiricist misframing and the descriptive failure that prevented Locke from carrying it out.

What was lacking here lies, more exactly designated, in *different lines*. The one concerns the basic defect of the empirical-inductive manner of consideration. The new psychology, as it is already in the making through Descartes himself and his contemporary *Hobbes*, constitutes itself as a *purely inductive* science modeled on the new natural science — we could say, as a "natural science" of the psychical. It remains this even if, for certain philosophical purposes, value is laid on first forming it as a merely descriptive natural-*history* of the soul-life in the frame of inner experience, as Locke first did. But now it is to be considered, Husserl argues, that, just as the analytic logic, so the theory of reason appearing with it in unclear interweaving — as a principial doctrine, indeed as the most principial conceivable — had, according to its own sense, *eo ipso* to become an *a priori* theory: an a priori one in the original Platonic sense of an idea-science drawing out of eidetic intuition. The whole first line of critique turns on this point: a theory of reason is by its very sense an eidetic, a priori science, and to pursue it as an inductive fact-science is to mistake its kind.

There is certainly also an empirical doctrine of the understanding and an empirical *typics* of the human understanding, Husserl concedes — whether related quite generally to the empirical species human, or related in special types to races, peoples, ages, estates, individuals, life-ages. Such types let themselves be researched empirically-descriptively and inductively, and one can possibly draw use from these researches: individual-pedagogically, national-pedagogically. But when one therein everywhere speaks of understanding or non-understanding, of greater and lesser understanding-accomplishments, of typical errors, then there stands behind or above all, as sense-determining and norming, a *pure logic*, and for a deepest and ultimate understanding of such accomplishment out of the accomplishing carrying itself out unrecognized in the consciousness-life, a *transcendental theory* that, as clarifying the principial essence, is *a priori*. The reader should mark the two-storey structure: empirical typics of cognition is legitimate, but it presupposes above itself a pure logic (on the object-side) and a transcendental theory (on the subjective side), both a priori — and Locke's error is to put the empirical fact-science in the place that only an eidetic science can occupy.

If one finds a difficulty in that it belongs to the universal task of such a theory itself to clarify the essence of a priori cognition just as much as, on the other hand, the essence of empirical cognition as such — and if one finds, finally, difficulties in the self-relatedness imposed on every theory of cognition understood in fullest universality — then in any case so much is to be seen in advance, Husserl says, that if it is to be undertaken at all, it can be undertaken only in the form and pretension of an *idea-science*, a purely rational science of the essence of cognition-subjectivity and its possible accomplishments. He adds a sharp observation on the possibility of self-deception about one's own method: it is conceivable that someone *means* to carry out empirical cognition, e.g. cognition-psychology, and in truth wins cognition of a priori necessity. So there are in our positivistic age mathematicians enough who hold their purely a priori cognitions — de facto insighted by them in pure generality and necessity — for empirical, in that they, reflecting unclearly upon their clear doing, succumb to fashionable theories. The example is pointed: a thinker may do a priori work while telling himself an empiricist story about it, and this is exactly Locke's case with his immanent descriptions.

In this sense, Husserl explains, Locke's immanent descriptions could very well have been epistemologically fruit-bringing, for all the misinterpretation of their principial sense. And so it would in fact stand, if Locke had really carried out methodically correct consciousness-analyses — if he had, in purely held inner experience and inner phantasy, subjected the offering concrete shapes of actual and possible immanent lived-experiences to a systematic elementary analysis, and thereby, under careful, terminologically holding concept-formation, brought about exact descriptions. But to such descriptions it did not come, neither in Locke nor in other psychologists and psychological theorists of cognition. This is the second line of the critique, and Husserl marks it as one of the most remarkable facts in the history of the sciences.

That theoretical explanations referring to infinities of mathematical form (mathematical manifolds) succeed very hard — explanations achievable only through highly composite, stage-wise built-up, highly artful concept-formations and deductive theories — that is not astonishing. But what seems methodically simpler and easier than *description*\! To be sure, the systematics of description in the mastering of great world-domains that, like the natural-historical ones, are over-rich in exceedingly complicatedly built formations, also offers the scientific spirit difficulties. But there it is a matter of *objective* descriptions, each of which, in every stroke and with respect to the establishing of the objectively regularly belonging-together special marks, presupposes manifold objective observations and inductions, and for all that great undertakings like voyages of discovery. Otherwise in the *purely subjective* sphere: here every description, as soon as it begins, takes possession of its object in *adequate* experience. The experiencing grasping cannot itself already be a milieu of particular difficulties — as if there subsisted principial dangers of missing the objects to be described altogether. The reader should feel the force of the puzzle Husserl is setting up: in the inner sphere the object of description is, by all rights, adequately and indubitably given, so description ought to be easy — and yet it failed.

But precisely that is, remarkably, the state of affairs in the Lockean psychology and theory of cognition. One asserts to describe experienced in purely inner experience, and has never really carried out such pure experience, never really seen its genuine stocks, never been able to carry out a real analysis in its circle and therefore never to achieve a genuine systematic description. The failure of the Lockean method has deep grounds lying in the essence of the at-once describing and described subjectivity itself; the difficulties are by no means accidental. Husserl now locates the ground of the failure exactly. *Outer* experience, objective experience überhaupt, is experience of the natural attitude; to it belongs also the common self-experience of the human being — that self-experience which he, in his practically active life, in intercourse with his fellow-humans, again and again referred back upon himself, carries out in alternation with mere thing-experience, as natural, free, as-if-self-running life-activity. When now psychology as experiential science seeks descriptions and explanations, this natural self-experience serves it, as the spatial-thingly, the so-called "sensuous," "outer" experience serves the natural scientist. The reader should note the diagnosis: Locke takes over, uncritically, the *natural-attitude* self-experience — the everyday objective apperception of oneself as a human being in the world — and mistakes it for the pure inner experience that the epistemological task requires.

What methodical grounds psychology quite generally had, and a genuinely scientific psychology had to have, to carry out descriptions in the frame of the purely immanent self-experience, the purely inner — hence essentially in the frame of the evidence of indubitable self-givenness made valid by Descartes but perhaps critically to be limited — does not concern us, Husserl says, at this place. For Locke and the psychologizing theory of cognition such grounds lie, as we already heard, in the problematic of cognition as to essence and understanding-wise validity. There the surely indubitable thought leads: that one can clarify the accomplishments of cognition only if one looks at them oneself, lays them out for oneself in fixing analysis — that scientific clarifying can ensue only on the ground of scientific descriptions. But then self-evidently the object of these descriptions is cognition as it is in pure own-essentiality, and in this own-essentiality is given only in the pure *ego cogito* or, as Locke says, in inner experience. The reasoning is impeccable as far as it goes; the failure lies not in the demand for inner description but in the inability to attain its pure object.

But here, Husserl insists, there subsist grounds lying in the nature of the description-situation itself that stand opposed to the carrying-out of a really pure inner experience, and of a self-evidently observing, fixing one. The difficulties of an *inner observation* were talked about enough in the course of the times and especially in the most recent — occasioned by the fact that the descriptions of different observers, quite otherwise than with those in the realm of outer experience (although the latter claims no indubitable evidence for itself), would so little harmonize. Every attempt at an unobjectionable, really convincing exhibition of a descriptive finding as the in-itself-true, by which the dispute could in the given case be decided, failed. But all talking-about of the difficulty helped little, so long as one in truth had no inner experience at all and no pure description of the corresponding pure inwardness. Here Husserl makes the decisive methodological announcement: such pure inner experience needs, as he will later show in detail, in order to come about at all, to obtain its purity, and to carry with itself scientific security of the preservation of this purity, *a proper method, the method of the phenomenological reduction*. The reader should register that the *phänomenologische Reduktion* is introduced here precisely as the solution to the historical failure of inner description — the method that first makes the pure field accessible and keeps it free of objective admixture.

Only, namely, when the natural objectivistic attitude with all its givennesses is *inhibited* (*unterbunden*) — which precisely this method teaches to accomplish — only when accordingly the otherwise unavoidable *mingling-in* of objectively experienced (or, what is the same, of what is transcendent to the pure inwardness) is made quite impossible, can this difficulty be overcome. Only then can it in fact be seen what lies before us under the title of the "inner" — that purely enclosed in the evidence of the *ego cogito*. And only then steps forth, can and must be seen — provided that prejudices in a kind of hypnosis do not again make blind to the really seen — that the whole inner life is through and through *consciousness and at once conscious*, and therefore to be described only as such. The reader should mark the positive result the reduction discloses: the inner field is not a collection of soul-data but consciousness-of-something throughout, intentional in its whole texture.

Indeed it shows itself at once, Husserl continues, that the genuine "inner experience" is not, as in the Lockean *tabula-rasa* conception, something like a field, a plane, or a space, over which a mobile spiritual gaze would only glide on all sides, simply grasping and fixing one after another the givennesses appearing therein, guided by the space-like order-sequence. Rather, in ever-newly setting-in reflections, and in reflections of different stage that set in on already reflectively given, there steps forth the *enormous fullness of consciousness-modes*; in many, very different ways consciousness can itself again appear as conscious of consciousness, and this again in progressing implication; indeed even manifold continua appear — continua of consciousness of consciousness of consciousness. Here the *perceiving* in which the inner stocks are grasped belongs itself among the inwardly perceivable stocks; it can be reflected upon, and only therefore do we know of it and reckon it, describingly, itself to the realm of "inner experience"; likewise naturally the describing, the theorizing — in active enactment ungrasped, unobserved, it becomes itself reflexively graspable and describable, this describing of higher stage again, and so on. The reader should hold this picture of *iterated reflection* against the flat Lockean image of a tablet scanned by a gaze: the inner field is a stratified, self-implicating structure of consciousness-of-consciousness, not a surface of data.

Continually the inwardly experienced or experienceable has, as conscious, the inseparable relation to its consciousness, through which it belongs — even unobserved, ungrasped — to the purely immanent sphere. This consciousness for its part is not something supervening and separated from its experience-content, but consciousness is consciousness *of* its content and the content is content *of* its consciousness: both inseparably one. What fullnesses of descriptive peculiarities belong to such a concretion "consciousness of something," most complicated structures already in simplest cases, is here not even to be indicated. Of all this Locke, and the following time, has no inkling. It is highly remarkable, and yet again, after knowledge of the inner grounds and the historical hindrances, intelligible, Husserl says, that a psychology and theory of cognition could for centuries speak continually of givennesses of an inner experience, of the different kinds and genera of these givennesses — such as perceptions, presentations, judgment-acts, will-acts, feelings — that one *meant* to have fixed all that scientifically in descriptive concepts, where one had never at all *seen*, never even learned to see, what lies before us in purity and what is really to be fixed in pure inwardness.

It bettered nothing, Husserl adds, that one wanted the method understood not as mere method of *inner perception* but as method of experience in the wider sense — hence preeminently of inner *experience*, thereby surrendering the absolute evidence of inner *memory*; for the principial situation is for memory the same as for perception. If it is true that a psychology is not thinkable that did not draw its entire system of constitutive primal-concepts (those concepts out of which all psychological concepts build themselves up) out of purely inner experience as the only source, then the modern psychology offers the peculiar spectacle of *meaning* to be well-grounded as science, to have its concept-material, even to have drawn it descriptively out of inner experience — and yet in truth not to have *recognized* the realm of purely inner experience that alone can furnish the genuine concepts. *Mutatis mutandis*, the same would be to say also of the modern theory of cognition, and not merely of the psychologistic one of the Lockean direction. With this double verdict — a psychology that never saw its own source-field, an epistemology that shares the blindness — the lecture closes, having located both the eidetic miscconstrual and the descriptive failure, and having named the phenomenological reduction as the still-outstanding remedy. The Fourteenth Lecture turns to a further, historical ground of the failure: the *exemplariness* of modern natural science as a hindering motive against the formation of a genuine intuitionistic science of consciousness.

### **Fourteenth Lecture: The exemplariness of modern natural science as a hindering motive for the formation of a genuine intuitionistic science of consciousness**

Husserl turns from the inner grounds of Locke's failure to the *historical* ones — motives sprung from the idea-historical situation of the modern age that functioned from the outset as hindering prejudices and prevented the given in the gaze-setting upon pure inwardness from being taken cognizance of in its own peculiarity. In this respect there worked, and works überhaupt upon psychology most harmfully, the *exemplariness of the new natural science*. To what degree it could blind even ingenious thinkers one sees already in *Hobbes*. To him natural science counts so much as the prototype of true and philosophically ultimately-possible science that he not only ascribes absolute being to material nature but conversely reduces all absolute being — including the inwardly experienced psychical being — to nature. Had Descartes set the purely grasped *ego* with its *cogitationes* as spiritual substance absolutely, Hobbes regards the subjective inner life as mere subjective semblance whose true being lies in the material psycho-physical correlates; he becomes thereby the father of modern materialism and of the new materialistic psychology. The reader should note that Husserl is charting a spectrum of ways the prototype of natural science deforms the science of consciousness — Hobbes at the extreme of outright materialist reduction, Locke in a subtler but no less fateful way.

In Hobbes' manner Locke does not let the exemplariness of natural science work on him. But it becomes fateful for him too, in another way. First: he too *absolutizes* it, and absolutizes nature in the natural-scientific determinations of his time, and as he understands these. Material bodies are absolute realities in their temporality and spatiality, in their physical properties — exclusively in geometric-mechanical determinations. Locke distinguishes here *primary properties and forces*. The primary or original properties — size, shape, position, motion or rest — are the qualities inseparably adhering to the material body in every state; by means of them it exercises force-effects on other bodies and our senses. If we now consider the experiential intuitions of bodies, the "ideas" in which the material things outside us sensibly appear to us, present themselves subjectively in us, then these ideas contain also primary qualities as analoga of the outer ones; but on the other hand also specifically sensuous qualities — color, tones, warmth and cold — to which like ones do not pertain to the material realities: they are only subjective, and only insofar objectively significant as they, by virtue of the connections of psycho-physical causality, *indicate* geometric-mechanical properties. The felt tones point to air-vibrations of certain regular forms and are causally "explained" by them, likewise the felt colors by emanations of the bodies or other physical motion-processes. The reader should recognize here the primary/secondary-quality doctrine, which Husserl is about to show carries, smuggled within it, a whole metaphysics that vitiates the epistemological task.

The in-themselves-existing material bodies are, Locke says, not only substrates of primary qualities but also substrates of *forces*; these are regarded by Locke as analoga of the psychical forces originally experienced in inner experience. Qualities and belonging forces are not self-standing elements composing the material realities in the manner of mere complexes or conglomerates, but subsist on a unitary substrate, a *substance*, which is a wholly unknown, a *je ne sais quoi*. This interpretation of natural science and its natural-scientific nature, as well as of the relation of the latter — the true nature — to nature in the sense of outer experience, works back, by virtue of the exemplariness of natural science, upon the interpretation of psychology and of the soul and the givennesses of inner experience. In Locke, as is known, also in the sense of a metaphysical significance of psychology: that, just as the physical, so the psychical acts and states have an unknown substrate, a *psychical substance* as bearer underlying them — from which it results for him that one cannot know whether it be not the same as that laid at the ground, in the scientific working of outer experience, as material substance. Husserl is drawing out how the natural-scientific substance-metaphysics propagates itself onto the soul, so that the *ego cogito* is reconstrued as the accidental surface of an unknown psychical thing.

This kind of influence of the new natural science and the metaphysics mingled with it upon Locke and the understanding-theory of the whole modern age needs, with respect to the particular pre-convictions taken over from there, no detailed critical examination, Husserl says — for here the critique is already settled through the general indication of the *faulty circle*: that a theory of reason is, according to its own sense, *critique of reason überhaupt*, and not critique in the ordinary sense of an examination of the right of particular cognition on the ground of presuppositions conceded as self-evident. He restates the positive sense of the task with precision, and the reader should hold this formulation as the standard against which Locke's circle is measured. The aim of a theory of reason goes to making clear *how*, in the unsurpassable medium of subjective meaning of whatever shapes (experiencing, theorizing, judging, valuing, practical meaning), something like *objective right* comes to light in so-called actions of reason, how it wins original sense in a particular validity-mode, that of *insight* (*Einsicht*), and how from there springs the force of an unalterable *norm* — a norm be it of truth simply, be it of possibility, probability. Theory of reason springs precisely from the becoming-aware that in the self-enclosedness of the "cogitating" *ego* all its consciousness, all its meaning, and so all its meaning of every kind of objective, carries itself out, and that all talk of truth and right draws its sense, in subjectivity itself, out of a certain particular, the insight-grounding meaning, which has its different sense-shapes according to the particular kinds of meaning and meant.

Therefore, Husserl reasons, when out of the unclarities riddles and doubts grow and objective cognition and objective accomplishment of reason becomes theme of a theory of reason, such unclarity, and so the reason-problem itself, concerns *all* cognizing, all objective meaning and grounding *in equal manner*. Hence every objective conviction is co-enclosed in the universality of the problem. The objective *presuppositionlessness* of a theory of reason therefore says the self-evident and nothing else: that one must remain constantly mindful of the sense of the reason-theoretical question-setting as a principially universal one, and accordingly may presuppose nothing in particular that has itself been put in question in the generality of the problem. In Locke's procedure, as in that of all naturalistic (anthropologistic, psychologistic) theories of reason, there lies therefore a kind of countersensical circle: it presupposes nature, natural science, as valid, while it at the same time asks after the possibility of their validity. The reader should see that this is the same circle Husserl exposed against Descartes' psychologism, now applied to the whole empiricist program: a critique of reason that helps itself to the deliverances of natural science has presupposed exactly what stands in question.

But the influence of natural science and of the naturalistic way of thinking sanctified through it shows itself, Husserl continues, in yet another and very significant peculiarity of the Lockean theory of cognition, one that determined the future development in a particularly fateful way: namely in what we call the *naturalization of consciousness* (*Naturalisierung des Bewußtseins*). According to what was just established, the Cartesian attempt — to make comprehensible, in a procedure purely grounding itself on the *ego cogito*, the possibility of objective cognition and science, and to ground it itself absolutely — finds in Locke no succession. Yet the thought of pure *immanence*, in which all transcendence must announce and exhibit itself, remains for Locke too, despite all naive inconsequence of the dogmatistic procedure, authoritative. *The only immediately given for the spirit are its own ideas* — that is an often-repeated basic principle in Locke's *Essay*. What Descartes had purely delimited under the title of the *cogitatio* with its *cogitatum qua cogitatum*, as immediate, absolutely indubitably secured givenness, as *clara et distincta perceptio* that the *ego* reflectingly carries out in pure consciousness, that is in Locke, in just this immediacy, "idea." The reader should mark this identification: Locke's "idea" is Descartes' *cogitatum qua cogitatum* — the intentional correlate as immanently given — and so Locke does, despite himself, work within the field of pure consciousness, even as he naturalizes it.

Upon the realm of these "ideas" refers that "history" of consciousness that Locke wants to give and through which he wants to solve the epistemological problems. Doubtless significant, and yet not coming to last ripeness, motives lead him in the points of departure and his further method, and work on too in the future ("theory of cognition"). Why, Locke asked himself (he himself indicates this, though somewhat vaguely, as the primal-motive of his whole undertaking), do the disputes of the metaphysicians run so unsatisfyingly, why do they in their efforts come so little to secure and mutually convincing results? Because they operate with vague presentations of God and world, body and spirit, substance and accident, space and time, number and magnitude, force, cause, effect, without asking after their origin, their clear, original sense — without convincing themselves whether these presentations, and the metaphysical thought-formations projected with them, do not perhaps lack every possibility of being realized on the clear intuition itself; or, as Locke expresses it, whether they do not overstep the limits set to human cognition by its nature. The reader should hear in this the genuine and fruitful kernel of Locke's program — the demand to lead concepts back to their intuitive origin — which Husserl will credit as a fore-intimation of intuitionism even while condemning its naturalistic execution.

Over against this, Locke wants to push back all metaphysics (which he so little made good) and, before all, project a "history of the first beginnings of human knowledge." He wants — what for him is the same — to go back to the *ideas* as the immediate objects of inner perception and the most immediate objects of thinking, to exhibit systematically the simple ideas and describe the spiritual operations that the spirit exercises on them, then to progress to the higher knowledge-formations and so to show überhaupt how the spirit stage-wise originally shapes all knowledge of which it is capable. That is the "historical" investigation that Locke sets over against the psycho-physical explanation and regards as the epistemological task. Husserl adds, explainingly, Locke's doctrine of clarity and language. "Ideas" in their original form, as they first appear in inner experience, are easy to distinguish, easy to recognize according to their agreements and differences; here flow no sources of error. But after the original appearing they return in merely reproductive shapes, more or less faint, unclear, and then easily run undistinguished into one another. With this hangs together the danger of the otherwise so useful linguistic thinking: we humans have the capacity to use sharply distinguishable sensuous ideas — so-called words — as signs for any other ideas, and to think in linguistic form. If the word-meanings are oriented by clear intuitions and we are further able at any time to go back from the faint reproductions to the originally clear ideas — to bring the meanings to clarity — then our linguistic thinking will have sense and truth; but if we operate continually with unclear words and word-meanings, forming from them ever-new word-thoughts and opinions without convincing ourselves, by regress to the original intuition, whether to these formations a possible clear sense corresponds, then the thinking is worthless.

Here grows, according to Locke, the great task of the *clarification* of all our self-acquired or inherited concepts — the more or less confused meaning-presentations with which we operate in our life, above all the basic concepts of our whole natural and scientific world-apprehension, which play a universal and dominating role in all sciences: spirit and body, thing and property, space and time. All these concepts lack clarity and distinctness, and the errors springing from this must have especially far-reaching consequences; it is therefore the most important task, precisely with these concepts, to go back clarifyingly to the original ideas, to give them according to these ideas new delimitation and firm shape, and if possible to analyze them into their last, originally clear concept-elements. Out of this grows for Locke, Husserl says, the at-core significant thought (for all the questionable unclarity of his presentations): could we systematically exhibit all the elementary ideas (here understood as *cogitata qua cogitata*) that grow in original intuitiveness in pure consciousness; could we further systematically bring into relief the manners of the originally intuitive composing of these simple ideas into complex ideas — then the entire universe of possible human cognition would be projected in advance; we would have determined in advance, for all possible concepts, all possible word-meanings, the *idea-material*, as it were the ABC of the elementary ideas and the legitimate concepts, and also the modes of connection into really intuitive complex ideas, the ABC of the formation-manners by which all possible true thoughts would be delimited.

That in this methodical project a significant motive presses toward shaping, and that from there a *goal* could be given to a theory of cognition, is unmistakable, Husserl affirms — and he names the kernel precisely. We will convince ourselves, he says, that here nothing less lies before us than a *fore-intimation of the genuine intuitionism* that belongs to the essence of transcendental grounding of cognition: it is the fore-intimation of the methodical style of a genuine theory of cognition and of a re-grounding of all sciences dependent on it, through which they first become strict sciences in a deepest and last sense. For with the strengthening of the transcendental theory of cognition a new science-ideal evidently lights up: that of a science understanding itself and answering for itself down into the last origin-sources of all cognition-formations, and thereby also of the origin-genuine sense of all being cognized therein. The reader should mark the generosity of this reading: Locke's demand to trace every concept to its intuitive self-givenness is, rightly purified, the very intuitionism of transcendental phenomenology — the insistence that nothing count as valid that cannot be brought to original self-givenness.

But to a fruitful working-out of this fore-intimation — already far overstepped, of course, by our own clarification — it could not come. Every way of procuring to this idea of a universal cognition-explanation the very necessary clarity was *barred* through the naive *naturalization of consciousness* into which Locke at once falls. It grows, Husserl explains, from this: that the realm of purely inner experience, this so-called realm of "ideas," is thought wholly on the analogy of the *spatial world*, as the realm of outer experience. Characteristic is the famous *tabula-rasa* simile, which Locke takes up again from ancient tradition: the soul resembles, when it awakes to consciousness, an unwritten white paper on which experience inscribes signs; what appears there in the soul, or rather in the inner experience-sphere, are in sequence ever-new such signs, ever-new ideas. In this simile expresses itself the *tendency to a reification* (*Verdinglichung*) that works itself out ever more sharply in the further development of the Lockean philosophy. Signs on a writing-tablet, chalk-strokes or ink-strokes, are thingly occurrences and symbolize only thing-like. As space is the being-field of the physical things, so the consciousness-field, the empty writing-tablet, is a kind of space for inner-psychical thingnesses; and as natural science treats of, describes, and causally explains the things and processes of outer experience, so psychology has in the consciousness-field the analogous tasks for the ideas and idea-formations. The reader should grasp the exact mechanism of the error: the soul is pictured as a space, ideas as little things scattered in it, and psychology as the natural science of that inner space — and this spatial-thingly image makes the genuine intentional structure of consciousness invisible from the start.

Husserl pauses on a meaning-bearing point that he develops in an extended editorial footnote, and it is worth folding into the commentary because it clarifies exactly what is salvageable in Locke. The confusion Locke wrought in his opposition of *sensation* and *reflection* — usually translated "outer" and "inner experience" — has passed, with his other confusions, into the psychological and epistemological tradition. The decisive point the footnote makes is that *sensation* functions doubly. First, it functions as *cogitatio* in the Cartesian sense, with its sensational *cogitatum* that, as phenomenon, as *idea* on the consciousness-tablet, remains unaffected by whether the experienced things exist or not, and whether the whole world remains, as Descartes wills, in "doubt" as possible transcendental semblance. The existence of this sensational *cogito* together with its *cogitatum* is the indubitable evidence of the *ego cogito* not contested by Locke, and precisely therefore it belongs to the *ideas* of the consciousness-tablet: form the correct concept of pure consciousness-experience, or if one will, "inner" experience, and it embraces all "ideas," including the ideas of sensation in this sense. On the other hand, this sensation is by no means *outer experience*, whose objects are not ideas but the spatial things experienced *through* the sensuous ideas. Whatever the relation of the pure thing-phenomenon as idea to the thing experienced in the natural attitude of outer experience, it is certain that here two things, and an attitude-change in the passage from the one to the other, lie before us: in the one attitude we carry out the perceptual belief and "have" this existing thing; in the other this belief is *inhibited*, and we have instead of the thing the "thing-phenomenon." Therefore we may not call both *outer experience*. The correct concept of outer experience is that of the (belief-carried) thing-experience, while the other concept yields a particular shape of "inner" experience — the experience of thing-phenomena, and reflectively of perceiving meaning and believing of things. Once this confusion is clarified, the Lockean procedure is to be designated, in the right sense, as the *reduction of all cognition-problems to the ground of inner experience, of pure idea-experience or consciousness-experience*. The reader should mark how, through this footnote, Husserl extracts from Locke's muddle the genuine reductive insight: the field of pure phenomena, indifferent to the existence or non-existence of the world, is the proper soil of the theory of cognition — Locke half-possessed it under the name "idea" and lost it again by reifying it.

The radical *mingling*, Husserl notes in closing the footnote's thought, which carries itself over onto the thing-properties as against property-phenomena with their adumbrating sensation-data, runs even today through the literature of psychology and theory of cognition — and despite his own long-given demonstrations. And the lecture's main line closes on the genealogy of the naturalization: the new, much-admired natural science had become the prototype of genuine science überhaupt, and this worked itself out also in that one took, as if self-evidently, the type of spatial-thingly reality as the prototype of every, including the psychical, reality. That had been already the source of the Cartesian *dualism*, of the Hobbesian *materialism*, of the Spinozistic *parallelism*, and had led in Locke himself to the interpretation of the consciousness-life as an *accidental being* on the bearing or even effecting ground of a soul-substance — in analogy with the material substance to be laid under the complexes of sensuous ideas or properties. But here lies also the source of the naturalization of the givennesses of inner experience in the Lockean *tabula-rasa* conception of the realm of "ideas." With this the lecture has fixed the deepest historical ground of Locke's failure: the soul construed on the model of nature, and inner experience construed on the model of a space full of thing-like data. The Second Chapter, opening with the Fifteenth Lecture, turns from the diagnosis of Locke's limitation to the *critical disclosure* of the genuine and abiding problematic that nonetheless lies hidden in his researches — beginning with the problem of immanence and the synthetic unity in consciousness.

## **Second Chapter: Critical Disclosure of the Genuine and Abiding Problematic Hidden in Locke's Researches**

### **Fifteenth Lecture: The problem of immanence and of the synthetic unity in consciousness**

Husserl opens the Second Chapter by turning from critique to *disclosure*: the genuine problems that lie hidden, distorted, in Locke's naturalized descriptions. He begins from a conditional that exposes the whole stake of the naturalization. *Were* the psychical reality really of the same ontological type as nature, then psychology, as a science strictly and exactly carried out, would have to look quite like a natural science — a science of purely inductive connections, with any fundamentally other kind of connection, merely interweaving with the inductive, and accordingly an essentially other-kinded method of psychological research and theory, principially excluded. This type of a purely naturalistic, purely inductive psychology, recommended by the exemplariness of natural science, received a particular stamp once Locke *seemed* to have brought into relief the *tabula rasa* of inner experience as the necessarily first cognition-field of psychology and theory of cognition, the self-evident ground-field on which all descriptions and inductive theorizings were to build. Locke himself, who was altogether not the man of ruthless consequence, did not carry through such a psychology and theory of cognition; that *Hume* first did — and we will hear what that philosophically meant, that it was nothing less than the end of all philosophy and science itself: in a word, a fundamentally countersensical skepticism, remarkable only through its wholly new historical style. In Locke's work lies, so to speak, only the *beginning of this end*, at first looking quite innocent, but driving on, through its inconsistencies sensible to his deeper thinkers, to new formations. The reader should hold this trajectory in view: Locke is read not for himself but as the seed whose consistent development is Humean skepticism, and whose hidden truth is transcendental phenomenology.

Inner experience comprises for Locke the totality of the immediate givennesses that the I, the "spirit," in each case has; self-evidently these givennesses are real occurrences in the frame of subjectivity, quite as the givennesses of outer experience are real events in outer nature. But this *parallelization* has its treacheries, which already become felt in the Lockean descriptions, and Husserl draws them out along several gaze-directions. On the one side stands the *I-side* of the *tabula rasa*: if this is the field of inner experience, it is yet the field *of the experiencing I*, and the inner experiencing is the having-conscious of the I *of* the occurrences of this field. Further: the I does not only *have* conscious as inwardly experienced; it is also *affected* by this, by the signs on the consciousness-tablet, and reacting it also exercises activities — it explicates properties, makes itself clear, brings dimly remembered to light, collects, compares, relates. Locke, in his honesty, never quite surrenders the seen; he therefore himself makes valid the activities of the "spirit," and also this: that these activities not only take place but become immediately conscious to the "spirit," hence are themselves again inscribed as ideas on the consciousness-tablet. The reader should credit Locke, as Husserl does, with a descriptive honesty that keeps registering the I and its activities even where his naturalistic framework cannot accommodate them — and this tension is precisely the hidden problematic the chapter means to disclose.

But how does it stand, in all this, with the *I* of which here implicitly there is constant talk, and in the description of inner experience must always be talk? Locke speaks of it — or, as he says, of the spirit — almost as though before the consciousness-tablet a human being stood, busying himself with the signs (which is a felt absurdity), and otherwise interprets it as unknowable substance; now he calls it an idea and now denies that it is an idea. The latter is his proper opinion; and if for him the field of (proper) ideas is the domain of possible knowledge, then the I reduces to the *complex of consciousness-experiences*, just as otherwise the thing to the complex of "properties" or, in the familiar confusion, to the complex of sensuous ideas. But that is uncomfortable, Husserl says; for the I as subject of the activities and as perceiving with respect to all ideas is not to be denied away, just as little as the identical thing of the changing sensuous complexes. And in last respect appears, moreover, the felt difficulty that idea-complexes überhaupt, the sensuous ones too, lie *within* subjectivity. The reader should mark that the bundle-theory of the I founders on the same rock as the bundle-theory of the thing: in both cases an identical pole is presupposed by the very talk of "complexes," and cannot be dissolved into them.

Descartes, Husserl recalls, had laid value not only on the evidence of the *cogitatio* but also on that of the *ego* of the *cogitatio* — and on the latter evidence even the main emphasis. But *as what* is this ego, and *how* is it given? Is it a metaphysical *substantia cogitans*? Is it, spoken in Locke's sense, a *je ne sais quoi*, a something that we must necessarily think-to, postulate-to, the lived-experiences of the inner consciousness — as, to the data of outer thing-experience, on the parallel of the Lockean doctrine, the unknowable material substrate? Husserl answers with the decisive phenomenological appeal to evidence. Is it not immediately evident that I, reflecting on my act-experiences of noticing perceiving, of judging, valuing, willing, never and nowhere find them as *I-less* facts, but necessarily in the general form *ego cogito*? Inseparably and quite immediately I find, on or in them, the everywhere-identical consciousness-I. Each such act-experience is, when I intuit it as act of the I, itself theme of a reflective act, and now reflecting again, I recognize it as act-experience of the I, identically the same that is enacting in it.

And now in fullest generality, Husserl extends this: *every* arbitrary consciousness-experience, even one that is not act of the I, is in just this way *my* experience. He gives the careful example: I recognize the hearing of a melody — upon which I was, during it, not directed in a noticing act, and of which I afterward become aware, as un-noticing hearing (or of the melody as un-noticed melody), in a backward-looking intuiting — in similar manner, in evident reflection, as *my* experience; and in the synthetic enactment of such reflections as the experience of the *same I* of all experiences that I ever call, and ever am to be able to call, mine — which I can so call only on the ground of such reflections and syntheses, under steady evident identification of the one and same I, of him that I am. Why, Husserl asks, is all this not pronounced as a *basic fact* within the descriptions of inner experience — why is it not said: in the realm of inner consciousness I find manifold changing experiences, but each as that of *my I*, and this I is in absolute identity? This is the first piece of the hidden problematic disclosed: the pure I as the identical subject-pole of all experiences, given with the strongest of all evidences, yet passed over by a description that has eyes only for thing-like data.

To be sure, Husserl grants, this I in its absolute identity makes difficulties. One might want to identify it with the well-known *person*, the I that I know from the experience of my life; but of an absolute evidence with respect to my character-properties, which determine the personal reality, there is no talk. Nonetheless — is it not quite correct of Descartes to claim, for the *pure ego* as that of the pure *cogito*, the strongest of all evidences? It is therefore by no means an empty and even metaphysical substruction, although within the absolute evidence I can assert almost nothing of it, except the one thing: that it belongs to all conceivable experiences I am to be able to call mine, as an *unlosable and numerically identical subject-pole*. But indeed *not as a part*: every part of an experience vanishes with it itself, and no new experience can have a part *really* (*reell*) identical with the previous one. The reader should mark the precise ontological status Husserl secures for the pure I: identical across the whole stream, evident, yet not a real component of any experience — neither a substance behind the stream nor a piece within it, but the persisting pole to which the whole stream is referred.

If one is from the outset naturalistically preoccupied — directed only on analoga of outer being, and if not on inner *things* (since of abiding things there can here be from the outset no talk), then at least on analoga of real *events* — then with the "pure I" not much is to be done. One cannot naturalize the experiences and insert into them such a natural nonsense as a numerically identical I, an absolute identical that belongs evidently to everything and yet is no real, no real part, no real annex itself. We understand here, Husserl says, the ground why all psychology standing under the naturalistic prejudice — and that concerns almost the entire psychology of the modern age — becomes *blind to the pure I*; why it must become blind, if the soul is to be thought purely naturally as a reality parallel to the physical, and the sphere of inner consciousness as a field of real experiences. Locke himself is not yet outright *I-blind*, but knows not what to do with the I; had he struck the naturalistic thought-direction and yet held fast to the I, that gave a tension of incompatible motives that had to be cancelled — and if naturalism remained determining, it had in the further development of the naturalistic doctrines to come to the *elimination* of the I (the soul-substance substructed to it). The reader should see the prophecy here: Hume's dissolution of the self is the inevitable terminus of the naturalistic premise, and Locke's wavering retention of the I is a half-measure that consistency must abolish.

Husserl now passes to a second, broader piece of the hidden problematic: the naturalization of consciousness makes blind not only to the I but to *everything* that is essence-proper to consciousness as consciousness. As consciousness is not thinkable without I, it is also not thinkable without *something*, without some "objectivity" that is conscious in it. Hence no description, let alone higher theorizing, of consciousness is possible that does not co-describe and co-theorize the I and the in-it-conscious *as conscious of this consciousness*. Consciousness "relates itself" — so runs a natural and usual manner of expression — to some objectivity; and therein the word "consciousness" designates lived-experiences such as some perception of something, a memory of something, the experiencing of a sign as sign of something, of a being-pleased as being-pleased at something. Where it concerns I-acts — I notice and take cognizance of something present, or I take cognizance, rememberingly and grasping the remembered, of something past — it is said in the particular sense: I relate myself to the objective in question, or, I am directed upon it; in the third person: the respective I relates or directs itself, while on the other hand the act-experience itself is still in its way called related to the corresponding something.

This relation, designated after *Brentano* as *intentional* (and whereby the experiences were also by Husserl called "intentional experiences"), has an essentially other sense than the other relations that we ascribe, be it to objects among one another, be it to the I or the respective consciousness toward some objects. The *object of the intentional relation* — as that which is enclosed purely in the act, in the intentional experiencing itself — is the *mere intentional object*, the *immanent* one, as Brentano too, in connection with Scholasticism, introduces it. It is the in-the-act meant purely *as* meant, without question and decision as to whether it in truth, whether it "really" be or not. The reader should mark the exact contrast Husserl now draws, for it is the methodological heart of the lecture. Where we simply, in the normal and modified sense, make a *relation-assertion*, it raises, according to its own sense, the claim to set existing into relation to existing, and the relation itself is posited, asserted as relation between really existing objects (whether real or ideal). For the relation lying in the act itself to its object it stands otherwise: the object to which it relates is and remains *its* object, however it may stand with that object's true being.

Husserl makes this concrete with the tree by the brook. I relate myself perceivingly to some object of my surrounding-world, say to this tree there by the brook, and say thereafter: I see this tree; then there lies, in the normal sense of such talk, that the tree in truth *be* there, while on the other hand it is at once meant as perceived in this perceiving — here we have a normal relation-assertion in which at once an intentional relation is co-enclosed and co-asserted. But if we put the existence of the tree in question, or arbitrarily abstain from every position-taking regarding its existence, that changes nothing in the fact that the perception-experience is in itself perception of "this tree," and it remains what it is, perception of the same, in itself related to its immanent object, even if it subsequently turned out that this perception is to be valued as illusion. For distinctness' sake, therefore, Husserl says, we will do well to distinguish: the *immanent object* of the respective consciousness (the immanent intentional one), as the in the immanence of this consciousness conscious *as such*; and the *object simply*, as that asserted in the normal assertion as substrate-object (as the about-which), asserted thus with the sense that it in truth *be*. Are we in the being-belief, does it count for us as really existing — as when we have simply experiencingly "the" tree, this one there, given — then we assert, in normal attitude and talk, simply: "this tree…," and every such assertion then self-evidently means the tree as *real*.

It therefore needs a *change of attitude*, the enactment of a *sense-modification*, to make recognizable the in the pure immanence of the experience itself "meant object as such" — "irrespective of existence or non-existence," hence under abstention from every position-taking toward it (Husserl's note here refers to Beilage XIII, Hua VII pp. 349 f.). Through these very necessary clarifications, Husserl says, one first understands the right sense of what, as consciousness-objectivity (as immanently intentional), is inseparable from every consciousness, and so also the sense of a *purely immanent description*. The reader should recognize in this "change of attitude" the working core of the phenomenological reduction operating already within the historical analysis: to describe the meant purely as meant, one must inhibit the natural being-belief — and this is exactly what Locke, falling back into the natural attitude, failed to do.

That sense of pure immanent description is overstepped and cancelled, Husserl warns, when the respectively meant is *not* described exactly as it, in the consciousness in question, in and for itself is meant — as when we, falling back into the natural attitude (in which we let our whole knowledge become determining for the asserting), draw into the description of the intentional object such marks as stem precisely from other convictions, from our other, however well-justified knowledge. This is the standing methodological danger: the contamination of the phenomenal description by the describer's world-knowledge, which substitutes for the meant-as-meant a fuller object the consciousness in question did not contain.

Husserl then secures a further, crucial point about the *kind* of immanence in play. If every consciousness-experience "bears" its immanent object in itself, it is now also to be noticed that this bearing-in-itself *cannot* have the sense of a *real (reell) immanence* — as though the immanent intentional object dwelt in its consciousness as a real piece, a real moment, a part. To regard it so would be evident countersense. The past of which we are mindful is, in the remembering itself, *remembered* past; the future we expect, in the expected itself, *foreseen* future; but as little as the actual past or future is the "meant as such," the "immanent intentional" one, a *real component* in the present experiencing. Real piece of the experience, as datum of the immanent time-stream, is every itself-immanent temporal component of the experience-process; but the manifold memories or expectations in which we re-remember the same or pre-expect the same are, in the immanent temporality, *separated* experiences and can have no piece in common. Husserl confirms this with arbitrary examples: the consciousness-act we call memory is in itself consciousness of the remembered past; likewise the consciousness we call outer perceiving is in itself consciousness of the perceived outer. This essentially inseparable immanence is therefore *no real immanence, no real being-contained*; to regard it so would be evident countersense. The reader should hold this distinction firmly, for it governs all phenomenological description: the intentional object is *non-really* contained in the experience — inseparable from it yet not a piece of it — and to confuse this intentional inexistence with real containment is the cardinal error.

This does not exclude, Husserl adds, that consciousness can also be questioned and described according to its *real pieces*, its parts: evidently a predicative judgment has its set-off steps and pieces, as inner temporal processes — its subject-positing, its predicate-positing referred to it. Likewise separated consciousness-experiences can also bind themselves *really* into a whole. But on the other hand it must precisely be seen that *binding of consciousness with consciousness* also means something *for the intentional objectivity*, and as binding of consciousness accomplishes an accomplishment that must be without analogy in the natural. This accomplishment consists in this: that it, as *synthesis*, produces a *unitary intentional objectivity* that is, for the bound consciousness as *one* consciousness, its own. Husserl emphasizes the latter: consciousness and consciousness bind themselves not only überhaupt, but — an in-itself remarkable peculiarity — into *one* consciousness, which as such has *its* immanent objectivity; and this objectivity of the synthesis then has necessary *foundation* in those of the joined consciousness-experiences. To treat synthesis like a kind of real conjunction — say, in modern manner, to want to satisfy it with real conjunction-forms ("Gestalt-qualities") — means to be blind to the peculiarity of consciousness and to fall into countersense. The reader should mark this as a decisive correction of associationist and Gestalt-theoretic accounts alike: synthesis is not a real glue between data but the intentional accomplishment of a *unity of sense*, and has no analogue in the natural realm of really conjoined elements.

In connection herewith, Husserl draws the two culminating consequences that the chapter has been working toward, and they concern the two poles already named. First, on the object-side: that the consciousness-wise *identity* of an object — precisely that which grounds the talk of *one* object — points back to a *synthesis* in which manifold consciousness, e.g. different and manifold perceptions, synthetically join into *one* consciousness of the one and selfsame object, whereby this "one and the same" is itself consciousness-wise co-there, itself intentional. Second, on the subject-side, in exact parallel: that, parallel with that constantly ruling kind of synthesis which makes conscious unity and selfsameness of this or that, and so überhaupt *objects* as objects for the I, conversely the *I* itself is the index of a *universal synthesis*, through which all the infinitely manifold consciousness that is mine has a universal unity — not the objective but the *I-ly* (*ichliche*) one; or: it must be seen that through this kind of synthesis the *"standing and abiding I"* of this consciousness-life is continually *constituted* and made conscious. With this double result — the object as identity-pole of an objective synthesis, the I as pole of a universal I-synthesis — the lecture closes, having disclosed beneath Locke's reified descriptions the genuine problems of intentionality, irreal immanence, and constitutive synthesis. The Sixteenth Lecture pursues the *irreality* of the immanent contents and their I–object polarization, and the problem of intersubjectivity, with remarks on Berkeley's critique of Locke.

### **Sixteenth Lecture: The irreality of the immanent contents of the consciousness-synthesis in their I–object polarization, and the problem of intersubjectivity. Remarks on Berkeley's critique of Locke**

Husserl resumes from the double polarization — under the titles *I* and *object* — that all consciousness-life as such has in absolute necessity, and underscores at once that its analogue is unthinkable in a nature-reality. A real has real components, real parts and moments, real conjunction-forms; but consciousness-synthesis has, in the form of these poles, immanent contents that are *irreal* (*irreal*). Once one has begun here to *see* — begun to recognize that these irrealities must be co-described as inseparable enclosures of consciousness, and indeed in all the changing modes in which they belong to the respective consciousness — there open *true infinities of descriptive work*. The reader should mark the positive announcement: the disclosure of irreal immanence is not a negative result but the opening of an unlimited field of phenomenological description, the very field Locke's reification closed off.

Above all one then becomes attentive to the *manifolds of possible reflection-directions*, with which it first clearly steps forth that having-conscious of something — perceiving of a perceived, expecting of an expected, judging of a judged — is *not* something empty or descriptively impoverished over against the in-it-conscious, having at most only qualitative differences, as though perceiving and remembering distinguished themselves only through an unsayable "quality of consciousness-ness." Rather they are highly complicated consciousness-manners, modifying themselves in very different dimensions and accomplishing ever-new intentional accomplishments, that stand already under each single one of these coarsely designating names — perceiving, and more specially thing-perceiving, or remembering, expecting, judging, conceiving, but also valuing, wishing, willing. *Never* is consciousness, even the seemingly simplest perceiving or without-any-attending having-conscious, an *empty having* of something, as though the subject therein had its intentional objects merely lying in it as in a pocket. The pocket-image names exactly the error: consciousness does not contain its objects as a receptacle contains things; it accomplishes them in structured intentional manners, and to describe consciousness is to describe these manners.

But it does not even occur to Locke and his successors, Husserl charges, once seriously to take this *having* into view and to describe it really as what it essentially is. That the natural researcher, when he carries out experiences, looks quite exclusively at the things and processes experienced therein and takes them as he experiencingly has them, and then goes only upon theorizing this had describingly and explainingly, is intelligible — it is an essential piece of natural-scientific method to be directed exclusively on objectivity, and to this belongs leaving the subjective out of question, even intentionally out of play. But for the psychologist and theorist of cognition all the subjective belongs to the theme; and so the subjective *having* of objective is not something for him to disregard. It is itself something, but from it the *had as such* is inseparable, to be described according to its own essence. The reader should mark the methodological asymmetry Husserl insists on: the natural scientist rightly brackets the subjective, but the epistemologist who imitates that bracketing forfeits his own theme, since for him the subjective having is precisely the matter to be described.

Consider any intentional experience, say a simple outer experiencing: reflective consideration shows that there is manifold to see — how much already in the mere seeing of a thing as such\! The infinitely changing subjective *aspects* (*Anblicke*) of the object, which are yet nothing in nature, in space itself, but precisely subjective aspects *of* the thing. The perceptually meant thing cannot at all be perceptually conscious, Husserl recalls, without somehow *looking*; and therewith a theme of rich descriptions is already indicated. Such themes of subjective descriptions there are very manifold not only in thing-perception; the same shows itself for every consciousness. Even that *universal* having-conscious through which all single experiencing is for its part conscious — the so-called "inner" consciousness — is a true wonder-construction of finest intentional structures, though deeply hidden. The reader should note that Husserl extends the descriptive infinity from object-perception to inner consciousness itself: the very awareness in which experiences are given is itself a structured intentional accomplishment, not a transparent medium.

To let no confusions arise, Husserl pauses to distinguish three senses of the ambiguous word *consciousness*, and the distinction is worth holding for the analyses that follow. (1) The *universal consciousness of the I* — that in which the I has conscious each and everything that is for it in any sense present and graspable, in which it comprises all this into the universal unity of a field of regard: outer and inner, non-I-ly and I-ly, intentional single experiences of most different stages and their real and ideal contents. (2) Consciousness in the proper *Cartesian* sense — that designated through the *ego cogito* of the Cartesian evidence, where the transcendent being, say physical nature, is not posited as actuality and taken as existing, but in artificial manner left out of validity. (3) The *intentional experiences* that appear there singly, as perceptions, wishes, volitions, in the Cartesian field. The reader should keep these apart: the universal field of regard, the reductively purified field, and the single acts within it — three things the tradition runs together under one word.

The soul-blindness for the peculiar essence of consciousness in all these universal and single shapes shows itself, for anyone who has once come to know genuine descriptions, in this: that the Lockean descriptions, like those of his successors, could not even bring it to a correct *real* analysis and description — that according to real parts and conjunctions — because they always again *misinterpret the intentional enclosures*, which are naturally inseparably co-there and in a certain way necessarily seen, *as real enclosures*. Through such misinterpretation there then spring fundamentally perverse problems that hopelessly trouble the centuries. Husserl is careful to distinguish this error from the other one already exposed: basic errors of this kind lie in a quite *other line* than those of dogmatic presuppositions (according to which, e.g., an objective world and psychophysical causalities are presupposed in epistemological investigations that should first make their possibility intelligible) — though the one and the other kind of misinterpretation mutually demand each other and bind themselves intimately in the theories. The reader should register the two distinct sins: (a) helping oneself to objective premises (the circle), and (b) misreading intentional inexistence as real containment; they reinforce one another but are not the same fault.

To clarify this by an example, Husserl points to the all-known and all-dominant Lockean doctrine of *primary and secondary qualities*. Outer experience, considered as an immanent experience — hence as datum of inner experience — is experience of things, plants, heavenly bodies. But these things are, one tells oneself, not themselves in the outer experience, in the subjective experience. So, self-evidently, what we inwardly have is an *inner perception-image* to which the outer thing only more or less perfectly corresponds. The age-old childish *image-theory* is thus taken, out of this supposed self-evidence, into the doctrine of inner experience. According to the Lockean interpretation of the new natural science (and that of the natural scientists themselves), the inner perception-image is a *mixture of proper image and of causal sign*: the former with respect to the so-called primary or original qualities of the objects sensuously appearing in outer perception — the seen extension is really as inner image and is really image, insofar as an analogue of extension is on the outer things themselves (according to natural-scientific doctrine they are in themselves extended); likewise with size, shape, position, motion, number. By contrast the specific sense-qualities, the so-called "secondary," "derived" ones, are without analogy with any qualities of the nature-things themselves — these have no visual, acoustic properties; in nature itself are certain motion-processes, things equipped exclusively with primary, mathematical-mechanical qualities, and these are, through such properties and belonging causalities, the explaining causes for the sensation-tones, sensation-colors exhibitable in our perception-image.

It is remarkable, Husserl says, that the *completed countersense* of this doctrine has so little harmed its almost general validity. *Berkeley* first recognized it, but could only imperfectly lay it clear; a *partial* countersense of the doctrine he irrefutably brought into relief — namely through the indication of the *unthinkability of an extension without any specific sensuous qualification*, hence of primary qualities without secondary. But the ultimately clarifying he, the pupil of the Lockean immanent naturalism, could not say. He says, to be sure, further good things: with his ingenious gaze he also intuits the countersense of the Lockean doctrine of outer existence and that of every causal inference leading into the transcendent-physical. The reader should weigh Husserl's balanced verdict on Berkeley, which the lecture now develops: a genuine destroyer of Lockean confusions, yet himself caught in the same blindness to intentionality, and so unable to put anything constructive in their place.

The inner perception-image of the outer thing is, according to Locke, an associative complex of sense-data of the various senses, causally stemming from the outer nature-things; the spirit cannot but underlay such an associative complex a *je ne sais quoi* as "bearer," whereby a causal inference from effect to a transcendent cause plays its role. Excellently, Husserl says, Berkeley objects that such an inference is *unexhibitable and unthinkable*. For on the ground of the, according to Locke, sole immediate givennesses — those of the *tabula rasa*, to which all sensuous data belong — it is well intelligible how from given psychical data one infers associatively-inductively to new data, from given sensuous complexes to new complexes; or also how one infers from a sensuously experienced bodiliness to a non-experienced foreign soul-life, on the analogy of the experienced unity of own bodiliness and own psychical. But *senseless* is an inference to a *je ne sais quoi*, a principially unexperienceable, for which in the own immanent sphere every analogue fails. However much Berkeley was, with the main stroke of such thoughts, on the good way, a *real clarification* and a *theory of the intentional constitution of externality in inwardness* he could not give, since he himself was for *intentionality* just as blind as Locke and therefore could not disclose an intentional problem-situation either.
