The reader should mark the precise location of Berkeley's limit: he can demolish the inference to the transcendent thing-in-itself, but having no concept of constitution he cannot explain how externality is nonetheless validly constituted within consciousness — he can only deny the outer, not account for it.

Husserl now develops the *multiplication of worlds* that the image-theory entails, as a reductio that Locke should have felt. First one must wonder that Locke and the natural scientists interpreting like him took so little offense at the *doubling, indeed thousand-folding, of the world*. On the one side we would have so-called nature itself, the alleged original-image; on the other side we would have in each subject a proper system of perception-images, which, however — with some difference from nature — would itself likewise be a nature, a real world for itself. And moreover we would have the curiosity that the subjects, as human subjects, are at once to be, through their bodies, members of the objective world, so that the subjective worlds would be at once interwoven into the so-called objective world. One may object: these are not worlds but only images of worlds, and they are properly only associative sensation-complexes in the single subjects, and associative complexes are no things. Quite well, Husserl answers, but *through what* are the things to distinguish themselves from the associative complexes? If one accepts Locke's doctrine, one would have to say: must we think, to the alone-given inner complexes of sensation-data as causes, outer complexes, analoga of those inner ones — and is it true that we cannot think such a true outer complex without a bearing substance — why must we then not think, for the inner complex, which is principially of the same kind, also an inner substance? So the inner total-images would in fact and unrejectably be nothing other than *inner things*, in which outer ones image themselves. The reader should follow the dialectic: Locke's own principles, consistently applied, turn the inner images into a second nature of inner things, and so multiply rather than explain the world.

And the same, Husserl continues, will have to hold for every other manner in which we may think the transcendent nature — hence also if we surrender Locke's *je ne sais quoi*. It will have to hold so long as we remain by the assumption of an even merely imperfect *image-likeness* of inner-being and outer-being. Has not Berkeley principially right to say: analoga of sensations can only be sensations, analoga of associative sensation-complexes are necessarily themselves again associative sensation-complexes, and sensations are unthinkable without a sensing subjectivity? So we would have only further increased the *number of subjects*, as correlate belonging to the so-called objective nature, without having in the least made intelligible the sense of the precedence of the allegedly original-image objectivity of just this one complex "objective nature." It is also to be noticed, Husserl adds, that each subject *purely in itself* experiences nature, that it therefore never goes beyond its so-called images; in that it joins experiences with experiences, experiences the agreeing of its images — convinces itself of the legitimate existence of the things, of nature überhaupt — it can evidently never find an occasion to undertake those inferences into the transcendent, sprung from misunderstanding and invented. So as single I in own direct experience. The reader should mark the result: from within a single subject's concordant experience, the inference to an extra-mental original is not merely unjustified but unmotivated — nothing in the experience itself calls for it.

The *only* way to transcend the own subjectivity and self-experienced nature, Husserl states — and here intersubjectivity enters the analysis for the first time — is *empathy* (*Einfühlung*) into another subjectivity, the inwardly motivated positing of analogous spirituality and analogous sensuous "images" like the self-experienced. But, Husserl presses, why do we then call these *the same things* that *we* see, why do we speak of *one* nature that we all see? So Locke would have had to ponder and ask. With this question — how the one common nature, the same for all, is constituted across the plurality of subjects, each shut up in its own experiences — Husserl marks the genuine *problem of intersubjectivity* as the problem Locke's image-theory both raises and conceals. The reader should register that this is left here as a posed problem, not yet solved: the analysis has shown that the unity of the one nature for all cannot be the alleged transcendent original, but must be a constitutive accomplishment reaching through empathy across subjects — the theme of a phenomenology of intersubjective constitution.

Having come so far, Husserl closes, we are prepared enough to look at the *intentional* state of affairs itself and to understand the radical blindness of the *tabula-rasa*\-psychologists. The lecture thus ends by gathering its critique into a transition: the image-theory, the multiplication of worlds, and the unanswered question of the one common nature all point to the same missing concept — intentional constitution — and the Seventeenth Lecture turns directly to the question of the *constitution of "externality,"* taking up the Cartesian evidence of the self-givenness of things in perception.

### **Seventeenth Lecture: On the question of the constitution of "externality": the Cartesian evidence of the self-givenness of things in perception**

Having won an eye for *intentionality*, for consciousness as consciousness of something, we will, Husserl says, for our part object: these entire inner *images and signs* on the *tabula rasa* — images and signs for a transcendent nature — are *inventions of a misled reflection*. They are plausible only at the very first stages of epistemological primitivity, which either do not yet know the pure subjectivity at all, or know not what to do with it as consciousness-subjectivity. That the image-theory appears already in the oldest Greek philosophy points only to this: that the first beginner-like leaving of the attitude of the natural world-life — with its natural devotedness to the experienced externalities — and the first passing-over into a philosophical reflection that sets the inner and outer in relation, presses at once toward such constructions. The reader should mark that Husserl treats the image-theory not as a sophisticated error but as the naive first reflex of philosophical reflection, a construction that more developed analysis dissolves.

If we now exploit the Cartesian method of bringing the pure *cogitatio* into relief — which yields the respective subjective experience as an absolutely evident one and permits analyzing it, in the frame of absolute evidence, according to real pieces and intentional stocks — and apply this method to experiences of the type *outer perception*: is it not then *absolutely evident* that when I see, e.g., a table, a house, a tree, I do not see something like subjective sensation-complexes, or something like inner images of… or signs for a table or a house, but precisely *see the table itself, the house itself*? Husserl grants the standing objection at once: certainly, the perceived thing could, although I perceive it, be an illusionary one; I am perhaps subject to a deception. With good ground I distinguish between merely meant perception-thing and actual thing. But where, he asks, does this good ground lie other than *within my experiencing life itself* — the same inner life that I can at any time, in this method, question and study according to its absolutely evident own stock? The reader should follow the move: the very distinction between illusion and reality, far from requiring an extra-mental original, is itself drawn within experience, from the way experience confirms or breaks down.

In the own passage from perception to perception, insofar as it carries itself out steadily as unity of concordant synthesis, I say that the once-as-bodily-self-there posited — say this table — gives itself continually as the one and same, and that the positing intention continually confirms itself. In other cases it may be that this experiencingly-identifying positing suddenly suffers an unexpected *break* through discordance, and I possibly see that the hitherto-as-existing perceived now receives the character of *nullity* — that the hitherto-untouched existence-character is, so to speak, *struck through* with the nullity-stroke. But so long as the latter has not occurred, so long as experience preserves its concordance in its synthetic unity, so long the perceived is, precisely as normally perceived, simply "there." It is already to be foreseen, Husserl says, that I could understand by its *true being* nothing other than the *idea*, grown out of the constancy of confirmation, of a confirmation in the course of future experience never and nowhere to be broken through — so that the experience I have once acquired of a thing could be surrendered by no later experience, but only supplemented and at once confirmed by it. The reader should hold this definition of *true being* as the regulative idea of endlessly continuing concordant confirmation; it is the constitutive sense of "actuality" that replaces the image-theory's transcendent original. Evident in any case, Husserl adds, is that if the thing is actual, the actual thing itself is no other than the perceived, and that it is fundamentally perverse to say the perceived as such is merely an image or sign for an in-itself-existing true thing that, as what it itself is, does not fall into my perception.

Husserl now blocks the image-theory at its root by analyzing what "image," "analogue," and "sign" actually require. When can I say I have in my intuition an *analogue* or a mere *image* of a thing and not the thing itself? An analogue is an analogue *of another*, something that resembles it; instead of one thing I have another, more or less resembling it. But trees and houses that I now see are not, because they resemble other houses and trees, *analoga* for them. An analogue is something in which another likens itself — a given that is, for another similar one, representative, similarity-symbol — and has therein not an *objective property* but a peculiar *way of functioning in subjective apprehension*; it presupposes a particular *consciousness of analogizing*, in which alone the analogue has its real sense-site. And all the more with the proper *image*: an image is image only for him who wins from it, in a peculiar consciousness — a depicting one — the meaning as image; consciousness-wise there must present itself to me, in an intuitively given (or its concretely offering single features), an *other*, itself not given, as in the painterly or drawn appearing landscape, as intuitively hovering, the not-itself-seen but here only depictively presenting one. Perceptually present here is the thing that hangs on the wall, the framed canvas, or the copperplate-sheet lying on the table; the painterly image is a *fictum* becoming conscious in one with this perception, and is itself there for me only by virtue of a peculiar consciousness through which, in such founding by perception, the *fictum* co-appears. The reader should grasp the structural lesson: image-consciousness is a founded, complex intentionality (a perceived physical thing bearing an appearing *fictum* that depicts an absent original) — utterly unlike simple perception, in which no such depicting consciousness is at work.

Evidently, Husserl concludes, fundamentally different consciousness-manners are here in play over against the simple perception, of which, when we simply see a thing, there can be no talk. And likewise it stands with the *having of a sign* for another over against the having of the thing itself: to the sign as such belongs the specific consciousness of being-sign for another, a consciousness of quite peculiar intentional structure. Should one now say: the ordinary perceiving is indeed not from the outset an analogizing representing or a representing through image or indication — it is perceiving and nothing further; but what is there perceived is precisely not the outer nature-thing itself, and we must, to gain the correct state of affairs, set these new consciousness-manners into play? Against this Husserl insists: here it is above all a matter of *faithfully describing* what the immediately perceived is, purely according to the own sense of the perception. It is to be established that it is not a complex of sensuous data belonging to the respective perception as real pieces — arising with it and vanishing with it — but that it is nothing other than, say, *this table here*, only coming to perception once from this and then from that side, and in the progress of synthetically self-unifying perceptions becoming ever richer, ever more many-shaped visible. But always *it itself*, this table — the synthetic unity, the consciousness-wise one and same object — is it that in the progress fore- and ex-hibits its being-content and confirms its actual existence; only presupposed that no entering discordance compels us to strike through its existence and say: it was a mere illusion.

What every conceivable confirmation, actuality-exhibiting exhibits, is therefore, Husserl states, the in the perception in the consciousness-character of self-existence conscious *synthetic unity* and nothing other than *the outer itself*, the spatial thing itself; it is from the outset the *transcendent itself*. Whence else should ever a knowledge of it come? How could a knowledge of it ever exhibit itself otherwise than through perception and through continuous confirmation of the perceptions themselves in the concordant perception-connection? What can analogizing, imaging, indication accomplish? *Nothing without perception.* Have I already experienced things themselves and in experience directly given and exhibited their existence — have I so in conceivably most original manner won knowledge of the outer world — then I can, through given things, analogize others, present them depictively, present them through indication, as a flag-signal the arrival of a ship. But what sense could it have to want to win, first of all through such analogy and symbols, what was never itself and in its being-kind experienced? The consciousness giving them sense points itself, according to origin and kind of its fulfilling confirmation, back to *possible perception*, and to a perception of *transcendent*, if it is to be able to indicate transcendent. The reader should mark the decisive argument: image- and sign-consciousness *presuppose* an original perception of the thing they represent; therefore they cannot be what first gives us the outer world — perception of the transcendent itself is primary, and the image-theory inverts the order of foundation.

It is therefore a *nonsense*, Husserl says, to substitute, for what one evidently has conscious in perception as perceived, an analogue or a sign for something other — and all the more an unknowable, unperceivable. That one can come upon this notion and find in it an intelligible theory comes evidently from this: that one, naively naturalizing, wants to see in the realm of inner experience only something like *data of a consciousness-tablet*, and now unnoticed, and quite naively, sets *behind* the tablet a whole human subject who naturally, outside the tablet, otherwise sees the world — who now, looking over and across, sets the signs on the tablet in relation to the world outside it, compares, recognizes the mutual causalities, and accordingly can make, out of data of the *tabula*, analoga or causal-signs for his cognition-use. Instead of making analyses in the immanent psychological and epistemological inner attitude — instead of deepening oneself into the purely grasped consciousness itself, into the pure *ego cogito* and its intentional contents — one takes, in the natural naive outer consideration, oneself and other humans as pieces of the pre-given world, and takes their inner life, since it localizes itself with the bodies in spatiality, as though it were itself something like spatial-thingly: a mere complexion of real data conjoined or fused through real unity-forms ("Gestalt-qualities," in modern apprehension), in their constant change exclusively regulated by a natural, i.e. merely inductively recognizable, causality. The reader should mark the self-contradiction Husserl exposes: the image-theorist must covertly station a whole perceiving human *behind* the tablet — a human who *does* see the outer world directly — thereby presupposing the very thing-perception the theory was meant to deny.

One has no eye, Husserl continues, for the fact that what gives itself in pure "inner experience" — in pure intuiting devotion of reflection to the given as stream of *cogitationes* — shows, over against all the natural, a *wholly other-kinded being-manner*: that it is precisely through and through *cogitatio*, consciousness of immanent-intentional "objects" in the *how* of manifoldly changing "modes of appearance," and universally centered through what we above called *I-centering*. To be sure, Husserl grants, it is nothing less than a simple matter to understand how externality constitutes itself in the experiencing consciousness-stream itself and its synthetic connections; how then the difference between meant and true being is to be satisfied, how the genuine difference of subjective modes of appearance and of the appearing itself, and this self in its truth; and further, how then, at a higher stage, possibility, essence, accomplishment of scientific cognition is to be brought to ultimately-clarifying understanding. But only an *essence-description of the pure consciousness itself* in all such accomplishments can yield this understanding. To have *intimated* this, in the form of the demand for an understanding-investigation on the ground of inner experience, makes up the not-slight *merit of Locke*. But not a naturalistically misinterpreted inner experience comes here into question, rather an establishing, running in the frame of immanent evidence, of what consciousness-life *as* consciousness-life in all its types is and accomplishes in itself — singly and according to its synthetic connections and its intentional motivations. The reader should mark again the doubleness of Husserl's verdict: Locke's guiding demand is genuinely the demand of phenomenology, but his naturalized "inner experience" is the wrong ground for fulfilling it.

No establishing may here fall out of the attitude of pure immanence, Husserl insists; what is exhibited must be taken and let count exactly as it lies in the pure consciousness itself, and the meant therein exactly as it is meant. So, e.g., perceived exactly in the sense in which it gives itself as perceived, or which perception itself imparts to it — hence in temporal respect the sense of temporally present existence; likewise remembered exactly as it gives itself as remembered, with the sense "past process" that wins sense only out of this sense-giving; and so everywhere. As here individual objects win their subjective time-modes out of certain consciousness-manners, so, according to what was earlier occasionally discussed, out of other consciousness-manners subjective modes like analogue, image, sign win their sense. The reader should mark the governing principle of phenomenological description: each kind of sense — present existence, pastness, image-character, sign-character — is to be traced to the specific consciousness-manner that bestows it, never imported from the describer's outside knowledge.

So objects simply, in every respect and every conceivable subjective and objective sense purely materially, objectively taken, have their sense — constituting them *as*, and as so-determined, objects — out of the *sense-giving consciousness*, through which they mean, for the consciousness-subject, what they mean, and according to possibility or actuality are what they are. For every basic kind of objectivity, Husserl states the program, the correlative basic kinds of consciousness and consciousness-syntheses must be studied according to their construction, in which, as consciousness-accomplishment, precisely a so-constituted objectivity constitutes itself as unity of validity. To this construction belong naturally the often-emphasized *givenness-modes* of ever-new stages: with respect to the temporality belonging to the immanent intentional individual object, the modes of the now, the just-now, the coming; or with respect to the spatial things and their spatiality, the modes of orientation according to here and there, left and right, the givenness-modes of manifold perspective, that of the spatial shape and of the colorings "extending" over it, the givenness-ways according to changing thingly sides — in short all that "merely subjective" that the natural-scientific manner of consideration *switches off*. But all and every objectivity, the ideal ones too, are *unities of manifold givenness-ways*. Parallel with the concrete consciousness-experiences, whose immanent-intentional objects they are, the "objectivities in the how" also come to "synthetic unity." That, however, must in every respect be disclosed in intuiting reflection, exactly described, and thereby made intelligible. The reader should register the full breadth of the constitutive program: not only spatial things but ideal objectivities too are unities of their modes of givenness, and the whole of this "merely subjective" — which natural science legitimately discards — is precisely the field of transcendental research.

For all such problems of the *correlation* of cognizing and other consciousness on the one side and "its" objectivity on the other — the subjective constitution of the world in cognizing subjectivity, in other words all problems of subjectivity as *source of all sense-giving and validity* — a naturalistic psychology and theory of cognition is *principially blind*. And that means it is blind for the properly epistemological and, in empirical turning, even for the properly psychological cognition-problems. So, however much we did not mistake the progress inaugurated in Locke's *Essay* — that, over against Descartes, he undertook to ground a science of the givennesses of the *ego cogito* — it is now clear that he was able to press through neither to the genuine ground-science for all cognition, nor on the other side to a genuine objective psychology on the ground of inner experience. With this Husserl begins the recapitulatory close of the chapter, gathering the whole critique of Locke into its two results: failure as theory of reason, and failure even as objective psychology.

The critique we have completed, Husserl says, showed us in a problematic main line the *methodological countersense of the immanent naturalism* of the Lockean theory of cognition. It has also become clear that even if one, remaining in the natural objective attitude, wants to form an objective psychology, the blindness for the *intentional* — the basic character of the psychical consciousness-life — must make a real psychology impossible. A naturalistic *tabula-rasa*\-psychology, as Locke set in train and as it has formed itself on through the centuries, had to fail, had to remain hanging on inductive externalities. All that is originally essential for a spiritual life — all the highly remarkable peculiarities of consciousness as consciousness of something and as consciousness of an I; all the *wonders of manifold syntheses* that give the consciousness-stream, according to actualities and possibilities, passivities and free activities, the character of an intelligible unity and an intelligible *genesis*, that make it the site of a thoroughgoing rational lawfulness — all that remained out of play; it could at most make itself valid, involuntarily and unscientifically, in naturalistic disguises and misinterpretations. The reader should note the introduction of *genesis*: the consciousness-stream has not only synthetic structure but an intelligible genetic order — passivity and free activity, motivation — and this whole dimension of rational lawfulness is exactly what naturalism cannot reach.

It remained out of play, Husserl stresses, although it lay within the reach of intuition and of the capacities of intuitive explication of the meanings and meant-nesses implied in consciousness. The method of natural science makes precisely *blind* for the spiritual experience constantly exercised in active life and in all the *Geisteswissenschaften* (human sciences), and the exercised method of the *disclosure of spiritual motivations*, hidden co-meanings, theoretical and practical premises. Experience and experience-method might be only of one kind: it had to be thoroughly of the same essence-kind as the natural-scientifically exercised one. The reader should mark that Husserl here aligns the phenomenological method with the interpretive procedures of the human sciences — the disclosure of motivation and implicit meaning — against the monopoly of natural-scientific induction.

Yet the question of right psychological method, Husserl says, is here not ours; our interest is the *possibility of an absolute science* sprung from ultimate self-understanding of cognition about its own accomplishment — hence only the grounding of a genuine theory of reason. Though by this interest every drawing-upon of objective psychology, like objective science überhaupt, is excluded, there shows itself here so intimate an *interest-community* of objective psychology and pure theory of reason and of subjectivity überhaupt that we not without ground also cast, and must cast, some glances on the psychology proceeding from Locke. The reader should register the careful boundary: transcendental theory of reason may use no psychology as premise, yet psychology and transcendental philosophy share a field (the same lived-experiences described), and this kinship is why the critique of psychological Locke bears on the transcendental task.

Husserl now reckons up the *acquisition* of the critique by naming the two distinct countersenses precisely. On the one side, the Lockean method of a theory of reason falls into countersense through its *objectivism and "psychologism"* — through presupposing everywhere the objective world and the objective sciences and grounding its theory of reason on psychology as an objective science interwoven with the other objective sciences. And, to lift out one important point especially, it falls into countersense through the whole way it understands and works out its highly significant *leitmotiv*: the motive of seeking the origin of all concepts and cognition-formations überhaupt in consciousness, of going back to the immediate intuition of the "ideas" themselves and the acts generating ever-new ideas — in the now-usual talk, to "inner" or "self-experience." This experience Locke interprets quite naively, contrary to the peculiar sense of the reason-problematic, as self-experience in the natural objective sense of a component of *psychophysical* experience (experience of the psychical objectively bound with the bodily). That is the first countersense: the leitmotiv of origin-in-consciousness, executed on a naturalized self-experience.

But our last-carried-out critique, Husserl says, concerned *another*, in its consequences still more significant countersense. For still worse than this incapacity to hold apart psychological and transcendental self-experience — and thereby also psychological and transcendental unity of a consciousness-stream — is the incapacity to *see consciousness in its essence-peculiarity as consciousness* and to subject it as such to pure experience-analysis, and in further sequence to pure intuitive analysis überhaupt: the analysis of the possible consciousness-shapes and their essence-lawful modifications, implications, and syntheses. The enormous tasks that the title *intentionality, consciousness as consciousness of…* opens remain naturally closed, if one is, like Locke and the whole subsequent psychology, blind for this basic peculiarity of all consciousness-life — and is so through the naive prejudice we called *naturalization of consciousness*. Husserl recapitulates it: the apprehension of the consciousness-stream as a *being-together of "ideas," of "data,"* as physical data on an at first unwritten white paper or in a "dark room," whereby the parts are meant as real parts, the conjunctions as real conjunctions, the unity-forms as real unity-forms, and only as such. Of intentional stocks there is indeed, as unavoidable, always again talk, but never on the ground of a *systematic essence-intuiting and fixing description* in which they would have become thematic, as systematically to be established and to be pursued in their intentional entanglements.

Therein consists, Husserl says, the *"sensualism"* bequeathed by Locke to the future — for nothing other than the principial sense, or rather *countersense*, of all epistemologically wayward traditional doctrines of outer and inner senses, and thereby of all operating with "data" of outer and inner experience, is laid bare through our contrasting. This sensualism *crippled* both developments proceeding from the Lockean work: that of a psychology as objective science as well as that of the theory of reason as philosophical ground-science. Without overcoming "psychologism" and objectivism (without positivism in a good sense) überhaupt, no philosophy of reason is possible — and that says as much as a philosophy simply. But without the overcoming of *sensualism*, of consciousness-naturalism, not even a psychology as a genuine objective science is possible. A psychology that knows the ground-field of all psychological experience-facts — that of consciousness — only in naturalistic misinterpretation, hence according to its original essence not at all, we will have to refuse to recognize as a proper science. The reader should mark the two-tier diagnosis: *psychologism* (grounding reason-theory on psychology) blocks philosophy; the deeper *sensualism* (naturalizing consciousness itself) blocks even genuine psychology.

Should psychology be able to begin as a science at all, Husserl declares, it must do so in the shape of a *systematic and purely immanent consciousness-analysis* — a psychological *"phenomenology."* Phenomenological elementary analyses and descriptions create for it the *ABC*; the research of this ABC and of the shapes a priori to be formed from it — of the structural and genetic essence-laws belonging to it — makes up, as showed itself first in the present, a whole science, and indeed an *a priori* one. In necessity this precedes every experiential psychology (a science of the facts of psychological empiricism) "that is to be able to step forth as a strict science." It is precisely nothing other than the science of the *own-most essence of the psychical as such*. Here Husserl introduces *phenomenological psychology* as the a priori science of the essence of the psychical, the necessary ground of any empirical psychology — and immediately draws the consequence for the status of psychologism. A *psychologism on the ground of such a phenomenological psychology* (and even one that had mistaken its a priori character) is *curable*. It would have damaged, indeed in principle made impossible, the formation of a genuine theory of reason and philosophy, and yet would have been a relatively easily improvable error — *if only* the consciousness-analyses were drawn from real intuition and were really intentional analyses. For through change of attitude from the natural into the transcendental, through "bracketing" (*Einklammerung*) of all pre-posited and co-to-be-posited objectivity, all immanent analyses would remain preserved in their essence-kernel and epistemologically usable. The reader should mark this remarkable concession: a merely *psychologistic* misclassification of genuine intentional analyses is reparable by the reduction, because the analyses themselves survive the bracketing of objectivity with their kernel intact.

On the other side, Husserl concludes, a *sensualistic* psychologism is *incurable*. Its positings about the psychical itself are from the outset no real establishings, none drawn from the own essence of the consciousness-life itself. To disclose intentionality is to *understand* spiritually — to understand cognition and its formations, therein especially the formations truth, right — that is, to understand intentional formations through methodical disclosure of the intentional constitutive-forming connections. To do it scientifically-descriptively is to understand them scientifically. Where, by contrast, one has not even begun to *see* the kind of intentional implication — hence to create elements of an understanding — there is überhaupt nothing understandable and so nothing intelligible. But also psychological *genesis* is according to its essence *intelligible* genesis, and so every naturalistic psychology necessarily offers only a *pseudo-explanation* of psychological genesis. With this final stroke — sensualism as incurable because it never even attains the elements of understanding, and naturalistic genesis as mere pseudo-explanation — the Second Chapter closes, having disclosed the whole genuine problematic (intentionality, constitution of externality, true being as confirmation, synthesis, genesis, the curable/incurable distinction) hidden beneath Locke's reified surface. The Third Chapter turns to the *abstraction-theory of empiricism* as the index of its missing the idea of an eidetic science of pure consciousness.

## **Third Chapter: The Abstraction-Theory of Empiricism as Index of Its Missing the Idea of an Eidetic Science of Pure Consciousness**

### **Eighteenth Lecture: The misrecognition of the intuitive self-givenness of the general essences**

Husserl opens the Third Chapter by marking off its theme from the preceding critique. A particular substrate of our critique was above Locke's doctrine of the material substances and their qualities — his attempt to show how the true externality presents itself inwardly in the idea-domain, how the subject, which immediately has only the *tabula rasa* of its ideas, wins on it an image of externality and conviction of its true being. In the same sense our critique could go on to the whole line of Locke's expositions referring, in continuation, to the *constitutive categories of nature-cognition*: space, time, force, cause, effect. But it has for us no considerable interest to go on in this direction. Quite otherwise it stands, Husserl says, with the Lockean chapters on *language and thinking, on truth and knowledge, science* — with Locke's *abstraction-theory*. Did behind investigations of the first kind stand the problematic of the *constitution of nature*, of the in-itself-existing world in cognizing consciousness, it is now a matter of the problematic of the *specific logos* — of the formal scaffolding of logical categories that the experienced world, with its real categories, must take on in order to become the scientifically true actuality, the one determining itself in theoretical truth. The reader should mark the shift of register: from the constitution of nature (the real categories) to the constitution of the logical, the categorial forms through which experienced nature becomes scientifically determinable truth.

Here it is now a matter, Husserl announces, of bringing into relief a *new type of basic errors* that, from English empiricism, fatefully determined the whole modern philosophy. They are, to be sure, an old inherited evil — heritage of ancient skepticism and medieval nominalism, which with *Hobbes* overflows into the modern so-called empiricism. Our new theme is the *blindness for Ideas and ideal laws in the rightly understood Platonic sense*. What characterizes the traditional empiricism, so to speak definitorily, is the principial *prejudice that only the individual can be originally intuitive*. With the denial of intuitive graspability goes hand in hand the denial of the possible being of generalities — self-evidently under the guidance of the intuitionistic thought that legitimate thinking must exhibit itself intuitively; what does not let itself be intuitively presented and, where an actual being is in question, does not let itself be perceived, can also not be. The reader should note the irony Husserl is building: empiricism's governing principle — that all legitimate thinking must be redeemable in intuition — is itself a sound intuitionistic insight; the error lies only in arbitrarily restricting intuition to the *individual*, so that the genuine intuition of essences is excluded from the start.

This *apparently self-evident identification* of intuition with individual intuition, of self-grasping intuition with perception, passes over to the empiricism of the new Lockean stamp, that of immanent naturalism. An *intuiting of conceptual essences*, the correlates of general words — and on the other side therefore also the *being* of such essences themselves — is not acknowledged. The universe of intuitive cognition is the totality of the ideas exhibitable on the consciousness-tablet; intuition and "perception" or "experience" is one and the same. In other words, the data of this tablet are, just like data of nature, thoroughly temporally single facts, psychically individual, given in an immanent experience that is of the same kind as the outer. The reader should see how the naturalization of consciousness (the *tabula rasa*) and the nominalist prejudice (only the individual is intuitable) reinforce each other: if inner experience is a tablet of individual data like outer nature, then no general essence could ever be among its givennesses.

Apparently this is contradicted, Husserl grants, by Locke's detailed doctrine of the "general ideas," likewise his doctrine of intuitive and demonstrative cognition and the recognition, grounded thereon, of pure logic, pure mathematics, and pure morals in sharp distinction from the empirical sciences. But if one looks more closely, an abstract idea is, according to Locke, only an *individual* single moment to be lifted out of an arbitrary single thing from a manifold of mutually resembling things — a moment that repeats itself in them all in likeness — and besides, "abstract idea" designates a certain *representing function* that we usefully give it in asserting and thinking. If, e.g., several things are alike as red ones, we can detach for itself the moment of agreement, recurring in all in like manner, as a proper idea, which naturally is an individual; but this single one the spirit uses as *representative* or *pattern-image*, in order to think accordingly every occurring concrete thing that likewise has in itself such a moment red as a red one — namely to think it as a thing having in itself a moment red *like* the abstract pattern-image red. Only that makes possible, on Locke's account, the so exceedingly useful general designation, the formation and use of general words (red, round) and accordingly general asserting. The reader should mark the structure of Locke's theory exactly: the "general" is reduced to an individual moment functioning representatively by resemblance — there is no genuine general object, only a particular used as a sample.

But however much here recourse is taken to *consciousness-functions* — acts of comparison, abstraction, representation, norming — and however detailed Locke otherwise treats psychical acts (collecting, relating, conjoining, distinguishing, identifying), there is yet of an *analysis and description of the generality-consciousness as consciousness of…* and of an *intentional clarification of its objectivating accomplishment* here, as in all similar cases, no talk. The understanding for the problematic of intentionality is wholly lacking. He therefore also does not recognize that in the functions of general thinking there grow, as their specifically objectivating accomplishment, *peculiar objectivities in stage-wise implication* — and indeed, in the original forms of this thinking, originally intuitive, namely as *immediate self-givennesses*. The reader should mark the decisive constitutive claim: general thinking does not merely manipulate individual samples but *objectivates*, brings to original self-givenness, objectivities of a new kind — the general essences themselves — and this objectivating accomplishment is exactly what Locke's representative theory cannot see.

If it does not even come, in Locke, to the descriptive establishing that different consciousness-experiences of sensuous perceiving can in their immanence have conscious *numerically identically the same thing* — if it already escapes him that a real, an individually transcendent, can not falsely but quite literally in bodily selfhood step forth in perception as its perceived — then, Husserl says, he sees all the less that *quite analogous* holds for the general intuiting, in relation to general essences, Platonically spoken, for "Ideas," and that it holds no less for the *insighting of general states-of-affairs or idea-relations*. This remains unseen in the whole of empiricism; and whatever modifications the Lockean abstraction-doctrine undergoes in later times up to the present day, it remains unseen what lies, consciousness-wise and as undeniable sense, in all general thinking and asserting, and lies in insightful asserting as indubitable self-givenness. The reader should note the parallel Husserl is pressing: just as the perceived thing is itself, bodily, given in perception (not an image of an inaccessible original), so the general essence is itself given in eidetic intuition — and empiricism misses both for the same reason, the blindness to intentional self-givenness.

Also general essences are *objects*, Husserl insists, are consciousness-wise meant as objects; of them is predicated, in rightness and wrongness, insightfully or un-insightfully, as of objects otherwise and especially as of individual objects. Like other, like individual objects, they are unities in manifold consciousness meaning *them and nothing else*, and can also, like other objects, possibly be conscious in the distinguished way in which they come to *immediate self-grasping* — hence quite analogously as perceived things in perception. Can a thing become conscious now unclearly or emptily un-intuitively, now in self-grasping perception or in memory re-presenting an earlier self-grasping, so no less also a *general*, a conceptual essence of different generality-stages — like color and tone überhaupt, like triangle, figure überhaupt. It can now be unclearly thought-of and talked-about, now clearly and in full intuition intuited and grasped *as itself, and as existing general*. And here too it becomes evident, in the corresponding synthesis of identification, that the once-so and the other-time-otherwise — and in any case the in different and separated experiences conscious — is *numerically identically one and the same*, possibly the same once merely meant and then itself given. Here too the *fulfillment-synthesis*, leading the meant back to the self-grasped, is synthesis of confirming corroboration, bringing into relief the "right" of the fore-meaning; and here too the meaning can, in regress to self-giving intuitions, *shatter in conflict* — e.g. the nullity of the meant regular triangle can turn out. The reader should mark how Husserl maps the entire structure of perceptual evidence (meant vs. self-given, fulfillment, identifying synthesis, possible disappointment) onto eidetic intuition: the intuition of essences is no occult faculty but has exactly the same intentional form as perception of things.

The synthetically self-covering experiences have, Husserl specifies, in that they mean general — bear it in themselves *originaliter* as intuitings of the general — not, say, a *real piece* in common, quite as, e.g., several thing-experiences as experiences of the same thing; only with the evident difference on the side of the consciousness of generalities: while the experiences themselves are naturally individual immanent data, the *general* that they intentionally mean in themselves or themselves have is *not an individual* but precisely a general. That there is, therefore, in the true and actual sense general thinking, general presenting, general intuiting, is *not the invention of an overwrought Platonism*, but a doctrine that consciousness itself gives us, provided we only question it, and question it according to what lies in it itself as meaning and accomplishment in absolute evidence. The reader should weigh this defense: Husserl's eidetics is not a metaphysical hypostatization of universals but a faithful reading of what consciousness, interrogated, already contains — the general as the identical objectivity meant and self-given across a manifold of acts.

No theory of reason, no philosophy is scientifically possible überhaupt, Husserl declares, that remains, in the sense of the general tradition, by talking much of evidence, insight, but is incapable of questioning, in the way of intentional description, the *evidence-consciousness* — does not recognize it as what it according to its essence is: *self-giving, self-grasping of objectivities* that, in the process of the originally proper, here actively self-shaping thinking, become conscious *as themselves*, so to speak bodily. But grasping of self-given is the same as *immediate intuition*; and it would be a foolish objection against this *extension of the concept of intuition*, with its immediacy, if one were to say (as has happened): thinking is a *mediate* consciousness, over against immediate intuition; intuition means passivity, means taking-in of a given, but thinking is a manifold activity proceeding from such given — and should we then cease to call concept-formation, and indeed inferring, proving, mediate? The objection, Husserl says, is foolish. For just as, in their way, also the "passivity" of outer experience has its manifold implication — which, e.g., in changing perceiving, in the temporal succession too, brings manifold apperceptions to synthesis, while yet the experienced object is "immediately" intuited, precisely self-given — so similarly in thinking. The reader should follow the reply: immediacy of self-givenness does not mean absence of synthetic structure; perception too is synthetically built up and yet gives its object immediately, and the same holds for the active syntheses of thinking.

To thinking belongs precisely, Husserl recalls, a manifold of acts conjoined into a synthetic act-unity, in order to make conscious unity of the object; and if the acts have the form of properness and originality, they *constitute generatingly* the unity of the object, which is in this generation *self-given*. This whole construction of *active synthesis* is here the unity of self-giving — accomplishes here immediately intuitive consciousness of the objective here in question, e.g. of the objective unity of the inferential consequence or of a whole proof-connection, a whole theory. Such a thing, Husserl adds with a striking thought-experiment, even a *God* could have given immediately only if he carries out this act-connection of actual generation; even he can have thoughts only if he thinks, and thinks all that in conjunction which the conjunction-unity of the thought demands. *Every kind of objectivity has its immediacy of givenness, its kind of intuition, of self-giving evidence.* The reader should mark the force of the divine example: there is no shortcut to the categorial; even an infinite intellect must *generate* the proof-unity by carrying out the synthesis, because that unity simply *is* the accomplishment of such synthesis — which secures, against any psychologistic deflation, that categorial self-givenness is the only mode in which logical objectivities can be had at all.

Instead of this, Husserl concludes, the centuries do not properly get beyond what the medieval talk of the *lumen naturale* already pictorially says: an enigmatic light-like character, a "feeling" of thought-necessity, is supposed to make up the precedence of evident thinking — whereupon then the *absurd problem* arises of the question after the ground why this distinction must *indicate the truth*. And so we are, since Descartes' recourse to the divine *veracitas*, still in the old embarrassment. With this the lecture closes the loop back to the Cartesian circle diagnosed in the First Section: so long as evidence is construed as a mere *index* — a light or feeling accompanying certain judgments — one must then prove, circularly, that this index points to truth; whereas the phenomenological insight that evidence *is* the self-givenness of the matter itself dissolves the pseudo-problem entirely. The reader should register that the abstraction-theory's failure and the evidence-as-index failure are one and the same blindness to intentional self-givenness, now traced through the categorial domain. The Nineteenth Lecture develops the consequence: the *necessity of the extension of the idea of intuition* required to do justice to the self-givenness of the general.

### **Nineteenth Lecture: The necessity of the extension of the idea of intuition**

Husserl resumes the analysis of the intuiting grasping of the general, claimed as the exact analogue of perception of the individually present or memory of an individual past. It is certain, he grants, that during the general intuition concrete *single intuitions* lie in the consciousness-field — indeed that they belong, as necessarily functioning *substrates* of the general intuition, into it itself. It is certain too that the way single intuitions are therein carried out — that, say, in the intuition of a red überhaupt, red single things are conscious — is essentially other than where the single intuitions do *not* stand in such function, do not serve to shape out and intuitingly grasp a general on their ground. For otherwise the individual single thing is grasped, meant, posited *as this here*; now the *general*, red überhaupt, and only this, is meant, intuited and grasped *as existing general*. The reader should mark the precise phenomenological difference: the same red sample functions in two utterly different consciousness-manners depending on whether the individual or the general is meant — and the empiricist, having no eye for this difference of intentional function, can only see the individual sample.

We, who have already won the gaze-setting upon consciousness itself and its intentional contents, Husserl says, and in this gaze-setting make such assertions, would now have the further procedure clearly prefigured. The task would be to characterize these different consciousness-manners against one another and to describe analytically what modifications the single-intuiting consciousness undergoes, in what manner, through what structures it accomplishes the new accomplishment called general insightful intuiting. Therein regard is to be taken of the perfection-stages of clarity or intuitiveness possible here as everywhere, their peculiarity, and finally of the so important *un-intuitive thinking* and its modes — its way of constituting an un-intuitive sense, a meant generality, in a mere empty *fore-grasp* (*Vorgriff*) meaning it in itself. And then justice must be done also to all the essentially belonging-together most general shapes in which general is meant in thinking, in insightful thinking itself intuited and given — but also to the *special types* appearing only in particular spheres. Husserl distinguishes them grammatically: if I judge "red is a kind of color, the triangle is a kind of the genus rectilinear figure," then kinds and genera are *substrate-objects*, objects "about which." But if I judge "a triangle überhaupt has as angle-sum two right angles — namely *every* triangle," or "a red ribbon, in plural red ribbons flutter in the wind," then I have thought, instead of the universal "a" and "überhaupt," the *particular*, likewise a form of generality. In all — in "the red," "some red," "every red" — there is something common, and yet the consciousness-manners are different, and then also the manners of originally evident self-giving. The reader should note the fineness of the program: not "the general" in the lump, but a whole differentiated grammar of generality-consciousness — universal, particular, the substrate-use of genera, the predicative use — each with its own mode of self-givenness.

In quite another direction, Husserl continues, lie the investigations concerning the general of the distinguished shape "pure or ideal straight line," and so überhaupt the "ideal" purity of the geometric concepts and essences, over against *conceptual generalities* such as the descriptive general concepts of botany show — those likewise naturally intuitively to be grasped types that the words grape-shaped, umbel-shaped, also circular, elliptical express, where all geometric *ideality* is quite far and outside the meaning. So not only for spatial shapes but in all individual and as-such-to-be-typified spheres. *Ideal concepts and types*, although they are no single things, are seen in their way, in their way also graphically presented; and for these and all special shapes of the general the analogous problems of the clarification of the consciousness constituting them must be posed and solved. The reader should mark the distinction between the *exact* (ideal-geometric) essences and the *morphological* (descriptive, type-) essences — both intuitable, but in different manners — which Husserl is careful to keep apart, since the empiricist's failure flattens the whole field. So the problematic, an endlessly progressing one, presents itself for him who has learned to grasp and describe consciousness as consciousness.

Quite otherwise the empiricist abstraction-doctrine since Locke. It *conjures away* (*eskamotiert*) the generality-consciousness and the general itself by pointing to the single intuitions necessarily functioning in really evident intuitive thinking and then saying: beyond these lies "nothing further" than mere representation. For example, the seen or fictionalized triangle serves in geometrically intuitive thinking as representative for any arbitrary triangle. But what is this "mere representation"? Exactly seen, Husserl answers, if we hold to the insightful thinking in question itself — say in the example "a triangle überhaupt," to it itself as it is to be interrogated in immediate reflection — this representation is *nothing other than the denied general intuiting itself*, however little the ambiguous word "representation" may fit. The naturalistic attitude it is that makes theoretically blind for it; it is naturally also *experienced* by the empiricist and in a certain way *seen*, but it is not *let count*. Everywhere one wants, blinded by the model of natural-scientific explanation, to explain in like manner; to the problems of epistemological *origin* — those of the clarification of the consciousness constituting every kind of objectivity — one substitutes problems of a natural and causal explanation, in that one regards the realm of inner experience like a closed field of things with belonging explanatory natural laws. The reader should mark the recurring substitution: *origin-clarification* (how objectivity is constituted in consciousness) is replaced by *causal explanation* (how data arise by psychological law), and this substitution is the empiricist's standing error across every domain.

Instead of carrying out *transcendental pure self-knowledge* — as backward-looking consideration, analysis, and immediate description of the contents making the consciousness-intentionality and its accomplishment intelligible — one falls into psychological-causal constructions on the ground of a naturally misinterpreted inner experience; so here too for the realm of the general givennesses and the generality-consciousness. And therein all such constructions draw the *semblance of explanation* adhering to them from the theoretically disregarded consciousness, whose sense-contents mingle shimmeringly with the causally substructed ones. The reader should note the diagnosis of the illusion's source: the causal pseudo-explanation borrows its plausibility from the very intentional consciousness it disregards, smuggling sense-relations in under the guise of causal ones.

The principial perversity not only of the Lockean abstraction-theory, Husserl stresses, but also of the highly famous *Berkeleyan* one and of all later, concerns thinking not at a single point. Equally affected is rather the *whole realm of the logos*. Thereby all and every thinking-accomplishment, all and every theory and science has become unintelligible; nay more, for him who is able to see consequences, the *possibility of all science* is principially cancelled. The *tabula-rasa*\-psychologism and empiricism is, already through its theory of thinking, an *extreme, though self-hidden, skepticism*, and falls under the reproach of the conceivably most radical *countersense* peculiar to all genuine skepticism in any form. For if one discloses what is consistently enclosed in its theories, it is to be made evident that it, through the content of these theories, *negates* the possibility of all thinking-accomplishments überhaupt and principially — and thereby negates what it claims, in its own thinking doing, in the shaping of its own theory, as possible and really accomplished accomplishment. The reader should mark the classic self-refutation Husserl deploys: empiricism's theory of thought, consistently carried through, denies the very possibility of the theorizing by which empiricism itself proceeds — the signature countersense of all skepticism.

Even *Hume*, so to speak the skeptic by métier, has, Husserl notes, in other respects disclosed the skeptical consequences of empiricism, but with respect to general thinking he did not see the radical skeptical consequence — unless he intentionally kept silent about it, in order not to deprive his skeptical theories of all impression and let them appear from the outset as objectless and ridiculous. In any case Locke and all other naturalists of inner experience *honestly* mean not to have surrendered the accomplishment of thinking and of the thinking of the sciences, but only to have psychologically *explained* it, made it intelligible — and quite especially the accomplishments of pure thinking in the purely rational sciences. The reader should register Husserl's fairness: the empiricists are not cynics but sincere; they believe they are saving rational science by explaining it, not destroying it — which is precisely why the hidden countersense is so fateful.

Since here the critique of the centuries has at bottom failed, Husserl says, it is of great interest to exhibit the skeptical situation at a principial fundamental point — at the *axiomatic cognitions and truths*. We here criticize, in other words, the doctrine historically famous under the Humean title of *cognition of relations of ideas*, which Hume himself uses as fundament for his skeptical argumentations; but in everything essential the doctrine stems from Locke. *Immediately and fully evident* are certain propositions of lower and higher generality — propositions one can regard as general propositions about relations: e.g. "red is different from green," "2 \< 3." According to the dogma of the empiricist doctrine, intuitively given and really existing is only the *single*; hence in each case I have, insighting such general propositions, really given only individual relations of individual data. There is no seeing of the *überhaupt* and of relations überhaupt. But what, Husserl asks, is now to give us a *right* to assert such general propositions? Can here the mythical *representation*, in transfer also onto the generality of the relational state-of-affairs, help anything — if it is to be bound precisely with the assertion that the general as such is not itself intuited? Whence do I know that I can so claim the individually seen relation "this red and this green" as "representative" for arbitrary similar cases in the sense here in question? It lies in the sense of such propositions that, *whenever* I present a red and *wherever* a green, the relation in question unfailingly subsists.

Husserl drives the point against the psychologistic reinterpretation. If one interprets: it belongs to the nature of a seen or phantasy-intuitive red and an equally such green, given together in human consciousness, to be able to appear only in this relational conjunction — then we will not even ask where and on the ground of what inductions this *psychological law* has been brought into relief, and whence the empiricist knows anything of it as natural law. In any case, whoever simply judges and insights what the *axiom* says speaks not about the soul — his own and others', of the present and all the past — and about psychological laws, but of nothing further than what he sees and insights, quite immediately, and that runs simply: "red is different from green." And is it not clear that every drawing-in of psychological laws *completely changes the sense* of the axioms? Moreover: were it a psychical natural law that such relational conjunctions always set in where the relation-points are conscious, then in the given single case only the *single* relation would be there; and what is in question is not, like the mere single case, but how a *general law-knowledge*, precisely the axiomatic, is as genuine possible. The reader should follow the two-edged refutation: the psychologistic reading both falsifies the *sense* of the axiom (which says nothing about souls) and fails to explain its *generality and necessity* (which no single psychological fact could ground).

At bottom it is therefore always the same method, Husserl says, that sets *behind* the consciousness-tablet a subject who knows and thinkingly accomplishes all that which would have to appear precisely *on* the tablet itself — i.e. in the inner consciousness itself — as consciousness-accomplishment, but which one would forcibly explain away. The empiricism is therefore, we see, only a *pseudo-intuitionism* or only a *pseudo-empiricism*; for it is only semblance that it carries through its principle of asserting nothing it does not draw from intuition, only semblance that it goes back to experience, to self-grasping intuiting, and measures everything asserted against the things and states-of-affairs themselves. The reader should mark the irony driven home: empiricism, which prides itself on fidelity to experience, is the least empirical of doctrines, since it refuses to let the most evident self-givennesses — the general essences and relations given in insight — count as what they are.

We convince ourselves of this, Husserl continues, not only at the psychologistic interpretations of axiomatic and then überhaupt of rational thinking — which, taken seriously, would have to lead to its open *rejection* — and not only of the countersensical skepsis lying therein, lying in it in most extreme form because now also the cognition and validity of the purely logical axioms, like that of contradiction, is affected by it. No: we convince ourselves also that at bottom, according to empiricism, not even the *possibility of a judgment about the individual* remains intelligible and preserved for us. We need only notice the following. An individual assertion like "this tone is higher than that tone" has a unitary assertion-sense whose truth I immediately recognize when I immediately recognize that itself which is asserted — the state-of-affairs itself. Now let emphasis first be laid on this: that here the *concepts* "tone" and "being-higher" appear as components of the assertion-sense and, in conformity to intuition, as components of the asserted *state-of-affairs* itself. According to sense, the word-senses "tone" and "higher" fulfill themselves through intuition — but not through the merely *sensuous* experience of two tones and a sensuous conjunction of both; rather the intuitive fulfillment concerns these sensuous singularities precisely *as singularities of generalities*. The skepsis against the general cancels also the general at the single case; and since individual assertions are unthinkable without co-meant conceptual generalities, that would already suffice to recognize that the empiricism does not even let singular assertions about the individual appear intelligible and possible. The reader should mark this consequence: the nominalist denial of the general undermines not only general but even singular judgment, since every predication ("this is a tone," "this is higher") already deploys conceptual generalities — empiricism saws off the branch on which even individual cognition sits.

But of greater interest still, Husserl says, is the following: how does it stand with the entire *grammatical forms* of the assertions — already of individual assertions — with the subject-form and predicate-form, with the *is* and the *not*, with the *and* and the *or*, the *if* and the *then*? We say in ordinary talk: I *see* that this house has a red roof, I *hear* that this tone is higher than that; and we speak not merely of seeing or hearing the house, roof, tone itself. In nature are *things*, but in nature are not these *states-of-affairs* with their subject- and predicate-forms, the relation of the higher, and on the other hand, as another thing, the counter-relation of the lower, which in each case is itself again a non-self-standing moment in the intuitive of the whole proposition, in the state-of-affairs. In fact, Husserl insists, "experiencing" is not merely *experiencing individual data*; experiencing is *consciousness of self-giving, of self-grasping, self-having* — and indeed of such as can be meant überhaupt and in any consciousness-shapes, meant in innumerable assertion-shapes with single forms, and then, exactly in this sense-forming, be precisely given, itself intuited, and grasped as truly existing. Without this *extension of the idea of intuition*, which measures itself exactly to the consciousness-wise meaning — the possibly quite un-intuitive one — in all shapes, there is of a description of the cognition-situation and an understanding about cognition and true being no talk. The reader should grasp the breadth of the extended concept of intuition: not only individuals and essences but *categorial* objectivities — states-of-affairs, relations, the syntactical forms *is, not, and, or* — are themselves given in a corresponding intuition, the *categorial intuition* without which no judgment, however singular, could be redeemed.

It is clear, Husserl declares, that only if we resolve to put aside all prejudices and to *identify experience or intuition with evidence, with cognition in the pregnant sense*, and only if we become aware that this widened "experience" is nothing other than *self-grasping of the meant exactly as it is meant* — only then can we seriously think of understanding *cognizing*: of understanding how not only the world of simple concept-less experience but also the logical objectivity, and so the objectivity of every kind and stage with all its real and ideal forms, can have for us sense and exhibitable being. *Consciousness in itself, in its essence-shapes, creates sense*, and in the evidence-shapes possible and true sense — as shape of possible fulfillment of unfulfilled thought-intentions, a fulfillment in the shape of self-giving, or in "measuring" to such shape. The reader should mark the culminating identification: experience \= intuition \= evidence \= self-grasping of the meant as meant; this single equation, won against empiricism, is the methodological foundation of the whole phenomenological theory of cognition.

But clear too, on the ground of our critical analyses, Husserl says, is the *only possible method* that can serve to take in hand the problems of possibility and sense of cognition — problems so sensibly pressing from the old skepticism and, in its further effect, from the newer empiricism — indeed, even before that, to free them from the confusions, vaguenesses, contradiction-full unclarities, to transform them into the genuine and pure problems as which they are to be set over against all objectivistic problems. It is no other method than that of going back to the *Cartesian ground*, to the pure cognition-subjectivity and its pure consciousness, and then *questioning consciousness itself* according to the sense evidently proper to it and according to the essence-shapes of its possible sense-fulfillment or evidence, in which objectivity of every kind originally constitutes itself consciousness-wise, as self grasped in the self-grasping. The reader should register that the entire critique of Locke and empiricism has been, all along, the via negativa toward this single positive method — the reductive return to pure consciousness and the constitutive interrogation of its evidence-shapes.

But not only that, Husserl continues — *a great further step* will prove necessary. The questions of possible cognition, the clarification of the *how* of the original consciousness-constitution, did not historically appear as questions addressed to *singular* objects and their consciousness; enigmatic, through the skeptical negations, became the possibility of an experience and a knowledge *überhaupt* of in-themselves-existing things *überhaupt*; likewise later the generality-consciousness überhaupt and the being-kind of the idea überhaupt, evidence überhaupt, related to truth überhaupt. Here so much is clear: that once one has pure ideas — like the mathematical ideas and the ideas of pure apophantic logic — the pure generality on the objective side must carry with itself an equally pure generality for the *consciousness-side*. In other words, one becomes aware that the *transcendental consciousness* in its basic shapings and its transcendental accomplishments *can and must be considered in the method of essence-intuition* (*Wesensintuition*) — hence, as we can say, in the purely grasped *Platonic method*. Every pure idea of a genus or a mathematical form of objectivities überhaupt points back to an *eidetic problematic* of the consciousness-manners related to such-constituted or such-formed objectivity, and therein these consciousness-manners are themselves thought in eidetic generality and must, in the actual investigation, be exhibited in eidetic method as "Ideas." The reader should mark the decisive methodological elevation: the science of consciousness must be not merely descriptive of facts but *eidetic*, an a priori science of the essence-shapes of consciousness, correlative to the eidetic generality already present on the object-side in logic and mathematics.

So there marks itself out here, Husserl says, from the critique of empiricism, the thought of a *wholly other science of subjectivity out of pure "inner experience"*: an *eidetic science of an I überhaupt, possible pure consciousness überhaupt, possible consciousness-objects überhaupt* — whereby all *facticity* is switched off and is co-enclosed only in the extent of free possibilities, as one of them. At deeper entering it even turns out that all transcendental questions to be posed to individual objects and individually determined consciousness — say of *these* humans and *this* world — can and may be treated only as geometric questions concerning a determinate nature-thing and determinate nature überhaupt are treated. That says: the necessary method is that the single case be considered *as single case of an a priori generality*, and so the problem be transposed out of the fact into the realm of pure possibilities and their a priori. *Transcendental philosophy is first, and with necessity, a priori philosophy, and then application to the fact.* The reader should mark this as one of the load-bearing methodological theses of the whole work: the transcendental investigation of the factual ego and world proceeds, like geometry, by treating the fact as an instance of eidetic necessity — the eidetic reduction precedes and grounds any transcendental study of the factual.

How that is meant, Husserl says, cannot yet here come into discussion; in any case we hold fast what lights up to us, still quite from afar: the *idea of a universal essence-science of the pure subjectivity and its pure consciousness-life* — a science that, as eidetic ("a priori"), systematically researches the *universe of ideal possibilities* of such life and of the objectivities constituting themselves in it according to ideal possibility: in short, an *eidetic science of the ego cogito*. With this culminating idea — phenomenology as the eidetic science of the *ego cogito*, won as the positive yield of the entire critical history of Locke and empiricism — the Second Section closes. The reader should register the arc completed: from Locke's naturalized inner experience, through the disclosure of intentionality, irreal immanence, synthesis, the constitution of externality, and the self-givenness of essences and categorial forms, to the demand for an a priori, eidetic phenomenology of pure consciousness. The Third Section, opening next, turns to the *formation of skeptical pre-forms of phenomenology through Berkeley and Hume, and the dogmatic rationalism* — reading the empiricist development as, despite itself, a preparation of phenomenology, set against the rationalist line that runs toward Leibniz and Kant.

## **Third Section: The Formation of Skeptical Pre-forms of Phenomenology through Berkeley and Hume, and the Dogmatic Rationalism**

## **First Chapter: From Locke to Berkeley's Radical Consequence of a Purely Immanent Philosophy**

### **Twentieth Lecture: The positive historical significance of the renewal of skepticism through Locke and his successors**

Husserl opens the Third Section by taking stock of the critique just completed and announcing the new historical task. Our critique of the Lockean philosophy, he says, has come to a natural conclusion insofar as we have critically exhibited on it what could be exhibited on it itself, without compulsion and without anticipating any further developments. We conducted the critique throughout at a certain *distance*, so that it became the critique of *every* philosophy of the new type grounded by Locke. Precisely this new type — essentially determining for the entire picture of modern philosophy, that of an epistemological *tabula-rasa*\-psychologism — is what with good ground so long held us fast, and what must hold us further in its only-consequent further development into a so-called *immanent philosophy* or immanent "positivism." This development attaches to the two ingenious pupils of Locke, *Berkeley* and *Hume*. In inseparable historical unity with these two thinkers, completing himself so to speak in them, Locke is one of the main sources of the spirit of the living philosophical present. The reader should mark the framing: the Third Section reads the Berkeley–Hume line not as a digression but as the consequent unfolding of Locke, whose hidden truth and hidden skepticism alike come to light only in the successors.

Just this source, however, Husserl says, interests us, according to the whole course and sense of our idea-historical-critical considerations, before all else. For it is a matter, in these considerations, of nothing less than laying bare the *unity of the motivation* running through millennia, which lived as developmental drive in all philosophy insofar as it wanted to become true philosophy, and in all philosophical method insofar as it wanted to become true method. Temporarily relatively satisfied in some philosophies, and yet never ultimately satisfied, philosophy is driven on to ever-new methodical *Besinnungen* (reflective self-examinations); it takes on ever-new methodical shapes and yet thereby never comes to an end — never comes to *the* end that is called: *true beginning of a true becoming out of the force of the one true method*. True method, however, can here only mean that which can be understood and considered, in absolutely indubitable evidence, as the one uniquely fulfilling the sense of philosophy, uniquely demanded by it. The reader should register the teleological frame restated: the whole history is read as the single drive toward the one method that alone satisfies the idea of philosophy as self-justifying science, never reached until phenomenology.

It is a matter, Husserl continues, of bringing to understanding that philosophy, according to its methodical leading-idea as absolutely self-justifying science, could *not* find satisfaction in the early-grown methodical ideal of objective-rational science — that it rather demanded a *completely new kind of methodical procedure*, without which it, and thereby genuine science überhaupt, could never become such, indeed could not even begin to become such. It is a matter of showing that certain *hindrances*, grounding in the nature of the cognition-situation itself in which all philosophizing roots, deflect the spirit's eye from the gaze-direction upon *pure consciousness*, and thereby from the appointed workshop in which all foundational work must be done; and further, the hindrances must be shown that prevent the grasping of the *evident intentional stocks* and so the formation of the genuinely intentional method in which here alone work can be done. And naturally it is a matter of laying clear the *developmental stages* in which the becoming philosophy gradually became aware of this pure consciousness-sphere, recognized it as the necessary work-site, while it at first remained blind for its peculiar essence and the peculiar working-manner — so up to the final breakthrough of the true method and the first beginning of actual philosophy in the last decades, and indeed, as Husserl is convinced, in the shape of the *new phenomenology*. The reader should mark the explicit self-location: phenomenology is presented as the historical terminus toward which the whole skeptical-rationalist development of modernity tends, the first actual realization of the long-sought method.

So it becomes intelligible to us, Husserl says, that Locke was in our connection of quite particular historical significance. He may be ever so boring and long-winded — and he is amply so; he may be ever so poor in metaphysical profundity, in worldview-intuition, poor in all that can tear hearts aloft or admonish them to the tragedy of world-fate in the struggling life of mankind; his empiricism and that of his school may be ever so offensive, as it was felt to be in all times; nonetheless the Lockean philosophy, both in its original type and in its type further developed into the immanent philosophy, was an *essential stage on the thorny way to the true method*. Already by this must it excite our interest: that it is, as we could demonstrate, a — to itself, to be sure, veiled — *skepticism*, which in fact in the further development, though not in every direction, disclosed itself, and now as Humean skepticism poses to the new philosophy the great demand to overcome it in the form that is the necessary one for every radical skepticism, namely *to make it true in a higher sense*.
