The reader should hold this recurring formula: a radical skepticism is overcome not by mere refutation but by *making it true* — by redeeming, at a higher level, the genuine insight its negations distortedly express.

The Lockean psychologism interests us, therefore, Husserl says, already as a *new form of skepticism*, new over against the ancient skepticism, which, as we showed, was so significant for the development of ancient philosophy — for in reaction against its primal-form, against the Sophistic, there grows first of all the *idea and the problem of philosophy as science out of absolute justification*. Let us bring the known to re-remembrance, Husserl says, and recalls the ancient parallel. We convinced ourselves that antiquity, despite hot and in a certain respect relatively very successful effort, was not really able to do justice to this idea. It created the objective rational science — a science seemingly fully satisfying, but yet, for all its accomplishment, inwardly *skeptically burdened*: incapable of answering the riddle-questions that could be posed to its objects in respect of their cognition, precisely the *transcendental* questions. So in antiquity the skepticism remained as a broad stream, and in fact remained insurmountable. The reader should mark the diagnostic parallel: ancient objective science, like Locke's, was internally undermined by an unanswered transcendental problematic, so that skepticism persisted alongside it as its permanent shadow.

As for the modern age, Husserl continues, it begins, as we saw, as *renaissance of the Platonic intentions*. *Descartes* renews with primal-force the idea of a radically self-justifying universal science and attempts to set it to work in a new method. The undertaking fails, although he takes the first absolutely necessary step and in the *ego cogito* really discovers the Archimedean point — or rather Archimedean *ground*, the absolutely sure and necessary work-ground of the first foundations. But precisely that it *is* work-ground, and what there must be the kind and method of the work, that Descartes does *not* discover; and so he gives only a powerful impulse that must first work itself out historically. He commits therein the basic error of letting objective science — as it was created as the ancient science-type, at most in the new shape of rational natural science — justify itself absolutely only through a *supporting foundation*, without methodically *changing it itself*. Precisely thereby — the ground-consideration and the theological-metaphysical universal perspectives it opened seemed for the positive-scientific work after all dispensable — Descartes made the way free for a *new dogmatism*: he gave the positive sciences the freedom to stand on themselves and to leave a remainder to a supplementing metaphysics, a science just as objectivistically-dogmatic as these single sciences themselves. The reader should mark the precise indictment of Descartes: he discovered the ground but not the method, and by leaving objective science methodically unchanged he licensed the very dogmatism his radicalism should have overcome.

After Descartes, Husserl says, a new great step is first carried out by *Locke*. He is the first who seeks from the Cartesian *cogito* the way to a *science of the cogito*, and the first who raises the methodical demand to carry out a *universal intuitionistic grounding of cognition and science*: namely to lead all cognition back to its intuitive primal-sources in consciousness, in inner experience, and to clarify it out of these. For all immaturity and unclarity he yet *sees* that if everything which for a subject offers itself as actuality and truth offers itself in its own consciousness-life and can offer itself only there, and if all exhibiting of right and wrong, of true and false, of probable and improbable is a accomplishment carrying itself out only in the immanence of consciousness — an accomplishment carried out by the subject and in the subject — then only a *systematic study of the consciousness-sphere*, of the realm of Cartesian immediate evidence, can bring the cognition-problems to determinate formulation and to actual solution. The naive, and in this developmental situation unavoidable, *transfer of the apperceptive habits* of objective nature- and world-consideration onto the Cartesian realm of immediate evidence leads him to the naturalistic psychologism of the *tabula-rasa* interpretation, which, as our critique showed, was already according to its type necessarily skepticism, and so cancels itself in countersense. The reader should note the balanced double verdict yet again: Locke's intuitionistic *demand* is the genuine methodological advance; his naturalized *execution* of it is the fatal relapse into skepticism.

And now there results, Husserl says, with reference to this new-kind skepticism and with regard to the fact that it could nonetheless make epoch and continuously determine the modern age up to the present, for the modern age up to the present a *picture similar to that for antiquity*. As there to Platonism and the stream of rationalist philosophy proceeding from it there ran continually parallel the stream of *skeptical schools*, so in the modern age to Cartesianism and the stream of rationalist philosophy proceeding from it the counter-stream of the *empiricist philosophies*. One can then say: on both sides the *ineradicability of skepticism* proves that the rationalism was not yet *right* rationalism — i.e. that it was not able to realize the idea of a truly rational science, one justifying itself in the full, absolute sense, and then all the more of a universally unified system of such sciences. The reader should mark the diagnostic principle: persistent skepticism is the symptom of an incomplete rationalism; only a rationalism that has fully grounded itself transcendentally would leave skepticism no foothold.

But however much truth lies in this parallelization, Husserl cautions, it is not the full truth. The ancient skepticism is constantly and consciously *negativism*, the anti-philosophy, which lets no philosophy überhaupt — and that includes no objective one überhaupt — count as genuine, and declares none principially possible. It has no sphere of positive cognition and work; it knows no true method, unless its *technique of constructing skeptical paradoxes*. Of this only the empiricism of the later medical empiricists is to be excepted, which however little determined the total picture of ancient philosophy. Otherwise it stands with the modern empiricism, Husserl says, and here the comparison breaks in a decisive way. Setting aside the isolated great phenomenon Hume, the empiricism did *not want* to be negativism, and did not even want to be skepsis. Even the later imitators of Hume, the philosophers of the "as if," did not want to take from objective science its honor, but only to interpret it in the right way. Indeed Hume himself finds it wholly reason-less, but he does not want to surrender it. The reader should mark the difference Husserl insists on: modern empiricism, unlike ancient negativism, is a *constructive* enterprise that means to secure science, not to destroy it — and this is precisely why it is fruitful despite its countersense.

However much countersense may stick in the empiricist philosophy of the Lockean *tabula-rasa* type, Husserl grants, and all in all skeptical consequence, it is yet a theory of cognition and psychology that *has a method* and in its method really *does something*. It is not simply empty construction, it is no concept-scholasticism. The empiricist is thoroughly directed on concretely graspable problems and on their actual settling through self-grasping work. He really has something in his hands, and his work is not quite without fruit; something shapes itself for him in his hands — and therefore one can *learn* something from Locke and his successors; one always sees what they see, and *that* they see something, and that something shapes itself thereby, in executing work. The reader should register Husserl's respect for the empiricist's genuine descriptive labor: however misframed, it works on real phenomena and brings real connections to sight, which is why it remains instructive.

But how does that accord with our critique, one may ask. Husserl answers: the great thing — and not merely factually epoch-making but *abidingly* significant — is the *first breakthrough of the method of an intuitionism*, precisely the discussed principle of going back to the primal-sources of intuition, of evidence, and that in a systematic procedure, clarifying all cognition out of these primal-sources. Here the *insight* is determining that under the title *ego cogito* there is a self-enclosed realm of all primal-sources, the realm of the uniquely absolutely self-given and immediately evident, which must become the *primal-field of all studies*. In this *formal* respect consists the *abiding right of empiricism*. Even if it is incapable of understanding consciousness purely as consciousness and, as we expressed it, of *questioning* it itself according to essence and intentional accomplishments — even if it naturalizingly misinterprets and substitutes naturalistic constructions for the really seen — it yet moves *de facto* in the large and whole on the *ground it has itself chosen*, and for all misinterpretations and even malicious constructions, *seen connections* are the underlay. They become visible too for everyone who reads attentively. Only, to be sure, they are never *scientifically grasped*, described in their pure sense and sense-connection, and treated in the method demanded by the essence of intentionality. The reader should mark the exact location of empiricism's permanent merit and permanent failure: it correctly identifies the field (the *ego cogito* as the primal-source of all evidence) but cannot work it with the intentional method, so its real findings remain scientifically uncaptured.

Only so, Husserl says, are intelligible the enormous force of modern empiricism and the unremitting attempts to better it, to bring about through it a really scientific psychology and theory of cognition. And so the *preponderant interest* that Locke and his school have for us becomes intelligible. It is intelligible because we are on the way, from ever-new sides and on the historical-critical material, to show that the *sense of philosophy itself* and of the method demanded by this sense *drives toward an intuitionism* — but that the true method, the genuine intuitionism, is *not* that of Locke, nor that of the immanent philosophy issuing from him (such a one ends necessarily in skepticism and countersense), but the *intuitionism of transcendental phenomenology*: that science of the *ego cogito*, or as we also said, that *egology* which takes the *ego* and *cogito* and *cogitatum* exactly as it really gives itself in intuition — the livingly streaming consciousness-life and the in-it-conscious in its concrete life-fullness — and now forms the pure methods of *intentional analysis* and of the *disclosure of hidden intentionalities*, which proceed in absolute and undaunted prejudice-lessness and in every step *cover themselves with the purely intuited, with the absolutely indubitably given*. The reader should mark the precise self-definition of phenomenology against its empiricist forerunner: same intuitionistic principle, same field, but now worked through with rigorous intentional analysis and the disclosure of hidden (anonymous, functioning) intentionalities, in unbroken self-givenness.

Radical critique of the empiricism of inner experience is therefore, Husserl concludes, *more* than an otherwise philosophical critique. It is a critique that, showing what the empiricist really has before his eyes in absolute givenness and what he on the other side *substructs*, frees us from the objectivistic prejudices that make us blind for the specifically *transcendental* — for the pure subjectivity and the life and accomplishing carrying itself out under the title of pure consciousness, in which all possible objectivity appropriates to itself sense and being for a possible I. Precisely thereby the critique opens the way to that accomplishment of the overcoming of empiricist (as of all) skepsis that we earlier indicated with the words that to overcome a radical skepticism means to *make it true in a good sense*. The making-true itself, in the fullest sense, says of course: the really executing work, whose method and horizons the critique could make visible only in general. But already the critique makes empiricism true insofar — and in a higher sense than, say, a critical transition from the Sophistic to Socrates or Descartes makes these true — as it helps the *empirical intuitionism as intuitionism to its true right*, and so defends empiricism, as it were, *against itself*, lets its true I and its leading-idea come to word, or prepares out of the *pseudo-empiricism the true and genuine empiricism*. With this the lecture closes, having established the positive historical significance of the empiricist skepticism as a veiled intuitionism whose leading-idea, rightly redeemed, is phenomenology itself. The reader should register the generous hermeneutic principle governing the whole Third Section: the critique aims not to defeat empiricism but to extract its buried truth — the genuine intuitionism it half-saw and betrayed. The Twenty-first Lecture turns to Berkeley's discovery — and naturalistic misinterpretation — of the problem of the constitution of the real world.

### **Twenty-first Lecture: Berkeley's discovery and — naturalistic misinterpretation of the problem of the constitution of the real world**

To lead our idea-historical considerations toward their conclusion, Husserl says, I have first to speak of the development of the Lockean psychologism into a *purely immanent philosophy*. The contradiction-full manner in which Locke presupposed as pre-givennesses the existence of a transcendent world, the new natural science, the among-the-natural-scientists-handed-down interpretation of subjective and objective qualities of the sensuously intuitive things — while he yet on the other side laid out his intuitionism upon a methodical analysis of the pure givennesses of inner experience — had to *excite offense*. For in this intuitionism lay, in itself and also for the time, the new, significant thing of the Lockean work. Immediately given are only our own "ideas," is only the field of our immediately evident inner experience; that must therefore be the original field of scientific-psychological research, and so also of the scientific clarification of all cognition-problems. That was illuminating. At the *naturalization of consciousness* no one could take offense; it corresponded to the natural thought-pull. So illuminating too was the further methodical procedure of Locke: to view the givennesses of inner experience toward their *elements* and subject them to a *genetic origin-analysis* — the complex stocks appearing in the developed consciousness must be genetically built up out of the elements and thereby become at once descriptively and developmentally intelligible. The reader should mark the inner logic Husserl is reconstructing: granted the intuitionistic premise (only one's own ideas are given) plus the naturalization (those ideas are data on a tablet), the program of immanent genetic analysis follows naturally — and presses, with consistency, toward a purely immanent philosophy.

It was then properly self-evident, Husserl continues, that the consciousness-transcendent objectivity, the outer reality, came in this intuitionistic-genetic method into question only as *inner phenomenon of the tabula rasa* — hence not as reality itself, but as experience-content, as sensuous appearance. And again: if only this phenomenon is originally alone given and evident, and all cognition (even that lying in experience) is first, according to its possibility, to be explained through such inner analysis, then *no objectivity might be presupposed*. So that lay in the horizon of every reader who places himself on the ground of the intuitionistic method and thinks principially clearly. So in Locke's work itself the *tendency toward a purely immanent philosophy* was laid out; in many broad single expositions it comes already in Locke clearly to light — so in his express doctrine that cognition, as becoming-aware of a truth, is to be defined only as *perception of the agreement or non-agreement of our own ideas*, wherein lay that a cognition of principially transcendent is unthinkable — with which Locke's doctrine of *transcendence* would, to be sure, accord little. The reader should mark the internal contradiction Husserl pinpoints: Locke's official definition of knowledge (the perception of relations among one's own ideas) already entails that nothing transcendent could be known, flatly contradicting his simultaneous assumption of a transcendent material world.

It lay therefore very near, Husserl says, to *methodically purify* the Lockean intuitionism and, under strict switching-off of transcendent presuppositions, to think through the problematic of the cognition of the transcendences under exclusive presupposition of the immanent givennesses — to be sure, in the *naturalistic* attitude, since the time was not yet to see consciousness as consciousness and to be able to exercise intentional method. Here now *Berkeley* sets in, one of the most radical and in fact most ingenious philosophers of the modern age. The modern empiricist theory of cognition and the modern psychology venerate in him their greatest pathbreaker; but the best of his spirit — that which lies *beyond* his admittedly admirable further-formations of the naturalistic inner-analyses of Locke — the modern age has, as it might seem to me, not been able to grasp. The reader should register the double estimate Husserl is preparing: Berkeley as the celebrated founder of immanent empiricism, but also as the bearer of a deeper insight (the restitution of natural experience) that his admirers missed.

Of the for-its-time admiration-worthy critique — one touching the principial, though not principially exhausting it — that Berkeley exercised on the Lockean doctrine of the material substances and their primary qualities, Husserl recalls, we have already spoken earlier. It serves Berkeley for the grounding of the first immanent — though naturalistic — *theory of the material world*. In one with this, and more generally spoken, it is the *first systematic attempt to make theoretically comprehensible the constitution of the real world* (the physical and animalic-human) in cognizing subjectivity. Indeed even the *problem* was properly first seen by Berkeley, even if in a merely primitive beginning-shape. To be sure, a germ-form of the problem lies already in the Cartesian *Meditations*, insofar as their first task is to show how the *ego* comes, from the immediately evident domain of its *cogitationes*, to the belief in a transcendent objectivity, in an outer world and God. But however much here the beginning of the whole new theory of cognition and the germ also of the constitutive problem lies, there yet *fails the insight* that here it is first of all a matter of taking the immediate consciousness-field itself into systematic work, of questioning it itself and, out of it itself — insofar as it alone, in its phenomena, makes conscious an outer world — fathoming the *sense* of this outer world. The reader should mark the precise advance credited to Berkeley over Descartes: Descartes posed the question how the ego reaches belief in a transcendent world, but Berkeley first saw that one must interrogate the immanent field itself to fathom the *sense* of "outer world" — the genuine constitutive question.

On this way Locke's intuitionism leads, Husserl says, when it is precisely purely grasped, and only when it is. As soon as Berkeley produces the pure inner attitude, he forthwith intuits the problem and attempts its solution. In ingenious boldness he *restitutes the right of natural experience*. Purely immanently, taken as experience of the *ego*, the outer experience gives itself as experience of the *outer world itself*. The seen, heard, sensuously grasped gives itself as *nature itself, it itself, originaliter* — and not any depictions. Perception does not *substruct*, makes no inferences. The reader should mark this as the genuine kernel of Berkeley Husserl wants to rescue: against Locke's image-theory and inference to the transcendent, Berkeley sees that perception gives the thing itself, not a copy — the very insight (perception as self-giving of the transcendent) that the Seventeenth Lecture established as phenomenologically decisive.

On the other side, Husserl says, Berkeley remains *caught in the naturalism* of the *tabula-rasa* conception. *Sensualistically* he confuses the respectively perceived thing in its evidence of perceivedness with the respective *complex of sensation-data* — of visual, tactual, acoustic and other data — without becoming aware that the in the *continuity* of perceiving evidently given *identical* thing, precisely as evidently identical, cannot possibly *be* the constant change of the sensuous data. To him as to all sensualists, indeed all psychologists of the naturalistic school, escapes the evident and within immanence to be grasped *distinction between the change of the modes of appearance* — the steadily shifting aspects, which already concerns every single thing-mark — and the *appearing thing itself* and its appearing marks, and indeed purely as appearing. If one lets these adumbrating appearances count as sensation-data, then the Berkeleyan thesis says that the experienced world of the cognizer is nothing other than his respective sensation-complexes — that he substitutes, for the unity of the experienced thing, the *manifold of its adumbrating appearances*; which hangs together precisely with this, that he is *blind for the thing-consciousness as unity-consciousness* and for the consciousness-synthesis ruling in continuous experiencing and for the experienced thing itself as *synthetic unity in the continuity of the manifold meaning*. The reader should mark the exact diagnosis: Berkeley's *esse est percipi* collapses the perceived thing into the flux of sensations precisely because he lacks the concept of synthetic unity — he cannot see that the identical thing is given *across* the changing adumbrations as their synthetic pole, not as their sum.

Husserl reinforces this in a meaning-bearing editorial footnote that is worth folding in, since it makes the confusion exact. The confusions are old, he notes, as on the other side they ineradicably still serve today's psychology and theory of cognition; perhaps therefore a word of explanation is useful. *Hobbes* (as afterward also Locke) identifies the *perception-thing* — the substrate-unity of perceived properties — with the complex of sensual data becoming visible at any time in sensation-reflection, a complex that "has" its single data as *collective elements*, but nothing less than as *properties*. It is sharply to be kept in eye that already *every single property*, in the continuity of its perception, is given as *evident synthetic unity*. The perceived thing-color that I see *unchanged*, while I normally move the eyes, step nearer, is precisely seen *as this identical one*, while the sensation-data that present this same color from moment to moment — the adumbrations of the color-perspective — constantly change. In the *change of attitude*, in the passage from the genuine \[thing\] to the perceived thing and the perceived properties, to the perspectival presentations of the same, this connection — and as a *necessary* one — becomes evident. The reader should hold this as the precise phenomenological correction of sensualism: even a single perceived quality (this color) is an identity given across a continuous manifold of changing sensation-data; the thing is not the data but the synthetic unity exhibited through them.

What gives *experience-unity* to the in-themselves-separated data of the different sense-genera — color, tones — is therefore, for the sensualist, *mere association*. The things *are* nothing other than associative complexes that habitually point to one another, that empirically regularly appear in coexistence and succession in sensuous experience. Already for Berkeley, Husserl notes, the *causality of nature reduces to merely habitual expectations*. Association is the principle of all experience-inferences; infer can I so from immanent data to immanent data, never to transcendent, to unperceivable. As a transcendent nature, as a realm of transcendent material existences in a transcendent space, is a *fiction* and reduces only to the experienced nature — which is nothing other than the immanent, associatively unified sensation-complexes — so the *nature-lawfulness* reduces to an *inductive lawfulness* of these complexes, and ultimately to an associative regulation in the coming and going of the sensual data in consciousness. Here there is no proper *effecting* and being-effected, no proper causality, only regular *antecedence* for a regular, inductively to be expected, *following*. Actual causality is only *I-causality*. The reader should mark the terminus of Berkeley's naturalism: nature dissolves into associatively regulated sensation-complexes, causality into habitual expectation — and the only causality left standing is that of the I (the active ego), a point that will lead, in the next lecture, to Berkeley's monadological motif. With this the lecture closes, having shown Berkeley as at once the discoverer of the constitution-problem and the restorer of perception's self-giving right, yet the captive of a sensualism that, blind to synthesis, can only deliver the immanent philosophy over to association and habit. The Twenty-second Lecture pursues Berkeley's *monadological approach*, the connection to Leibniz, and the transition to Hume.

### **Twenty-second Lecture: Berkeley's monadological approach; comparison with Leibniz. Transition to Hume**

Husserl resumes with the consequence of Berkeley's reduction of nature to sensation-complexes. Sense-data, sense-complexes are only thinkable as *perceived*, as conscious in a subject; it has no sense to assume for them proper material substances, whose unthinkability already Locke's *je ne sais quoi* intimates. They need for their being only the *spirit* that is conscious of them. On the other side — the spirit itself is *substance*; it is the only thinkable being that is self-standingly for itself, and its being is to have consciousness, to perceive, and on the other side to be *active*, to exercise genuine causality. The *true actuality reduces to spirits*. The reader should mark the inner movement of Berkeley's system: having dissolved material substance into perceived data, he retains *spiritual* substance as the one self-standing being, whose two essential functions are perceiving and active (genuine) causality — and this prepares his monadological turn.

But whence do I know, Husserl has Berkeley ask — I to whom immediately only my own idea-field is given — of the being of *other I-subjects*? Is it not perverse to want to deny the possibility of a transcending of the idea-field in favor of a transcendent nature, and on the other side yet to admit transcendent, namely in the form of foreign I's? But the inference to *spirits outside me* has, Husserl notes, quite other foundations than that to a material transcendence and is subject to no serious misgivings. Berkeley treats this problem of the *spiritual outer world* unfortunately not in the almost exclusively heeded *Treatise*, but in the *Dialogues between Hylas and Philonous*. His thought-course, very worthy of interest, Husserl presents here in free (somewhat sharpened) manner. As things can indicate one another associatively-inductively, so they can also indicate specifically I-ly things — I-acts, I-meanings, judgments; in this respect too habitual conjunctions and expectations can form. Certain *sense-things* in my consciousness-field can now, by virtue of the *resemblance* with the thing I call my *body*, indicate spiritual things, I-acts, subjective experience-connections that are *not mine*. My body, as continually remaining in my consciousness-field, is intimately associated with my spiritual life; comprehensibly a thing that resembles it sufficiently in its kind and behavior will indicate an *analogous soul-life*. The inference is quite intelligible and goes not to an unknowable but *from analogue to analogue*. The reader should mark that Berkeley here reaches, in naturalistic form, the very structure of *empathy* (the analogizing apperception of the foreign subject by way of bodily resemblance) that the Sixteenth Lecture identified as the only way to transcend one's own sphere.

With this, Husserl continues, that I have foreign subjects given with reference to their bodies — co-given in the manner of empirical indication — this too is indicated: that the foreign subjects have *their* sensuous perceptions, that they experience just such complexes of sense-things as I, that they have "one and the same" world or nature "in common" with me. But that, Husserl stresses, is only a *façon de parler*: in *substantial actuality* there is only me and the others, and each of us has his perceptions, his sensuous complexes; in each his complexes are inductively ordered and recognizable as nature-order — only that, *wonderfully*, as the mutual understanding of the subjects teaches, in all is constituted *the one and wholly alike nature*, with the alike sensation-complexes and orders. The creator of this wonderful *intersubjective order*, of the all-common nature, is *God*, upon whom precisely from this fact one must infer — and therewith we have the *immanent type of a teleological proof*. The reader should mark the structural result: Berkeley's pluralism of spirits each enclosed in its own perceptions cannot itself ground the *one common nature*; that unity must be referred to God's institution — a theological substitute for the constitutive account of intersubjective world-constitution that Berkeley lacks.

However primitive this whole theory is, Husserl says, however much it lacks an into-the-detail-going and scientifically careful execution, it is yet a *first theory of transcendence on the ground of immanence* — the first attempt to determine scientifically the *sense of the experienced world* out of the necessary theoretical demands that the immanently running experiences themselves yield, and *purely* out of them themselves. Therein, Husserl says, we also see the *essential difference* of the Berkeleyan theory from the contemporaneous Leibnizian *monadology*. As nearly related as they are in result, so different are they in their layout and their grounding-manner. The reader should mark the comparison that follows, which is one of the lecture's chief contributions: a like-sounding result (reality reduces to spirits/monads) reached by utterly different routes — Leibniz's metaphysical-interpretive, Berkeley's immanent-descriptive.

Leibniz's monad-doctrine, Husserl explains, has the style of a *metaphysical interpretation* of mathematical natural science and of the material nature determining itself, in its exact theories, in truth — in natural-scientific truth. For Leibniz it is a matter of *reconciling* this natural-scientific truth with the religious and theological truth — of bringing the by-natural-science-determined sense of nature, this mechanics of the atoms, into accord with the by-religion-demanded *teleological* sense of all worldly, hence also all natural, being and happening. Leibniz gives in his monadology an ingenious *aperçu*; he finds the possibility of reconciliation through a *spiritual interpretation of nature* that interpretively underlays the natural-scientific sense an inner *monadological* sense and gathers arguments for it. Berkeley, on the other side — the Bishop of Cloyne — is naturally also theologically interested, even exclusively theologically, and not, like Leibniz, two-sidedly, theologically and natural-scientifically; for he is by no means also a natural researcher. But the *new thing* in him is that he does *not* metaphysically and theologically interpret, but gives a piece of *presuppositionless systematic-scientific investigation*, for which the theological interests originally determining for it are quite irrelevant. The reader should mark the decisive contrast: Leibniz interprets nature metaphysically to reconcile science and religion; Berkeley, whatever his theological motives, actually carries out a presuppositionless descriptive analysis of the immanent field — and this methodological difference, not the similar result, is what matters phenomenologically.

The critique of Locke, Husserl says, gives Berkeley the *purely immanent ground*, and on it he attempts a *descriptive and genetic exhibition* of the purely immanent peculiarities of outer perception — peculiarities in which the at-any-time evidently exhibitable sense of the experienced and experienceable nature *as such* is immanently enclosed. He therefore does not interpret and substruct, but *exhibits*, and seeks further to show that on this immanent sense of outer experience *all natural science refers* — that the things it cognizes are no others than the really and directly perceived, and that thereby all scientific procedure of the natural researchers can be satisfied. Here first, Husserl says, there marks itself out — as we already intimated in advance, even if in all-too-primitive and with naturalistic countersense afflicted form — the *problem of a constitutive theory of externality as phenomenon of the purely in-itself-enclosed consciousness-inwardness*. This problem lay near enough to Leibniz; it lay in a certain measure in his field of sight. But the *central philosophical significance* of this problem he probably did not see; the idea of a systematic and strictly scientific research of the pure consciousness-sphere and of the sense-givings carrying themselves out in its pure immanence does not become for him the driving-force of his philosophizing. The reader should mark the precise location of Berkeley's superiority over the far greater metaphysician: Leibniz had the constitutive problem in view but did not grasp its centrality or make the pure consciousness-sphere his work-field, whereas Berkeley, for all his crudeness, actually began that work.

In the Berkeleyan *purification* of the confused Lockean approaches to an immanent intuitionism, Husserl says, lies the *first germ* of a new-kind consciousness-science, which as science of pure consciousness necessarily *separates itself from the entire traditional psychology as objective world-science*, however much every psychology has to do with consciousness-experiences. Yet Berkeley himself has not yet carried out a systematic working of this pure consciousness-science — indeed not even a staking-out of its full idea as *fundamental science for all cognition and science überhaupt* — although his *Treatise*, his *Dialogues*, and also his ingenious first writing on the theory of seeing (the latter with some necessary modifications) prepare the idea of this science and order themselves to it as primitive preliminary estimates. The reader should register the careful measure: Berkeley provides the *germ* and *preliminary estimates* of a pure consciousness-science but not its full idea or systematic execution — that step belongs to Hume, and its true realization to phenomenology.

The *completer* of Berkeley, but far surpassing him in immanent naturalism, is *David Hume*. His unique significance in the history of philosophy lies first in this: that he intuits in Berkeley's theories and critiques the *breakthrough of a new-kind psychology* and recognizes in it the *ground-science for all possible sciences überhaupt*; further, that he seeks to bring this science systematically to execution — using the work done by Berkeley and, in impure form, partly also by Locke — and indeed in the *style of an immanent naturalism of sharpest consequence*. Precisely thereby Hume has grounded a *radical psychologism of an essentially new type*, which grounds all sciences on psychology, but on a *purely immanent and at the same time purely sensualistic* psychology. The reader should mark the formula that governs the reading of Hume: a psychologism that is at once purely immanent (working only with the data of the *tabula rasa*) and purely sensualistic (admitting only impressions and their faint copies) — and so the consistent terminus of the whole Lockean line.

Precisely in this decisive point, Husserl says, Hume has always been *misunderstood*. One has understood, so to speak, nothing of his theories if one understands by the Humean psychology the psychology in the ordinary sense of an *objective science of the soul-life of the human being in the objective world*. To be sure, Hume's own talk is misleading, and he never carried out the necessary contrasting; but one must *read out* the sense of his psychology from his almost wholly consequent methodical procedure, and interpret it moreover out of the historical connection. If one reads in the preface of his *Treatise* that the doctrine of man is the only firm foundation for the other sciences, or reads that there is no question of significance whose solution is not included in the doctrine of man, and none can be decided with any certainty so long as we have not become acquainted with this science; if it further says that in the laying-clear of the principles of human nature the complete system of the sciences is enclosed — and if one reads also other propositions confirming that no conceivable science is to remain here excluded, and even the last grounding of mathematics and natural science is to ensue through the doctrine of man — if one reads, Husserl says, all that, then one stands before a *crass anthropologism* that seems to leave room for no other interpretation. The reader should mark the interpretive crux: Hume's own self-presentation as a "science of man" invites an anthropological reading, which Husserl is about to overturn.

And yet, Husserl continues, if one studies the memorable work itself more closely — and partly looks to the *method*, in which nothing further than occurrences in the sphere of perceptions is presupposed or theoretically established, and on the other side to the *results*, through which not only the transcendent physical nature but *all objective world*, with all the categorial forms belonging to it, is to be proven as a *fictive formation* in the perceptional sphere — then it is yet clear that all that is anything but an ordinary psychology: hence nothing less than an experiential science on the ground of the as-existing-given and as-existing-let-count spatio-temporal real world. A science that *proves* that the whole world — including the humans, the human souls, the persons, personal associations — is nothing but *fictions* can be no science of the human being and the human soul in the ordinary sense, no science that *presupposes* the experiential actuality of humans. Such a nonsense cannot be imputed to a Hume, and it lies also not in his work itself. The reader should follow the decisive argument: Hume's results (the whole objective world, including man, is a fiction constituted in the perceptual sphere) are flatly incompatible with reading his "science of man" as an objective psychology that presupposes real humans — the content of the doctrine refutes the anthropological self-description.

In truth, Husserl declares, this Humean psychology is *the first systematic attempt at a science of the pure consciousness-givennesses* — I would say, it is the attempt at a *pure egology*, if Hume had not also set up the *I* itself as a mere fiction. It is a *tabula-rasa-psychology* that, in radical abstinence, wants to utilize nothing but the immanent findings of the *tabula rasa* — hence only the immediately evident consciousness-stocks — and now seeks, in this domain (hence in the sensualistically interpreted domain of the *ego cogito*), *psychological laws* by which psychological explanation is to be won. We can also say, Husserl continues, it is the *first systematic and universal project of the concrete constitutive problematic*, the *first concrete and purely immanent theory of cognition*. At most we could also say: Hume's *Treatise* is the *first project of a pure phenomenology* — but in the shape of a *purely sensualistic and empirical phenomenology*. The reader should mark this striking and carefully qualified verdict, which is the hinge of the whole Third Section: Hume's *Treatise* is, in its method and ambition, the first pure phenomenology — the first attempt to constitute the entire objective world out of the immanent givennesses alone — yet vitiated throughout by the sensualism (only impressions and copies, the I a fiction) that turns it into the completion of skepticism rather than the grounding of science. With this the First Chapter of the Third Section closes. The Second Chapter, opening with the Twenty-third Lecture, takes up *Hume's positivism — the completion of skepticism and at once the decisive preparatory step to a transcendental ground-science* — beginning with Hume's nominalistic reduction of all ideas to impressions and the countersense in this principle.

## **Second Chapter: Hume's Positivism — the Completion of Skepticism and at the Same Time the Decisive Preparatory Step to a Transcendental Ground-Science**

### **Twenty-third Lecture: Hume's nominalistic reduction of all ideas to impressions and the countersense in this principle**

Only in *Hume*, Husserl begins, does sensualism come to fully-conscious and universal unfolding. Berkeley had been sensualist only in the interpretation of the intuitions of outer nature: things are complexes of sensual data given in the immanence of consciousness itself, the transcendent material things are fictions, there are no material substances. But the sensuous perceptions presuppose, according to Berkeley, the *perceiving subject, the I*; for Berkeley the I is *not* a mere designation for the collective of, say, merely associated psychical experiences. Rather, all sensuous perceptions, but also all other subjective happenings, all I-acts and \-states, have *in the I as spiritual substance* a principle of unity. But precisely that Hume denies. What do I find before me, reflectively, as consciousness-wise exhibitable, whenever I reflect on myself? Perceptions of heat and cold, light and shadow, love and hate — but something like an I, a particular "impression" corresponding to this word, I cannot find. *I*, that is nothing further than a *bundle* of different perceptions following one another in incomprehensible rapidity. The reader should mark the consistent terminus: Berkeley retained the I as spiritual substance, the one self-standing being; Hume, applying the sensualist principle without remainder, dissolves even the I into a bundle of impressions, since no impression of the I can be found.

Consequently there lies here, Husserl notes, afterward a great *problem* for Hume himself: how does it come that I hold myself for an identical I, that everyone apprehends himself not merely as a heap of experiences but as one and the same *person*, given this constant change of the perceptions? But in any case, as the bodily substances — as the unities underlying the sensuous data — so the *spiritual unities*, the unities underlying the totality of psychical experiences, are *struck out*. The soul can now not even be compared with a *tabula rasa*, or with a stage on which all sorts of fleeting psychical shapes appear; for to this tablet, this stage, *nothing real corresponds* — it is not a thing in which the experiences are, but the only connecting thing is the *lawfulness* that regulates the psychical experiences purely materially, according to coexistence and succession. The reader should mark how far Hume drives the naturalization past even Locke: Locke kept the tablet (a quasi-space holding the data); Hume removes even the tablet, leaving only data and the laws regulating their sequence — a pure atomism of consciousness.

So the sensualism of world-interpretation, Husserl says, has attained its full extension and closedness. All being — the being of bodies as of spirits — reduces to *psychical data, to heaps of I-less perceptions* (the editorial note refers to Beilage XIV, Hua VII p. 350). The analogy with the atomistic-mechanistic nature-apprehension of the beginning exact natural science leaps to the eyes: the physical nature is thought as a temporal-spatial together of for-themselves-existing *atoms*, spanned only by the unity of the nature-lawfulness that unequivocally regulates all physical happening, all atom-movements. So the consciousness-naturalism dissolves subjectivity in similar manner into *consciousness-atoms*, into last material elements under merely material laws of coexistence and succession. These consciousness-atoms are the *perceptions* (which in Hume says the same as "ideas" in Locke); to the outer nature-laws correspond here the inner laws of *association and habit* and some laws closely connected with them of similar type. But these are not properly opposing parallel laws; rather the psychical laws are the *true radical laws of all being*: to perceptions and formations out of perceptions, by virtue of the inner-psychical laws, all and every being reduces, with all belonging and supposedly self-standing lawfulness. The reader should mark the totalizing claim: for Hume the laws of association are not one regional set among others but the ultimate laws of all being whatever, since all being is reducible to perception-complexes governed by them.

Yet Hume does not *presuppose* this, Husserl stresses, but *proves* it — precisely by means of a systematically progressing and presuppositionless psychology that starts from the immediate psychical givennesses and establishes empirically the basic laws subsisting for them (law of association, of re-memory) as *primal-laws of all inner-psychical genesis*. Everything that is there for the respective subject under the title experience-world of bodies and spirits — all the well-familiar objective forms space, time, causality, thing, force, faculty, person, community, state, right, morality — must be *explained* through this psychology, just as method and accomplishment of all sciences that pretend to cognize this whole world or single world-domains. To be sure, the result of this deepest psychological explanation of all being and all sciences runs: the whole world with all objectivities is *nothing but a system of pseudo-formations, of fictions* that necessarily grow in subjectivity according to immanent psychological laws; and science is a *self-deception of subjectivity*, or an art of organizing fictions usefully for life-purposes. The reader should mark the skeptical terminus stated baldly: Hume's "science of man," consistently carried through, proves the whole objective world and science itself to be fiction — the completion of skepticism announced in the chapter title.

It is now necessary, Husserl says, to take the supposed presuppositionlessness and radical materiality, and überhaupt the whole methodical form of the Humean psychology and theory of cognition, a little more closely into view. First we notice that every *fundamental-consideration* — such as Descartes holds necessary for the systematic grounding of a philosophy — *wholly fails*. For Descartes it was so great an affair that he attempted it in ever-new shapes, as his *Discours*, his *Meditationes*, his *Principia* on the one side and his posthumous writings on the other prove. Since philosophy is to become an absolutely self-justifying universal science, the fundamental-consideration wants to be the *grounding meditation* that weighs the procedure of a universal and absolute cognition-justification — as one systematically spanning all scientific and überhaupt genuine cognition — and projects and justifies the necessary procedure as such. Such a radical *Besinnung* about the method of last grounding fails, as said, in Hume; and his radicalism is therefore *not* that genuine radicalism that means last self-responsibility out of last self-reflection and self-clarification. The reader should mark the precise charge: Hume is radical in execution but not in foundation — he never subjects his own starting point (the evidence of immediate givennesses, the empiricist principle) to the radical self-examination that genuine philosophy requires.

The evidence of the immediate givennesses — namely the respectively findable own experiences — is, Husserl says, a heritage *taken over as self-evident* and not itself acquired in careful critique; and likewise the empiricist principle of the grounding of all cognition through experience. The sense of this principle determines itself out of the Lockean intuitionism of *clarification*. In an impressive *pseudo-clarity* the principle presents itself in Hume in the methodical form of the *reduction of all ideas to impressions*. *Impressions* are the originally living and forceful perceptions, of which, after they are past, fainter after-images, depictions, reproductively recur — the by Hume so-called *ideas*. In that they mix with one another, are conjoined in so-called thinking into new ideas, there now grow ideas, thoughts, that *seem* like depictions, while they as these formations do not themselves stem from original impressions and are also not to be referred to possibly afterward demonstrable actual impressions. Therein lies, for Hume, the *source of all errors*, of all perversities of a matter-far thinking. To exercise cognition-critique says accordingly: to investigate all our thoughts, our "ideas," as to whether and how far original impressions correspond to them, are demonstrable for them. The reader should mark the structure of Hume's criterion: a concept is legitimate only if a corresponding impression can be exhibited — the sensualist coarsening of Locke's intuitionistic demand to redeem every concept in its intuitive origin.

Unmistakably authoritative here is, in crudest sensualistic coarsening, Husserl says, the opposition guiding the Lockean intuitionism as all empiricism: between *unclear, matter-far meanings* — empty, even if artificial thought-formations, such as the speculations entangling themselves in scholastic word-subtleties — and over against them *clear intuitions*, judgments saturated with clear intuition-fullness, judgments that assertingly bring to expression exactly what they have intuitively self-given. All actual *confirming* of meanings must consist in *measuring* them against self-giving intuitions, in making them evident. Here, Husserl says, an immanent cognition-psychology that seriously wanted to clarify cognition theoretically would need a careful *description* of all that this principial basic-apprehension (which is after all nothing other than the laying-out of the cognition-accomplishment itself) presupposes and makes valid in essence-pieces: the description of the *synthesis of clarification and confirmation*, of justification and its negative counterpart, dis-justification; it would need the exact description of the synthetic consciousness-passage in which an unclear thought appropriates fulfilling clarity and the right or correct confirming it — how it now "itself" has the very same that it before "merely meant," and has it exactly as it meant it, with respect to all articulations and forms of this meaning; or, in the opposite case, how the *dis-justification* looks, how here a meaning comes up against an intuition to which it points and which yet does not fit it but cancels it. But all that would naturally have to be preceded by a careful description of the at-first only vaguely designating titles "mere meaning," empty thought, empty word-concept — and over against them "intuition." The reader should mark the wholesale demand: Hume's principle of confirmation tacitly presupposes the entire phenomenology of intention and fulfillment, which he never describes — and the absence of that description is the void at the center of his method.

But of that, Husserl says, in Hume and in the further psychology and theory of cognition of sensualistic style, *nothing* is to be found. All is crudely *leveled* in the talk of impression and idea and in the demand to demonstrate corresponding impressions for all ideas. Not even to the basic-essential distinction of *idea as immanent memory-image or phantasma* and *thought* in the sense that distinguishes the in-specific-sense judging thinking and all its components does this sensualism press through. And likewise it stands with the so-called impression, in which *individually intuitive* — as it is given before all thinking-formation — is not distinguished from intuitive *with* this formation. It is the *naturalistic preoccupation* that makes blind for all the basic-essential and psychologically as epistemologically decisive — blind for that which makes being as consciousness and as in-consciousness-conscious a *toto coelo* other-kinded over against what, in natural objective world-consideration, offers itself as real thing. The reader should mark the two conflations Husserl charges: impression/idea fails to distinguish memory-image from judging thought, and fails to distinguish the bare individual datum from the same datum already categorially formed — both because the sensualist sees only thing-like data, never intentional form.

The whole distinction between impressions and ideas, Husserl argues, and the demand of a leading-back of the ideas to impressions, becomes *wholly senseless* if to these consciousness-types *psychical things* are substituted that merely have their material peculiarities. Hume and the positivism following him make, out of the *characters* impression and idea, *material marks*. His thought is: everything that is to be there for me or to count as existing must exhibit itself in my consciousness; my consciousness, the realm of the immediate findables, is a realm of immediately experienced being that must be considered in "theory-free," "metaphysics-free" materiality and accordingly is to count as a *mere thing-field*. So the difference of impression and idea is regarded as a *mere thing-difference*: experiences, tone-data, touch-data appear for the first time in primal-forceful freshness and liveliness, with material marks such as those of *intensity*; and according to material lawfulness — that of reproduction and association — there appear later, as depictions deriving from them, weaker echoes: these are the *ideas*. The reader should mark the reduction Husserl is isolating: the difference between perceiving-something and remembering-something (an intentional difference of consciousness-manner) is recast as a mere difference of *vivacity*, a thing-property of psychical data — and this recasting is the root error.

Already the first steps carried out in the *Treatise* as basic-determining are, Husserl says, methodically *countersensical*. They only feign the semblance of methodically scientific establishings. Whence comes it, e.g., that a faint-weak red-perception means *so much more* than just a faint-weak red-perception? How comes it to the assertion that it is "after-image of an earlier perception" — as though that were not something *totally new*: namely that someone experiences a present faint red as something quite other than an "earlier," and moreover not faint but lively, as an earlier "impression"? And again, how comes it that a present faint is addressed as *fore-image of a coming*? Further, how is it intelligible that a present faint counts now as memory of a strong, now as such of a weak, and so in different specifications? But there, Husserl says, we speak precisely of a *counting-as-something*, of a *meaning with this or that sense*. The reader should follow the argument: every characterization Hume needs (after-image, fore-image, memory of this rather than that) is an *intentional* characterization — a counting-as, a meaning-with-sense — none of which can be a mere material mark of a datum, so that Hume's own descriptions covertly deploy the intentionality he denies.

We also become attentive, Husserl continues, that we must do justice to the difference between *memory or expectation* and *mere phantasy* — be it of past or present or future — and that in the repetition of "ideas" that are ideas *of the same*, very different "entities" serve to present one and the same; that thereby this same presenting is consciousness-wise a *meaning of the same*, possibly of something ever more clearly stepping forth, determining and exhibiting itself. All that which is given precisely as first and in the Cartesian sense indubitable — namely given before all objective facts and hypotheses, before all to-be-substructed and explaining theories — the *presenting-as-this-and-that, holding-for-this-and-that as such*, in short, *consciousness*, falls, so to speak, under the table; and so precisely that which makes subjectivity into subjectivity, subjective life into subjective life. The reader should mark the irony driven home: Hume, who prides himself on starting from the indubitably given, discards the very thing that is most indubitably and first given — consciousness as meaning-of-something — because his sensualism cannot see it.

So *impression is a thing* and as such distinguished through material marks — and nothing less vanishes than that it is *experience of experienced, of self-given*. It vanishes, and is yet *presupposed*, already by this: that one continually pretends that these data are *given in immediate evidence*. But mere things are what they are in their material properties; as things they *are*, but they *mean* nothing, signify nothing, bear nothing in themselves of *sense* — nothing of differences between meaning and meant, of empty-presentation or self-grasping, of selfsame that is repeatedly meant and given, or at once meant and given. To want to find all such in things or as material properties is *countersense*. The reader should mark the pivotal contrast: the *being* of a thing (its material properties) and the *meaning* of a consciousness (its intentional reference and its modes of givenness) are toto coelo different, and Hume's reduction of consciousness to thing-data simply abolishes the dimension of sense.

Certainly, Husserl concedes, immanent experiences, insofar as they are considered *exclusively* as events running in the universal form of immanent time, stretching through an immanent time-stretch, have also a kind of construction out of real parts and peculiarities that makes possible a kind of *material description*. To such descriptions following the temporal course belongs surely that "ideas" tend to be more fleeting than impressions; whether one may seriously also reckon here intensity-like differences of liveliness, and whether already here a mode of *intentional intuitiveness* is not confused with a mode of *individual quasi-being* in the immanent temporality, would still be seriously to weigh. But even if it were correct, Husserl insists, how fundamentally perverse remains a description that wants to teach us that what makes *impression* into impression and *idea* into idea is nothing other than such *material moments*\! How should a relatively very faint and fleeting red-perception be more and other than just a faint and fleeting red-perception, and a strong and persisting one more than a strong and persisting one? Why in all the world already the talk of *perception*, which is so meaning-full and expresses so much more than a material thing\! And how comes specifically the one perception to be called *impression of something* and, more closely seen, to be for us consciousness of a bodily present red, and the other perception to be called *idea* and more closely *memory or expectation* and accordingly to be for us consciousness of a past red or fore-intuition of a coming red, and again, as pure phantasy, intuition of a fictionalized red, a presentified and by no means present one? What nonsense it is, Husserl exclaims, to say the impression — which perceivingly presentifies as bodily present — is merely something strong, lively, and the *hodgepodge* "idea," with such deep-reaching differences as memory, fiction, and so überhaupt presentification of so manifold kinds, is nothing other than a faint\! The reader should mark the reductio: the single word "perception" already carries the intentional reference (of something, as present, as past, as fictive) that Hume's material description cannot account for, so the description fails on its own terms.

Naturally it helps nothing at all, Husserl says, to say there are peculiar, possibly unique livelinesses or fleetingnesses or whatever else of this style. It is downright *grotesque* how here not only the Humean but the whole psychological analysis of the modern age has failed — which continually attempts *material* description where every step beyond the formal of time-distribution at once leads into *intentional analyses*, in which so much is to be intuited and fixed in a spiritual sense that one must really say the psychology, for sheer psychological *trees*, does not see the *forest*. It helps here very little, Husserl notes, that one — like *William James*, made attentive through Brentano's first indications of the peculiarities of intentionality — seeks to do justice to this peculiarity through material "colorings," through *fringes*, "overtones," and similar simile-talk that points precisely only to material characters, however unheard-of-uniquely. One needs no images at all, Husserl insists; nothing else is needful than to *question consciousness of every kind itself as consciousness* and to hear its assertions. As soon as one does that, at one stroke and quite of itself a new-kind, and the only possible, psychology is in the making — a psychology in which the method is quite self-evidently determined by the *one great theme*, the infinitely manifoldly branching theme *consciousness, intentionality*; which does not exclude that time-distribution and material peculiarities connected with it, and especially inductive manners of consideration, must always play a certain, but only secondary, role. The reader should mark the methodological prescription: the cure for sensualism is not better images for the "fringe" but the resolve to interrogate consciousness as consciousness — to read off its intentional structure directly — with material and inductive description retained only in a subordinate place.

Supplementing, Husserl adds a clarification of what *impression* truly is, rightly understood. Impression is for Hume the epistemological title for the *intuitions appointed to the consciousness-accomplishment of evidence-confirmation*. That presupposes evidently that these have conscious *in themselves as self-given* the respective objects, conceptual essences, individual or general judgment-contents, on which the to-be-measured mere meanings can, so to speak, *saturate* themselves. Impression is therefore in truth a title for *evidence-consciousness überhaupt* or, in the widest sense, *self-intuition überhaupt*, as possible underlay for any making-evident, for any confirmation. A real thing, which as such merely *is*, confirms nothing and has in itself nothing confirming. Not the thing can confirm, but only the *self-intuition* of the thing — the perception or memory of the real can confirm, and it can because it is precisely *self-grasping of the thing*, can unite with every corresponding mere meaning of the same thing in a higher, synthetic experience in which the same is at once conscious as meant and as true, confirming itself. The reader should mark the rehabilitation Husserl performs: Hume's "impression," correctly understood, is not a strong datum but *self-giving evidence-consciousness* — the very intentional accomplishment Hume's official theory denies, smuggled back in as the unexamined ground of his whole method.

Impression, so understood as it must be understood, has therefore a *double-sidedness*, precisely as *self-giving consciousness of the self-given to it*. This double-sidedness is not a *façon de parler* but a *double direction of very content-rich descriptive moments*; and the analogous holds naturally for the counter-title "idea," as title for all confirmable and confirmation-needing meanings: each is double-sidedly consciousness of in-it-conscious, in-it-meant, but *not* self-given. So all descriptions, especially those of the syntheses, must self-evidently have this double-sidedness — of the consciousness-manner and of its meant or self-given correlate. Let us notice still this, Husserl adds: even if one does not heed the intentional relations lying in the single different-kinded experiences, their objectivities, and considers these experiences as mere temporal data in immanent time, as mere so-called sensations, it yields *countersense* if one, in the usual way, makes the immediate *having-given* of these data into a *nothing*. This immediate having is again *having-conscious*; the experiences are not in a *nowhere-home*. Their being is essentially *consciousness*, and all experiences that are mine are it in the all-spanning unity of my consciousness, and so they are accessible for the I in special reflections. The reader should mark the final point: even at the most reduced level — bare sensations in immanent time — the *givenness* of the data is itself a consciousness-having, so the sensualist cannot even secure his atoms without presupposing the consciousness he denies.

So we could, Husserl concludes, step for step exhibit *countersense*, consisting in this: that in fact consciousness-occurrences are the themes for the descriptions, that intentional accomplishments are therein constantly in talk and claimed, while all supposedly purely objective description goes upon *fixing nothing* of all these intentional occurrences — indeed principially not letting them count as something to be taken seriously and actual. So already in the *method* a principial *skepticism* is enclosed, and it is no wonder that then everything runs out upon explaining the cognition-accomplishment of the intentional life, the objective world and science, as *mere fictions*. With this the lecture closes, having shown that Hume's positivism is skeptical already in its method — that it must end in fictionalism because it disowns at the outset the intentional consciousness it cannot help using. The reader should register the chapter's governing thesis taking shape: Hume's *Treatise* is at once the completion of skepticism (all is fiction) and, in its very method and ambition, the decisive preparatory step toward transcendental phenomenology (the first universal constitutive project), the two faces inseparable. The Twenty-fourth Lecture takes up the *necessary eidetics of the science of consciousness* against Hume's inductive-empirical objectivism.

### **Twenty-fourth Lecture: The necessary eidetics of the science of consciousness and the inductive-empirical objectivism in Hume**

We have still one side of the Humean methodics to discuss, Husserl begins: it concerns the *inductive empiricism* out of which the basic concepts and the explaining basic laws of his psychology and theory of cognition are drawn. In the preface the empiricist principle is pronounced like a self-evidence about which there can be no dispute, with the words: as the doctrine of man is the only firm foundation for the other sciences, so the only sure foundation we can give to this science lies in *experience and observation*. The appeal to Bacon, Locke, and others of the more recent suffices. But here above all, Husserl says, the *lack of a radical Besinnung* avenges itself — a reflection about the method that the sense of the by-Hume-planned ground-science demands, and first of all already about the *sense of this ground-science itself*. The reader should mark that the whole lecture turns on a question Hume never posed: *why* must the science of pure subjectivity be the ground-science, and *what method* does that grounding role require — a question whose answer dictates that the method be eidetic, not inductive.

Let us consider, Husserl says: what was it that *motivated* such a ground-science? Or: what was it properly that gave to the cognition of this pure subjectivity the *precedence* for whose sake it had to precede all other cognitions and sciences and function as last-grounding for them? Further: of what kind had to be the grounding of this cognition itself — the grounding of this new-kind psychology in the field of pure subjectivity — in order to give it the precedence over all other sciences and to be able meaningfully to ground all others on it? For us, Husserl says, the answer is clear. To go back to the pure subjectivity, to the *ego cogito*, means to reflect on the *last-unquestionable, last-indubitable*, as that which for its part is presupposed in every putting-in-question and putting-in-doubt. But as soon as one has grasped this pure subjectivity, one becomes aware that it is, in its pure consciousness-experiences, the *primal-source of all sense-givings* — the primal-site in which everything objective that is to mean something for the cognizing I and to count as existing receives its meaning, receives its validity. The reader should mark the two-fold ground of the precedence: the *ego cogito* is both the last-indubitable (presupposed in all doubt) and the source of all sense and validity (everything objective gets its meaning there) — and these together require that the science of it be the absolutely first and grounding science.

That says, Husserl spells out: object is for me nothing other, and can be nothing other, than *appearing of my manifoldly changing appearances*, conscious of my manifoldly changing consciousness-experiences — of intuiting, of symbolic presenting, of thinking. Only in such subjective experiencing springs every kind of objectivity, as *sense-content* of the respective consciousness; and this objective as such counts for me, as the case may be, as existing or non-existing, as possible, as probable; then possibly also exhibits itself — again in these and those shapes of consciousness — as *actual and truly existing, non-existing, possibly existing*. In the consciousness-life itself lies the subjective "counting" (judging meaning) and the particular subjective: exhibiting itself to me as legitimately valid, objectively valid, as true actuality, or else as deception and semblance. And when then science is in question, science is yet through and through *accomplishment in the cognizing subjectivity* and receives therefore again, as science, from it — from its respective scientific cognizing — sense and truth-exhibition. The reader should mark the comprehensive constitutive thesis restated: object, actuality, validity, and science itself are all accomplishments within cognizing subjectivity, drawing their sense and their exhibited being from the consciousness-shapes in which they constitute themselves.

Here springs, Husserl says — already when one has become aware of this state of affairs in the first, still vague anticipation, in the manner of a mere fore-view — the necessary *leading-thought*: if I want to understand the accomplishment of cognition überhaupt, and especially of scientific cognition, I must study it itself in its pure peculiarity, hence at the primal-site of pure subjectivity. But to do this is something quite other than to pursue common "objective," "positive" science. For the latter a respective object-domain is *simply given*, and what it wants is to bring out theoretically the being and being-thus of this domain. What lies in this "simply given"? That is no problem of objective science. That everything pre-given to its theoretical working — the objective domain with all the objects becoming in each case special themes — is a sense *immanently constituted in the consciousness-shaping of pure subjectivity*, of that objective science knows nothing. On the other side, a science of the pure, the transcendental subjectivity, has precisely *pure consciousness überhaupt*, the constituting pure shapings, as theme. Precisely thereby it has all meant, all exhibited and exhibitable objective, *not in naive absolutization* but as *enclosed in the for-it-thematic concretion of actual and possible consciousness, as its intentional accomplishment*; and the *how* of this accomplishment, its purely subjective coming-about in these and those consciousness-manners, is its problem. The reader should mark the exact demarcation of the transcendental science from positive science: the positive sciences take their domains as simply given and never ask after that givenness; the transcendental science makes precisely the constituting of all such givenness its theme.

From here, Husserl says, the sense of the *demand of a last grounding of all cognition and sciences on that peculiar "psychology"* — i.e. on the transcendental science of the "pure" subjectivity — becomes perfectly intelligible for us; and at once intelligible is the sense of a *radical intuitionism* and its demand of a last clarification of cognition, a clarification of the basic concepts and überhaupt of all "foundations" of all sciences. For all sciences — meant naturally all *pre-transcendental* sciences, the historically grown "objective" or "positive" sciences — have, in the naivety in which they take pre-givennesses (these and those reality-domains and a whole world spanning them) and in which they shape concepts, basic principles, indeed ideal sciences, ideal object-realms, a *radical lack*. Only thereby can they become absolutely grounded sciences: that from their beginnings and grounds a *descent* ensues to the *primal-grounds, primal-beginnings*, to the true *archai*. These, however, lie one and all in the *pure consciousness*, in which everything possibly existing — according to content or sense and according to being-value of actuality and truth — constitutes itself subjectively in essentially belonging consciousness-shapings. The reader should mark the figure of the *descent to the archai*: the positive sciences begin from pre-given domains; absolute grounding requires descending beneath those beginnings to the constituting consciousness, the true principles.

So long as the sense-giving and confirming accomplishment carrying itself out (or to be carried out) in the pure consciousness itself — in which being itself and truth itself originally arise for every possible cognition-subject — is not understood; so long as consciousness is only *lived*, evidence only objectively exercised, but not itself intuited in reflective evidence and scientifically studied: so long has all science and all in it theorized objectivity an *enormous dimension of unintelligibilities*, hence of possible questionabilities and doubts. That, Husserl says, teaches impressively enough every open or hidden *skepticism*, which gains its weapons only out of, so to speak, *robber-like break-ins into the transcendental sphere*. Such, however, remains at all times possible so long as that sphere has not disclosed its own essence in reflective evidence, and disclosed it theoretically in adequate concepts and insights. The reader should mark the diagnosis of skepticism's permanent possibility: skepticism feeds on the un-clarified transcendental dimension; only its scientific disclosure removes skepticism's foothold for good.

Having in this way, in a recapitulating joining-together of the long-recognized, set before our eyes the sense of a transcendental ground-science, Husserl says, the further question also easily answers itself: in what *method* such a science is to be grounded — a method prefigured by its sense as the only possible one. From the outset noticeable is that it will be a matter of *general* clarifications about cognition and cognition-objectivity, cognition of these and those general cognition-types, related to objectivities of these and those kinds and forms. By what method, it asks itself, are *general and lawful peculiarities* of the transcendental sphere to be established? Here it is clear, Husserl says, that this whole idea of an absolute cognition-grounding would be an *empty delusion* if there failed possibilities of grounding, in indubitable manner, *general essence-peculiarities, essence-lawfulnesses* as principles of all further-to-be-accomplished clarifications. The indubitable exhibition of pure consciousness as the alleged primal-ground of all cognition-clarification can only then be serviceable for such clarification if on it an *indubitable science* — first of all a *system of absolutely indubitable immediate consciousness-truths* — can be established. So, Husserl says, would that immanent psychology have to look, which according to Hume is to function as ground-science for all other sciences, whether being-sciences or norm-sciences, real sciences or ideal. The reader should mark the conclusion the recapitulation has been building toward: the ground-science must yield *general, essential, indubitable* truths — hence it must be *eidetic*; a merely factual, inductive psychology cannot ground anything absolutely.

And now, Husserl says, we look again to Hume's *Treatise* and to the manner in which he wins basic concepts and basic laws. His naturalism consists not only in this, that he *thingifies* consciousness, as though it were something like a nature, but that he lets prevail, on the ground of the inner consciousness, a *bad empiricism* that means: what is here alone to be accomplished consists in bringing inner experience-facts to empirical concepts and then being able to set up empirical laws inductively. Naturally Hume knows very well, Husserl grants, that *inductive laws* are not to be grounded absolutely, that every induction can have only provisional validity; nay more, he knows that all inductive inferring rests on *association* (for to prove that is a famous main-piece of the work itself), and further he knows that it could carry with itself *necessity of validity* only if the principles of association themselves were necessities — or, as we can also say for that, were absolutely to be grounded. But precisely these *primal-principles of all experience-inferences*, which for him are last basic laws of his psychology überhaupt, are claimed by him merely as *inductively established*. So on an underground of immediate evidence — which however unfortunately is only an absolute evidence of merely immanent experience — *laws* are set up, instead of likewise in absolute evidence, rather in *absolute irrationality*. For absolute insightfulness the naive trust in induction is a bad substitute. The reader should mark the self-undermining structure Husserl exposes: Hume grounds all inference on the laws of association, yet establishes those very laws only by induction — hence by the inference they are supposed to ground — so the whole ground-science floats in irrationality.
