For now, Husserl says, the psychological ground-science stands wholly *in the air*; if it is not grounded in absolute evidence but in the same naivety as objective science, then the whole undertaking of last cognition-grounding loses precisely *from this primal-ground* every sense. Again, here a *last ground of the Humean skepticism* is exhibited. Implicitly the complete *irrationality of all cognition* is already presupposed therewith, that one makes the pure consciousness into a *site of mere irrationality* — that one imputes to it a lawful regulation, but such a one as never lets itself be rationally insighted: merely empirical laws, for which on this pure ground there are no absolutely insightful validity-grounds. So the Humean psychology has, after all, with the common objective psychology an essential character-trait in common, insofar as it like the latter is *inductive* psychology. But with a great difference, Husserl insists: for what for objective psychology can be thoroughly legitimate (namely if an epistemological source- and norm-doctrine can justify the right of objective inductions überhaupt out of apodictic principles), and what is then also right for an objective psychology on the ground that the psychical, in the nature-connection like everything natural, must be considered according to inductive connections — that is for a *subjective psychology, a purely immanent one, principially illegitimate, indeed countersensical*, insofar as this is to be the ground-science, science of right-grounds for all possible cognition and science. The reader should mark the asymmetry: induction is legitimate for objective psychology (which can borrow its right from a prior norm-doctrine), but ruinous for the would-be ground-science, which cannot borrow its right from anything and so must rest on absolute, eidetic evidence or on nothing.

The *prōton pseudos* (πρῶτον ψεῦδος, the first falsehood), Husserl says, lies in the *prejudice of empiricism*, which as bad intuitionism knows only experience of individual or temporal singularities as self-giving and is blind for the fact that *general* — conceptual generalities and state-of-affairs generalities — let themselves be immediately insightfully intuited, and so to speak are constantly intuited. We need in fact, Husserl says, only to point out that consciousness is self-evidently a *site of immediate essence-insights of pure generality and necessity*; we would even venture to demonstrate this for the *association-laws* themselves, which would only need a more correct purely immanent grasping. That sounds today still very paradoxical, Husserl grants, so much is one accustomed since Hume and Mill — and not merely on the empiricist side — to consider association as an empirical peculiarity of human soul-life and to parallelize the association-laws of the psychical inwardness with the gravitation-law as law of the inert masses of outer nature. The reader should mark the bold claim: even the laws of association, rightly purified, are not empirical generalizations but eidetic insights — a claim that overturns the standard parallel of psychology with physics.

Over the question, Husserl continues, one believes all-too-easily to be able to step: whether it is possible, and not rather countersensical, to ground the *last principles of the right of all inductions* themselves again *through inductions*. But at least the question, Husserl says, no one who has such views might refuse: *where* then the scientific inductions are, *who* ever carried them out, to which the association-laws owe their scientific grounding. For the gravitation-law we have the history of physics, and we know what natural-scientific labors and arrangements the carrying-through of this induction cost. Where is there the parallel for psychology? It simply *fails*, Husserl says, out of the same ground for which it also fails for the *logical and arithmetical axioms*, which a Mill likewise wanted to claim as inductive. It fails because it is a matter of *general essence-insights* that are precisely *not induced* but drawn *out of pure general intuition* — drawn as originally self-given generalities. The extreme *nominalism* that lives on in the Humean empiricism is wholly *blind against the general intuiting*; and, as we already discussed in the critique of Locke, in this blindness it attempts to *conjure away* all general thinking through substitution of *natural connections of singular singularities* — connections about which it self-evidently makes *general assertions*, after whose right to ask one must only *forget*. And precisely that, Husserl concludes, the *Treatise* really imputes to us: namely that we may not think of asking after the *reason-right* of the induction of the grounding laws. With this the lecture closes, having shown that Hume's inductive grounding of the science of consciousness is self-refuting — that the ground-science must be eidetic, resting on essence-insights (including, rightly understood, the laws of association), since induction can never ground the very principles on which induction itself rests. The reader should register the constructive yield beneath the critique: the necessity of an *eidetics of consciousness*, an a priori science of the essence-laws of the transcendental sphere, as the only possible form of the ground-science Hume sought and missed. The Twenty-fifth Lecture takes up the *constitution-problem in Hume* — and its ending in complete skepticism.

### **Twenty-fifth Lecture: The constitution-problem in Hume — but its ending in complete skepticism**

Once one has analyzed the methodical principles of Hume, Husserl begins, one would at bottom no longer need to go into his theories, whose countersense is only an unfolding of the countersense enclosed in the grounding principles. What must determine us yet to cast a glance on them is not their enormous historical effect, but the circumstance that in the question-settings for these theories there mark themselves out, *for the first time*, problems of highest philosophical dignity, which despite their naturalistic-skeptical devaluation are yet to be regarded as *pre-forms of constitutive main-problems of the new phenomenology*. In a certain way one can, to be sure, say the similar already of Locke's problematic; but only in the Berkeleyan *swing to a transcendental psychology* do they win a *transcendental face*, and in the systematic execution of such psychology through Hume a new relief and a meaningful *deepening to problems of synthetic unity*. Hume's sharp gaze intuits, on Berkeley's ingenious expositions about nature and natural science, the *lack of theoretical out-forming*; where Berkeley believes himself finished, new-kind and great problems open for him. The reader should mark the double estimate that governs the lecture: Hume's theories are skeptically worthless in result, yet the *problems* they raise — above all the synthetic unity of thing and of self — are the first transcendental formulations of phenomenology's constitutive problems.

Husserl lays out the first problem. Sense-data in consciousness *associate* into complexes; those, said Berkeley, are the things, their unity is habitual belonging-together. The complexes themselves associate, since they factually appear in empirical regularity, again with one another; we therefore expect, under similar thingly circumstances, similar consequences — and to this reduces all that we call *nature-causality*: nothing other than a relation of subjectively habitually regulated succession. But all that, Husserl says, does not suffice even for one who, like Hume, wears the sensualistic spectacles. First: things are supposed to be merely associative complexes. But however much the phenomenal things in perception point back to data of the various senses and, as Hume the sensualist acknowledges, are at first really nothing other than complexes of such data unified through association and habit, *one thing* was not at all seriously weighed and clarified by Berkeley: *how we come to see ever such a complex, given the change of its elements, as the same — now altered, now unaltered — thing*; nay more, how we come to ascribe to it an existence *independent of actual perception or non-perception*. Why is the table here identified by me as the one and same, even when I leave the room in between, since yet the remembered sensation-complex and the now newly appearing are not the same but each another and both separated from one another? Precisely this — as we would say, *synthetic* — unity, the *experienced thing itself as unity of actual and possible experiences*, falls in Berkeley *under the table*; and precisely this unity of the same thing designates one of the main problems of Hume. The reader should mark that the very phenomenon sensualism cannot accommodate — the identical thing given across the changing data — is what forces itself on Hume as a problem, the problem of synthetic unity that phenomenology will solve through the analysis of constitution.

To this joins itself for Hume, Husserl says, as parallel problem, that of the *unity of the I, of the person*. Hume had denied a proper impression for the I and shattered all subjective unity into a heap or bundle of perceptions. But everyone yet means to experience himself *as person*, similarly as he believes to experience unitary things; and on both sides these experience-unities are also supposed to *be* when they are not experienced — such a sense, that of an *in-itself-being*, we constantly ascribe to them. The reader should mark the exact parallel: thing-identity and self-identity are the two great unities that sensualism dissolves and yet cannot do without, and both pose the constitutive question of how an identical, in-itself-existing unity is given across a manifold of separated experiences.

Further, Husserl says, with the clarification of natural science as science — as cognition out of mere habit — Berkeley had made it too easy for himself. Certainly, association creates complexes of coexistence and succession. But is that *all* — and how would natural science be possible if that is all? Then there would be only inferences from habitual circumstances to habitual consequences, inferences we carry out in everyday life but do *not* regard as scientific. Can one doubt that natural science is genuine, hence by *rationality* illumined science — that its inferences have *necessity* dwelling in them, that the laws it cognizes are mathematically exact, in strict generality valid laws? How should they be mere general expressions of habitual expectations? The rationalism had most vividly represented this rational character of the new natural sciences, set them on a stage with mathematics; in any case account had to be taken of what it made valid for that. The reader should mark the third problem: the genuine rationality and necessity of mathematical natural science, which Berkeley's reduction to habit cannot explain and which the rationalists rightly insisted on — a problem Hume sharpens but cannot solve.

To be sure, Husserl recalls, Berkeley had denied that *causality* — which is the title of all experience-inferences — is a genuine causality, as which only belongs to the spirit, in spiritual effecting and generating. But even if he might be right therein, that the originally spiritual concept of effecting and force may not be animistically imputed to the material things, he yet may not *overlook the peculiar sense of rational necessity and lawfulness* that belongs to the natural-scientific concepts of cause, effect, force, force-law, and on which alone it depends for the natural scientists. Berkeley has therefore given no intelligible account of nature and natural science, since he took no regard of the basic sense in which both are generally taken: *nature* a necessity-connection in space and time, related to change and non-change of identical and in-themselves-existing things; and *natural science* precisely science, cognition of rational necessities out of apodictic principles. The reader should mark the precise deficiency: Berkeley's denial of natural causality, even where partly right, ignores the rational necessity internal to natural-scientific lawfulness — which remains a genuine problem demanding constitutive clarification, not mere dismissal.

But how is this *sense-giving* to be understood out of consciousness, Husserl asks, how does it grow in the genesis of the original consciousness? For us, who see beyond the historical barriers of that time and have before our eyes a *transcendental science of consciousness as consciousness*, it is clear that the problem of an inductive immanent-psychological explanation after the manner of a natural-scientific explanation is a *complete countersense*. But nonetheless there hides behind the perverse problem, *fore-felt and in a certain way fore-formed, the genuine and great problem* — which already as *descriptive* is to be posed for every basic kind of objects constitutive for the building of the world (and, ideally spoken, for that of a world überhaupt), and demands a powerful fullness of essence-establishings. Namely, for each of these basic kinds of objectivities — lowest for material objectivity and physical nature überhaupt — it is a matter of definitely exhibiting the *essence-shapings of consciousness* and subjecting them to an *intentional accomplishment-analysis*, in which such-formed objectivity überhaupt, first in original experience, constitutes itself as *synthetic unity*. Then it is a matter of studying the *higher shapings* of the scientific consciousness, in which such objectivities determine themselves as substrates of in-themselves-valid truths in their *theoretically true being*. The latter designates the *transcendental problem of scientific method*, as e.g. of the natural-scientific. The reader should mark the program disclosed behind Hume's false problem: a descriptive-constitutive analysis of how each region of objects (first, material nature) is constituted as synthetic unity in original experience, and a transcendental account of how scientific method determines those objects in their theoretically true being.

Once this problematic is seen, Husserl says, it is essentially the same for all highest *object-regions* and the object-totalities to be separated in them, which are or are called to become the "domains" of principially self-enclosing sciences — hence also those standing under the great titles "culture" and "human community." On the other side, in the problem of the inductive-psychological genesis there hides the problem of the *consciousness-genesis*, or the problem, so to speak, of the *history* (the eidetic or empirical) of the purely transcendentally grasped *intersubjectivity* and the history of its accomplishments — hence of the real and ideal "worlds" constituting themselves individually and communally in the pure subjects. The reader should mark the breadth of the constitutive program disclosed: not only the regional constitution of nature, culture, and community, but the genetic constitution of intersubjectivity and its historically accomplished worlds — the whole later phenomenology of genesis and intersubjectivity fore-formed in Hume's misframed "genesis."

It is an exceedingly remarkable spectacle, Husserl reflects, to observe in the history of philosophy — and here at the development of a through-and-through countersensical empiricism — how behind all the confused and countersensical problems very deep, significant, and sense-full problems press to light, and how properly the *suggestive impression* that those inauthentic problems with their adapted theories continually exercise — and which lends them developmental force and historical far-effect — grounds in the *through-feeling of these genuine problems*. The transcendental — consciousness purely *as* consciousness — makes itself continually valid and is the *secret spiritus rector* (guiding spirit); only that the empiricist philosophies, not in a position to do it justice, accomplish the *opposite* of what philosophy according to its own essence wanted to accomplish: namely to be science in the fullest and strictest sense, which says, as we meant, a science ready and well-armed for theoretical question and answer in all essentially conceivable question-dimensions — or, otherwise expressed: *absolute and in one absolutely self-justifying science*. The reader should mark the deep diagnosis: the persuasive power of skeptical empiricism derives, paradoxically, from the genuine transcendental problems it dimly feels but distorts — the transcendental is the hidden guiding spirit even of the philosophy that betrays it.

Husserl declines to expound in detail the *fictionism* into which Hume falls — at first not without horror at the consequences growing for him; for according to these even the exact natural science, and exactly seen also the pure geometry, is to be nothing further than *semblance of a science, fiction*, as nature itself and its pure space is only a psychological semblance of the imagination that the philosopher first discloses. The horror soon loses itself, Husserl notes drily, and later Hume seems to take all-too-much pleasure in the role of the superior skeptic. What is of interest for us, Husserl says, are only the most general character-traits of this Humean skeptic. The whole construction of it, as a theory that seeks to prove all reality and all reality-science as *fiction*, becomes possible only through a kind of *intellectual dishonesty*, of which it is hard to say how far Hume confessed it to himself and ever brought it to clear consciousness. The reader should register the grave charge Husserl is leveling, and the precise place where he locates it, which the next paragraphs specify.

The *fundament* of Hume's famous theory of the transcendental correlation between the validity-source of the cognition of natural causality (validity of the causal inferences) and the legitimate sense of natural causality itself is, on the one side, the *acknowledgment of purely rational truths* — like the purely mathematical and purely logical (precisely here lies the intellectual dishonesty) — and, on the other side, their *contrasting with the mere fact-truths*. The latter's rationality is his problem, and the end-thesis of his theory their *absolute irrationality*, wherever they go, in causal inferring, beyond immediate experience as impression and memory. The reader should mark exactly where the dishonesty lies: Hume helps himself to the rationality of mathematics and logic as unproblematic, while reducing all factual-causal rationality to irrational association — yet his own nominalism, consistently applied, would dissolve the rational truths too.

Let us try, Husserl says, to procure a deeper understanding of the structure of the Humean problematic and argumentation. Historically considered, Hume's doctrine — above all that of his *Enquiry* — characterizes itself as the *annihilating victory of empiricism over rationalism*, or rather over the since-Descartes-prevailing *mathematizing rationalism*, whose essence consisted in the *indistinct mingling of purely logical-mathematical and mathematical-natural-scientific causality*. The cognition-accomplishment of mathematical physics one held for the same as that of pure arithmetic or geometry; one saw it only as an *extension of pure mathematics*, hence like a geometry of material nature. The extreme and formally consequent carrying-through of the mathematical rationalism lay in *Spinoza's* metaphysical system, whose offensive content had to excite suspicion also against a purely rational method. The reader should mark the genealogy: rationalism's error was to assimilate natural-scientific (causal) necessity to mathematical necessity, conflating two distinct kinds of necessity — an error whose offensive consequences (Spinoza) discredited rational method itself.

Only *Leibniz* and his contemporary *Locke*, Husserl says, recognized the *unremovable difference* between purely ideal truths (as Leibniz says: pure truths of reason), whose negation is a countersense, a contradiction, and *fact-truths*, whose negation yields indeed a false but not an unpresentable, a countersense. This distinction Hume takes over as the famous separation of *cognitions about relations of ideas* and *cognitions of facts*. Therewith it proves that mathematical natural science, as empirical science, is to be separated from the mathematical-logical sciences, which occupy themselves thoroughly with pure truths of reason, immediate and mediate. Application of mathematics to nature creates, to be sure, a higher rationality, yet its essence-character as empirical science it cannot change. But the distinguished and so highly valued rationality of mathematical natural science was thereby *not clarified*, and the mingling with the purely mathematical rationality continually had a hold on the real-causal necessity that played its role in all natural causal inferences and was not separated from the rational necessity of the kind that guided in the mathematical and logical inferences. The reader should mark the unresolved residue: even after Leibniz and Locke separated truths of reason from truths of fact, the *specific rationality of mathematical physics* — neither pure-logical nor mere habit — remained unclarified, and this is the problem Hume sharpens into skepticism.

Hume's impressive, though not essentially new, separation of *causal* and *purely rational* necessity is the point of departure for the problem of the rationality of this latter necessity überhaupt — that of the rationality of the natural-scientific inference-manners. Thereby it is done as though the rationality of the *relations of ideas* and the reason-inferences belonging to them were *no problem*, hence fully intelligible, since precisely the denial here leads to countersense; while, on the other side, the pretended rationality of the *causal inferences* is dissolved, through reduction to the wholly irrational origin out of *idea-association*, into *fiction* — into a psychologically explicable *confusion* of the associative-habitual blind belief-compulsion with that alone genuine rationality. The reader should mark the asymmetry that constitutes the dishonesty: the rationality of ideas is granted without examination, the rationality of causality is reduced to habit — although Hume's own nominalist account of generality should have dissolved the former no less than the latter.

The *skeptical art* of Hume, Husserl says, consists in treating human cognition like a *theater* on which *reason* and *imagination* (Einbildungskraft) appear as actors and annihilate one another as irreconcilable enemies. Reason has its firmly delimited dominion-sphere; its boundary-mark bears the inscription: *countersense*. Within this right-sphere there are only ideas and idea-relations, but nothing of a real world. This belongs in the domain of another faculty, the imagination, which according to immanent-psychological laws — especially (but not alone) those of idea-association and habit — brings forth the experienced nature as its *fictive creation*, in that it thereby secretly permits itself right-less, indeed countersensical, *boundary-oversteppings*. The process is thereby always this: that the imagination, according to its blind lawfulness, first generates a countersense, and then, to make this first countersense more palatable, *fictionally adds a new countersense*. The reader should mark the picture of imagination as a blind, inertial faculty that overshoots experience and props up its first fictions with further fictions — Hume's account of how the belief in an independent world and in causal necessity arises.

The general principle of the imagination, Husserl explains, lies in a peculiar *inertia* (*Trägheit*) belonging to the human soul, by virtue of which it, having come through previous experiences into a habitual *swing*, cannot stop and must *shoot out beyond experience*. Where something of regularity of coexistence and succession had offered itself in actual experience, it must at once pass over to *prolonging* this regularity beyond the previous experience, projecting it into the future, *absolutizing* it as simply objectively subsisting. So it invents, on the ground of approximate coexistence of data, *abiding things* as independent of consciousness; so it invents *causal connections* with supposed necessities. Reason permits here neither to let the appearing world (the imaginative thing-world substructed to the actual sensation-data) count in any sense as existing, nor permits it to regard this world as *manifestation* of a still-further-behind-lying transcendent. A in our consciousness non-findable and for-itself-existing is, according to Hume, at most an *empty thought-possibility*. The only way to infer from given to non-given is that of association and habit, which however itself entitles to nothing. The reader should mark the closed circle of Hume's fictionism: imagination fabricates the world by overshooting experience; reason forbids both the world's existence and any inference behind it; and the only available inference (habit) grounds nothing — so all reality is fiction.

Hume means here, to be sure, Husserl notes: a thinking led by the imagination, hence in the style of induction, is at least "natural," while every inference to the metaphysical — or rather *metapsychical* — is not only unreasonable but also *unnatural*. But that cannot be taken seriously, Husserl objects, since natural and unnatural are supposed to be in like manner wholly unreasonable, and the unreasonable metaphysical inference ensues, in the given case, just as naturally according to some psychological laws as the unreasonable causal inference. To be sure, Hume repeatedly speaks as though he were an *agnostic*, as though there were in actuality an unknown and unknowable transcendent world, to be assumed as being-principle also for our consciousness-course. But that contradicts his theories so crassly that it is to be regarded only as *accommodation* to the ruling and ecclesiastically guarded apprehensions. The reader should mark the verdict: Hume's occasional agnostic gestures (a real but unknowable world) are inconsistent with his fictionism and to be read as prudential concessions, not as his doctrine.

The Humean philosophy is accordingly, Husserl declares, the *open bankruptcy of every philosophy* that would give, about "the" world, scientific account through natural science or metaphysics. Philosophy proves, as *last science*, that all fact-science is unreasonable, hence no science. The conclusion is naturally *completed countersense*, since philosophy itself, as universal psychology, is supposed to be fact-science. One may not say, Husserl insists, that the skepsis concerns only the science of the transcendent (natural) reality. For one must notice that the whole proof-conduct of the irrationality of the experience-inferences is led *on the immanent ground* — so led that it lets itself at first be related überhaupt only directly to *impressions and ideas*, hence to immanent perceptions. On the one side, therefore, the *reasonableness of the immanent psychology* is constantly presupposed, since through it the Humean theories themselves are to be proven as reasonable; on the other side, the *result* of these theories is that *no experience-science überhaupt* (hence also not this psychology) can be reasonable. The reader should mark the global self-refutation: Hume's skepticism, conducted on the immanent ground, must presuppose the rationality of the immanent psychology by which it proceeds, while concluding that no fact-science is rational — and his own psychology is a fact-science.

Husserl returns to the intellectual dishonesty he had set in front. These whole skeptical theories, he had said, presuppose so to speak the *reasonableness of reason itself* — presuppose that the necessity adhering to the cognitions about idea-relations is a really genuine and intelligible one, recognizable as such by means of the self-evident criterion that the negation of such necessity yields a countersense. But precisely here, Husserl says, I see that earlier-mentioned *intellectual dishonesty* (which also otherwise must repel us from Hume). Hume as skeptic resembles all-too-much a *plastic artist* who, to attain an aesthetic effect, intentionally makes *distortions*. For the demonstration no long expositions are needed; for we already know that he has *taken over, indeed exaggerated, the Berkeleyan abstraction-doctrine*. In purely rational judging one judges, after all, in *pure concepts about essence-generalities*, and not about the momentary single ideas, the momentarily hovering phantasms. One judges *generally*, the generality is asserted as even pure, unconditioned generality, and the countersense of the denial is unconditioned and general countersense. This too reduces, according to the nominalistic interpretation of general thinking, to *associations* and other belonging psychological irrationalities. *General ideas, general insights are at bottom a Humean main-title for merely subjective fictions.* The reader should mark the decisive consistency-point: by his own nominalism, Hume's "relations of ideas" — the supposed firm dominion of reason — must dissolve into association no less than causality, so his exemption of mathematics and logic is arbitrary, a distortion for skeptical effect.

Were Hume as skeptic *consistent*, Husserl says, he would have to *say simply nothing*; even the general proposition that it is unintelligible how anything could be asserted überhaupt beyond the respective perceptions may *not* be asserted. Of the precedence of pure mathematics there is then no talk at all. The end is the *absolute bankruptcy of all cognition*. And yet, Husserl says, the empiricist sensualism is *not worthless*, and yet Hume's writings are worthy of an exact study. Almost in all of Hume's expositions there lie, co-seen and entering into the field of sight of the reader, *phenomenological connections*; behind all naturalistically misinterpreted problems stick *true problems*, behind all countersensical negations stick *moments of valuable position* — only that they are not by Hume himself brought to validity, theoretically grasped, shaped into theoretical fundamental positions. The reader should mark the recovered yield: Hume's text is a quarry of genuine phenomenological connections and positions, present to the attentive reader though never grasped by Hume himself.

Precisely that, Husserl concludes, is for us the significant thing of the Humean skepticism — this consequent sensualistic subjectivism — that, although there is no proposition in it that could be scientifically held, it is yet an *intuitionistic and purely immanent philosophy*, and therewith a *pre-form of the alone genuine intuitionistic philosophy, phenomenology*. With this verdict the Second Chapter closes, having shown Hume's skepticism as at once the complete bankruptcy of cognition (every proposition untenable) and the pre-form of phenomenology (intuitionistic, immanent, teeming with genuine constitutive problems). The reader should register the chapter's resolved paradox: the most consistent skepticism is, precisely in its consistency, the nearest approach to transcendental phenomenology — the negative whose making-true is the positive science of constitution. The Third Chapter turns from the empiricist line to the *rationalism and metaphysics of the modern age* — the positively constructing line that runs through Descartes' successors toward Leibniz and Kant.

## **Third Chapter: Rationalism and Metaphysics of the Modern Age**

### **Twenty-sixth Lecture: The basic features of the positively constructing line of modern rationalism and its dogmatism**

Husserl turns from empiricism to its rationalist counter-stream, and announces at once that it requires, for his special purpose, a much *less detailed* consideration. The great developmental line of rationalism, rich in so great thinkers — leading from Descartes over Spinoza, Leibniz to Kant and over him to Hegel — does not, for the present aim, demand the close critique empiricism received. For empiricism had in the modern age the great function of helping to breakthrough the method (which first makes possible the grounding of a philosophy überhaupt) of the *regress to the phenomenological origins of all cognition*, and of procuring emphasis for the demand of a radically intuitionistic philosophy. The function of rationalism lies on a quite other side. The reader should mark the asymmetry of treatment Husserl is establishing: empiricism, despite its skepticism, pioneered the immanent method and so demanded close critique; rationalism, for all its constructive achievement, did *not* pioneer that method and so is treated more summarily.

In constant struggle with empiricism, Husserl says, rationalism never understands how to let it, at the deepest, receive justice; it does not grasp the significant essence-kernel lying behind its skeptical perversities, and therefore never takes a run-up to form, in place of the skeptically countersensical, a better *immanent philosophy*. In itself it would have been called to that. For if empiricism is at bottom the continuation of the ancient skeptical-negativistic philosophy, then rationalism is the continuation of the positively building, toward true and last-perfect science and so toward genuine philosophy directed developmental-pull. It is therefore continuation of *Platonism* and of *medieval realism* — hence enemy of all nominalistic *conjuring-away* of the general ideas and of any truly rational cognition; defender of the "eternal" truths and of every experiential-scientific method that procures for the empirical a share in pure rationality, and that above all in the exemplary shape of the mathematical natural science. The reader should mark the irony Husserl is drawing: rationalism, the heir of Platonism and the champion of the eternal truths, was *better positioned* than empiricism to build the immanent philosophy (the eidetic science of subjectivity), yet failed to do so precisely because, as dogmatism, it never took the immanent turn.

The rationalist Descartes, Husserl recalls, opened the modern age precisely by opening the access to the *immanent sphere* as the absolute ground of all cognition-grounding. Who would therefore have been more called than rationalism to take this new sphere into work — into the work of purely rational concept-formations and insights, hence to form an *eidetics of transcendental subjectivity*? But we know already that Descartes did not understand the proper philosophical sense of his discovery, and that it was to serve him only as *anchor-ground* to give the positive ("dogmatic") sciences a hold. So he led the whole further development onto the way of a dogmatistic metaphysics and dogmatic particular sciences. An unbridled drive for knowledge, and at once the reverse side of a co-unleashed drive for practical nature- and world-mastery, satisfied itself in ever-new theories, specialized itself in ever-new sciences in infinite fruitfulness; and over these methodically self-standing particular sciences there raises itself a *metaphysics*. Related to them all, it sees therein its function: to represent the universal idea of philosophy and to pose to the unsplit, as full universe taken, actuality the so-called highest and last questions — questions that, like already those of the Aristotelian general doctrine of being, were precisely *not* special questions bound to single domains, and culminated in *theological* problems. The reader should mark the structure of the post-Cartesian situation: self-standing positive sciences crowned by a metaphysics that poses the highest (theological) questions to the whole of reality — a metaphysics that is, however, itself dogmatic.

This metaphysics, Husserl says — the modern as the ancient and medieval — was a *dogmatic science*, quite as the natural sciences and the other ever-newly establishing special sciences were. Its basic concepts and basic principles, its methods and theories were *not drawn from the last origins in the transcendental subjectivity* and so received not from there their last sense and their last truth. This transcendental subjectivity — of the transcendentally, through possible mutual understanding, connected all-community of the transcendental single subjects — remained *un-intuited*, in the state of naive anonymity; let alone that it was recognized as the *most radical and most important of all scientific themes*. One did not yet see that it is the *essence-correlate* of the All of objectivities that, seen exclusively as "positive," were themes of all natural experience and then exclusively themes of the positive sciences. "Essence-correlate," however, expresses that *objectivity without transcendental subjectivity is simply unthinkable*. The reader should mark the cardinal thesis the dogmatists missed: subjectivity and objectivity are essence-correlates, so that no objectivity is thinkable without the constituting subjectivity — which makes the science of that subjectivity the most radical of all themes, not an afterthought.

One did not yet notice, Husserl continues, that the entire natural experience, and so überhaupt the one-sided gaze-direction upon the *positivities* (the world-All and the mathematical idea-world), carries out a kind of *abstraction* and seduces the philosophical thinking to *absolutize mere abstracta*; and that therefore, without cancellation of such abstraction — through a methodical *dimming-out* of positivistic captivation, through making-visible the in the naturality self-hidden transcendental subjectivity, and through systematic study of this subjectivity as constituting all kinds of the positive — a really *concrete* cognition is impossible. The reader should mark the diagnosis: the positive attitude is an *abstraction* that takes the objective pole in isolation from its subjective correlate; the dogmatist absolutizes this abstractum (the world taken without its constituting subjectivity), and only the reduction to constituting subjectivity restores the full concretion.

An epistemological *upward-drive* was, to be sure, Husserl grants, from Descartes' *Meditations* and as continuing-effect of the still older question after the method of genuine science-grounding, continually in force; and the metaphysical work interwove itself almost everywhere with epistemological intermediate-considerations, just as conversely occasionally epistemological investigations unhesitatingly made metaphysical and particular-scientific *presuppositions*. One simply did not yet know what a theory of cognition, what a doctrine of the understanding or reason had to accomplish — and that here nothing less was demanded than a *ground-science preceding all objective cognitions and sciences, putting them all in like manner in question, hence independent of them all*. One did not see that without such a science — whose only work-field had to be the *purely grasped subjectivity* — no philosophy, no science of nature and of spirit, no metaphysics as universal science of the highest being-grounds was possible, that could, as full-sided and last-grounded, also give *last* account about the existing in general and its special-scientific specifications. The reader should mark the recurring indictment: rationalism mingled epistemology with metaphysics because it never grasped that the theory of cognition must be an *independent ground-science* preceding and questioning all objective sciences — the very point that the whole work has been establishing.

Or rather, Husserl corrects himself, there had indeed (according to our above demonstrations) struggled through, in the generation following Descartes, on the side of *empiricism*, a certain awakening consciousness of the necessity of a ground-science of pure subjectivity — but in the shape of an *immanent sensualistic psychologism*, which the rationalists had to reject in sharp critique as a new variety of the from-their-side-ever-combated nominalism and skepticism. But the rationalistic critique, Husserl says, did *not fulfill its historical function*. For as already in antiquity over against the skeptical subjectivism, so in the modern age over against the new, the immanent psychologism, the task was posed *not* to remain stuck in the mere demonstration of perversities and countersenses of the skeptical theories, but, through a *positive critique* of the effective inner motives, to do justice to their *genuineness-content* — therewith the task to make the subjectivism true in a higher sense, to *reshape the bad subjectivism into the necessarily demanded one*. But even a Leibniz, who everywhere and in all philosophies saw positive values, was not able, in his detailed and in the single very instructive critique of Locke, to *intuit out* of Locke's sensualistic-empiristic intuitionism the genuine idea of a *transcendental intuitionism*, and to envisage the for a scientific philosophy decisive grounding of an *essence-science of the transcendental subjectivity* in place of an immanent empirical and even sensualistic psychology (the editorial note refers here to Beilage XVI, Hua VII pp. 357 ff.; the Spinoza/Locke discussion to Beilage XV, Hua VII pp. 350 ff.). The reader should mark the missed historical opportunity Husserl is naming: even Leibniz, the most generous of critics, refuted Locke without redeeming the genuine intuitionism buried in him — without converting bad subjectivism into the transcendental science it pointed toward.

It would nonetheless be perverse, Husserl insists, to *underestimate* the enormous and for a future scientific psychology itself highly fruitful spiritual work that was accomplished in the rationalistic philosophy of the modern age, as no less in that of antiquity. That holds whether we understand philosophy in the indispensable widest word-sense — hence count in the under-the-idea-of-rational-grounding ever more perfectly self-forming sciences — or whether we understand philosophy in a narrower sense and assign to it only the disciplines that treat the all-spanning being-questions and the principial normative questions. Certainly, Husserl grants, for us — who already insight a transcendental philosophy of phenomenological style as the *unum necessarium* for the enabling of last-sufficing cognition and last-scientific science — none of our sciences, be it now called exact mathematics and natural science or methodically ever so acknowledgment-worthy *Geisteswissenschaft*, is science of such last sense. And at bottom all acknowledge that who hold a "philosophy of mathematics" outside or beside mathematics, a "philosophy of physics" or "philosophy of nature" beside physics itself, and so everywhere philosophies to be attached to the positive sciences in analogous manner, necessary — and not, say, for fields of useless word-battles. The reader should mark the careful concession: the positive sciences are genuine sciences, but none is of *last* (philosophical, transcendentally grounded) sense; the felt need for a "philosophy of" each science testifies to the missing transcendental foundation.

Meanwhile, Husserl says, we may probably assume in advance — even if in a certain naivety of evidence — that that higher transcendental cognition-grounding would change *nothing considerable* on the main- and core-stock of the theoretical methodics of the old-proven objective sciences. The gain would consist in an *origin-clear foundation*, in an essential *back-relation to the absolute subjectivity*, whereby they would be enriched by mighty cognition-increases in the transcendental counter-dimension and would experience *last sense-determinations* for their object-spheres. The reader should mark the measured claim: transcendental grounding does not overturn the positive sciences but founds them origin-clearly, relates them back to absolute subjectivity, and enriches them with the correlative transcendental dimension and ultimate sense-determinations.

Not so well, to be sure, Husserl says, does it stand with the *traditional philosophical disciplines* in the pregnant sense, and above all with *metaphysics* as universal doctrine of being. For here it never came, and could not come, to a methodically firmed, generally acknowledged science — because precisely for this a transcendental ground-science of our sense was *indispensable*. And yet, valuable *pre-forms* of metaphysical insights, and metaphysical theories of a rich, even if scientifically not really grounded, system-content, came also in metaphysics to development and functioned, in a really ascending development, with the abiding *vocation to prepare a future genuine metaphysics*. The reader should mark the positive estimate of rationalist metaphysics: not science, but the *preparation* of a future genuine (phenomenologically grounded) metaphysics — pre-forms with an abiding vocation.

Husserl pauses here on a methodologically rich point, developed in an extended footnote that is worth folding in because it states precisely how the rationalist *ontologies* function for phenomenology. What rationalism formed of new disciplines — namely the *ontologies*, in a priori procedure — had to win a significant function in the moment where the task of a phenomenology as purely immanent transcendental philosophy was rightly understood and posed. In the language of the *Ideen* (the first book of Husserl's *Ideas Toward a Pure Phenomenology*): the basic concepts and basic principles of the ontologies are the necessary *"guiding-threads" (Leitfäden)* for a universal phenomenology in the higher stage of a *phenomenology of reason* — for the systematic project of the constitutive problematic, which relates on the one side to the formal-ontological quasi-region "object überhaupt" and on the other to the highest regions of objectivities. It needs therefore a systematic grounding of all formal and regional ontologies and of a universal "category-doctrine" leading them in systematic community — the eidetic grounding of the system of a priori prefigured regions, framed by the formal region. Although, after the methodical bringing-into-relief of the universal ground of phenomenological research (the phenomenological reduction), this whole work carries itself out *within the phenomenological attitude itself*, it is yet clear that every ontology — every purely rational discipline — projected in naively-positive evidence is to be *taken over* into phenomenology (even if perhaps to be bettered through its origin-clarification) and so hands over to it pre-done work. The reader should mark the constructive role assigned to rationalism's a priori ontologies: they supply the guiding-threads for the constitutive analyses of phenomenology — the eidetic structures of "object überhaupt" and of each region — and so the rationalist labor, rightly reinterpreted, is taken up and grounded within transcendental phenomenology.

The footnote draws the consequence for metaphysics in a new sense, and it is worth carrying through. All this transfers itself onto the positive fact-sciences, in whose phenomenological interpretation the *last-scientific fact-sciences* grow — the in-themselves philosophical ones, which beside themselves tolerate no more special philosophies to be appended. Through the last interpretation, accruing to them in application of the eidetic phenomenology, of the objective being researched in them as fact, and through the in this phenomenology co-demanded universal consideration of all regions of objectivity in relation to the universal community of transcendental subjects, the *world-All* — the universal theme of the positive sciences — wins "*metaphysical*" interpretation: which says nothing other than an interpretation behind which to seek another gives no scientific sense. But behind that there opens, on phenomenological ground, a *no-further-interpretable problematic*: that of the *irrationality of the transcendental fact* that expresses itself in the constitution of the factual world and the factual spirit-life — hence *metaphysics in a new sense* (the note refers to Beilage XVII, Hua VII p. 365). The reader should mark the horizon opened: phenomenology grounds a "metaphysics" that is simply the last interpretation of the world-All in relation to transcendental intersubjectivity, beyond which lies only the ultimate, no-further-interpretable problematic of the *irrationality of the transcendental fact* — the sheer facticity of the constituted world and spirit-life, which is metaphysics in the new, residual sense.

Husserl returns to the main line under a new sub-heading, with critical remarks on the *regressive procedure* in the rationalistic constructions since occasionalism, and the task of progressive research. He declines to lose himself in endless and aimless single critiques of the systems; such critiques cannot come into question for the rationalistic theories in similar intent as for the empiristic. With the latter, with Locke, a new methodical type had appeared, and the critique of the method had for us the significant goal of making visible, *behind* the immanent-psychological method, the historical tendency toward a thoroughly necessary and true philosophical method, the phenomenological. But rationalism, as *dogmatism*, is not at all laid out upon an immanent method, and in its method there does *not* work itself out a tendency toward the true one, even if imperfectly. In this respect, Husserl says pointedly, the skeptical negativism is *more positive* than the in positive rational work progressing rationalism. But of interest here is to show how this dogmatism was *motivated* through the Cartesian awakening of the transcendental subjectivity and at once through the new empiricism — how it was *compelled* to take account of the transcendental in the dogmatic attitude, finally to develop connected theories for it, which, although they did not do justice to the demanded sense of an immanent phenomenology, could yet bring much that was conformable to it. The reader should mark the precise interest Husserl takes in rationalism: not its method (which harbors no tendency toward phenomenology) but the way the Cartesian discovery and the empiricist challenge *forced* dogmatism to reckon with the transcendental, producing theories useful for phenomenology despite themselves.

Husserl now characterizes the reconciliation-metaphysics. Already in the Cartesian school, he observes, we notice a pull toward the formation of a metaphysics out of motives of *reconciliation* between causalistic and theological world-consideration, and at once the formation of an a priori *ontology* in pretended exact method after the model of mathematics. In the latter respect *Spinoza* is meant, with his *Ethica ordine geometrico demonstrata*. To him remained far every other-than-theoretical-motive-following regard for the needs of positive religion and theology; rather, in abrupt ruthlessness he attempts to develop an *atheological* doctrine of being and God, and an ethics, out of purely axiomatic basic-determinations and strictly deductively. Otherwise the *occasionalists*. The Cartesian philosophy with its two-substance-doctrine — which demanded a science of spirit after the same kind and method as natural science and granted *both* sciences absolute significance — seemed metaphysically to press toward a *causalistic* world-apprehension that could not do justice to the demands of religion and the ethical needs interwoven with them. So there grew already in occasionalism, and still before the effectiveness of Spinozism, attempts to shape metaphysics *under the guidance of religious-ethical postulates*. The reader should mark the two divergent responses to Descartes' two-substance dogmatism: Spinoza's ruthless geometrical-atheological system, and the occasionalists' religiously-ethically guided reconciliation — the latter setting the pattern Husserl is about to analyze.

Still stronger, Husserl says, becomes the drive to such attempts since the stepping-forth of the *Ethics* of Spinoza, which appeared as the *pure consequence* of the by the new mathematical natural science guided metaphysics and had to excite greatest offense. Out of *God* had become a kind of *mathematical essence*, to which all properly spiritual predicates were lacking; the proceeding-forth of the physical and spiritual realities of the ordinary world-apprehension out of this absolute substance had become a *mathematical* proceeding of mathematical consequence-determinations out of the definitory basic-determinations. In this system of rigid mathematical consequence there was for *freedom, purposive activity, for a divine teleology no room*. The further development, Husserl says, is essentially governed by the philosophical need to *reconcile* causal and final world-apprehension, natural and spiritual, mathematical-mechanical necessity and human and divine freedom. Precisely this driving-force of reconciliation gives the metaphysical attempts a not-unessential *methodical character*, which is at once co-determined through the necessity of regard for the since-Descartes-effective motive of the *for-itself-enclosed res cogitans*, of the immediately only of itself conscious spirit. The reader should mark the two pressures shaping reconciliation-metaphysics: the need to reconcile mechanical necessity with teleological freedom (against Spinoza's consequence), and the need to reckon with the Cartesian self-enclosed mind — pressures that issue in a reconstructive method.

Husserl now connects this to the later sense of "transcendental method." When in the nineteenth century, in departure from the interpretation of the *Kantian* critique of reason, one speaks of "transcendental method," one means thereby — in contrast to what we here so designated — a peculiarly *regressive and constructing* method, which goes after the "conditions of the possibility" of objectively valid cognition in the sense of the question: *what must be presupposed for the cognizing subjectivity*, how must its cognition-faculties, how the in it self-grading cognition-functions of intuiting and thinking be assumed, how must they work cognition-forming, so that cognition of a true objectivity in the form of in-themselves-valid truths and sciences is to become possible and intelligible? In a similar sense *reconstructive* is from the outset the *reconciliation-metaphysics*; and it will seem to me, Husserl says, that the powerful role which *reconstruction* has won for the theory of cognition has its original historical source in this *reconstructing metaphysics* (the note refers to Beilage XVIII, Hua VII pp. 365 ff.). The reader should mark the genealogical thesis: Kant's regressive transcendental method — arguing back from the fact of valid cognition to its subjective conditions — descends historically from the reconstructive method of reconciliation-metaphysics, and stands opposed to the *progressive*, intuitive method that phenomenology requires.

What concerns the latter, Husserl explains, the philosophy still firmly stuck in dogmatism stood, on the one side, before the *world in the sense of the new objective sciences*: mathematics, mathematical natural science, and the naturally-projected psychology and *Geisteswissenschaft*; and these sciences and their world wanted to count as *absolute*. On the other side was pre-given, from religion and theology, *God as world-creator*, as the last principle from which the whole world according to sense and being springs, and in it enclosed the free reason-beings in whom *self-responsibility* out of own logical and ethical conscience interweaves itself with the responsibility before God — own free decision and deed and the all-decision of the ultimately all-effecting God. In the religious demand lay thereby — so it was at least understood — that the content of all factual being, and even of all lawfulnesses of this being, but also sense and absolute validity of all last-right-giving reason-norms, must have its *teleological grounds in the divine spirit*. Positive truth and theological wanted and had to come to *reconciling unity*; and therewith in one: divine being and being of the finite beings; reason and will of God, reason and will of man. The reader should mark the double pre-givenness that defined the dogmatic task: the absolute world of the objective sciences and the creator-God of theology, which had to be reconciled — mechanical nature with divine teleology, finite freedom with divine all-effecting.

Metaphysics as science of the existing in the absolute sense was therewith pressed onto the *constructive way*, Husserl says: *how must we above all lay out for ourselves the natural world, that of the objective sciences, so that it can become a God-effected, a teleologically intelligible world?* The methodical procedure is therefore a *similar* one as theology always had to follow when it wanted to make its theological doctrines, in the manner of a so-called *natural theology*, also rationally intelligible. But what can be granted to theology, Husserl insists, does *not yet* stand to philosophy. It may have *no preceding dogma*, no however-formed pre-conviction. Its essence is precisely to want to be absolutely grounded science, or more simply, *pure* science and nothing but science. Principially it can only proceed *from absolutely insightful primal-grounds* and ascend in an absolutely prejudice-less grounding-way justifying itself in every step out of evident principles. Its procedure can and may only be *progressive*. The reader should mark the decisive methodological contrast that closes the lecture: theology may reason back from its dogma (God, creation) to a reconstruction of the world that fits it, but philosophy, having no dogma, must proceed *progressively* — forward from absolutely insightful origins, justifying each step — and the reconstructive/regressive method of reconciliation-metaphysics (and, by descent, of Kantian transcendental argument) violates this requirement.

In a certain sense, Husserl concedes, every scientific researcher proceeds *constructively and regressively* — he does it in his *inventing thought-courses*. All invention presupposes *anticipation*; one can seek nothing and seek to generate nothing without having in advance a *leading-presentation* for the to-be-sought, to-be-generated. And in advance the inventor will try to shape phantasy-wise possible ways that might lead, over already firm-standing truths as stages, to the anticipated result. But with all that only an *estimate* is won and a provisional probability. The really settling, accomplishing work then *follows after*: *progressively* it passes from firmly grounded to thereon grounded. But in that it goes forward in really ascending grounding-work, the *insightful and concretely full cognition* is properly first won, according to *way and goal* — a cognition that in both respects, as a rule, not only looks *richer* but in manifold respects also *otherwise* than the original estimate. The reader should mark the careful nuance with which Husserl closes: regressive construction has its legitimate place as the *heuristic anticipation* that guides discovery, but it yields only a provisional estimate; the genuine, insightful cognition is won only by the *progressive* grounding-work that follows — and the rationalist error was to take the reconstructive anticipation for the science itself. The Twenty-seventh and final lecture turns to *metaphysics and theory of cognition* and the significance of Leibniz's monadology and Kant's critique of reason.

### **Twenty-seventh Lecture: On metaphysics and theory of cognition. The significance of Leibniz's monadology and Kant's critique of reason**

Accordingly we understand, Husserl begins, that a dogmatistic rationalism can *never* lead to a definitive philosophy — though out of quite other grounds than empiricism. Rationalism is nothing other than the continuation and modification of ancient *Platonism*; there works on continually the significant primal-thought that *true being is correlate of the insightful conceptual thinking, of logical judging*. But the modern rationalism is determined by this: that through Descartes the *cognizing subjectivity* — as experiencing and logically thinking, but also in every other sense meaning and deciding — had become *visible in its pure immanence*. It demanded now a regard as *absolute ground* on or in which, for the cognizing I, the appearing and true world constitutes itself. Everything now depended on *how this demand was understood*. The reader should mark the hinge on which the whole final lecture turns: Descartes made cognizing subjectivity visible as the absolute ground, but the entire fate of modern rationalism (and of Kant) hangs on whether that ground is grasped rightly — as the field of a descriptive eidetic science — or wrongly, as a mere anchor for dogmatic construction.

The attempt of Descartes, Husserl recalls, to make the *ego cogito* the absolute fundament of the building of all objective sciences and at once to give the particular sciences and the metaphysics spanning them unity and definitive grounding, had failed — because Descartes could not yet see the necessity of making the realm of the *ego cogito*, as field of a *transcendental experience* (or eidetic intuition), the theme of a *descriptive science*, and of showing, in purely immanent research, how here in the pure consciousness and according to own essence-necessities all possibilities of objective shapings are enclosed *as cognition-shapings*. The reader should mark the recurring formula for Descartes' failure, now stated for the last time: he discovered the ground but did not grasp it as the field of a descriptive, eidetic science of constitution.

The following time up to Kant, Husserl says, *cannot lose from sight* the immanent subjectivity and its evidence of the subjective experience-processes; but it has before itself the *intuitive world and the finished objective sciences* that determine truths for it, it has its religious and moral convictions, and it now *reflects* over them: how the realities must be *re-thought*, how they must be *interpreted*, so that the demands of science, of religion and morality can be satisfied — and to that, and not least, also *the demands that the immanence of cognition made valid*. Metaphysics as general doctrine of the existing in its absolute actuality becomes, in last respect, *dependent on the interpretation of the immanently self-carrying-out cognition*. The reader should mark the situation of post-Cartesian rationalism: it cannot ignore immanent subjectivity, but it approaches it *reflectively and interpretively* (how must the world be re-thought to satisfy science, religion, morality, and the immanence of cognition?), rather than making it the theme of direct descriptive research.

To be sure, Husserl grants, just as an objective science of nature and of spirit, so also a general doctrine of being could be projected *straightforwardly* (*geradehin*), hence in the manner of positive science. After the separation between *pure and applied mathematics* had become clear, there could also be projected, for nature, in distinction from the empirical (even if mathematically formed) science, a *purely rational, an a priori natural science* — in other words, an a priori *ontology of nature*, a science not of the factual nature but of an *ideally possible nature überhaupt*; exactly in the sense in which geometry is not science of the factual space and its shapes but of ideally possible space-shapes and an ideally possible space. And likewise an *ontology of the soul* could be attempted and projected, and finally a *general ontology* of possible reality überhaupt — but that quite in the *naivety* in which mathematicians produce their a priori truths, unconcerned about all theory of cognition. Other expressions for such intended ontological disciplines are *metaphysical or rational doctrine of nature and of the soul*, and, more comprehensively, *rational cosmology and theology*. Already Spinoza's *Ethics*, Husserl notes, is a purely rational metaphysics that was to enclose in itself all particular ontologies. The reader should mark the legitimate kernel of the rationalist ontologies: as a priori sciences of ideally possible regions (nature, soul, reality überhaupt), they are genuine eidetic disciplines — but projected *naively*, without the epistemological grounding that would secure their metaphysical claim.

But over against such attempts, Husserl says, when they gave themselves out as *definitive sciences*, the *naivety* had to become sensible in which the absolute validity and the metaphysical value of the results was claimed. *Metaphysics* was the title under which always last-valid being-cognition was claimed. But since with Descartes' *Meditations* the problem of the possibility of objective cognition *in the immanence of the cognizing subject* had appeared, the value of all objective science — and so also of all naive metaphysics — had to appear *problematic*. In the immanence of the cognizing *ego* carries itself out the "clear and distinct" cognizing, the rational theorizing of science. The so-cognized is supposed to *be in truth*; what is in truth is rationally cognizable, and what is rationally cognized is true, is "in itself" as that which the cognition-judgment conceptually determines it. But how, Husserl asks, is this *rationalistic basic-conviction*, on which after all all science rests, to be held through and made explicable, if the cognizer, with all his cognition-shapings, after all only *in himself, in his pure subjectivity*, shapes what he shapes? The reader should mark the problem that Descartes' discovery forces on all subsequent rationalism: if all cognition is shaped within the cognizer's immanence, the rationalist faith that the rationally cognized is "in itself" true becomes precisely the thing to be made intelligible — the transcendental problem.

All scientific positings, Husserl says, be they now empirical or *a priori* — be they in the latter case designatable, by virtue of their principial generality and apodictic evidence, as *metaphysical* — needed an *interpretation* according to "sense" and "reach," i.e. an *epistemological interpretation*. There had to be posed and solved, with respect to them, the problems that concern the "cognition-value" of the cognition-accomplishments carrying themselves out in the immanence of cognizing. Only then, Husserl says, can there be *last philosophical truth* — or, if metaphysics remains the title for it and for the last-principial, only then can there be a *real metaphysics*. In the wider sense it then spans *all objective science freed by epistemological interpretation from its naivety*. These are methodical convictions, Husserl notes, that, as workings-out of the Cartesian impulses, establish themselves early in the rationalistic philosophy, already determine the whole philosophizing of Leibniz, then are effective in new and powerful force in Kant's critique of reason, and in the nineteenth century revive, even if mostly flattened, in the Neo-Kantian schools (the note refers to Beilage XIX, Hua VII pp. 377 ff., and later to Beilage XX, pp. 381 ff.). The reader should mark the genuine demand rationalism inherited from Descartes: that all science, empirical and a priori (metaphysical) alike, requires epistemological interpretation of its cognition-value before it can count as last truth — the demand that runs through Leibniz to Kant and the Neo-Kantians.

It is now the question, Husserl says, *in what method* the epistemological interpretation and the whole epistemological work accomplished thereby carries itself out. It is comprehensible that one at first *begins by letting science count* — quite as the natural man in life lets the experience-world count as self-evidently existing actuality. He has, in the concordant progress of experience, *lived* its self-confirming evidence — or rather naively *exercised* it — and its force lies in the self-evident, immediate *for-me-existence* of the experienced things. Likewise whoever has self-actively, insightfully worked through a piece of science is *sure* of the theoretically proven and its truth. But there is, Husserl warns, a *dangerous and methodically countersensical confusion* if one, as this attitude easily brings with it, *mingles* the objective-scientific assertions one *lets count* with epistemological question-settings — say weaves psycho-physical knowledge as intermediate-member into epistemological considerations. In crude and clumsy form the empiricist Locke committed this error, and philosophizing natural scientists and nature-scientizing philosophers commit it — a mass-phenomenon — still into our days. The reader should mark the recurring sin in its final formulation: the *metabasis* of mingling objective (e.g. psycho-physical) premises into the epistemological investigation, which Locke committed crudely and which persists wherever the transcendental question is confused with a positive-scientific one.

But even if this reproach cannot be made to the great philosophers of the eighteenth century, to a Leibniz and Kant, Husserl says, there yet fails in them much of the *last and pure method-consciousness* on which a scientifically genuine epistemological foundation of all sciences depends. What here above all is needful is a general and so-to-speak *pedantic methodical fixation*: all cognition, beginning from the simple experience-cognition up to all science, must be treated as *epistemologically questionable*, and according to the sense of this questionability all and every cognition (its meant object as well as the truth supposedly determining it) is to be set up *merely as phenomenon*, instead of having it as valid cognition and making use of it. But *phenomenon* is all cognition for me *in the transcendental subjectivity*; and so the genuine and pure method requires as first to posit nothing other than this in fact in-itself-first of all givennesses, the "absolutely" evident *transcendental subjectivity*. On the other side, everything pre-given however self-evidently — the sense-world and the science determining it — may *only* be posited as *experienced of the experiencing*, as judgment-content of these and those scientifically formed judgment-experiences. The reader should mark the methodical prescription stated in its purest form: the universal *epoché* that converts all cognition into mere phenomenon, posits the transcendental subjectivity as the first of all givennesses, and takes the whole world only as the experienced-correlate of experiencing — the reduction as the indispensable first step the rationalists never made pedantically.

Has this bringing-into-relief of the *universal realm of subjectivity with its phenomena* consciously ensued, Husserl says, then the next step lies near enough — and one can let oneself be made attentive to it by empiricism — namely the step now to say to oneself: *here is a possible proper, closed research-field that can and must be systematically researched*. But so the historical theory of cognition does *not* proceed. However much it *de facto* claims its problematic cognitions — be they now sensuous experiences and empirical judgments, be they purely rational concepts and judgments, be they whole sciences like mathematics, exact natural science — *as phenomenon*, and however much their validity may express only the immanent peculiarity of the subjectively insightful grounding: the procedure is yet *not a methodically conscious one* that first of all assures itself of the transcendental subjectivity as the *primal-ground* and makes the cognition-shapings on it the systematic research-theme. It is *not enough*, Husserl insists, to *have* cognition-formations as phenomena and to pose to them questions of the sense of their objective validity; one must *make clear to oneself that these phenomena must first be studied as phenomena, and as phenomena of intentionality need an intentional laying-out*. The reader should mark the exact deficiency of rationalist epistemology: it does treat cognition as phenomenon and ask after its validity, but it never takes the decisive further step of making the phenomena the theme of *systematic intentional analysis* — it stops at interpretation and construction.

Naturally, Husserl grants, at first fore-groping general considerations offer certain *leitmotifs* for interpretations; so, e.g., when *Leibniz* reflects over sensibility and thinking in the following way: in the merely sensuous experience I am sensuously *affected*, the sensuous affects me as *I-foreign*; in thinking I activate myself *purely out of myself*, the pure concepts are free of contingent experience, they are drawn from my pure essence. In all a priori insights expresses itself a lawfulness belonging to the *pure essence of subjectivity*, which must be common to all subjects as essence-lawfulness. How does it then stand with sensuous experience and the experience-laws conditioned through it; how do pure concepts, as *primal-forms* of my purely intellective essence, work formingly in the experience-science; how must, so progressing, sensibility be interpreted, and in further sequence the sensuously experienced and natural-scientifically cognized nature, if experience-cognition is to become intelligible as objective? The reader should mark that these Leibnizian reflections (the I-foreignness of the sensuous, the self-activity of pure thought, the pure essence-lawfulness common to all subjects) are genuine *leitmotifs* — anticipations pointing toward constitutive analysis — but, as Husserl is about to insist, only anticipations.

I do not want to go further, Husserl says, but it is visible that *neither* the thinking cognition of purely rational kind *nor* the experiencing of concrete nature-objects is *directly investigated* and subjected to a systematic intentional essence-analysis, and that such manners of consideration can count only as *fore-groping anticipations*, but not as *theories*. Without actual analysis — while the phenomena in question remain in far matter-distance — *reconstructive thought-formations* are carried out, *conditions of the possibility* for the coming-about of this or that cognition-accomplishment, or for the possibility of a rationally intelligible cognition-world, are sought; whereby the structure of sensibility (say as confused thinking) and that of thinking is *not really investigated but postulated*. The reader should mark the precise charge against the rationalist (and proto-Kantian) method: it *postulates* the structures of sensibility and thought and argues regressively to their "conditions of possibility," instead of *investigating* those structures directly by intentional analysis — construction in place of description.

An intuitive thinker like Leibniz, Husserl grants generously, invents nothing, to be sure, where his ingenious phantasy could not also anticipate fitting intuition; and so his whole *monad-doctrine* is one of the most *magnificent anticipations of history*. Whoever fully understands it cannot but ascribe to it a great truth-content. Leibniz has, in the discussion of the basic-properties of the monad — under the titles *perception, striving passage from perception to perception*, and especially *representation of the reell not-present and yet perceptively conscious* — grasped the *basic-properties of intentionality* and metaphysically worked them up. But on the whole he yet remains stuck in the *occasional aperçu, in anticipation and construction*. The reader should mark the precise measure of Leibniz's greatness and limit: his monadology anticipates, with real truth-content, the basic structures of intentionality (perception, the striving passage of consciousness, the representation of the non-present) — yet as anticipation and metaphysical construction, not as carried-out intentional analysis.

Kant too, Husserl says, however much he is directed on systematic investigations and in fact progresses in a deeply-thought systematics, does *not see the method required for a genuine transcendental science*. His is closely related to that of Leibniz; and if he himself believes himself far removed from Leibniz, that lies in this: that the proper sense of the Leibnizian philosophy could be disclosed only in our days on the ground of the fuller knowledge of his thoughts splintered in sketches, letters, small treatises. Certainly, Husserl grants, one can say that *factually* the entire researches of Kant play themselves out *on the absolute ground of the transcendental subjectivity*. And moreover he has, with an *exemplary intuitive force*, seen *essence-structures* in this subjectivity that are of incomparable significance and that no one before had intimated. In Kant's critique of reason there lies a *chain of great discoveries* spread out before us — and yet not only hard of access, but also methodically grounded in a shape such that we must say: Kant's critique of reason remains just as far removed from a transcendental philosophy as last-grounding and last-grounded science as that of Leibniz. The reader should mark the carefully balanced final verdict on Kant: factually working on the ground of transcendental subjectivity, seeing genuine essence-structures with unequaled intuitive power, yet methodically failing to reach transcendental philosophy as a rigorous science — for the reasons the next sentences specify.

The *regressive methodical procedure* plays in Kant the greatest role, Husserl says: how is pure mathematics possible, how pure natural science; how must we think sensibility so that purely geometric judgments become possible; how must the manifold of sensuous intuition come to synthetic unity so that strict natural science — hence determination of experience-objects in in-themselves-valid truths — becomes possible? Kant himself demands and carries out "deductions," the by him metaphysically and transcendentally called deductions of the intuition-forms, of the categories; deduced is likewise the schematism, the necessary validity of the basic principles of the pure understanding. It is, to be sure, not *merely* deduced, and naturally not in the ordinary sense. And yet, Husserl insists, it is a *constructive thought-procedure* that *following* intuition accompanies — and not a from-below-ascending, from exhibition to exhibition intuitively progressing *making-intelligible of the constitutive accomplishments of consciousness*, and indeed according to all the gaze-directions open to reflection. The reader should mark the decisive methodological objection to Kant: his transcendental deductions are *regressive construction* (arguing back from the fact of valid cognition to its presupposed conditions), with intuition only following after, rather than the *progressive*, intuitively ascending intentional analysis of constitution that phenomenology requires.

The so-to-speak *innermost sides of the constituting consciousness*, Husserl says, are in Kant überhaupt scarcely touched; the sensuous phenomena with which he occupies himself are already *constituted unities*, of an over-rich intentional structure that is never subjected to a systematic analysis.
