Likewise the *judgment* plays, to be sure, a basic-determining role, but of a *phenomenology of the judgment-experiences* — and of the manner in which, in their change, the proposition and its modalities of being come to unity — nothing is attempted. Therefore, to be sure, very much is *seen* of shapings in the pure subjectivity, and significant *stratifications* in them are discovered; but everything *hovers in an enigmatic milieu*, is accomplishment of *mythically remaining transcendental faculties*. The reader should mark the final diagnosis of Kant's deepest limit: his "sensibility" and "understanding" are *mythical faculties* whose accomplishments are postulated, not constitutive analyses of consciousness carried out to its innermost intentional sides — Kant sees stratifications but leaves the constituting consciousness itself in an enigmatic obscurity.

Perhaps it would have been otherwise, Husserl reflects, if Kant had been awakened from the dogmatic slumber not by the Hume of the *Enquiry* but by that of the *Treatise* — and if he had exactly studied this great ground-work of the English skeptic. Perhaps then there would have dawned for him, *behind the skeptical countersense, the necessary sense of an immanent intuitionism*, and the idea of an *ABC of the transcendental consciousness and its elementary accomplishments* — that idea which already Locke had. The reader should mark the poignant counterfactual: the whole defect of Kant's method is traced to his having known only the popular *Enquiry*, not the *Treatise* in which (as the Twenty-second Lecture argued) the first project of a pure, if sensualistic, phenomenology lay — so that Kant missed the immanent intuitionism and the constitutive ABC that even Locke had glimpsed.

In *one main point*, Husserl says — co-decisive for the enabling of a scientifically sufficient theory of the transcendental consciousness and of reason — Kant *remains behind Leibniz*. The latter has the precedence of having been, in the modern age, the *first* who understood the *deepest and most valuable sense of the Platonic idealism* and accordingly recognized *Ideas* as in a peculiar *Idea-intuition (Ideenschau)* self-given unities. One can well say, for Leibniz already *intuition as self-giving consciousness* was the *last source of truth* and of the sense of truth. And so every in pure evidence intuited general truth has for him *absolute significance*; it therefore lies near to him also to claim, for essence-peculiarities of the I intuited in such evidence, a *simply absolute* significance. But in Kant, Husserl says, the concept of the *a priori* sets us in *constant embarrassment*. The character of generality and necessity through which he characterizes it points to *absolute evidence* — hence, as we must expect, it would be expression for an *absolute self-giving* whose denial would be a countersense. But then we see at once that it is *not* so meant, and that the a priori lawfulness, through which transcendental subjectivity shapes objectivity in itself (according to its rational form, which precisely makes objectivity possible), has yet only the significance of a *general anthropological fact*. So Kant's critique of reason *misses the idea of an absolute ground-science*, which can impossibly be a priori in *his* sense, but only in the genuinely *Platonic* sense. The reader should mark the cardinal contrast on which the work's final argument turns: Leibniz grasped the a priori as *absolute essence-truth* given in Ideenschau (the Platonic sense), whereas Kant's a priori is ultimately a *general anthropological fact* (the necessary constitution of the human mind) — and an a priori that is merely a fact about human cognition cannot ground an absolute science. This is the decisive defect that keeps Kant's transcendental philosophy from being the last-grounding science.

For Leibniz, therefore, Husserl concludes, the thought lay *near* — although he did not pursue it — to *conceive a systematic science of the pure and absolutely necessary essence of an ego überhaupt* as subject of a consciousness-life, and of one constituting objectivity in itself: a science that, intuiting and then systematically deriving, brings out *absolute truths, and absolutely general truths*; an a priori science that is a priori in the *good and uniquely valuable sense*, insofar as it sets up nothing that would be deniable without countersense — and it would be a science that is *last source-science for all cognition and science überhaupt*, of the *deepest a priori in which all other a priori first constitutes itself in higher stage* (the note refers to Beilagen XXI, Hua VII pp. 395 ff., and XXII, pp. 408 ff.). With this culminating idea — the eidetic science of the *ego cogito* as the last source-science, the deepest a priori grounding all other a priori, lying near to Leibniz yet unpursued by him and missed by Kant — the *Kritische Ideengeschichte*, the main text of *Erste Philosophie I*, closes. The reader should register the arc the entire critical history has completed: from the ancient idea of philosophy as absolutely self-justifying science, through Descartes' discovery of the transcendental ground and his failure to grasp it, through the empiricist line (Locke–Berkeley–Hume) that pioneered the immanent intuitionistic method while sinking it in sensualistic skepticism, to the rationalist line (Spinoza–Leibniz–Kant) that defended the eternal truths and the a priori while remaining dogmatic and reconstructive — the whole history converging, by its successes and its failures alike, on the one demand it never fulfilled: a progressive, intuitive, eidetic science of constituting transcendental subjectivity, the phenomenology toward which the *Erste Philosophie* lectures are the historical-critical introduction. Beyond this point, at Hua p. 203, the volume continues with the *Ergänzende Texte* (supplementary treatises and texts), which lie outside the main lecture-course covered by this commentary.
